# Decisions

- A cell's journal update shares its calls and tool catalog, and a Claude checkpoint is serialized once (2026-11-10, Georgios: "yes on the Value trees, you can change the datatypes"). Profiled at `39276d8d2` (20,000 agents on JSONL, `sample` at 1 ms, output/durability-ledger `inclf.py` and `callersf.py`): `serde_json::Value` trees cost about 5.8% of CPU, as 2.0% building them from typed data, 2.4% serializing them and 1.4% dropping them. Two owners were concentrated. (1) `CellRecord::persist`, 2.8%: every update cloned each journaled call out of its `Arc` (its `input` is a `Value`), and every cell's first update cloned the session's tool catalog into the header, where `DurableCellJournals::save` serialized and hashed it only to find the key it had already pooled. `CellJournalUpdate::calls` is `Vec<Arc<JournaledCall>>` and `CellJournalHeader::tools` is `Arc<[Value]>`, both the same JSON as before; the sink keeps each live catalog's encoding by the pointer of the shared metadata, so it is encoded once per process, not once per cell. (2) `ClaudeBoundary::checkpoint`, 0.85% plus the drop: a child's checkpoint was built as a `Value` tree of its whole snapshot and serialized from the tree. `SessionCheckpoint`'s payload is `CheckpointPayload`, either a tree or encoded JSON; the Claude backend writes policy, version and snapshot straight to JSON, readers call `payload().decode()`, and a checkpoint still deserializes from any serde source because the payload reads as a tree. The stored JSON has the same fields, in declaration order rather than sorted. 20,000 agents, three interleaved runs at load 14 to 19: user CPU 51.0 to 51.5 s before, 49.4 to 51.1 s after. The swarm matrix, kill and resume (which replays the root's cell from its journal), kill twice, trees with and without hibernation, cancel, close and reopen end as before. Measured and left: the Claude turn's events are `json!` trees serialized in `State::publish` (0.9%), `nested_call` builds each nested call's metadata as a tree (0.6%), `persist_initial` and the execution policy take `Value` checkpoints (0.5%), and a `MessagesRequest` clone and drop (0.6%); each needs its own typed payload and none is large.
- Shared hash maps are `nanocode_sync::Map` and `Set`, not imbl's (2026-11-10, Georgios: "Yes on imbl"). imbl with imbl_sized_chunks and archery was 7.1% of a 20,000-agent swarm's CPU samples and 11.9% with its copies and frees (the entry two below): its hash trie stores keys and values inline in 32-slot chunks, so every copy-on-write step of an `Atom` copies and later drops whole chunks of `(String, AgentId)` keys and their values. `crates/core/sync/src/shared.rs` is a persistent hash trie that branches on 5 bits of the hash, keeps a node's children in a vector sized to its population, and holds each entry behind its own `Arc`, so a path copy clones pointers and never a key or a value; equal hashes share a bucket. It has the calls the workspace used (`get`, `get_mut`, `insert`, `remove`, `remove_with_key`, `entry`, `retain`, iteration, `Extend`, `FromIterator`); `Set::insert` and `Set::remove` answer `bool` as std does, and the four call sites that read imbl's `Option` are changed. All 27 `imbl::HashMap` and `imbl::HashSet` uses in sync, durability, subagents, code-mode, the Claude backend, sandbox and agent-host move to it, and `AtomMap::update` hands its closure the new map; sandbox and agent-host drop imbl. `OrdMap`, `OrdSet` and `Vector` stay on imbl: they are ordered or sequences, and were not what the profile showed. Checked with output/durability-ledger: `mapcheck` runs 4.4 million random operations against `std::collections::HashMap` over string, integer and deliberately colliding keys with snapshots held across later writes; the swarm matrix (memory, JSONL, SQLite, rows), kill and resume, kill twice, trees with and without hibernation, cancel and close, and three reopens after a clean shutdown all end as before. 20,000 agents on JSONL with mimalloc, three interleaved runs at load 16 to 18: user CPU 53.9 to 54.5 s before, 50.9 to 52.1 s after; resident unchanged at 2.0 to 2.1 GB. `cargo clippy --locked --lib` and `cargo check --locked --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` pass for the seven crates.
- `nc` allocates with mimalloc (2026-11-10, Georgios: "Comfortable also with mimalloc"). The system allocator was the function running in 17.4% of a 20,000-agent swarm's CPU samples, and `memmove` and `memset` another 12.7% (the entry below). `bin/nanocode` sets `mimalloc::MiMalloc` (0.1.52, no default features) as its global allocator; the library crates are untouched, so a host that embeds them keeps the allocator it chose. Measured with output/durability-ledger, whose `mi` feature sets the same allocator, 20,000 agents on JSONL, three interleaved runs at load 28: user CPU 61.3 s to 54.6 s, resident 2.6 to 2.9 GB to 2.1 to 2.2 GB. jemalloc did better on the same runs (52.2 s, 1.8 to 1.9 GB) and is not what was asked for. `Cargo.lock` gains the two packages and nothing else.
- 2026-11-10: **Where CPU goes by dependency, and djs against QuickJS at djs 0e58f62.** Measured, nothing changed (Georgios: "find again where time is spent in our dependencies, so we can find what to reimplement / pull in"). `sample` at 1 ms of seven runs of output/durability-ledger at `bd7dbf986` and one live `nc exec`, each sample given to the nearest frame that is not std, the allocator or libc (`deps.py`, `deptable.py`, `depcallers.py`, `incl.py`; symbols through `rustfilt`; kernel time kept apart). A 20,000-agent swarm on JSONL: our crates 33%, QuickJS 26.4%, serde_json 10.9% (14% with what it allocates under our calls), imbl with imbl_sized_chunks and archery 7.1% (11.9% with its copies and frees), arc_swap 4.7%, tokio 4.2%, blake3 2.6%, kernel 4.5%; by the function running, the allocator is 17.4% and `memmove`/`memset` 12.7%. The same swarm on memory, SQLite and rows differs only by the store (SQLite 6% plus 16 to 18% kernel); a hibernating tree is 41% ours, serde_json 16%, QuickJS 7%; one session of 400 turns is 42% `F_FULLFSYNC` (`sync_journal`) and otherwise the mock; reopening 100,000 agents is 44% file reads, serde_json 20%, blake3 5.6%, sha2 3%. The live CLI run (17 subagents, 8 s) burns 0.36 s: the journal's fsync a third of it, then serde_json 4.6%, QuickJS 3.4%, the OpenTelemetry trace writer about 4%, gzip of request bodies (miniz_oxide) 2.6%, TLS (graviola) 1.8%. What each is: (1) QuickJS is per cell, about 0.9 ms of CPU: a new context's intrinsics 6.1% of the swarm, loading the bootstrap bytecode 5.4%, installing natives 2.3%, the collection that tears the context down 6.2%, running the script about 7%; low-water trimming of spare runtimes was tried on 2026-11-10 and gave nothing. (2) serde_json: building, cloning and dropping `Value` trees 5.1%, serializing 5.0% (string escaping 2.3%, byte at a time; the JSONL store escapes every record a second time because it stores JSON as a string), parsing 2.7%. (3) imbl is the persistent maps inside `Atom`s: `code_mode::remote::executors::State` 3.7%, the registry's per-agent maps about 5%, session handles 1.8%; each write copies 32-slot nodes. (4) The allocator: the same swarm with mimalloc as the harness's allocator 61.3 s to 54.6 s of user CPU, with jemalloc 52.2 s and 2.6 to 2.9 GB resident to 1.8 to 1.9 GB (three interleaved runs, load 28). (5) blake3 hashes small records one block at a time, which on aarch64 is the portable code. djs (earendil-works/djs at 0e58f62, `djs-embed` 0.1.1) against the engine we ship, `jsbench` in the same harness, CPU per cell, our whole `CodeModeRuntime` for QuickJS (of which QuickJS itself is 90%, by `sample`) and a bare `djs-embed` loop that resets an instance to a snapshot per cell: `text('ok')` 416 to 430 us against 19 to 24 us; two tool calls one after the other 475 to 527 against 40 to 56; sixteen parallel tool calls 638 to 661 against 120 to 126; 20,000 objects through JSON 28.4 to 29.4 ms against 20.3 to 20.8 ms; split, join and `replace` with a regular expression and a callback 8.5 to 9.1 ms against 27.4 to 27.6 ms (djs three times slower; 4.4 times on 2026-11-02); 5,000 cells waiting at once 514 to 565 MB against 811 to 908 MB with a four-line shim (an instance is a copy of the image, 196 KB here and about 350 KB with our bootstrap). It differs in language too, as output/djs-experiment/NOTES.md found: code is always strict (an assignment to an undeclared name throws), no direct `eval`, no `performance`; it is not on crates.io and calls itself experimental. Not adopted here: the engine crate is 3,200 lines of Rust and 775 of JavaScript with journaling, replay, hold and release, bytes, preludes and a WASM build to carry over.
- An `Atom` write no longer takes every thread's debt slots (2026-11-10, Georgios: "one more optimization pass"). `sample` of a 20,000-agent run with the machine quiet (load 5) put 16% of all CPU in `arc_swap::debt::Debt::pay_all`, spread over some sixty `Atom` types: every `set`, `update` and drop of an `Atom` (an `ArcSwap`) walks the debt node of each thread that has used one (24 here: ten Tokio workers, ten QuickJS workers, the store's) and, on each, adds and removes a writer reservation and runs a compare-and-swap on nine slots, which takes each of those cache lines exclusively whether or not the slot holds the pointer; an agent's life makes a few hundred such writes. arc-swap 1.9.2 is vendored at crates/patches/arc-swap (`[patch.crates-io]`, like the two patches before it; its tests removed, nothing else) with two changes to `pay_all`, neither of which changes what it does. A slot is read before it is paid: `pay` is a compare-and-swap whose failure is a `SeqCst` load, so a `SeqCst` load that finds another value is the same observation without the write. And a node whose helping control reads idle is neither reserved nor helped: `help` returns at once on idle, and the reservation exists so that a writer holding a reader's generation cannot see it reused after the node's cooldown, which a writer that read idle never holds; a node that is mid-reservation is reserved and helped as before. Measured with output/durability-ledger, 20,000 agents on JSONL, interleaved: user CPU 73.7 s to 66.3 s with the first change and to 61.4 s with both (load 30 to 60, three runs each), 71.2 s to 61.1 s on the vendored copy; every child spawned in 1.9 to 2.2 s instead of 3.1 to 4.5 s; 2,000 agents 7.2 s to 6.1 s, a hibernating 30x100 tree 23 to 24 s to 18.8 s; `pay_all` is 9.6% of the profile with the first change. Checked: arc-swap's own suite on the patched source (57 unit, 6 random, 38 doc and 9 stress tests, and the 9 large stress tests one at a time with a 4 GB stack, which the unpatched source also needs: `storm_unroll_large` overflows the default stack in both), including the `full_slots` strategy that forces the helping path; then 2,000 agents on memory, JSONL, SQLite and rows, kill and resume and kill twice of 3,000, 30x100 trees with and without hibernation, cancel and close, clean reopens and `coldcheck`. Harnesses with their own `[workspace]` take the patch by naming the same path. Unsafe code stays forbidden in the workspace, which is why this is a patch to arc-swap and not a different cell in nanocode-sync. The same profile, for whoever goes next: QuickJS 16 to 22% (each cell reads its bootstrap bytecode and collects), the allocator about 12%, `memmove` 7%, the registry's `Atom` maps about 3%.
- 2026-11-10: **Codex asks the host before it runs a tool call, as Claude does.** `CodexBuilder::before_tool_call(Arc<dyn execution::BeforeToolCall>)` is the Codex driver's counterpart of `ClaudeBuilder::tool_hooks`' `before`, after Codex's own `PreToolUse` hook (openai/codex `codex-rs/hooks`): before a model's call that is not being recovered or replayed runs, the hook gets its session, turn, call ID, name and input (`ToolCallRequest`) and answers `ToolCallDecision::Allow` or `Deny(reason)`. A denied call returns `Tool blocked by host: <reason>; tool was not executed` and a failed hook `PreToolUse hook failed: <error>; tool was not executed`, as Claude's `hooked_handler` words them, as the call's failed result, journaled like any other, with nothing run. Tool search, whose output carries no text, is never asked. Like `before_compaction`, the hook is the root's: a branch's spawner sets none. Only `Deny` is offered, not Claude's `UpdateInput`, because no host needs it yet. Why: tempoxyz/pyramid's turn machine refuses a top-level call while a message the agent read mid-turn has no reply, so a member's steer is answered before the agent goes on; on Claude it does this with `ClaudeToolHooks`, and Codex had no way to. Checked on 2026-11-10: `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-codex` natively and `cargo check` for wasm32-unknown-unknown; an out-of-repo harness (output/tool-gate) against a local mock of each provider, whose first response makes two `exec` calls and the hook denies only the first, then one more call: on Codex (HTTPS Responses) through this hook and on Claude (Messages) through `ClaudeToolHooks`, the hook was asked once per call, the denied call's result was the text above and its cell never ran, the call after it ran, and the turn completed, 6 of 6 checks on each. Not checked against the live APIs.
- 2026-11-10: **Cold subagents through the CLI on the live API, and what the three changes below leave.** `nanocode exec` built from `22226aab6`, Haiku 5.5, its own `--home`: one cell spawned 17 subagents and waited for them (8 s); the saved journal is version 9 with a stub for leaf 0 (agents 1 to 15) and no deltas, and the index was saved at exit with no temporary copy left. Resumed with `--resume`, the session listed 17 agents, all completed (the cold leaf read back for the listing), sent agent 3 a message, waited, and got its turn's result; the journal folded to the same shape again. Left as it is: the CLI lists every subagent when a session opens (`refresh_agents`, to build its tree), which reads every cold leaf back, so the CLI opens a large tree no faster than before and gains only the memory once the listing is done; a host that does not list (the harness, an SDK caller) gets the faster open. A tree killed while its root holds unread completion notices has no settled leaf and restores whole, as before; the tenfold cut asked for on that store (2.0 to 2.3 s to the first resumed request at 100,000 agents) was not reached and cannot be by this route, because every agent there is running, unstarted or owed a notice. Of a clean 100,000-agent reopen's 1.3 s, 0.5 s is the JSONL index header (JSON for 170,710 states), 0.13 s the root journal state's 290,000-record block and about 0.15 s the message log, each read whole. A finished child still costs about 9.5 KB live: the JSONL index 4.4 KB, the root cell's journal 2.4 KB until that session's next cell, the registry's session and spawn maps 1.1 KB, the root's conversation about 0.9 KB. A 100,000-agent run's peak (7.1 to 8.7 GB over four runs of both builds) is set by the agents alive at once and did not move.
- `JsonlStore::flush` saves the index, so a runtime that shut down cleanly opens without replaying its journal (2026-11-10). `Nanocode::shutdown` ends with `StateStore::flush`, which for JSONL was an empty job: the index sidecar was saved only when a journal doubled and when its `Journal` was dropped on the store's worker thread, after the last handle went, which a process that exits right after `shutdown` does not wait for. The 100,000-agent harness left a half-written `.index-*` copy beside an index from 3.9 GB into a 4.8 GB journal, and the next open replayed the rest (`durability.jsonl.recover` 1.4 to 4.4 s under load); a 10,000-agent store 0.31 s. `flush` now runs `Sessions::flush`, which saves the index of every open journal whose index is behind (`Journal::flush_index`, what the drop does), and is awaited by its caller. After it the 10,000-agent store opens in 0.05 s with no replay and no leftover copy, and a finished 100,000-agent tree reopens (store, root session and subagent registry, `reopen.sh`, load 35 to 60) in about 1.3 s where the build before the registry changes took 2.5 to 2.6 s: the index header 0.5 s (170,710 states as 78 MB of JSON, not changed), the root session and the rest of the open 0.3 s, the registry 0.47 s instead of 1.6 to 1.7 s. Loading a state's record block also took a second pass over its keys to note which state first held each one; `decode_records` now hashes each key as it reads it (`durability.jsonl.index.records` for the root's 290,000-record journal state 0.12 to 0.13 s, 0.14 to 0.17 s before). Decoding the message log on every worker was tried and gave nothing (0.44 to 0.77 s against 0.46 to 0.58 s) and is not in. Checked: `jsonlcheck` as in the entry below, 2,000 agents, kill and resume and kill twice of 3,000, a 30x100 tree and three rounds of clean reopens on JSONL.
- The JSONL index keeps a record's key as its 32 bytes (2026-11-10). A state's record map was `HashMap<String, Record>`, and nearly every key is `b3:` or `c:` followed by 64 hex digits: a 24-byte `String` and an 80-byte allocation beside each 56-byte record. `records::RecordMap` keys the map by `RecordKey` (`Blake([u8; 32])`, `Chunk([u8; 32])`, `Text` for anything else, which round-trips unchanged) and takes and gives keys as text, so the index file, the journal and every caller are as they were. Measured with output/durability-ledger `heapdone.sh` (10,000 finished agents, JSONL): the store's live heap 5.42 KB to 4.38 KB an agent and 32.5 to 13.4 allocations an agent, the process's footprint 800 MB to 721 MB; what is left is the table slots themselves (96 bytes a record, tables sized to a power of two). Checked: `jsonlcheck` reads every record of every session of a 10,000-agent store and prints the same count, bytes and key checksum with the saved index, with the index rebuilt from the journal, after `compact` (which relocates every record through the map) and after reopening, and the count matches the journal read by `jsonlcheck.py`; 2,000 agents, kill and resume and kill twice of 3,000, 30x100 trees with and without hibernation, and three rounds of clean reopens, all on JSONL.
- Finished subagents leave memory and are read back when something names them (2026-11-10, Georgios: "Cut the rest too"). The registry keeps a `ChildSession` only for agents that are doing something; a leaf of the journal's agent tree whose 16 agents are all settled is cold: `AgentScope::cold` holds its record key and stub key, the task tree keeps each agent's parent and session id, `Sessions::by_spawn` its spawning call, and nothing else of it is in memory. Cooling (`AgentScope::cool`) runs in the journal writer's own registry call, so it sees the writer's cache: a leaf goes cold when it has a stub, every agent in it is settled in memory too (`ChildSession::settled`: no harness, task, hibernation, watcher, held residency lock or in-memory checkpoint), every record is the one the leaf holds (`JournalCache::written`), nothing is pending for it (messages, status jobs, captures, unsaved or unrecorded checkpoints), no operation pins it, and all its agents' children are cold or in the same leaf, so a warm agent always has warm ancestors. The writer offers a leaf when a fold writes its stub and when a snapshot finds an agent of a stubbed leaf touched but unchanged, and runs once more after either. Warming (`Registry::warm`) pins the leaves of the ids it is given and of every cold ancestor, reads cold ones back under one sequencer per root (`thaw`: leaf, profiles, `restored_session`, the same registrations a restart makes) and returns a guard that unpins on drop; cooling and warming an agent is a restart of that agent and nothing more. `wait`, `rewait`, `interrupt`, `close` (their subtree), message delivery and every other rehydration, `child_session_id`, `host_context`, `set_budget`, `respawned`, a reservation by a cold parent, and lookups by session id warm what they name; `list_agents` warms the page it returns (`directory_page`), `directory` with completed agents and `resent` warm everything, `resent_to` only the recipient; `running`, `stop_all` and `activity` read the agents in memory, which are all that can be active; `close_all`, shutdown and `cancel_all` act on the agents in memory, and a full journal write (`close_all`'s final one, a fresh writer) carries the cold leaves over (`JournalSnapshot::cold`). A restart reads a stub instead of a leaf and its 16 profiles unless a delta touches the leaf or a warm agent descends from it (`durable::load_leaves`). Two things this made visible are fixed with it. A root's completion notice now leaves its mark in the child's journaled `waited` when the root reads it, so a restart or a cooled child does not hand the root the same result again as new from `wait_agent`. And a clean shutdown could journal children as closed: `release_scope` marks them closed in memory, and a deferred journal slot open at that moment was filled with that state; this build opens such slots more often (unpinning wakes the writer) and hit it in 2 of 4 rounds, the build before in 0 of 12. `release_scope` now saves the journal first, a released scope answers every later save as frozen, and a slot open when the writer finishes is filled with the last payload saved (`refill`). Measured with output/durability-ledger, Claude mock, JSONL, load 45 to 100, against the build before (`swarm-a1`): memory of a finished child held by the registry 4.1 KB to 1.15 KB (`heapdone.sh` 10,000: the whole heap 21.6 KB to 18.6 KB an agent; what remains is the JSONL index 5.4 KB, the root cell's journal 2.4 KB and the session and spawn maps); reopening a finished 100,000-agent tree (`reopen.sh`, 6,250 of 6,251 leaves cold) restores the registry in 0.46 to 0.54 s instead of 2.5 to 3.0 s, of which 0.30 to 0.33 s is the 100,000-message log, which is still read whole, 0.07 to 0.09 s the stubs and 0.08 to 0.10 s placing them, and the process holds 0.92 to 0.96 GB instead of 2.05 to 2.09 GB; at 10,000 agents 40 to 55 ms instead of 130 to 150 ms and 170 MB instead of 236 MB. What it does not change: opening that store's 4.8 GB journal and the root session takes 2.1 to 7.6 s under this load, far more than the registry (`durability.jsonl.recover` 4.4 s of one 6.2 s open: the harness exits before the final index save finishes); a tree killed while its root still holds unread completions has no settled leaf and restores as before (a 100,000-agent run killed at 25,000 finished: 0 stubs); listing every agent warms every agent (0.33 s at 10,000, the same as before). CPU of a 20,000-agent run 72.6 s to 74.7 s, mean of four interleaved, including the final listing that thaws all of it. Checked: 2,000 agents on memory, JSONL, SQLite and rows; kill and resume and kill twice of 3,000 on JSONL, SQLite and rows; 30x100 trees on JSONL and SQLite, hibernating on JSONL; cancel and close with and without hibernation; a 100,000-agent run (6,245 leaves cold at the end, all listed completed after a thaw); `coldcheck` on JSONL and SQLite (session id, interrupt, a message, close and a budget on cold agents, then the same tree after reopening); `relcheck.sh`, 18 rounds of three clean reopens on JSONL, SQLite and rows with no agent closed.
- The subagent journal says which leaves hold only finished agents, and folds when the tree goes quiet (2026-11-10, journal version 9; first step of loading finished children on demand, Georgios: "Cut the rest too"). A level-0 page of the agent tree now lists, beside each leaf's record, a stub record for every leaf that is full (all 16 ids, 15 in leaf 0) and whose agents are all settled: completed, failed or closed, no turn in flight, no completion pending, no accepted output, not unstarted (`AgentRecord::settled`). The stub (`LeafStub`: the leaf's size and, per agent, its id, parent, session id and the call that spawned it) is what a restart needs to place such an agent in the task tree without reading its record or its profile; nothing reads it yet. Stubs are written when a leaf is folded (`JournalSnapshot::encode`, `AgentTree::set_stub`), cost 160 B an agent, and a writer set to version 8 or lower writes none. A journal also folds its deltas when no agent is active, owed a completion notice or being resumed (`Sessions::quiescent`, kept by `refresh`; `JournalCache::folds`), where before it folded only when the deltas outgrew the leaves, so an idle tree's journal is its leaves alone: after a 2,000-agent run 125 of 126 leaves have stubs and there are no delta pages (`output/durability-ledger/stubs.py`). A version 8 journal opened by this build is folded whole on its first save so every leaf gains its stub: on the 100,000-agent store killed at 50,000 that once costs 170 to 179 MB written instead of 82 to 99 MB and a first resumed request at 3.1 to 3.2 s instead of 2.4 to 2.8 s (`restart.sh`, interleaved, load 45 to 52); builds before this one refuse a version 9 journal. `Sessions::by_context` is a set per context, where it was a list searched on insert. Checked with output/durability-ledger: 2,000 agents on memory, JSONL, SQLite and rows; kill and resume of 3,000 on JSONL, SQLite and rows, killed twice on JSONL, SQLite and rows; 30x100 trees on JSONL and SQLite and hibernating on JSONL; cancel and close of 300 running agents with and without hibernation; all complete.
- 2026-11-10: **A session with no executable to name keeps no request template.** `4236c177f` wrote the template reference into every paged snapshot, but a rebuilt session only uses it when the snapshot and the process name the same executable, and on wasm32 there is none, so the record was written and never read. The snapshot now references the template only where `environment::build()` has an identity. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32; `output/cache-loss` `retool` unchanged (reads 17,507, writes 3,911 after reopening).
- 2026-11-10: **Where the restart of a 100,000-agent tree spends its two seconds, and what a tenfold cut needs.** Measured, nothing changed. `output/durability-ledger` `restart.sh` on the JSONL store killed with 100,000 agents added and 50,000 finished (1.65 GB journal, 130 MB index), stage timers added for the run and removed: the store opens in 0.55 to 0.72 s (`durability.jsonl.recover` 0.39 s reading the part of the journal written after the last index save, `index.load` 0.10 s); `subagent.restore` takes 1.29 to 1.36 s, of which the delta pages 0.18 s (36,254 changed agents), the 6,251 agent leaves 0.14 s to read (45.6 MB) and 0.19 s to decode and fold, the agents' profiles 0.36 s to read (100,000 records, one per agent, each distinct) and 0.40 s to decode, and building the sessions in the registry's owning task 0.28 s; the first resumed request leaves 2.0 to 2.3 s after start, with 2.2 to 2.5 GB resident. No stage is more than a fifth of the total, so no single change is a tenfold one: reading the profiles in pages like the leaves, keeping an appended index so a killed store has no long tail to scan, and overlapping the delta and leaf reads would together take it to about one second. Ten times needs a restart that does not read per-agent data at all: a head that lists the agents that are running, interrupted or owed a completion notice, the rest (finished and not yet started) left on disk and loaded when a message, a wait or a listing names them. That changes the journal format and every path of the registry that looks an agent up, and it is the same change that would stop a finished child from costing memory (22 KB live, 42 KB resident, of which the JSONL index 6.3 KB and the registry 5.7 KB; a completed child also holds its result twice, in its status and in `last_output`). Not started. Also today: a 100,000-agent run on JSONL settles in 75 s, one second after the last agent starts, with 12,000 to 14,000 completion notices pending throughout and no backoff tail (the tail recorded on 2026-11-10 was not reproduced on this store); peak 6.7 GB resident, 67 KB an agent. The ledger's mock answered compactions by matching the old prompt text; it now answers the `compaction` parameter with a block and the stock prompt with text, without which a 100,000-agent root fails its first compaction.
- 2026-11-10: **A Claude session rebuilt in the same build sends the system prompt and tools it sent before, and appends what changed.** A session's `system` and `tools` were fixed for as long as its state lived, but a reopened, reloaded or rehydrated session rendered them again, and a different tool list (a tool added, removed or described differently) re-keyed the whole conversation. The snapshot now references the request template the session last sent (the content-addressed record the turn cursor already pools) together with the identity of the executable, and a session rebuilt by the same executable for the same model keeps that `system` and `tools` byte for byte. What the current tool list has that the kept one lacks or defines differently goes out as `tool_addition` blocks carrying the definition, and what it no longer has as `tool_removal` blocks, in a `role: "system"` message after the prompt, with the `inline-tools-2026-09-15` beta (Anthropic's mid-conversation tool changes; Claude Code 2.1.304 carries the same header). What has been announced is read back from the history, so a compaction that summarizes the message away makes the next request announce it again, and tool calls are checked against the current handlers. A static system block the kept prompt does not hold is listed in the "A section of your instructions was updated" reminder next to AGENTS.md. A system message a later user message would follow is moved after it on the wire, since the API accepts one only between a user turn and an assistant turn or at the end. A request refused for its tool changes drops the kept template for that state, sends the current tools and retries once. A new executable, another model, a switch between Code Mode and plain tools, or a store that does not page records renders fresh as before, and tool changes left in the history are removed then. Forks take their parent's template. `output/cache-loss` `retool` (JSONL store, a session reopened with one tool described differently and one added; the mock refuses a misplaced system message and a tool block without the beta): before, the first request after reopening read 0 tokens and wrote 17,563; after, `tools` is byte-identical, it reads 17,507 and writes the 3,911-token redefinition of `exec`, later turns append nothing, a cell calls the new tool, the turn after a compaction announces again, and a second reopening with a tool removed appends one more change; `REFUSE_TOOLS=1` falls back to the fresh tools and completes. Live on the subscription wire with Haiku 5.5 (`LOG=1 live retool`): the reopened session sent the same `tools` (16,771 bytes, same digest) and one `tool_addition(exec)`, got 200, read 8,704 cached tokens and wrote 5,855, and the model called the new tool through `exec` and answered with its word and the code word from before the reopening; `live tools` shows the API accepting an addition by value, a removal, a redefinition and text beside them, and refusing them without the beta. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude-client -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32; `cargo check --locked -p nanocode-cli`.
- 2026-11-10: **A hibernating child that wakes during a cancel reads the interrupt when it resumes, not when the wake was planned.** The run that still stalled after `07226bbc6` had three children in the same state when the 30 s ran out: resident, turn marked interrupted, their harness already told to stop (`harness true hibernated false interrupted true told stopped true`). The wake had read "not interrupted" before the cancel marked the turn; the cancel then told the new harness to stop while it had no turn yet, and the queued resume re-attached the parked call as an ordinary turn. `Harness::resume` now asks the registry whether the turn is interrupted when it runs. `output/durability-ledger` `swarm` with hibernation, 500 agents, cancel at 350 to 500 ms: 160 of 160 runs end with every agent interrupted in under a second (before, about one run in thirty left children running for the 30 s). `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-subagents` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **The cache refresh, seen end to end on the live API.** `LOG=1 output/cache-loss live idle 3700`: a nanocode root session on the subscription wire (Haiku 5.5, one-hour cache) takes one turn, waits 3,700 s and takes another. The session sent one request of its own in between, 3,595 s after the first: unstreamed, `max_tokens: 0`, answered 200 with `stop_reason: "max_tokens"`, 8,526 tokens read from cache, 0 written, 0 output. The turn 3,701 s after the first, past the hour, read the same 8,526 tokens from cache and wrote 86; its reported usage holds both reads (17,052 cached input tokens). Docs only.
- 2026-11-10: **Cancelling a tree stops the children that had not started, and no longer waits 30 s on the ones it missed.** `cancel_all` and `interrupt` marked the turns that existed and waited for them to end. A child still queued for a turn slot had no turn, so it started afterwards and ran to completion; a child attached but not yet in its first turn got its interrupt before the turn began and then ran un-interrupted; and both kinds then blocked on the message pause the cancel was holding, so the cancel sat until `AGENT_STOP_TIMEOUT` and the children failed or completed afterwards. Three changes. A queued child that has not started becomes `Interrupted` and keeps its unstarted runtime; its queued start finds it no longer pending and stops, a later message starts it with its task and then delivers the message, and a restart restores it interrupted. The interrupt loops instead of waiting: on every change it marks whatever turn is active, tells each harness once and wakes each sleeping child once, so a turn that begins after the first pass is stopped on the next. And a message's delivery is settled by a task of its own once it is committed, so a sender whose turn is cancelled between the commit and the release no longer leaves a message pending for ever (which kept a hibernating tree from ever settling after a cancel). `output/durability-ledger` `swarm`, 500 agents, 300 ms model latency, cancel issued between 60 and 700 ms: before, any cancel that found queued children took 30.0 s and left them running (`before {"pending": 379, "running": 121} after {"interrupted": 288, "running": 212}`, then 212 completed and 9 failed); after, 27 of 27 runs without hibernation and 25 of 27 with it end in 0.02 to 0.4 s with every agent interrupted, one is the harness's own peer messages restarting children after an early cancel, and one hibernating run still left 14 running for the 30 s (hibernation is opt-in and nothing but this harness turns it on; open). The suite after the change: `matrix` 2,000 agents on memory, JSONL, SQLite and rows, and hibernating on JSONL and rows; `killresume` and `killtwice` 3,000; `treecheck` 20x50 both ways; `stopcheck`; `cellresume`: all complete. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-subagents` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **A compaction drops the request the cache refresh was keeping.** The refresh resends the last request a session opened; after a compaction that request is the conversation that was just summarized, so an idle root would have paid a cache read of the whole old conversation every hour for a prefix no later request reads. The kept request is now cleared when a compaction lands, and the next real request is kept as usual. `output/cache-loss` `keepalive compacted 320` (a root on the five-minute cache compacts, then waits): 0 refreshes, and the next turn writes its 5,483-token prompt (system, tools and the summary); before, it refreshed the 17,459-token conversation at 299.6 s. Also checked today against a mock that refuses a compaction request ending in an unanswered tool call or a block that is not first (`autocompact`): a compaction at the start of a turn, one in the middle of a turn (the summary covers the request already made, the tool round since then follows the block) and a second compaction over a block all complete on the server path; and asked on the subscription wire with Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.2 (`MODEL=… live warm`, `live compact2`): the unstreamed `max_tokens: 0` refresh reads the whole prompt and writes nothing on both, compaction behaves as on Haiku 5.5 in every case, and a block written by one model is accepted by the others (Opus to Fable, Fable to Opus, Haiku to Opus). `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **Cache refreshes are counted in the session's usage, and the traces say where refreshing pays.** A refresh's tokens and estimated cost are added to the usage of the next model call the session completes, so a turn's usage, a subagent's budget and the session total include them (`keepalive idle 320`: the turn after one refresh reports 34,916 cached input tokens, the refresh's 17,458 and its own 17,458, with the cost of both). The stop rule stays "as many refreshes as one cache write costs", the ski-rental bound: keeping the cache never costs more than rebuilding it once. Measured on the traces of 2026-11-04..09 (`/tmp/cache/idle.py`, `idle_root.py`, in input-token equivalents): for roots, forks and side conversations on the one-hour cache, 728 idle periods, of which 536 resumed within the hour, 35 in 1 to 2 h, 18 in 2 to 4 h, 10 later and 129 not in the trace; refreshing for up to 4 h would have spent 7.8M to save 62.3M, up to 12 h 16.8M for 70.5M, and the price bound (20 to 80 refreshes) 24M to 39M for 70.5M, so the bound is past the best point but well inside the gain. For subagents between turns on the five-minute cache, 875 idle periods: 421 resumed within five minutes, 108 later, 346 never; two refreshes would have spent 22.9M to save 28.9M and five 53.9M to save 45.7M, so a subagent that has ended its turn is still not refreshed. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **Claude compacts on the server, as Codex does; Claude Code's prompt is the fallback.** Anthropic's compaction guide says to use on-demand compaction wherever it is available ("Claude writes the summary on the server, so you need no summarization code of your own"); `8f066cbea` had adopted Claude Code 2.1.304's client-side prompt, and that binary carries no `compact-2026-09-04` header. A compaction is now the conversation as it stands, same system and tools, with `compaction: {"type":"summarize"}`, without `context_management` (the API refuses the two together) and with the `compact-2026-09-04` beta; the reply ends with `stop_reason: "compaction"` and one signed `compaction` block, whose tokens arrive in `usage.iterations` and are counted from there. The block is kept exactly as returned and becomes the first block of the first user message, followed for an automatic compaction by Claude Code's "Continue the conversation from where it left off…" line and, when messages are kept after it, by its note that the summary did not see them; every request whose first message holds a block sends the beta. A request the API refuses as invalid, or one that ends without a block, falls back in the same compaction to the prompt of `prompts/compact.md`; when the refusal names compaction the session stops asking. When a later request is refused for the block it carries (another route or model), the block's summary is resent as text under Claude Code's continuation line and the request retried once; both refusals close their step as `cf44244d2` does. Context-overflow recovery keeps the prompt path. Asked on the subscription wire with Haiku 5.5 (`output/cache-loss` `live compact2` and `LOG=1 live session`, about 25 small requests): the compaction request returns 200 streamed and unstreamed and reads the conversation from cache (27,906 of 27,972 tokens; through nanocode 8,606 read, 6 written); the block is accepted alone in a user message, followed by text in the same message, and followed by a user message; `context_management` on the compaction request is a 400 and on a request carrying the block is accepted; `tool_choice: none` is accepted; a session of two turns, a compaction, a question, a second compaction and a question answered "heliotrope, 4172" and "heliotrope", each post-compaction request reading the 7,830-token system and tools prefix from cache. Against the mock (`compact`): the server path, `REFUSE_COMPACTION=1` (falls back to the stock prompt, later compactions go straight to it) and `REFUSE_BLOCK=1` (summary resent as text, turn completes). Not verified: models other than Haiku 5.5, Bedrock/Vertex/Foundry routes, message threads. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude-client -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32; `cargo check --locked -p nanocode-cli`.
- 2026-11-10: **A model step the provider refuses is closed before the request is sent again with different history.** When Claude refuses the thinking signatures in a session's history (they belong to another API key's organization), the turn drops the thinking blocks and retries under a new step id. The refused step stayed `EffectPending`, and a durable operation may not complete with an unfinished step, so the turn that had just recovered failed with `invalid durability state: operation … completed with an unfinished step` and the session stopped accepting prompts. This is the located cause of the "invalid durability state" turn failure left open on 2026-11-09. The refused step is now completed with `{"refused": <error>}` where the provider error is returned, and a replayed step holding that value fails the same way, so a recovered process takes the same retry. `output/cache-loss` `unsigned` (JSONL store; the mock answers with a thinking block, then refuses histories that hold one): before, the second turn fails with that error and the third with "the agent stopped before accepting the command"; after, both complete, the retry carrying the history without thinking. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **The cache refresh is sent unstreamed, because the API refuses a streamed one.** The refresh of `6f49aa8cf` went out as a streamed request and had only met a mock that accepted it. Anthropic's pre-warming section lists what `max_tokens: 0` refuses: `stream: true`, `thinking.type: "enabled"`, structured output, a forced `tool_choice`, and batches. Asked on the subscription wire with Haiku 5.5 (`output/cache-loss` `live warm`, four requests): an ordinary streamed request wrote 27,514 tokens; the same request unstreamed with `max_tokens: 0` and adaptive thinking returned 200, `stop_reason: "max_tokens"`, read 27,514, wrote 0, 0 output tokens; streamed it returned 400 `stream cannot be true when max_tokens is 0`; the ordinary request then read 27,514 again. So the refresh now prepares the same body with `stream` off and reads usage from the JSON reply (`ClaudeClient::create_prepared`); nanocode sends adaptive or disabled thinking and no forced tool choice, so the other refusals do not arise. The mock now answers a streamed `max_tokens: 0` with that 400, and `keepalive idle 320` against it refreshes once at 299.6 s reading 17,458 and writing 0. On the installed `6f49aa8cf` build the refresh fails with that 400 and stops, which costs nothing. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude-client -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **A Claude session waiting past its cache lifetime refreshes the cache instead of rewriting it.** Traces of 2026-11-04..09 re-wrote 556.6M tokens on the five-minute cache; 474M of them (778 calls) were one turn waiting in a single tool call (a Code Mode cell blocked in a long Bash call 142M, cells polling with Bash and sleep 219M, `TaskOutput` 56M, `hands`/`runners` polls 25M, `wait_agent` 19M), 59M subagents waiting for their next instruction, 23M side conversations; idle roots lost another 34M on the one-hour cache. Anthropic documents the fix: the same request with `max_tokens: 0` reads the prompt, renews the cache entry and bills no output, at the cache-read price (0.025x of input on Fable 5.1/5.2, 0.05x on Opus/Sonnet 5.5, 0.1x otherwise, against 1.25x/2x for a write). `agent/keep.rs` keeps the last request a session opened (the messages are the shared `Arc`s the conversation already holds) and one task per session sends it again with `max_tokens: 0` when the lifetime, less twice the time that request took to reach response headers, has passed since the last request. It refreshes a root, fork or side conversation while idle and a subagent only while its turn is running; it stops after as many refreshes as one cache write costs (`ClaudeModel::cache_refreshes`: write price over read price, 12 to 80), when the lifetime has already lapsed, when a refresh fails, and when a refresh reads nothing or writes anything, until the next real request. Not done: refresh tokens are recorded on a `claude.cache.refresh` span but are not added to a subagent's usage or budget; a hibernated or evicted child has no state to refresh; a subagent waiting between turns is left alone because most never get another turn; `max_tokens: 0` has not been sent to the live API or the subscription wire from here. Checked against the mock with 200 ms of latency in `output/cache-loss` `keepalive`: `idle 700` (a root on the five-minute cache: refreshes at 299.6 s and 599.2 s read 17,458 and write 0, the turn at 700 s reads 17,458; before, it re-wrote all of it), `worker 660` (a subagent in an 11-minute tool call: two refreshes read 18,023 and write 0, its next request reads 18,023 of 18,109, and no refresh in the 22 minutes after its turn ended). With a zero-latency mock the refresh lands microseconds late, is seen writing and stops, which is the stop rule working. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-agent-types -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **Claude compaction asks with Claude Code's own prompt and continues with its own message.** The Codex backend already compacts the stock way (the server-side `compaction_trigger` item); the Claude backend sent a prompt of ours and wrapped the result in a preamble of ours. It now sends `prompts/compact.md`, the default (`control`) arm of Claude Code 2.1.304 rendered from the pinned executable (tools-denied preamble, the nine-section `<analysis>`/`<summary>` instructions, the closing reminder), still as one user message after the unchanged cached prefix. The reply is treated as upstream treats it: the first `<analysis>` block is dropped, `<summary>…</summary>` becomes `Summary:` and its trimmed body, runs of blank lines collapse to one. The next window opens with upstream's "This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context…" message, the `<tools-used-before-summary>` line (names of the tools called in what was summarized, carried over from an earlier summary), and for automatic compactions the "Continue the conversation from where it left off without asking the user any further questions…" line; when messages are kept after the summary, upstream's note that the summary was written without seeing them follows. The PostCompact hook receives the model's reply as written, as upstream passes it. Upstream's transcript-path line, file re-reads and the other prompt arms are not carried. `scripts/verify-claude-upstream.py` now renders the prompt from the official package (integrity and executable digest checked against `prompts/upstream.json`) and fails when `prompts/compact.md` (sha256 `63e2bfb4…db1fb0`) differs; it passes. Checked with `output/cache-loss` `compact`: the summary request's last block is the stock prompt and reads 17,537 of 19,168 tokens from cache, the next request opens with the stock message and `<tools-used-before-summary>exec</tools-used-before-summary>`, and a second compaction with no tool call in between keeps `exec`. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32.
- 2026-11-10: **A Claude session's system prompt is never re-rendered while its cache is alive; what changed is appended.** The date and the project instructions sit in the cached system prompt, so they stay as first sent, and a turn now opens with a `<system-reminder>` user message when either one differs: the upstream date attachment text ("The date has changed. Today's date is now …", from the pinned 2.1.304 executable) when the local date is no longer the one last told, and upstream's prefix-ledger wording ("The following changed since it was first sent in this conversation… A section of your instructions was updated:") with the current AGENTS.md block when a rebuilt session finds different instructions than it kept. What was last told is read back from the conversation's own messages, so nothing new is persisted and a compaction that drops the notice makes the next turn tell it again. A date fixed by the caller through `ClaudeEnvironment::date` is never compared with the clock. Before this a live session kept the date it started with forever, and a session restored inside its cache lifetime kept stale instructions without being told. A session rebuilt after its cache lapsed still renders fresh, which costs nothing. Tool definitions are already fixed per session state; a different catalog after a reopen still re-keys the prefix, and delivering that by `tool_addition` needs the `inline-tools-2026-09-15` beta checked against the live API first. Checked with `output/cache-loss`: `rollover` (one session, clock moved a day forward and back: system text identical on all 6 requests, one notice per move, every request reads the previous prompt from cache), `restore sub 0` (store reopened on another date with AGENTS.md edited: root reads 17,890 of 18,025 tokens and the worker 18,112 of 18,618, system identical, one date and one instructions notice each, none repeated on later turns) and `KEEP_AGENTS=1 restore sub 0` (date notice only). `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32.
- Where a swarm's memory is, and three things measured and left out (2026-11-10). output/durability-ledger with `MallocStackLogging=1` and `malloc_history -allBySize`, grouped by owner (`heapdone.sh`, `heapsleep.sh`, `heapcat.py`, `heapfind.py`), JSONL, Claude mock; about 43 MB of each heap is thread stacks and tokio and does not grow with agents. A child awake on a model call: 184 KB, of which the Claude turn's futures and request state 55 KB, the serialized request body 24.6 KB, the subagent harness future 15 KB, the durable session task 14 KB, the child's durable state 9 KB, event channels 7 KB. A child asleep on a model call (hibernation): about 17 KB live, of which the registry 4.3 KB, the parked call 3.7 KB, the root's cell that spawned it 5.2 KB, the JSONL index 2.2 KB; 39 to 48 KB of footprint with the allocator's slack. A completed child: about 22 KB live (JSONL index 6.3 KB in 33 allocations: one key string and one table slot per record; registry 5.7 KB; the root cell's journal entries 2.5 KB, kept until that session's next cell), 42 KB of RSS. No single owner is large enough for one change to halve these; what would is not holding completed children in memory at all (a registry that reads them from the journal on demand), which is not done. Measured and left out: (1) mimalloc and jemalloc as the harness's global allocator: CPU 36.4 s, 32.9 s and 31.4 s on 10,000 agents, peak RSS 1.19 to 1.70 GB, 1.11 to 1.40 GB and 1.04 to 1.33 GB, and for 20,000 hibernating agents a peak footprint of 1,086, 1,126 and 912 MB; a 10 to 20% gain that belongs to whoever builds the binary, and the CLI keeps the system allocator. (2) Dropping a JSONL state's record map once an index save has written it (reloading it from the index file on the next use): the index's memory after 5,000 agents went from 5.86 to 4.50 KB an agent, because the index is saved when the journal doubles and everything written since stays loaded; not worth making every later access of the default store a lazy load. (3) Restart of the 100,000-agent store is still 3.3 to 3.6 s (`restart.sh` on the store killed at 50,000); nothing was changed there, and the machine was too loaded by other work (load 30 to 100) to measure sub-second changes. Found on the way: at 100,000 agents the last third of every run is the parent consuming completion notices, which retry with a backoff of up to 300 s (`completion_retry_delay`); the stores are idle through it.
- RowStore writes on every connection its host has (2026-11-10, Georgios: "parallelizing RowStore seems good to do"). RowStore kept its record index (`Index`: where each record key lives, and each state's unsealed tail) inside the store value, so it had one handle and SharedStore ran one transaction at a time. The index is now an owning task (`nanocode_sync::Mailbox<Index>`) shared by every handle; a commit asks it which records are new and whether the tail seals (`Index::plan`, one call for a whole group), runs its transaction on its own connection, and tells it what landed (`Index::apply`). `group_writers`, `pipeline_handle` and `handles` hand out handles over `Sql::concurrent`, as SqlStore does, so SharedStore drives one group per writer and reads run on the caller's task; the first load of the index is taken in turn (`Sequencer`); page reads use the host's read access. Two states that first write the same record at the same moment may both store it, where the serial store stored it once; a record is only skipped once the write holding it has landed, because skipping on a write still in flight would let a state name a record a crash could lose. On SQLite the host has one writer and nothing changes. output/durability-ledger `matrix.sh`, Claude mock, interleaved with the build before on one Mac whose other processes kept the load at 30 to 100, so only the pairs compare: RowStore/Postgres 10,000 agents with 1,000 running 34.8 and 49.4 s before, 10.6 and 11.4 s now, and in a second pair 33.1 and 37.3 s before, 16.6 and 23.4 s now; with hibernation 85.1 and 115.8 s before, 29.6 and 30.6 s now; 100,000 agents with 5,000 running 493.5 s before, 252.5 and 319.0 s now, of which the last third waits on the parent's completion notices and not on the store. RowStore/SQLite 10.8 to 13.7 s before and 9.8 to 14.1 s now. Logical writes are 39.6 to 40.1 KB an agent on both; Postgres WAL 50.3 to 50.5 KB an agent on both; the Postgres table is 16.3 to 18.1 KB an agent against 14.6 to 15.4. `killtwice.sh` 10,000 agents killed at 5,000 finished and again after 1,500 requests recover 10,000 of 10,000 on RowStore/SQLite and RowStore/Postgres with and without hibernation; `treecheck.sh` 100 coordinators of 100 workers complete on both. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-durability-core` native and wasm32.
- Claude Code Mode declares the values its tools actually return (2026-11-10, Georgios: useful return contracts rather than Promise<unknown>). The native and host adapters now attach output schemas for Bash, Read, Write, Edit, TaskStop and the Nano TaskOutput extension. Input signatures, result constructors and error channels are unchanged: Read is rendered text or media blocks, Edit/Write are success strings, Bash distinguishes foreground output from background handles, and TaskOutput distinguishes running tasks, outcomes and failures. Host task statuses and its image-only Read block variant have their own schemas rather than borrowing native-only shapes. These are Nano runtime contracts, not copies of Claude Code's richer SDK objects assigned to incompatible strings. Real tool execution fixtures and projected TypeScript checks live in ignored output/claude-return-contracts-v1; the official reference audit examined 2,013 saved tool returns from 178 Claude Code 2.1.318 runs. Focused native and wasm32-unknown-unknown cargo checks use --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude. Tool descriptions change once on upgrade, so existing provider prompt prefixes may need to warm again; no per-call variation was added.
- A record with an empty value asks a store to drop it, on every store (2026-11-10). A cell journal blanks the records of a cell it forgets or replaces (`cell_journals::blanked`). SqlStore and RowStore delete such a record; JsonlStore and MemoryStore treated the empty value as different content for a key they hold and refused the whole write with `durability record … has conflicting content`. So on JSONL, the default store, a forgotten cell stayed in the journal's head, and a cell resumed after a restart could not journal again (`a Code Mode cell journal could not be saved, so the cell cannot be resumed after a restart`), which a second kill would have turned into a lost cell; the content-addressed links of a021386a8 made the resumed header's key equal the blanked one, in one write. JsonlStore now leaves an empty record out of the write (`validate_records`; it cannot drop what its log holds, and the head no longer names it) and MemoryStore removes it. Journals written before this that hold an empty record for a key keep it. output/durability-ledger `cellresume` (a cell killed 1 s into an MCP call, the store reopened, the turn resumed; `WARN=1` prints warnings): on JSONL the warning is gone and the resumed cell writes its header and updates as revisions 3 and 4 of the journal, where before it wrote only a head without the cell; SqlStore/SQLite and RowStore/SQLite are unchanged, each with one work run for a resumed call and the outcome-unknown error for a call that cannot resume. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-store-jsonl -p nanocode-durability` native and wasm32.
- A Claude session keeps the environment section and project instructions it was sending for as long as its prompt cache can still be read (2026-11-10, Georgios: "the prompt caching thing is very important"). The system prompt ends with the environment section (working directory, host, today's date) and the AGENTS.md instructions, both rendered once per `State`. Every new `State` for the same session rendered them again: reopening a session, a reload, a child evicted or put to sleep and rebuilt. If the date had changed or AGENTS.md had been edited the system prompt differed and the whole conversation was written to the cache again; in the traces of 2026-11-04 to 11-09 that is two subagents rebuilt after midnight, two roots reopened after an AGENTS.md edit (449K and 857K tokens rewritten) and a side conversation after a restart. Now the snapshot carries `context` (`SystemContext`: the date line, the key of the instructions, and the Unix second of the last accepted request, `State::requested_at`), about 110 bytes; the cursor's snapshot leaves it out because the cursor's template already holds the system prompt. A restored `State` (`resolve_context`) keeps that date and those instructions when the last request is younger than the cache lifetime it was sending (`cache_holds`: one hour or five minutes) and renders fresh ones otherwise, so a session resumed after its cache has lapsed gets today's date and the current AGENTS.md for free, which a process that stayed up never did. The instructions' text comes from whoever still has it: a fresh read whose key matches, the parent a child is restored under (`lend_instructions`), or, for a root, a record its paged snapshot references (`PagedSnapshot::instructions`, hydrated into `context.text`), so a root survives an edit of AGENTS.md; a child's checkpoint stores the key only, and a child whose text nobody has renders fresh. Not covered: a reload into a build whose tool definitions changed still rewrites everything, because tools come first in the cache order (four calls and 2.8M tokens in those traces), and a first turn killed before any checkpoint. output/cache-loss `restore sub <wait>` (a root and a spawned worker on the subscription wire, the store closed, AGENTS.md rewritten and `TZ` moved a day ahead, the store reopened, the root prompted and the worker messaged): before, both system prompts changed and the next request read 4,984 tokens and wrote 12,922 (root) and 13,406 (worker); now both are identical and the requests read 17,889 and 18,111 and write 16 and 341. With a 310 s wait the root (one-hour cache) is still identical and the worker (five-minute cache, lapsed) has the new date and AGENTS.md. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` native and wasm32; output/durability-ledger `matrix.sh` 10,000 agents write 39.7 to 40.5 KB an agent as before, and with hibernation on all complete and `killtwice.sh` recovers 10,000 of 10,000 on JSONL and SqlStore/SQLite.
- A child waiting on a model call can sleep: opt-in hibernation (2026-11-10, Georgios: "optimize memory way way much, sleep/wake subagents"). `SubagentControl::set_hibernation(true)`, off by default. A child parked on a model call is dropped from memory while its HTTP call stays alive in a detached task that buffers the response (`ParkedCall`, `Flight`, `ModelCall` in backends/claude `agent/parked.rs`; `LifecycleBackend::{set_parking, parked, park, unpark}` with defaults that never park, so Codex children never sleep). When the call finishes the child is rebuilt from its journaled checkpoint and the pending durable turn is run again, which adopts the buffered response only if operation, step and request digest (`PreparedWire::durable_input`) are the same; a mismatch drops the call, sends the request again and keeps the child awake for the rest of that turn, so a replay that differs costs one call and cannot loop. What is established and why: (1) a sleeping child gives its turn slot back and waits for one to wake (`Hibernated::capacity`, `Capacity::acquire`), so with hibernation on `max_concurrency` bounds the children that hold a runtime, not the model calls in flight; without that gate a 100,000-agent run with every child in a turn reached a 39 GB footprint because nothing bounded how many were awake. A wake caused by a message, an interrupt or a child's restore does not wait (`Capacity::overdraw`), since the sender may hold the slot the wake would wait for. (2) A child does not sleep unless its tools say nothing else is alive: `CodeModeHost::quiet` (default false), `CodeModeRuntime::quiet` and `CodeModeControl::quiet` (no live cells), `ToolRuntimeControl::quiet` (no live cells, no running tasks, quiet remote hosts), `ClaudeTools::code_quiet`; a child with a yielded cell lost it when it slept and its `wait` then failed. (3) A turn recovered after a restart does not sleep: its replay sends the first model call again each time, which looped. (4) Queued messages travel with the sleeping child and are failed like a live child's when it is closed. (5) Interrupt marks the turn (`ActiveTurn::interrupted`), wakes the child and cancels the turn in the same harness command, so the durable operation is cancelled as for a live child; close and shutdown settle it as stopped, the turn staying pending for the next start. (6) The event task of the runtime that went to sleep cannot close the one that woke (`ChildSession::hibernations`, passed to `forward_events`). (7) A restore locks the child's ancestors when hibernation is on, so a parent cannot go to sleep under a child that is being rebuilt, and a parent with resident or unstarted descendants neither sleeps nor is evicted when idle (`AgentScope::holds_descendants`); the idle eviction of a parent with unstarted children is a fix on the default path too, the build before failed one of 10,100 agents of a tree with `the agent stopped before accepting the command` in one of four SQLite runs. Not done: a sleeping child's event stream shows a cancelled run and then a new one; nothing caps model calls in flight when hibernation is on; a message to a sleeping child still wakes it for a moment. Measurements, output/durability-ledger, Claude mock, `QUEUED=1`, ten cores at a load of 4 to 18: 20,000 agents all in a turn, model latency 15 to 35 s (`memseries.sh`, JSONL): awake with 20,000 slots a peak footprint of 5,677 MB, 3,477 MB while all wait (174 KB an agent), max RSS 5,641 MB, settled in 137 s, 63,023 requests; hibernating with 2,000 slots 1,086 MB, 786 MB (39 KB an agent), 1,943 MB, 131 s, 62,400 requests; CPU 125+21 s and 203+32 s. 4,000 agents with 400 slots and a model that takes 3 to 6 s: 144 s awake, 18.3 s hibernating. 100,000 agents with 5,000 slots and a model that takes 15 to 75 s, all in a turn at once: all complete on JSONL in 347 s, SqlStore/SQLite in 381 s and SqlStore/Postgres in 448 s, footprint 3.3 to 4.1 GB while all wait and 13.3 to 14.3 GB at the peak, 300,196 to 301,082 requests. With hibernation on, `matrix.sh` 10,000 agents with 1,000 slots complete on MemoryStore, JSONL, SqlStore/SQLite, RowStore/SQLite, SqlStore/Postgres and RowStore/Postgres in 16.6, 8.2, 10.7, 25.1, 21.7 and 63.0 s without model latency and 18.7, 11.6, 13.9, 26.4, 23.9 and 65.5 s at 300 ms, at 41.9 to 46.8 KB of logical writes an agent; `treecheck.sh` 100 coordinators of 100 workers complete on all six at both latencies; `killtwice.sh` 10,000 agents killed at 5,000 finished and again after 1,500 requests recover all 10,000 on the five persistent stores at 0 and 200 ms; `stopcheck.sh` 200 sleeping children end interrupted after cancel-all and closed after close-all with 200 child requests. With hibernation off, interleaved with the build before: flat 7.7, 5.8 to 6.2 and 7.8 to 8.1 s against 7.6 to 8.0, 6.0 and 7.6 to 7.8 s on MemoryStore, JSONL and SqlStore/SQLite, tree 7.8 to 8.1, 5.8 to 5.9 and 7.3 s against 8.0 to 8.5, 5.9 to 6.0 and 7.4 to 7.5 s. Checks: `cargo clippy --locked --lib` native and wasm32 on nanocode-agent-core, nanocode-code-mode, nanocode-tool-host, nanocode-backend-claude and nanocode-subagents, native on nanocode (its wasm32 check stops in the nanocode-code-mode-engine build script, which wants a WASI sysroot).
- A checkpoint handed to a commit stays readable until that commit is stored (2026-11-10). A child's checkpoint record waits in memory (`JournalCheckpoint::pending`) until the subagent journal writes it. Since the journal fills at the root's commit (f250801f3), `fill_with` handed the records to the commit and marked them stored at once, so until that commit landed the record was neither in memory nor in the store, and a child rebuilt from its journaled checkpoint in that window failed with `checkpoint b3:… is unreadable: missing payload record`. Found while putting children to sleep in the middle of a turn (next entry): 3, 1 and 16 of 10,000 children on JSONL could not be rebuilt in three runs, and the run never settled. The payload is now one lifecycle, `Payload::{Unsaved, Saving, Stored}`: a fill moves it to `Saving`, which is still read from memory (`held`) and is not written again (`unsaved`), and `acknowledge_deferred` moves it to `Stored` when the commit reports success and back to `Unsaved` when it fails, also for a fill nobody waits on. Checks: `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-subagents` native and wasm32; output/durability-ledger `matrix.sh` 10,000 agents with 1,000 running complete on MemoryStore, JSONL, SqlStore/SQLite, RowStore/SQLite, SqlStore/Postgres and RowStore/Postgres in 8.3, 5.9, 7.8, 10.5, 9.1 and 25.4 s at 39.6 to 40.4 KB of logical writes an agent, as before; `killtwice.sh` 10,000 agents killed at 5,000 finished and again after 1,500 requests recover all 10,000 on JSONL, SqlStore/SQLite, RowStore/SQLite and SqlStore/Postgres.
- Where the swarm work of 2026-11-09 and 2026-11-10 stands on every store, against the build before it (2026-11-10, Georgios: "being done means showing that it works on all the databases, with 10x better perf, and very fast spawning time"). output/durability-ledger, Claude mock, `QUEUED=1`, one Mac with ten cores at a load average of 4 to 12; "before" is the harness built at the commit before a021386a8 (`swarm-base`). Restart (`restartdb.sh`: 10,000 agents with a model that takes 1 s, killed at 5,000 finished with 1,000 running, then `quiesce.py`: seconds from process start until the process uses less than a tenth of a core for a second, the model never answering): JSONL 5.95 to 6.23 s before and 0.37 to 0.48 s now, SqlStore over SQLite 6.25 to 7.14 s and 0.56 to 0.62 s, RowStore over SQLite 5.72 s and 0.63 to 0.68 s, SqlStore over Postgres 0.64 to 0.65 s and RowStore over Postgres 1.63 to 1.70 s now (the old harness has no Postgres host). The old build had handed out about 3,000 of the 10,000 IDs when it was killed and the new one all 10,000. All 10,000 IDs handed out in a run without model latency: 10.9 s on MemoryStore, 81.9 s on JSONL and 33.6 s on SqlStore over SQLite before; 0.9, 1.2, 1.0, 0.7, 1.5 and 0.7 s now on MemoryStore, JSONL, SqlStore/SQLite, RowStore/SQLite, SqlStore/Postgres and RowStore/Postgres (`matrix.sh` 10,000 agents, 1,000 running), settled in 7.5, 6.3, 7.7, 10.4, 9.1 and 24.1 s at 39.6 to 40.3 KB of logical writes an agent (80 to 88 KB at the start of this work), JSONL 67.9 KB of disk an agent (102 KB), Postgres 44 to 50 KB of WAL. The old build did not get through 100,000 agents: 10,927 IDs in nine and a half minutes on JSONL. Now 100,000 agents with 5,000 running complete on every store: JSONL in 68 s, SqlStore/SQLite 88 s, SqlStore/Postgres 85 s, RowStore/SQLite 113 s, RowStore/Postgres 246 s, ten to twelve times the 10,000-agent run, IDs out after 11 to 23 s, peak RSS 7.5 to 10 GB. A 100,000-agent store killed at 50,000 with 5,000 running is back at 5,000 waiting on the model 3.4 to 3.5 s after the process starts on JSONL, 3.2 to 3.5 s on SqlStore/SQLite and 5.3 to 5.6 s on SqlStore/Postgres; the build of 2026-11-09 took 8.2 to 8.6 s on JSONL. What is not done: the 100,000-agent restart is 2.4 times faster than the day before, not ten, because it still decodes every agent of the registry (1.2 s), reads the unindexed JSONL tail (0.5 s) and rebuilds each interrupted child's agent at about 1 ms of CPU; memory is 220 KB for a child waiting on a model (two 24.5 KB request bodies, 38 KB of futures, the rest in some 500 small allocations), about 280 KB more while it runs a cell (a QuickJS runtime), and about 42 KB of RSS for each completed child (JSONL index 6 KB, registry 3 KB, the finished cell of its parent 4 KB, the allocator's slack); RowStore still runs one statement at a time and keeps its whole index in memory.
- A store without pipeline handles looks again at the commits it passed over (2026-11-10). `drive_grouped` scans its queue for one write per state, and passes over a second write to a state that already has one in the forming group (`skipped`, `scanned`). A store that hands out pipeline handles resets both when a group completes; a store that does not (RowStore, MemoryStore) ran the group inline and went on scanning after the write it had passed over, so that write, and every later write to its state, stayed in the queue for good and its owner waited for ever. Since the queued spawn of 2026-11-09 two writes to one state are queued together often enough that a 10,000-agent run on RowStore over SQLite stopped with about a thousand store operations open and no CPU in 8 of 52 runs (output/durability-ledger `matrix.sh`, limit 1,000; none in 10 runs of the build before the queued spawn); it is also what stopped the MemoryStore run under MallocStackLogging at 3,929 agents. The inline path now resets both too: 24 of 24 runs complete. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-durability` and the wasm check.
- Idle children are evicted as a batch (2026-11-10, Georgios: "you can sleep/wake subagents"). `enforce_resident_limit` took one idle child at a time and waited for its harness and tasks to close before it looked for the next, so with children finishing thousands a second the resident set ran far past `max_resident`: 49,063 children were active at once in a 100,000-agent resume limited to 5,000 running, and the process peaked at 9.9 GB. `RegistryState::take_resident_evictions` takes every idle child over the limit in one pass and `Registry::evict` closes each on its own task; a child whose snapshot fails stays resident and ends that pass, as before. output/durability-ledger, 20,000 agents on JSONL with 1,000 running, two interleaved runs: peak RSS 2,600 to 2,787 MB before and 2,068 to 2,224 MB after, RSS once all have completed 1,351 to 1,638 MB before and 972 to 1,100 MB after, the run as fast (12.0 s). `killtwice.sh` on all five stores; clippy and the wasm check on nanocode-subagents.
- The subagent journal writes the fields that changed, and a restart decodes it on every worker (2026-11-10, journal version 8). A delta carried the whole record of every changed agent, about 0.8 KB nine times in an agent's life. The writer keeps the last written JSON of an agent that has a turn in flight or a completion pending (`CachedAgent::json`, at most the running agents) and writes `"patches":[[id,{field:value}]]` for those: the top-level fields whose text changed, `null` for one that went away; an agent without it gets its whole record as before, and `Changed::onto` applies the fields over the stored record on restore. `matrix.sh` 10,000 agents, limit 1,000: 47,455 to 39,720 logical bytes an agent on MemoryStore, 48,509 to 40,379 on JSONL with 77.9 to 66.9 KB of disk an agent. After a restart the save that records the resume attempts of 4,128 agents is 44 ms instead of 430 ms. A version 7 journal is adopted and written on as version 8 (`JournalCache::adopt`); a build before this one refuses a version 8 journal. Restoring: leaves and profiles are decoded by `platform::spread`, one task per core; a store that verifies the records it loads says so (`ChildJournalStore::verifies_records`, `SubagentStore::verifies_records`) and they are not hashed a second time; the conversations and checkpoints of the restored agents are recorded in one update each; and the router asks the session store once for the checkpoint and the provider (`SessionStore::recovery`), one peek fewer for every resumed child. Same store and measure as the entry above, on top of it: 3.03 to 3.13 s to have 4,267 children waiting on a model again, the first request at 1.9 s; 6.23 s or more before this series. A 100,000-agent version 7 store killed at 50,000 resumes to 100,000 completed. `killtwice.sh` on JSONL, SqlStore and RowStore over SQLite and Postgres. clippy and wasm checks on nanocode-agent-core, nanocode-durability, nanocode-subagents and nanocode-agent.
- JSONL reads a large batch on every core, replays a tail without loading the states it touches, and no longer loads old states to look for shared records (2026-11-10). The restart figures of the two entries below replayed the whole journal: `restart.sh` copied the killed store, a copy has another inode, and `checked_index` rejects an index whose journal identity changed. `fixindex.py` now points the copied index at the copied journal, and on the same store (100,000 agents, 4,267 waiting on a model, seconds until those 4,267 have a model request outstanding again, `TARGET=4267`, best of three interleaved runs) the build before both entries takes 6.23 to 8.43 s and the build of the entry above 4.98 s, of which the store opens in 0.82 s. Three more things in the store. `Journal::read_records` resolves the keys on the worker and, when they hold more than two read buffers, reads, digests and unescapes the values on scoped threads with positional reads (`read_spread`, Unix); restoring the registry reads 106,000 records, and `read` alone was 364 of the worker's 541 samples: 4.98 to 4.13 s. A replayed entry of a state whose record block the index has not loaded is added to the state's map without loading the block; `Index::load_records` puts the block under those records when the state is first read and rejects a repeated key whose digest differs, and `write_index` loads such states before it saves them: opening went from 0.78 to 0.53 s, 34,000 block loads fewer. `Journal::share` asked `holders` for a state that already stores a record and then walked back through every state the index had not loaded, loading as many index bytes as the commit wrote, to find one: after a restart that loaded 32,800 blocks, 1.04 s of the worker in the first 50 MB written, and put the whole index back in memory; of 608,777 records in this journal 25,727 are shared, all of them one 62-byte assistant message. A record is now shared with a state this process has read or written; the walk is gone: 3.9 to 3.03 s. `killtwice.sh` on all five stores, `cellresume` on JSONL. `cargo clippy --locked --lib -p nanocode-store-jsonl`.
- The JSONL store replays its tail on every core and its worker waits for no slot, stat or fsync, and readers skip the store's driver thread (2026-11-10, Georgios: "figure out how to accelerate the restart overhead 10x"). Measured with output/durability-ledger on a 100,000-agent JSONL store killed while 4,267 children waited on a model (`restart.sh`, `LATENCY_MS=600000 CAP=5000`: seconds from process start until 5,000 model requests are outstanding again, best of three interleaved runs at a load average of 9 to 15, each build including the ones before it): 8.31 s before. `Journal::consume` reads the unindexed tail in 64 MiB blocks, parses the lines and digests their record values on scoped threads and applies them in order; a batch group cut by a block is carried into the next, which reads at least twice what it carried, so a group is parsed a bounded number of times: 7.51 s (the replay of a 955 MB journal without an index 2.19 s to 1.32 s). Jobs queue without a bound: with a capacity of one every job cost the worker a park and a tokio wake, and it sat parked in 1,703 of 5,507 samples with 60,000 reads waiting. The reads of one drain of the queue share one `stat` of the journal and its index (`Journal::read`): a job queued before that stat sees a state no older than its request; writes still check every time. The worker appends without syncing, and a second thread, `nanocode-jsonl-sync`, syncs the files of each drain and then answers its writers, so reads and later appends run during an fsync (298 of the worker's 1,086 busy samples were `fcntl`); a failed fsync now stops the store, where it failed one commit whose lines stayed in the journal. An acquisition is answered once applied and is made durable by the next write: it carries no data, and an OS crash leaves no owner to fence. `StateStore::handles` hands out `Send + Sync` handle sources (JSONL; SqlStore natively), and `SharedStore` peeks and reads through one on the caller's task instead of crossing the single `nanocode-durability-store` thread twice. With the journal change of the entry below in both: 6.77 s without these four and 6.19 s with them. A 10,000-agent run (`matrix.sh`, limit 1,000) is as fast or faster on every store: JSONL 6.30 to 5.83 s, SqlStore over Postgres 10.1 to 8.8 s. `killtwice.sh` (3,000 agents killed at 1,500 finished, resumed, killed again at 1,500 requests, resumed): 3,000 completed on JSONL, SqlStore and RowStore over SQLite and Postgres; `cellresume` and `statecheck 300 600` pass. `cargo clippy --locked --lib` on nanocode-durability-core, nanocode-durability, nanocode-store-jsonl and nanocode-store-sql, and `cargo check --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` on the first, second and last.
- A restored subagent journal is written on from where it stopped, and interrupted children resume on every worker (2026-11-10).
