The journal writer started every process with an empty `JournalCache`, so the first save after a restart folded the whole task tree again: 100,000 profiles, 6,250 leaves and their pages, 142 MB of records and 0.93 s before the first child resumed. `Registry::restore` now rebuilds the cache from what it reads (`AgentPages::tree`, the stored text of each agent record and message, the delta levels and their bytes) and hands it to the writer (`JournalRequest::Stored`, taken only by a cache that has written nothing and writes the same version); a session restored unchanged keeps its generation and is not marked changed, so the save that records the resume attempts writes those agents as one delta. A journal in an older layout still folds once. Then `resume_interrupted` ran every child's rehydration inside one `join_all`, on one worker (686 of 736 samples of one thread); each is a task now, and `Ownership::children`, an `Atom` of an imbl vector copied on every insert, which ten threads retried against each other (about 2,000 of 4,331 busy worker samples), is a `nanocode_sync::Inbox` pruned when its count doubles. Same measure as the entry above (100,000 agents, 4,267 waiting on a model, seconds until 5,000 requests are outstanding): 7.51 to 6.77 s from the cache, 6.19 to 5.92 s from the tasks and the inbox; 8.31 s before both entries. The first request after a restart comes at 4.3 s instead of 5.9 s, and the restart writes 187 MB instead of 262 MB on the store killed without model latency. `killtwice.sh` as above on all five stores. clippy and wasm checks on nanocode-subagents and nanocode-agent.
- A tool offered again under the same name is skipped without a warning (2026-11-09). `first_normalized_definitions` keeps the first definition of each Code Mode name and warned about every later one. With a runner pool, `route_workspaces` puts a `RunnerProvider` before the providers it wraps, so every tool of an MCP server is offered twice by design, routed and then plain, the plain one is dropped, and each cell logged a warning a tool as it started: 4,124,782 in this machine's traces of 2026-11-06 to 2026-11-09, 127 for each of 32,363 cells with the two servers `nc` attaches. A warning is a 672-byte span event, so they were 2.8 GB of an 8.8 GB trace directory, and 54% of the bytes of a 248 MB sample of recent files. A repeated name is the same tool, which the first provider serves; only another name with the same identifier leaves a tool unreachable, so only that is warned about now (a build already refuses both among the providers it starts with). output/cache-loss's `dups` (127 definitions from one provider, 300 cells of `text(String(ALL_TOOLS.length))`, warnings counted by a subscriber): with a runner pool 127 warnings a cell before and none now; a second name with one identifier arriving after the build, 1 a cell before and now; the provider alone, none. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-tool-host`, again for wasm32-unknown-unknown, and clippy.
- A queued `spawn_agent` returns once the child is journaled, and the child is built when a turn is free (2026-11-09, Georgios: "very fast spawning time … this is just a concurrency thing so you can sleep/wake subagents"). With `set_queued_spawns(true)` a `spawn_agent` call waited for a free turn, then built the child's agent and session before it answered, so a script that spawned 10,000 children under a limit of 1,000 held 9,000 calls open, each with its future and its journaled call, and the ten-thousandth child got its ID only when the 9,001st turn came free. Such a call now validates what it can without the child (budget, policy, output contract, the options against the spawn policy when no router decides them), reserves the ID, and inserts a session that has no runtime: status `Pending`, `StoredRuntime::Unstarted` holding the spawn options (`durable::Unstarted`, journaled in the agent's record), a session ID the factories honor (`SpawnOptions::session_id`, Claude and Codex). One journal save later the call returns. A single task per registry takes queued children in order as turns come free (`Registry::enqueue_unstarted`, the capacity's spawn queue) and `tools::start_unstarted` does what `start_child` did after admission: routes, builds, admits the child's settings, attaches the harness and starts the first turn; a child that cannot start fails with that error as its completion. After a restart a child that never started is queued again under a new session ID (`Unstarted::restored`, `TaskTree::rebind`), because a crash may have left a session under the old one. Without `set_queued_spawns` nothing changes. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger (`matrix.sh`, `SWARM flat 10000`, `MAX_CONCURRENCY=1000 QUEUED=1`, this tree against the build before it): all 10,000 children have their IDs after 0.8 s on MemoryStore instead of 7.9 s, 1.1 s instead of 9.9 s on JSONL, 1.0 s instead of 21.8 s on SqlStore over SQLite, 1.0 s instead of 11.5 s on RowStore over SQLite, 1.4 to 2.1 s instead of 8.3 to 9.2 s on SqlStore over Postgres, 1.4 s instead of 24.1 s on RowStore over Postgres; the swarm settles in 7.9 s instead of 8.3 s on JSONL, 10.9 s instead of 22.3 s on SqlStore over SQLite (530 KB written to disk per agent instead of 1.5 MB, 111,496 commits instead of 137,562), and about the same on the others. `killresume.sh` with 1,900 to 2,500 children still unstarted at the kill ends with 3,000 completed on all five durable stores.
- A journal waiter is answered when its write commits (2026-11-09). The subagent journal defers its writes (`save_deferred`: the store asks for the journal's content when it is about to commit it), and `write_journal` answered every waiter the moment a deferred write was reserved, before anything was written. `submit_result` is such a waiter: it told the child `accepted, durable: true` and the cell journal recorded that answer, while the acceptance itself could still be lost. A process killed in between restored the child with a turn whose script had been told its result was accepted and a registry that held none, and the turn ended as failed. `DeferredJournal` now delivers a `JournalCommit` beside the `JournalFill`, the store's reply to the deferred replace settles it (`child_journal::save_deferred`), and the journal writer keeps its waiters until the write that holds their change commits (`acknowledge_deferred`). Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger on SqlStore over Postgres with 16 lanes, where commits to different states overlap and the gap was widest: 3,000 agents killed at 900 to 2,400 completions resumed with 1 to 5 failed agents in five of eight runs before, and with 3,000 completed in eight of eight after.
- A recovered turn takes its revision from the turn, not the journal (2026-11-09). `harness_turn_started` gave a turn it was recovering the journal's `next_instruction_revision`. A child's own session commits its turn before the journal's next save says the turn began, so a process killed between the two restored a registry one revision behind the turn it resumed, and the turn's `submit_result` (carrying the revision of its prompt) was answered `superseded`. The harness decodes the pending prompt first and the registry takes the larger of the two revisions. Found by the same Postgres runs as the entry above (`turn 0, instruction 1` in a debug print of the refusal).
- A call whose record another save carried does not save the journal again (2026-11-09). Since calls queue their records, a call waiting for the save found its own record already written, but still saved whatever entries had been added meanwhile, and with children that return at once (queued spawns) each of 3,000 calls wrote a link holding one result: 2,832 commits and one disk flush each, 35.7 s to hand out 3,000 IDs. `CellRecord::persist` returns when every call it was asked to record is already saved at that state or a later one. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger (`SWARM flat 3000`, `MAX_CONCURRENCY=3000 QUEUED=1`, JSONL): the IDs are out after 0.78 s, the cell journals take 12,007 commits instead of 14,833, and 33,620 commits and 211 MB reach the disk instead of 44,216 and 401 MB.
- A Claude Code login too long for `security -i` is saved through the command's arguments, as Claude Code does (2026-11-09). `write_keychain` hex-encodes the login into one `add-generic-password` line for `security -i` and refused a line over 4,032 bytes (`KEYCHAIN_COMMAND_LIMIT`, Claude Code's number), and it refused after `exchange` had traded the refresh token for a new one. On 2026-11-09 the login was 2,024 bytes (four MCP servers' entries beside the OAuth tokens, a 4,121-byte line): the process that refreshed it failed with "exceeds the 4032-byte security -i command limit", the keychain kept the spent refresh token, and within 25 seconds five more turns in four other processes failed with `invalid_grant: Refresh token not found or invalid`, some after 40 to 100 model calls; the login could only be restored by signing in again. Claude Code 2.1.316 passes the same command as arguments when the line is too long ("Keychain payload (…B JSON) exceeds security -i stdin limit; using argv"), and `write_keychain` now does that, with a warning; `save` still reads the item back and compares the access token. Checked on macOS 26.3.1 with a throwaway item: a 5,036-character payload through `security -i` exits 1 with `unknown command` and leaves a truncated item, so the limit stands; through arguments 2,518 bytes and then 3,118 (an update in place) read back identical. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude-client`, again for wasm32-unknown-unknown, and clippy.
- A Claude request whose upload the server's TLS layer rejects is sent again at once (2026-11-09, asked for by Georgios: "I think this does happen a lot, the bad record mac thing so it's time for you to go and fix it"). In this machine's traces from 2026-11-04 12:00 to 2026-11-09, 3,103 of 91,130 Messages attempts (3.4%) failed within a second, before any response, and of the 1,378 retried from 2026-11-08 on, 1,335 read `received fatal alert: BadRecordMac`: the server could not authenticate a TLS record of the request. The share grows with the upload (none of 328 under 50 KB gzipped, 0.6% at 50 to 150 KB, 1.6% at 150 to 400 KB, 4.0% at 400 to 800 KB, 5.8% above 1.5 MB) and depends on the network: on one, 1 of 5,187 attempts failed so, on the other 2 to 8% in every hour. Each cost the upload so far (median 185 ms) and the 500 to 625 ms backoff before the retry, which then succeeded 374 times of 418. The cause is outside this SDK, checked with output/tls-mac. `aead`: graviola 0.4.1 and ring give the same ciphertext and tag for every record length from 0 to 16,500 bytes at 16 alignments under AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCM and ChaCha20-Poly1305 (792,048 records). `upload`: reqwest 0.13.4 over rustls 0.23.45 with graviola sent 30 GB in 18,394 requests to a local rustls (ring) server that reads slowly, beside 10 spinning threads, and every body arrived as sent. On the failing network, curl with LibreSSL, connecting directly and so with no part of this SDK, uploaded 4 MB bodies to three speed-test endpoints in turn: 8 of 60 to Cloudflare's failed with `bad record mac`, and none of 60 to each of two servers outside Cloudflare; through `nc`'s forward proxy, which tunnels these hosts, 11 of 60, 0 and 0; rustls with graviola, rustls with ring and curl each failed once or twice in 96 MB to Cloudflare's, and over IPv4 alone 7 uploads failed in 300 MB. So the bytes change between this machine and Cloudflare, which serves api.anthropic.com, or inside it, on that network alone, and nothing here prevents that. What changes: `nanocode_tls::peer_discarded_record(&error)` finds a rustls `AlertReceived(BadRecordMac | RecordOverflow)` among an error's sources, inside an `io::Error` too; those two alerts say the peer discarded a record it was sent, so it cannot hold the whole request. `send_reqwest` (the Claude client's native transport) then sends the same request once more on a new connection without waiting, with a warning and `request.resent` on the attempt's span; a second such failure reaches the retry loop and its backoff as before, now as `unsent`, so a request with server tools is retried instead of failed with a recovery notice. `resend` puts a proxy that flips one bit between `ClaudeClient::create` and a local rustls server: with one record of the upload corrupted the call failed (`BadRecordMac`, one connection) and now succeeds over a second connection, the server answering once; with the resend corrupted too it fails as `unsent` after two connections; a corrupted record of the response fails as it did (`cannot decrypt peer's message`, not `unsent`, not sent again, the server having answered). Not changed: every other failure's backoff, and a reset or broken pipe during the upload (13 of the 1,378), which does not say what the server read. `cargo check --locked --lib` of nanocode-tls, nanocode-backend-claude-client and nanocode-backend-claude, the last two again for wasm32-unknown-unknown, and clippy.
- A sqlx Postgres host says how many connections its stores may use at once (2026-11-09). `SqlStore` already runs commit groups and reads side by side when its host offers `Sql::concurrent` and more than one writer, but `SqlxSql` offered neither, so every state of a swarm went through one transaction at a time and each waited for the one before it to reach disk. `PgConnections::lanes` (1 by default, so a host that says nothing is as before) is what `SqlxSql` reports as its writers and readers, and with more than one it hands itself out as the concurrent host. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger against a local Postgres 15 (`fsync` and `synchronous_commit` on, `autovacuum_naptime` 1 s so a database that starts empty has statistics within a second; without them the cached plans of `GROUP_HOLDS_PG` scan `records` and twelve backends sit in that statement), `SWARM flat 10000` on SqlStore, `MAX_CONCURRENCY=1000 QUEUED=1`: 9.4 to 17.2 s with 16 lanes (four runs, the slower two under a load average of 90) and 51.4 s with `PG_CONNECTIONS=1` on the same build. RowStore keeps its index in the store handle and still runs one transaction at a time: 25 to 30 s on Postgres.

- A root's sessions keep the records they all hold in one pool (2026-11-09, Georgios: "design all of these from scratch … bump all of them so don't worry about backwards compatibility"). Every Claude child wrote its own copy of the request template (system prompt and tool definitions, 22 KB in two chunks and a manifest) and every child's cell journal its own copy of the tool catalog (14 KB): 36 KB of the 81 to 88 KB a three-request subagent handed its store, identical across the children of a root. `SharedStore::pool(state_id, records)` writes records once to the state `<root>:pool` (its head lists their keys, so they stay reachable; one writer per process, which reacquires once if fenced), holds them in memory, and `read_records` answers from that memory before the store and falls back to the pool for a key the state itself lacks. A state's root is its session's root: `DurableSession::prepare` tells the store when it is given the record (`adopt`), and a head that passes through `peek` or `acquire` names it (`Pools::observe` reads `nanocode_session.lineage`), so a restored child's root is known before anything reads its records. Claude pools its template before the first continuation that names it (`ClaudeExecutionPolicy::pool`, false by default so other policies still inline it; `DurableSession::pool`) and the cell journal pools its catalog. No reader of an older store is kept: a continuation written before this still carries its own template and reads as before, but stores written now need this build. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger (`matrix.sh`, `SWARM flat 10000`, `MAX_CONCURRENCY=1000 QUEUED=1`, 8dc23e36f then this tree): bytes handed to the store per agent are 46.0 KB instead of 83.7 KB on MemoryStore, 46.1 instead of 85.1 on JSONL, 54.3 instead of 88.5 on SqlStore over SQLite, 47.6 instead of 84.1 on RowStore over SQLite, 44.0 instead of 80.3 on SqlStore over Postgres 15 and 43.8 instead of 80.7 on RowStore over Postgres; JSONL writes 79 KB to disk per agent instead of 102 KB. Postgres writes the same 50 to 53 KB of WAL per agent either way, because SqlStore and RowStore already stored a repeated record once: what the pool saves there is sending and hashing it. `killresume.sh` (3,000 agents killed at 1,500 completions, then resumed) ends with 3,000 completed on JSONL, SqlStore and RowStore over SQLite, and SqlStore and RowStore over Postgres; `cellresume` and `statecheck 300 600` pass.

- An image `Read` peaks at a third of the memory, at the speed and to the bytes it had (2026-11-09). `nanocode_image::lanczos3` (3f148d8ec, the entry about `Read` below, which measured time and not memory) ran each pass over the whole image: the vertical pass wrote every output line as f32 (source width × output height × channels × 4 bytes) and the horizontal pass read a transposed copy of all of it, so a `Read` of a 2880x1800 screenshot peaked at 88.2 MB where `image`'s resize, with its one RGBA f32 intermediate, had peaked at 64.9, and as WASM, whose memory never shrinks, resizing a 4000x3000 image grew the module to 120.6 MiB where `image` grew it to 95.4. `resize` now works a band of output lines at a time: as many as fit `BAND_BYTES` (2 MiB as f32, and at least one) are resampled down, transposed, resampled across, transposed back and appended to the output, in four buffers sized once, so a reduction holds about 5 MiB beyond its source and its output whatever their size. The sums and their order are the same, so the bytes are. Peak resident memory of a process doing one `Read` (output/image-bench: `ROUNDS=1 /usr/bin/time -l read <one sample>`, median of 3, M1 Max) at bfe6249dd, at 3f148d8ec and now: 1440x900 PNG 33.0, 46.6 and 18.0 MB; 1280x2400 PNG 50.3, 69.3 and 24.5; 2880x1800 JPEG 65.2, 88.4 and 30.1; 2880x1800 PNG 64.9, 88.2 and 30.4. As WASM in Node 22 (`wasm/memory.mjs`: linear memory with the source decoded, then after one resize by `image`, by 3f148d8ec and now): 1440x900 4.8 MiB, then 26.8, 32.8 and 13.3; 2880x1800 15.9, then 56.5, 71.9 and 24.1; 4000x3000 35.4, then 95.4, 120.6 and 43.3; 1280x2400 9.9, then 41.9, 53.1 and 18.1. Time against 3f148d8ec: the resize alone (`resize`, best of 15 in each of 6 interleaved runs) 7.68 and 7.76 ms (1440x900), 13.43 and 12.71 (1280x2400), 4.48 and 4.29 (390x3200), 18.94 and 19.98 (2880x1800); as WASM 36.0 and 34.0, 80.7 and 77.7, 148.1 and 144.9, 56.3 and 54.3 ms; the nine samples' `Read`s together 171.5 and 172.9 ms of CPU (`read`, 5 runs of 9 rounds, load average 30 to 90). The band is the fastest of a sweep (`BYTES=1 bands`, a copy of the resampler that takes the band as an argument; 256 KiB, 512 KiB, 1, 2, 4 and 8 MiB, then the whole image): 1440 wide 13.5, 11.2, 9.4, 8.3, 9.4, 11.3 and 13.6 ms; 1280 wide 19.8, 16.1, 13.9, 12.8, 15.0, 17.4 and 22.0; 2880 wide 33.1, 26.5, 23.1, 20.1, 20.2, 23.2 and 32.7; 390 wide 5.1, 4.6, 4.6, 4.6, 6.6, 7.8 and 7.9. A smaller band leaves the horizontal pass lines too short to vectorize well and a larger one transposes out of cache. As WASM 2 MiB is within 4% of the best of those (8 MiB) up to 2880 wide and 7% behind it at 4000 (314.7 against 293.4 ms). Checked: `equiv2` resizes 4,400 random 8-bit images of the four layouts (sides of 1 to 4,000 pixels to sides of 1 to 1,600, 689 of them in more than one band) and none of 1,359,404,566 samples differ from `image`'s, 800 more with 16-bit ones among them, which still go to `image`; the bytes `Read` returns and the bytes the agent keeps are those of bfe6249dd and of 3f148d8ec for all nine samples (`DUMP`); `cargo check --locked --lib` of nanocode-image and nanocode-backend-claude-tools, each again for wasm32-unknown-unknown, and clippy. Tried and left: resizing in `load_for_prompt_bytes` with these bands and upstream Codex's Triangle filter in place of `thumbnail_exact`, which 49712b9ad chose because `image`'s Triangle builds a source-width × output-height RGBA f32 intermediate (measured here as WASM: 233.9 MiB for 4000x3000 to 3695x2771, where `thumbnail_exact` takes 64.8). In bands it gave `image`'s Triangle bit for bit (4,000 random images, 8- and 16-bit, 544,931,562 samples) and prepared a resized image 1.5 to 2.5 times sooner natively (2880x1800 PNG to 1996x1248: 92.9 to 48.1 ms of CPU, `prepare`) and 1.13 to 1.29 times sooner as WASM (`wasm-prepare`), but it peaked 4.5 MiB above `thumbnail_exact` (66.9 to 71.4 MiB for a 12 MP JPEG as WASM, where the budget of 49712b9ad was measured), its PNGs were 2 to 44% larger (393,210 to 412,447 bytes for that screenshot, 504,835 to 661,138 for 1280x2400 to 1092x2048) and its JPEGs 3 to 6% smaller, a Claude session resumed on the new build would send a different image for one it had resized and lose its cache from there once, and few images take that path here (in this machine's traces of 2026-11-04 to 2026-11-09, 87 of 351 computer-use calls returned over 100 KB, against 1,476 image `Read`s), so `thumbnail_exact` stays.
- The repository has a README (2026-11-09), asked for by Georgios ("we also need minimal readme"), which the rule against README edits does not cover: that rule keeps a change from rewriting a README as a side effect. It says what nanocode is in one paragraph, shows `nc` answering a question about this workspace (`assets/nc.gif`, a 26-second recording of a real turn made with asciinema and agg, the one on the organization's profile), lists five properties (durable, Code Mode, hands, subagents, any payer), gives the install line of the CLI and the dependency line of the SDK, and links the TypeScript and Swift repositories, the crate layout and this file.
- The README links nanocodexyz/libnanocode beside the TypeScript and Swift repositories (2026-11-09): the C library Georgios asked for ("make a new libnanocode c repo that has the C bindings to it"), which holds the C interface that until then lived only inside nanocode-swift.
- A fork or side conversation on the subscription wire takes the one-hour prompt cache from its first request, as the root it continues does (2026-11-09). It starts from its parent's conversation, so the first request it sends after five idle minutes writes that whole conversation again: in the five days of traces of the entries below, the 16 side conversations lost their cache 58 times after waits of 5 to 60 minutes and wrote 27.6M tokens for it (476K a time, and an answer that takes about twice as long), where the rule of the entry below would still let each lose it once. `State::cache_ttl` now answers one hour on the subscription wire for a session whose lineage origin is `Fork` or `SideConversation`, as it does for a root; the origin is in the stored lineage, so a fork reopened from the store keeps it. Spawned subagents still start at five minutes and move to an hour when they outlive it. Checked with output/cache-loss `ttl` on this change (the fork's Code Mode cell sleeping 310 s, the fork waiting 310 s between two prompts, and a subagent the main thread spawns through `spawn_agent` whose cell sleeps 310 s): every request of the fork carries `"ttl":"1h"`, also after the store is reopened in a new runtime, where d1ad0de20 sent five-minute markers until the wait and again after reopening; the spawned subagent's first request carries five-minute markers and the one after its 310 s cell one-hour markers. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` natively and for wasm32-unknown-unknown, and clippy.
- The image breakpoint counts the identity block the subscription wire adds (2026-11-09). f5a39cba9, on master from 21:17 to this fix a few minutes later, counted a request's breakpoints as the agent builds it, but on the subscription wire `subscription_wire::prepare` puts its identity block, with a breakpoint, in front of the system prompt. That block replaces the identity block of the pinned system blocks, which is why the default layout has a breakpoint to spare, and is one more when the host gave its own system prompt (`ClaudeBuilder::system`, a runtime's `instructions`): such a session failed each request with `at most four cache breakpoints are allowed` once its conversation held an image (output/cache-loss `images` with `INSTRUCTIONS` set, on the subscription wire). `cache_floors_with` now takes whether the request goes over the subscription wire, asks `subscription_wire::added_breakpoints` how many the wire adds for this system prompt, and gives up a system breakpoint for an eviction only when that frees one. With its own system prompt a subscription session has no breakpoint to spare (identity, the end of the system prompt, the last tool, the end of the conversation), so it evicts as it did before; the API wire and the default layout are as in the entry below (`images`: 857,368, 1,051,266 and 1,220,563 tokens written; 856,179 on the API wire with its own system prompt).
- A Claude request keeps a cache breakpoint just before the oldest image it still carries, so evicting images writes the conversation again from there instead of all of it (2026-11-09). `ImageHistory` replaces the oldest images of a request with a placeholder in batches of 4 (8 kept), and the decision that introduced it expected the cached prefix to change once per batch. It does more than change: a request's conversation has one breakpoint, at its end, and the cache holds an entry only where earlier requests carried a breakpoint (the API looks back about 20 blocks from each, https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching), so after an edit far above the end nothing but the system prefix matches and the whole conversation is written again. In the five days of traces of the entry below, 221 requests that followed an image read within the TTL, with the same tools, system prompt and first message as the request before, read only the 12,552 to 13,768 tokens up to the core prompt's breakpoint and wrote 99.6M tokens (0.45M each; 48% of the requests that followed an image read, against 0.07% of the others), and 47 more of the same shape whose prompt was a few thousand tokens smaller than the one before (25.6M) follow images that tools other than `Read` returned; one subagent read 808 screenshots in 158 steps and wrote its conversation, of up to 538K tokens, again on 141 of them (66M). `evict_images` now also answers which message holds the first image the request keeps and, when this request evicts more than the one before it (which saw all but the last two messages), which held the first image that one kept, and `MessagesRequest::cache_floors_with` puts the request's cache control on the last cacheable block before each, in a copy of that message. The first is in every request while the conversation holds an image: its entry is written when the image arrives and read, so kept alive, by every request after. When a batch is evicted the second finds that entry, and only what follows it is written, with the new first breakpoint inside it for the next batch. A request has four breakpoints: the two the pinned system blocks carry (identity and core prompt; the Code Mode, environment and project instruction blocks after them were never behind one, since `cache_system_prefix_with` adds none to a system prompt that already has one), the end of the conversation, and this one. The request that evicts needs a fifth and gives up the core prompt's for that request, which the entry it reads covers. A host whose own breakpoints leave none spare gets none of this. A step's `conversation` digest leaves markers out and the layout is a function of the messages, so a recovered step builds the same request. Checked with output/cache-loss (`images`: a mock Messages transport that keeps a prompt cache by the documented rules, with entries at breakpoints, a 20-block lookback, the longest hit read, the rest up to the last breakpoint written and matched breakpoints refreshed; 4 bytes a token and 1,500 tokens an image), 8dc23e36f against this change, the same on the subscription and the API wire: 40 steps of 2 images and 20 KB of text wrote 2,603,015 tokens before and 857,368 after (18 and 3 requests wrote more than half their prompt); 40 steps of 6 images 6,008,999 and 1,051,266 (40 and 5); 24 steps of 1 image with 3 steps of text between them 1,780,384 and 1,220,563 (4 and 2). Not checked against the live API: if it does not refresh an entry that a longer hit covers, the breakpoint expires between batches further apart than the TTL and that eviction writes everything, as it did. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude-client -p nanocode-backend-claude` natively and for wasm32-unknown-unknown, and clippy.
- A subagent or side conversation on the subscription wire moves to the one-hour prompt cache the first time it outlives the five-minute one (2026-11-09, after Georgios pointed at session 01a1c366: "there's also bugs here worth fixing"). Children kept the 5-minute TTL (d03201ef7: roots take one hour, "subagents and side conversations keep 5 minutes"), and a child whose tool call, or whose wait for its next message, lasted more than five minutes then sent its whole conversation as new input. In this machine's traces from 2026-11-04 12:00 to 2026-11-09 (`~/.nanocode/log/otel`: 87,930 Claude model calls with usage in 584 sessions, 42.35B input tokens, 41.37B read from the cache, 982M written), 735M of the written tokens were a conversation the session's previous request had already sent, and 555M of those (930 calls, 12.4 s each on average) were requests of subagents and side conversations sent more than five minutes after the one before; the rest followed an evicted image (about 125M, the subject of the entry above), a root idle for over an hour (34M), a compaction (13M) or a prompt that a new build or day changed (7M). `State::outlived_cache` marks a session on the subscription wire that is on the 5-minute TTL once more than `CACHE_LIFETIME` has passed since its last request was accepted (`State::requested`; 5 minutes is the default lifetime in https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching, counted from the last request that read the entry), and from then on `cache_ttl` answers one hour for it. It is checked when a turn's template is made and after each round of tool calls, where the turn's template takes the new TTL before the next request; that template is in the durable cursor, so a recovered step is built with the TTL it was recorded with. The request that finds the cache gone is itself written for an hour (twice the input price, where five minutes cost 1.25 times), so the next long call does not lose it again. The mark is `cache_one_hour` in the child's checkpoint in the subagent journal (`NativeChildState`), so a restored subagent keeps it; the durable snapshot a fork or side conversation is reopened from has no such field, and those start at five minutes again. Sessions on the API wire keep the TTL their host chose. Replaying each recorded call of the 381 child sessions under the list prices (read 0.1, 5-minute write 1.25, 1-hour write 2 times the input price): as recorded 4,195M input-token equivalents with 833M tokens written; one hour from the start 3,782M (-9.8%, 287M written, but dearer for the 236 sessions that never waited); this rule 3,740M (-10.9%, 359M written). Checked with output/cache-loss (`ttl`: a main thread and a fork of it on the subscription wire over a mock Messages transport and a JSONL store, the fork's Code Mode cell sleeping 310 s, or the fork waiting 310 s between two prompts), once each on 8dc23e36f and on this change: before, every request of the fork carries 5-minute markers; after, the request following the 310 s call or wait and every later one carry `"ttl":"1h"` on the system blocks and the last message, a 3 s call leaves the fork at five minutes, and the main thread's requests are unchanged; reopened from the store in a new runtime the fork is at five minutes again. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude` natively and for wasm32-unknown-unknown, and clippy.
- Adding a subagent does not scan its siblings (2026-11-09). `Sessions::insert` keeps each root-level child under its host context and looked through that context's whole list for the ID before pushing it, for every child added or restored, although only an ID the sessions already hold can be in a list. It scans only then. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger: restoring the 53,477 agents of a killed `SWARM flat 100000` JSONL store, `sample` over the three seconds in which one thread rebuilds the registry (`Registry::restore_inner`) shows 888 samples there instead of 1,642, the 565 of `Sessions::insert` gone. What a restart of that store costs before its children run again, measured the same way: about 5 s replaying the part of the JSONL journal its index does not cover (the index is saved each time the journal doubles), about 2 s rebuilding the registry and encoding its first journal save, then 7 to 9 s in which the 2,911 children that were running resume.

- A `submit_result` that was running when the process died is answered from the journal (2026-11-09). `submit_result` returns once the subagent journal holds the result, so under load many children sit inside it at once (661 of 4,170 live children in one killed 30,000 agent run). It had no `recover`, so each restored cell told its script the call's outcome was unknown, although the journal says exactly which it was: either the result is there, and the restored turn is `Submitted`, or it is not, and nothing accepted it. `SubmitResult` recovers by submitting again with `resumed`: a turn that already holds the same validated output answers with the accepted receipt (another output still gets `AlreadyAccepted`), and a turn that holds none accepts it now. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger, whose mock worker ends its turn after a failed cell and so counts every such call as a failed agent: the killed 100,000 agent JSONL store of the entry below resumes to 100,000 completed instead of 99,969 and 31 failed, and twelve `SWARM flat 3000` runs killed at 800 to 4,100 completions on SqlStore, JSONL and RowStore all resume to 3,000 completed, where most runs before ended with one or two failed.

- A restored `spawn_agent` call finds its child by index (2026-11-09). `Registry::respawned` answered each recovered call by comparing the caller's session and call ID against every child of the root. A root killed while its cell was still spawning has one such call per child not yet started (the cell's journal now holds them all as running, see the code-mode entry below), so the restart compared every pending call against every child. `Sessions` indexes its children by the call that spawned them (`by_spawn`, kept by `insert`; `durable::Spawned` is `Hash`). Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger on JSONL (`SWARM flat 100000`, `MAX_CONCURRENCY=5000 QUEUED=1`, killed at 53,477 agents with 2,227 running, the same killed store copied for each build): resumed and settled in 57.7 s instead of 119.0 s, 100,000 agents in both; `sample` during the 60 s in which the earlier build added no agent shows one core in `respawned`'s scan and `memcmp`. Of the 57.7 s, 17.8 s restores the 2,227 running children and the rest runs the 46,523 agents the cell had not started.

- The steers a model call consumes bind in one commit (2026-11-09). Claude's `consume_steering` bound each pending steer with its own `bind_steer`, a commit of the whole head each, before every model call: a root told of 14,000 completions wrote 14,000 heads to say they all belong to the next request. `DurableOwner::bind_steers(operation, first..=last, model_call)` applies the `SteerBound` transitions at one revision and commits once (`bind_steer` is that for one steer; one already bound to the same call is skipped, one bound to another call fails the batch before anything is applied), `ClaudeExecutionPolicy::bind_steers` defaults to a loop over `bind_steer` so other policies are unchanged, and `consume_steering` binds each run of consecutive durable steers with one boundary. With the three entries below, checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger on JSONL (mock model, zero latency, 10 cores under a load average of 10 to 60, so CPU and counts are the firm numbers): `SWARM flat 30000` with `MAX_CONCURRENCY=5000 QUEUED=1`, f97a41704 then this tree back to back, settles in 40.8 s instead of 138.9 s on 140 s of CPU instead of 266 s, in 384,207 commits instead of 449,484, 3,228 MB written to disk instead of 4,784 MB; the root's own commits are 13,230 (129.5 MB) instead of 28,757 (256.6 MB), the subagent journal's 27,740 instead of 60,721. `SWARM flat 100000` (all 100,000 completed in both): 90.8 s instead of 783 to 898 s, 410 s of CPU instead of 1,400 s, 1,104,079 commits instead of 1,576,401 to 1,588,368, 87 KB logical per agent instead of 102 KB, 60 KB stored instead of 88 KB, 73 KB written to disk instead of 182 KB (the earlier runs had no concurrency limit, which that build's serial spawning made moot; this one ran with `MAX_CONCURRENCY=5000 QUEUED=1` because a live agent holds about 280 KB of memory and spawning no longer paces them: 30,000 unlimited peaked at 9.6 GB). A 100,000 agent run killed at 53,949 agents had not finished its resume after 400 s before the `flush` change in the code-mode entry below and has not been rerun since; 30,000 killed at 19,329 with 4,170 live resumes in 30.2 s.
- A commit costs the same however many steers its operation holds (2026-11-09). A root that spawns a swarm inside one cell receives each child's completion as a steer, and none of them binds until the next model call, so the operation holds one steer and one receipt per child. The session `Driver` builds each commit on a clone of its state, and the clone, the walk that staged every steer's input (`OperationState::stage_unless`, a hash lookup each) and the drop of the old state all cost one pass over those steers: 13 ms a commit at 100,000 steers, the whole of a run's last 474 s (`sample` of `SWARM flat 100000` on f97a41704: one core in `Driver::applied` cloning `Vec<SteerState>` and `BTreeMap<String, IdentifiedSteerReceipt>`, `EncodedPayload::stage_tree` and their drops). `OperationState::steers` is an `imbl::Vector` and `steer_receipts` an `imbl::OrdMap`, which a clone shares, and a steer's input is staged when the steer is accepted (into the state's `staged` records, filtered by what the store holds like every other) instead of being looked for on every commit. Both fields are public and their types changed; `len`, `get`, `iter`, indexing and `is_empty` read as before, and the serialized state is the same (imbl's `serde` feature: a sequence and a map). Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger: `statecheck 300 600` round-trips the same 134,092 transitions (74,602 steers accepted, 14,736 withdrawn) through their records, and the run measurements are with the entry above this one.
- A spawned subagent is saved with the call that spawned it (2026-11-09). `spawn_agent` recovers after a restart by finding the child its call created (`Registry::respawned`, by the caller's session and call ID in `ChildSession::spawned`). `start_child` inserted the child, which saves the journal, and only then set `spawned` (`mark_spawned`), in memory until some later save. A process killed between the two left a child in the journal that no call owned, and the restored cell's `spawn_agent` call, journaled as running, found none and spawned a second child for the same call. `Registry::insert` takes the marker and the child's first record carries it; `mark_spawned` is gone. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger: before, one of four `SWARM flat 3000` runs on SqlStore killed at 1,500 completions resumed to 3,001 agents; after, twelve runs (kills at 1,000 to 4,000 completions on SqlStore and JSONL) all resumed to 3,000.
- A restored Code Mode cell runs the calls its script made that never started, and a cell's journal is saved in groups (2026-11-09). A cell's journal holds the script's entries (`Call`, `Result`, timers) and, beside them, a record of each call the host started. A call's record is saved before the call runs (`journaled_call`), but a save also carries every entry the script has made so far, so a script that makes many calls at once (`Promise.allSettled` over 3,000 `spawn_agent`) has `Call` entries in the store for calls whose own record is still waiting its turn. A process killed then left those calls with an entry and no record; on replay the engine matches the entry and raises no `ToolCall`, `resume_replayed` walks only the records, and nothing ever answered them: the cell never finished and its turn polled `wait` every 30 seconds for good. `CellJournal::fold` now gives each `Call` entry that has neither a `Result` nor a record a `Queued` record, which replay dispatches. That is not a replay of work: a call runs only after its record is saved, so one without a record never ran. To keep that true across a second restart, the first save of a cell's journal (its header, which replaces the chain a restored cell had) carries every call the cell holds and not only the one that asked for the save. Three costs went with it. Each call's save waited for the one before it, one commit a call; calls now queue their record (`Persistence::queued`, an `Inbox`) and whoever holds the save writes all that are queued, so a call whose record another save carried returns without a commit of its own. `flush`, asked for each time the script waits, cloned every call the cell held and compared each to what was saved; the three places that change a call without saving it (`queue`, a call that completes while the cell is held, a replayed result kept for a held cell) queue that call (`CellRecord::journal`) and `flush` saves what is queued. And `CellStatus::calls` and the saved marks were `BTreeMap`s cloned on every change of an `Atom`, a copy of every running call per call; they are `imbl::OrdMap`s. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger (`swarm`, mock model, `SWARM flat 3000` killed with `KILL_AT_FINISHED=1500`, then `swarm resume`): on 59b663bad, 7278ea6bf and f97a41704 the resume stopped after `continuing the root's turn` with 764 agents and an idle process (on f97a41704, 3 of 3 runs on SqlStore and one each on JSONL and RowStore); with this change the JSONL and RowStore stores f97a41704 left resume to 3,000 agents, as do SqlStore runs killed the same way, and `cellresume` still runs its slow call once on each. `SWARM flat 30000` on JSONL with the grouped saves and maps alone: 50.8 s and 128 s of CPU instead of 121.1 s and 271 s, 347,636 commits instead of 447,656, 2,543 MB written to disk instead of 4,721 MB. A 30,000 agent run killed at 15,000 and resumed spent 30 s on one core in `flush` without adding an agent before the queue; after it the 10,671 agents left were added and run in under 10 s.
- Fixes from driving `tempo/` and `tempo/boundless/` models hard (2026-11-09, Georgios: "go and brutalize this stuff again until theres more bugs"; `output/gateways/brutal.sh` and `brutal-tui*.sh`, Georgios's wallet, about $0.21 spent, $0.18 of it two Claude Opus 5.5 calls on Boundless). `exec --resume <id> --model <other>` ran the session's own model and dropped `--model` without a word (a `tempo/vercel/zai/glm-5.3-flash` resume of a Boundless session was paid to Boundless); it now refuses, naming the model the session runs, as the interface refuses a model change once a thread has started, and a `--model` naming the session's own model still resumes. The thinking picker and dial, the harness mismatch error and `nanocode sessions` named a Tempo or Boundless model without its route (`Thinking · z-ai/glm-5.3-flash`, `model z-ai/glm-5.3-flash does not belong to the claude harness`, `boundless/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash`, which `-m` does not take); they now use `Route::label` (`tempo/boundless/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash`), and native models keep their display names. `nanocode-tui`'s effort dial widens to its title, which a 45-character route label had cut at the border. Checked the same day: the empty `tempo/boundless/`, `--harness claude` with a Boundless model and an unknown Boundless model (400, no retry, nothing charged) fail with their reasons; a shell call, thinking none/max on DeepSeek V4 Flash and low to max on GLM-5.3 Flash, resumes with tool history across processes, `list_models({query: "boundless/deepseek"})` (4) and a `spawn_agent` child on `boundless/deepseek/deepseek-v4-flash-0731` from a Boundless root, six processes at once on one named session, an interrupted turn followed by another, and `tempo/boundless/anthropic/claude-opus-5.5` with a tool call all worked. Not fixed here: GLM-5.3 Flash on Boundless answers thinking none with "the model rejected the request" (`upstream_rejected`, nothing charged) while nanocode offers every level for a model it knows nothing about; Claude models on Boundless read no prompt cache over Responses (two Opus calls in one turn each cost $0.088, where GLM read 12.2k of 14.0k tokens cached); and processes sharing a named session each count the others' spend as theirs (six identical calls showed $0.000591 to $0.002680 each).
- Claude's `Read` resizes an image about ten times sooner, to the same bytes, and the agent no longer decodes it a second time (2026-11-09). In the four days of this machine's telemetry up to 2026-11-04, 3,028 of 8,464 local `Read` calls took over 120 ms, nearly all of them screenshots, where a text file takes under 5 ms. `image_block` (the native `Read` of an image, a PDF page or a notebook image) fits an image within 1568 pixels a side and 1,150,000 pixels with `image`'s `resize_exact(…, Lanczos3)`, which computes each output sample on its own through `get_pixel`: 151 ms of CPU for a 1440x900 screenshot and 419 ms for 2880x1800, nearly all of the call. `nanocode_image::lanczos3(&image, width, height)` (core/image, `resample.rs`) adds the same f32 products in the same order, so its bytes are `image`'s, but a line at a time: for each output line it adds every source line of its window times that line's weight into one line of sums, a loop over neighbouring samples that the compiler vectorizes, and it runs the horizontal pass as that same loop over the transposed lines, each transpose written in order (0.5 to 1.3 ns a pixel; filling a zeroed buffer in tiles of 8 to 256 pixels took 2.1 to 3.0). The last pass rounds half away from zero with a saturating cast and a compare, 0.16 ns a sample where `value.clamp(0.0, 255.0).round() as u8` took 0.95, because `f32::clamp` keeps the loop from vectorizing. It takes 8-bit gray, gray with alpha, RGB and RGBA; other depths, an empty image and an unchanged size go to `image`. The agent then prepared the tool's image again (`prepare_tool_images`, `load_for_prompt_bytes`) and decoded every pixel a second time only to learn its size: `nanocode_image::remember(bytes, width, height)` notes that bytes decoded to that size, by SHA-1 and for the newest 32 (`MAX_IMAGE_CACHE_ENTRIES`), `image_block` calls it for the image it returns, `load_for_prompt_bytes` for each PNG, JPEG or WebP it decodes, and one whose remembered size fits the limits asked for is returned as it is, which is what decoding it would return. Nothing is remembered in the brain for a `Read` that ran on another machine's hand, an MCP tool's image or a WASM host's `Read`, so those are decoded once as before; and an image already within the tool's limits is still decoded once by the tool, which is how it refuses a damaged file, so that call takes what it did. Measured with output/image-bench's `read` (`ClaudeWorkspaceFiles::execute_output("Read", …)`, then the agent's `prepare_base64` for a model of 2576 pixels; each round a first read, the file differing by a text chunk and forty one-pixel images clearing the caches in between; 5 runs of 9 rounds interleaved with a build of bfe6249dd, median CPU on an M1 Max at load average 20 to 30), tool then agent: 1440x900 PNG 156.6 and 6.3 ms before, 14.4 and 0.7 after; 1280x2400 PNG 296.4 and 6.4, 23.8 and 1.4; 390x3200 PNG 110.5 and 2.6, 9.7 and 0.9; 2880x1800 JPEG 449.1 and 6.7, 44.5 and 0.7; 2880x1800 PNG 450.1 and 6.5, 48.0 and 0.7 (25 ms of it decoding the PNG); 1280x800 PNG, kept as it is, 3.9 and 4.2, 4.0 and 0.5; 414x900 PNG 2.7 and 2.9, 2.7 and 0.4; the nine samples together 1,672 ms and 170. The resize alone (`resize`) went from 150.7 to 8.3 ms (1440x900), 288.7 to 14.2 (1280x2400), 102.2 to 4.5 (390x3200) and 419.1 to 19.2 (2880x1800); built for x86_64 and run under Rosetta from 279.5 to 12.5 and 756.9 to 26.7; as WASM in Node 22 (wasm32-unknown-unknown, no SIMD) from 235.7 to 33.8 and 613.8 to 77.0, what `thumbnail_exact` takes there (31.6 and 77.0). Checked: `equiv` resizes 3,000 random images of the four kinds (noise, noise of 0 and 255, checks, gradients, alternating columns, flat; sides of 1 to 3,000 pixels to sides of 1 to 400) both ways and none of 188,198,466 samples differ, `ratio` the same for reductions of up to 1,000 times, and the bytes the tool returns and the bytes the agent keeps are the same before and after for all nine samples (`DUMP`). Tried and left: 14-bit fixed-point sums took a quarter less (6.4 ms for 1440x900) but were one level off `image` on 0.05 to 0.2% of a screenshot's samples and two levels off past a 200-fold reduction; `fast_image_resize` 5.5.0 took 19.5 ms for the same resize and clamps between its passes (up to 29 levels off).
- A `/btw` side conversation answers from the main thread's prompt cache (2026-11-09, Georgios: "/btw threads feel very slow on long running threads, when they shoudl be using the prev conversaw … prev message id thing"). Two things kept it off the cache. (1) Since 5fede2a34 a fork took its parent's system context (the environment section and project instructions) only while the parent's environment still named today's date, so a fork of a thread started on an earlier day rendered a new date, its system prompt differed and its first request rewrote the whole inherited prefix. In the last five days of `~/.nanocode/log/otel` traces, every side conversation forked on a later day than its parent read only the tools from cache (12,552 to 13,768 tokens) and wrote 248K to 857K tokens (01a1b83a…, forked on 2026-11-07 from a thread of 2026-11-06: 870,750 input, 13,768 cached, 856,978 written, 23.8 s), while same-day forks read their parent's prefix (01a1b82c…: 916,953 of 917,782 cached). Forks and side conversations (lineage origin `Fork` or `SideConversation`) again take the parent's context whenever they run in its workspace, as abe100e33 had them; spawned and restored subagents still compute their own on a new day. (2) A side conversation forked at or over the auto-compaction threshold compacted the inherited context before answering. 01a1c365… was forked from 01a1c23d… at 967,506 tokens (threshold 967,000) while the main thread was compacting, from the boundary before that compaction, and each of its three questions started a 3.18 MB compaction that was cancelled after 41.6, 18.7 and 41.9 s (the main thread's own took 70 s). A side conversation's snapshot now starts with `auto_compaction_suppressed`, so its first request goes out over the inherited context, the prefix the parent's last request cached. The flag clears at its first response, so later steps compact as usual, and a request that does exhaust the window still takes the context-recovery compaction. Checked with `output/btw-speed` (a mock Messages transport; `btw compaction` reports 990,000 input tokens for the main turn, `btw date` starts under `TZ=Etc/GMT+12` and forks under `Etc/GMT-14`) in two interleaved runs each of b3b2f1b56 and this change: the side's requests went from a compaction plus the answer to the answer alone, and its system prompt went from dated 2026-11-10 against the main's 2026-11-09 to the main's own. The code landed in 9cbc3d97f, whose commit picked up these hunks from the shared index.
- A Boundless session pays its gas in the stablecoin the wallet holds most of (2026-11-09). Boundless sponsors no gas and names USDC.e as its fee token, so a wallet without USDC.e failed the channel open ("gas estimation failed … out of gas") unless `--tempo-fee-token` named another. Without that flag, the Boundless wallet now reads the signed-in account (`TempoAccountsWallet::active_account`, `tempo-alloy` 1.11.0, the version `mpp` already uses) and its USDC.e, USDT0 and pathUSD balances (`eth_call` `balanceOf` on `DEFAULT_RPC_URL`), the tokens nanocode names that pay gas, and pays in the largest (`Gas::Held`); none held is an error that names `--tempo-fee-token`. The Tempo gateway sponsors its sessions' gas, so its wallet keeps the challenge's fee token (`Gas::Challenge`). Checked on 2026-11-09 with Georgios's wallet (84.97 MACH, 9.67 USDT0, 0.000041 USDC.e) and no flag: `tempo/boundless/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash` opened a 1 MACH Boundless channel and answered, and `tempo/vercel/zai/glm-5.3-flash` still answered through the gateway; both sessions were closed.
- `tempo/boundless/<model>` runs on Boundless directly, paid from the Tempo wallet over MPP, without the Tempo gateway or a Tempo Key (2026-11-09, Georgios: "boundless/.. models MUST work without a tempo key, just with a tempo wallet … assuming our own Tempo gateway doesnt even exist"). The gateway's `boundless/…` IDs answer an MPP caller "send Authorization: Bearer sk-tempo-…", so nanocode goes to `https://api.inference.boundless.network/mpp/v1` itself: Responses only (its `/messages` answers 404), so a Boundless model is a custom model on the Codex harness, sent by Boundless's own ID (`z-ai/glm-5.3-flash`; `boundless/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash` is a 400 there), and `/harness claude` from one goes to the Claude default natively. The route is its own provider, `boundless` (`Route::Boundless`, persisted in `SessionRecord::provider`, a `SpawnProvider` beside `tempo`, so `spawn_agent` takes `boundless/<model>`), with its own client and MPP session (Boundless's suggested 1 MACH deposit, in `--tempo-currency`, MACH by default), its group in `/model` and `nanocode models` from Boundless's `/mpp/v1/models` (26 models with prices on 2026-11-09), and the gateway's `boundless/…` IDs still left out. Boundless sponsors no gas and names USDC.e as its fee token, so a wallet without USDC.e needs `--tempo-fee-token` (`NANOCODE_TEMPO_FEE_TOKEN`) naming a token it holds: Georgios's wallet (84.97 MACH, 9.67 USDT0, 0.000041 USDC.e) failed the open with "gas estimation failed … out of gas" without it, and with `usdc.e` as the currency with "no balance in 0x20C0…8b50". Checked on 2026-11-09 with that wallet (`dogfood.sh`, `--tempo-fee-token usdt0`): `tempo/boundless/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash` opened a 1 MACH channel with Boundless and answered ($0.001687), `tempo/boundless/openai/gpt-6-luna` answered on the same channel ($0.001326), and `--tempo-close-session` closed it with 0.996987 MACH left.
- Correction to the entry below (2026-11-09): the `vercel/…`, `openrouter/…` and `@cf/…` IDs the gateway listed with provider `boundless` never ran on Boundless for nanocode. The gateway's preference (`Models.prefer`) moved them to Boundless only for callers with a Tempo Key, and an MPP caller took the ID's own provider (`/api/speed`: `vercel/zai/glm-5.3-flash` served by `vercel:baseten`, `vercel/openai/gpt-6-luna` by `vercel`). Georgios: "When somebody chooses a provider, that is for a provider", so tempoxyz/gateway#601 (d3b58e1, deployed the same day) removed the preference and routes no call to auctions; `/api/auction/models` then named the ID's own provider for all 837 IDs, and with the `boundless/…` IDs still left out (they need a key over MPP) `nanocode models` (45dce1b94, no change needed) listed workers-ai 31, openrouter 456 and vercel 324, `tempo/claude-fable-5-2` and `tempo/gpt-6-luna` under vercel.
- Each Tempo model is listed once, under the provider that serves it, and the `boundless/…` IDs are left out because the gateway refuses to be paid for them over MPP (2026-11-09, Georgios with a screenshot of `/model` filtered on fable, showing a "Tempo through the Tempo gateway" group beside Fable under boundless, and one of `tempo/boundless/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash` failing). The separate group of built-in models is gone: a built-in Codex or Claude model appears under its own name (`tempo/claude-fable-5-2`, with its capabilities) in the group of the provider that serves its gateway route (the `vercel/…` ID, else the `openrouter/…` one) at that route's price, and every other row is one (provider, upstream) as before. A `boundless/…` ID answers on both `/v1/responses` and `/v1/messages` "runs on Boundless, which the caller's API key pays directly: send Authorization: Bearer sk-tempo-…", while the `vercel/…`, `openrouter/…` and `@cf/…` IDs it serves through Boundless are paid over MPP, and on 2026-11-09 each of the 26 Boundless upstreams had one of those (Fable 5.2 only as `vercel/anthropic/claude-fable-5.2` and `openrouter/anthropic/claude-fable-5.2`), so the groups are boundless 25 (Fable 5.2's two upstream names are one built-in model), workers-ai 24, openrouter 431 and vercel 300; `tempo/boundless/…` typed by hand still reaches the gateway and its error. Checked on 2026-11-09 with paid requests from Georgios's Tempo wallet (`exec --tempo-session dogfood -m <model> 'Reply with exactly: ok'`, `output/gateways/dogfood.sh`, about $0.05 in holds over two 1 MACH channels, both closed): answered `tempo/claude-haiku-5-5` and `tempo/vercel/anthropic/claude-haiku-5.5` (Messages), `tempo/gpt-6-luna`, `tempo/vercel/openai/gpt-6-luna`, `tempo/vercel/zai/glm-5.3-flash` (also on `--harness claude`) and `tempo/@cf/zai-org/glm-5.3-flash` (all served by Boundless), `tempo/openrouter/liquid/lfm-3-1.7b:free`, `tempo/vercel/reflection/beam-free` and `tempo/@cf/meta/llama-3.2-1b-instruct`; failed `tempo/boundless/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash` (above), `tempo/@cf/cloudflare/clef-flash` (Workers AI: "required properties at '/' are 'model,state,questions'"), and the batch IDs the gateway lists, `vercel/openai/gpt-6-luna-batch` (Vercel 404 `model_not_found`) and `openrouter/openai/gpt-6-luna:batch` (OpenRouter 404: "cannot be used with the chat/completions endpoint"), each retried five times as a `server_error` or `api_error` and each attempt charged $0.000001 after its hold came back. The spend a run shows counts each model call's hold, which the gateway credits back once usage is known (a Codex call held $0.003 to $0.011 and a channel's remaining rose again by the next run). In tmux (`picker-tempo3.sh`) the filter boundless listed the Boundless models with `tempo/claude-fable-5-2` at $8/$40 once, and fable the Claude group, Fable 5.2 and 5.1 under boundless, and the openrouter and vercel routes.
- The Tempo groups are the providers that serve each model, and the picker aligns each group by itself (2026-11-09, Georgios with a screenshot of `/model` filtered on boundless: "Still seems buggy to me"). The filter showed `tempo/openrouter/…` and `tempo/vercel/…` models labelled boundless, and their details were cut at the right edge. The gateway sells 56 IDs under another provider's prefix (25 `openrouter/…`, 24 `vercel/…` and 7 `@cf/…` on 2026-11-09) that it serves through Boundless at the price of the matching `boundless/…` ID, so each (provider, upstream model) is now listed once, under the ID named for its provider, and the groups follow the provider the gateway names (boundless 26, workers-ai 24, openrouter 431, vercel 300) instead of the ID's first segment; an ID left out still runs as `tempo/<id>`. A model's detail is its price, with "\$ per 1M input/output tokens" in the group header, and the thinking levels only for models whose levels are known (a custom model lists all six). `nanocode-tui`'s picker pads labels and badges to the widest in their group instead of in the whole list, and sizes itself the way it draws rows, since 50-character `tempo/` IDs pushed every native model's detail off the popup. Checked on 2026-11-09 in tmux at 135 columns (`picker-tempo3.sh`, run from a worktree of 7278ea6bf with this change, as the main checkout held another session's work in progress that did not compile): the filter boundless listed the 26 Boundless models and nothing else, and fable listed the Claude, Tempo, boundless, openrouter and vercel groups with every row whole.
- Restoring a subagent reads its running turn once (2026-11-09). Three places each read the whole of it. `RoutedFactory::restore` asked `SessionStore::recovery_checkpoint` for the child's stored checkpoint, which loaded the whole stored session, every turn's input, its transcript and the running turn's continuation included, to return the checkpoint alone; it now reads the head and that checkpoint (`session_checkpoint` over a record and a checkpoint). `persistent_recipe` loaded the stored session again for its record, its pending turns and whether it has a checkpoint; `SessionStore::resumable(id)` answers those three (`Resumable`) from one peek and the pending turns' inputs. And a Claude turn that resumes asked its policy for the continuation twice, once in `freeze_admitted` for the model and frozen prompt and once in `cursor`, each hydrating its messages, code calls and 22 KB request template from the store; `ClaudeExecution` keeps the continuation it hydrated until it is asked again (`hydrated`, taken by the second reader, dropped when the operation has none). Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger on JSONL, the store of 3,000 children killed once each had sent its first request (497 finished) and reopened by `swarm resume`: a child stopped in its first turn read 12 times instead of 24 (36 KB instead of 85 KB; `TRACE_STATE=<child>`), the 3,000 children 37,854 times and 108 MB instead of 51,209 and 176 MB, and all 3,000 completed. What repeats still: three peeks, the checkpoint twice and the turn's input twice.
- A host whose older builds share its store can hold back the formats this build added (2026-11-09). `SessionStore::with_state_format(8)` (`state_format()`; the method 0330349cb removed, now for formats 8 and 9 only: 8 keeps a running operation's steers in its head as before, 9, the default, seals them) and `SubagentControl::set_journal_version(6)` (6 writes the tree alone, every save a fold, and no `keeps_result`; 7, the default, writes deltas) make this build write what the build before format 9 and journal 7 reads; both refuse a number this build does not write. Why: a build starts writing a new format in the release that first reads it, so a host that deploys by rolling, or rolls back, has an older build meet heads it refuses (`unknown field \`steered\``, `unsupported subagent journal version 7`); tempoxyz/pyramid held format 6 this way from 2026-11-05 to 2026-11-09 and lost the means when the cap went. The rule for such a host: deploy the build that reads a format with the cap set, and lift the cap once no older build can open the store. Not capped: `JsonlStore` filing a subagent in its root's file and agent-host's cell journal format 2, which only the CLI writes. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger (`STATE_FORMAT`, `JOURNAL_VERSION`): a swarm of 600 killed at 300 finished on `SqlStore` with `STATE_FORMAT=8 JOURNAL_VERSION=6` wrote a format 8 head with 23.8 KB of inline steers and a version 6 journal without deltas, and the build from before these changes (59b663bad) opened it and restored its 600 agents; the same swarm uncapped wrote format 9 (a 5.9 KB head) and version 7, which that build refused.
- A subagent that had a result keeps it across a restart (2026-11-09). Two cases lost it. (1) A child whose `submit_result` was accepted and journaled but whose turn had not ended when the process stopped is restored as completed with that result, and its own session still holds the turn as pending; the next message to it runs that turn to its end, which ended without a submit in the new process and so failed the child with "subagent turn ended without a valid submit_result call" over the result it had. A turn that starts for a child that is completed (and was not delegated a new task) now keeps the child's last result whether or not the turn is a recovered one (`harness_turn_started`; it kept it only for a turn that was not recovered). (2) A completed child interrupted in a follow-up turn, which a peer's message starts and which keeps the earlier result when it ends without a submit, forgot that it kept one: the journal's agent record now says so (`keeps_result`, written only when set; a journal with it is refused by builds before this) and restore gives the interrupted turn the result back. Root children's completions that steer into a running root turn are watched by one task per root (`NoticeOutcome::of_steers`, `Registry::watch_steer`): it subscribes to the root's events once, learns each steer's carrier turn from `run.steered` and settles every watched steer at the run's end, where each steer had its own subscription and decoded every `run.steered`, so s steers waiting on one root turn cost s² decodes and s copies of every root event. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger on JSONL: `LATENCY_MS=1000 KILL_AT_FINISHED=500 swarm run jsonl <dir> 'SWARM flat 1000'` and then `swarm resume`, which now also resumes the root's pending turn as a host does (`pending_turns`, `durable_prompt`). A store killed with 507 children in follow-up turns, 431 accepted but unfinished and 61 in their first turn ended with 568 completed and 432 failed before, under the build that wrote it and under this one alike, and with 1,000 completed after; a second store, killed and resumed under this build (402 accepted, 85 in their first turn, 511 finished), ended with 1,000 completed. `SWARM flat 10000` used 54 and 63 s of user CPU instead of 80 and 86 s (two interleaved runs each at load average 27 to 80, so their wall times, 27 to 58 s, say nothing).
- `/harness` chooses the Codex or Claude harness (2026-11-09, Georgios: "i am not sure how do i select harness here? it should be obvious … just /harness should let me select which of the two"). `/harness` opens a picker whose two entries say what each would run, and `/harness codex|claude` switches directly, before the first prompt or after `/new` like a model change. A `tempo/` model keeps its ID on the other harness, since the Tempo gateway serves every model over both Responses and Messages; a native model, which a login ties to its harness, gives way to that harness's default (`claude-opus-5-5`, `gpt-6.1-sol`, or a host's first listed Codex model, `ModelClients::default_model`). A `tempo/` model that is not a built-in Codex or Claude model (`host::follows_harness`) runs on the current harness when `/model` picks it, where it always ran on Codex, and `--harness` chooses it for `-m tempo/…` (Codex unless set). `/model`'s title names the current harness and its footer points to `/harness`. Two SDK additions make a model of the other family possible on a harness: `SpawnBuilder::harness_model` and `RuntimeBuilder::harness_model` take any `ModelSelection`, which the CLI now uses for every start instead of the typed `backend::<Codex>().model(…)`, and `ClaudeBuilder::knows_max_tokens` reports whether the builder has an output budget; for a model the Claude backend has none for, the CLI's `configure` hook sets 32,768 (`Tempo::OUTPUT_TOKENS`), the output the Tempo gateway documents for a request that sets none ("Without max_output_tokens a request may write 32,768 tokens"). Checked on 2026-11-09 in `output/gateways` with a throwaway `HOME`: `exec -m tempo/boundless/deepseek/deepseek-v4.1-flash --harness claude` stopped at the gateway's payment step over Messages ("Unknown Claude model: configure max_tokens explicitly" before the hook), without `--harness` over Responses, and `-m claude-opus-5-5 --harness codex` was refused; in tmux (`harness.sh`) `/harness` on the native `claude-opus-5-5` offered Codex running `gpt-6.1-sol`, `/model tempo/boundless/deepseek/deepseek-v4.1-flash` stayed on Claude, `/harness` then offered that model on both, and `/harness codex` switched to Codex with it. WASM checks of `nanocode-backend-claude` and `nanocode-agent` and clippy on them and `nanocode-cli` are clean.
- The Tempo group lists every model the Tempo gateway sells (2026-11-09, Georgios after trying 8ae770531: "This doesn't seem to include the OpenRouter models? Or the boundless ones?"). The catalog is `/api/auction/models` alone, 837 IDs on 2026-11-09: 456 `openrouter/…`, 324 `vercel/…`, 31 `@cf/…` and 26 `boundless/…`, with every ID of the curated `/v1/models` (17) among them, so that second fetch is gone. `/model` and `nanocode models` show the built-in Codex and Claude models the gateway serves under their own names first, then one group per ID prefix (`tempo/boundless/`, `tempo/openrouter/`, `tempo/vercel/`, `tempo/@cf/`), each model with the provider the gateway says serves it and its price per million input and output tokens; a model sold through several routes appears under each, so a filter such as `deepseek-v4` shows every route and price, and `boundless` every model Boundless serves (which includes `@cf/…`, `openrouter/…` and `vercel/…` IDs). `list_models` takes an optional `query` that keeps the models whose full name contains it, since the catalog is 849 models, and `spawn_agent`'s definition is memoized on the policy without the providers' models and notes, so a catalog does not grow its key. Checked on 2026-11-09 in `output/gateways`: `nanocode models` printed the five Tempo groups and 849 `tempo/` models, `tempo/boundless/claude-fable-5.2` at "boundless · $8/$40 per 1M"; in tmux (`picker-tempo2.sh`) the filter `deepseek-v4` showed the `@cf/`, `boundless/` and `openrouter/` routes with their prices; a cell's `list_models({query: "boundless/deepseek"})` returned the four Boundless DeepSeek models, and `spawn-tempo.sh` routed as before.
- Models Tempo proxies are named `tempo/<model>`, and the gateway notion is gone (2026-11-09, asked for by Georgios: "do it all through the Tempo Gateway integration, remove the notion of the gateway, and let's just do tempo/... as the prefix if it's a proxied provider from us"). This replaces the two entries below about gateways. A bare model ID runs natively, as before 640b9ca50: on the ChatGPT and Claude Code logins, on a host's own clients, or, for Codex, through `--gateway openrouter|vercel|cloudflare|cloudflare-gateway`'s Responses route, which stays as it was. `tempo/<model>` runs on the Tempo gateway (`https://gateway.tempo-dev.workers.dev`), paid over MPP from the Tempo wallet: a Claude model's dashed ID on the Claude harness through `/v1/messages` and a Codex model's on the Codex harness through `/v1/responses`, each keeping its model's capabilities and prompts, and any other ID on the Codex harness as a custom model, since the gateway routes every ID it lists for sale and resolves Claude Code and upstream names to its providers (Vercel before OpenRouter). The picker's Tempo group and `nanocode models` list the built-in models whose upstream name `/api/auction/models` lists (837 callable IDs on 2026-11-09, without Claude Fable 5.5 or either Mythos) and the IDs of `/v1/models`' curated list that name no built-in model (9 of its 17), loaded in the background through the payment layer's one HTTP client, built when first used. One MPP wallet pays the gateway's models and tools, and the spend ledger tells models from tools by URL. Removed: `--gateway tempo` (Tempo's `openai.mpp.tempo.xyz` and `anthropic.mpp.tempo.xyz` proxies, their warm-up, and shell commands paying any mpp.dev service, which came with it), `--gateway-base-url`'s MPP endpoints and `--tempo-network`, the Boundless endpoint (its models are `boundless/…` IDs on the gateway), the gateway registry with its preferences, `/model <model> <gateway>` and `spawn_agent`'s `gateway`. `--tempo-currency` (MACH by default), `--tempo-fee-token`, `--tempo-deposit` (2 MACH unless set) and `--tempo-close-session` apply to the gateway. The SDK carries the route as a provider: `SpawnOptions::provider`, `SpawnBuilder::provider` and `SessionRecord::provider`, persisted, copied when a record is derived and kept when one is merged, so a resumed, switched or forked session keeps its provider, and `RoutedFactory` spawns and restores a same-harness child with a provider through its recipe instead of cloning the parent's client; `SpawnPolicy::providers` (`SpawnProvider`: name, about, models, note) lets `spawn_agent` take `<provider>/<model>`, infer the harness from the provider's models, and `list_models` list each provider model by its full name. A bare model in `spawn_agent` runs on the spawning agent's client for its harness and natively for another. Checked on 2026-11-09 in `output/gateways` with a throwaway `HOME` (no wallet, no logins, no proxy variables): `exec -m tempo/claude-haiku-5-5` failed at "Messages transport: payment failed … to pay gateway.tempo-dev.workers.dev", `tempo/gpt-6-luna` and `tempo/vercel/google/gemini-4-argon` at the same payment step over Responses, bare `gpt-6.1-sol` at the ChatGPT login, `claude-fable-5.2` as an unknown model whose error names `tempo/`, and `tempo/` alone was refused (`tempo-routes.sh`); a `tempo/gpt-6-luna` session resumed in a second run reached the gateway again, its record holding provider tempo. With a root on a mock through `--gateway openrouter --gateway-base-url` (`spawn-tempo.sh`, `cell-tempo.js`), `list_models` listed 21 tempo models, and `spawn_agent` with `tempo/claude-haiku-5-5`, `tempo/gpt-6-astra` and `tempo/vercel/google/gemini-4-argon` each reached the gateway's payment step while bare `gpt-6-astra` reached the mock and bare `claude-haiku-5-5` the Claude login. In tmux (`picker-tempo.sh`) `/model` filtered on fable showed the Claude and Tempo groups, and `/model tempo/claude-haiku-5-5` switched to a Claude session labelled `tempo/claude-haiku-5-5`. A debug `exec` failing at the missing login (`bench.py`, interleaved, 40 runs each, load average 7 to 20) took 39.9 ms before 640b9ca50 and 44.5 ms now with `--no-tempo-tools`, 44.9 and 47.6 ms with the tools; the gateway's background listing, sharing `exec`'s single Tokio worker, accounted for about 3.4 ms of it (47.9 ms against 51.3 ms without it, before the listings shared one client) and building the Tempo clients for about 1 ms. Not checked with a paid request or with a real model choosing to fan out. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-agent-core -p nanocode-subagents -p nanocode-agent -p nanocode-durability --target wasm32-unknown-unknown`, `cargo check --tests` of those crates and clippy on them and `nanocode-cli` are clean.
- Restored subagents resume together (2026-11-09): `Registry::resume_interrupted` sends every interrupted child its resume message at once (`join_all`) instead of one child after another, each of which waited for that child's session to be reopened from the store, its harness rebuilt and its turn admitted, a dozen or more store round trips, before the next began. `JsonlStore` offers `pipeline_handle()` (another sender to its one worker, so its order is the worker's), which lets `SharedStore` send reads as they arrive and form the next group while one is written, where every read was a barrier that ran alone between groups, and `acquire_many` writes a group's acquisitions as one batch, one write and one fsync per session file. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger on JSONL: 3,000 children killed once each had sent its first request (`LATENCY_MS=2000 KILL_AT_STARTED=3000 swarm run jsonl <dir> 'SWARM flat 3000'`, 762 finished, 2,238 running) and reopened with `swarm resume`: every agent was restored and resumed in 15.4 s before, 10.6 to 11.2 s with the pipeline handle and 7.4 s with the children resumed together; the same on `SqlStore` over SQLite took 5.1 s. `swarm run jsonl <dir> 'SWARM flat 3000'` settled in 7.4 to 7.6 s instead of 9.0 to 9.6 s. What a resumed child still costs is its reads, 9 to 22 store round trips of which most repeat: `persistent_recipe` loads the whole stored session, transcript and running turn included, to learn its record and pending turns, and the running turn's continuation (its messages and the 22 KB request template) is then read twice more.
- `JsonlStore` keeps a root's subagents in the root's session file (2026-11-09). `StateStore::colocation()` (default none; `SharedStore` and `CachedStore` forward it) answers a `Colocation` whose `colocate(state_id, beside)` asks a store to keep one state where another is; a session driver calls it for a session it is about to create with `Origin::Subagent`, naming its lineage's root, before its first acquisition. `JsonlStore` takes the hint on a channel its worker drains before each job and, unless the session already has a file of its own, files every state of that session (`<child>`, `<child>:cells`) in the root's journal and appends `["<child>","<root>"]` to `sessions/.homes`, which it reads when it opens, so a child is found by its ID in a later process without its root; a root's journal also names its members when it opens, so a line lost in a crash costs nothing once the root is open. `list_states` and `list_summaries` still list each child (after its root, newest first), with a remembered summary kept while the child's revision is the one it was remembered at, since the file's length now changes with every sibling. A journal with members keeps the index sidecar the shared `sessions.jsonl` had (`<root>.jsonl.index`), written when the journal has doubled since the index was last written and when it closes, so reopening reads the index and at most the newer half of the journal. `Journal::share`, which writes a record another state of the file already holds as a stand-in, finds the holder in an index of record-key hashes (`Index::holders`) and walks only the states whose records it has not loaded (`Index::unshared`), where it tried every state of the file for every commit. Why: with a file per child, 3,000 children were 3,001 files of which the store keeps 32 open, so nearly every commit reopened a file and replayed it, every child's file held its own 22 KB of request template and 14 KB of tool catalog, and a group of commits was a write and an fsync per file. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger, `swarm run jsonl <dir> 'SWARM flat 3000'`: settled in 9.0 to 9.6 s instead of 24.4 s, one 183 to 193 MB file (61 to 64 KB a child) and a 9.5 MB index instead of 3,001 files of 315 MB (105 KB a child), 483 to 497 MB written to disk instead of 669 MB; `swarm resume` reopened the 3,000 in 0.19 s (0.35 s without the sidecar); `listing jsonl <dir> <child>` read a child's record in a new process in 166 ms through `.homes` and listed 3,001 sessions (1 root, 3,000 subagents), the second listing from remembered summaries in 4.5 ms. A swarm of 600 killed at 300 finished resumed and settled from its one file, a store the build before this wrote (601 files) resumed under this one with its children left in their own files, and `cellresume` passes on JSONL. With every state in one file, `share` as it was made the same run take 37 s. A store that an earlier build opens finds no file for a colocated child; that build refuses the root's format 7 journal first.
