- Agents can run subagents on any gateway (2026-11-09, asked for by Georgios after session 01a1c2a0, where Claude Opus on the Claude login, asked "which one of the boundless gateway models is the abliterated deep seek?", searched the repository for "boundless" because nothing told it gateways exist: "i would've expected that it thinks 'i should launch a bunch of subagents w/ various models from diff gateways and find out' … some gateways would require MPP to work"). `SpawnPolicy::gateways` (`SpawnGateway`: name, about, the models it serves, an optional note) is a host's catalog; while it is set, `spawn_agent` takes a required, nullable `gateway` (an enum of the names, each described by its about text, and the description says a subagent can run on any of them), infers a model's harness from the gateways that list it before the parent's (a Claude parent asking for `deepseek/deepseek-v4.1-flash` gets the Codex harness, where it used to get its own and send the ID to Anthropic), refuses a gateway that is not offered or does not list the model on that harness, and `list_models` lists every gateway with its note and its models and their harnesses. A named gateway, or with none the only gateway that lists the model, travels as `SpawnOptions::gateway` to the `HarnessRequest`, and `RoutedFactory::spawn` builds such a child through its harness's recipe instead of the parent's native spawn, which cloned the parent's client, so the host's `client_for` picks the gateway and its payment: before this a same-harness subagent always ran on its parent's client (the earlier entry's "a subagent runs on its parent's gateway" held for that reason only), and a Claude-login agent could not run a Claude subagent on Tempo. With no gateway and several listing the model, a same-harness child still runs on its parent's client. The CLI publishes its gateways when the runtime is built and again once the background listings have answered (`Routes::offer`), and a requested gateway is the first hint `client_for` resolves with. A child restored after a restart is rebuilt from its checkpoint without its spawn options, so a same-harness child restores on its parent's client and one of another harness by the CLI's resolution. Checked on 2026-11-09 against `output/gateways/mock.py` with `spawn-gateways.sh`: a root on the mock endpoint ran a cell (`cell-gateways.js`) whose `list_models` listed chatgpt (4), claude (11), tempo (15), tempo-gateway (17), boundless (26) and 127.0.0.1 (2) once the listings had answered, and "listing models…" for the two endpoints in a cell run at once; `spawn_agent` with gateway 127.0.0.1 and mock-sub reached the mock, boundless with mock-sub was refused, tempo with gpt-6-luna reached Tempo's proxy and stopped at "to pay openai.mpp.tempo.xyz" (no wallet in the throwaway `HOME`), no gateway with deepseek/deepseek-v4.1-flash went to Boundless and stopped at "to pay api.inference.boundless.network", no gateway with mock-sub reached the mock, and no gateway with gpt-6-luna (chatgpt and tempo list it) ran on the parent's mock client. Before `RoutedFactory` took the recipe path, tempo with gpt-6-luna reached the mock. Not checked with a real model deciding to fan out, nor from a Claude-harness root, which the mock cannot serve. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-agent-core -p nanocode-subagents -p nanocode-agent --target wasm32-unknown-unknown`, `cargo check --tests` of the first two and clippy on them, `nanocode-agent` and `nanocode-cli` are clean.
- The subagent journal `<root>:subagents` is format 7 (2026-11-09): a save writes the agents that changed and the messages that arrived as one delta record, `{"agents":[[id,record],…],"messages":[…]}`, and the payload names the deltas since the tree was last written (`deltas`, the unsealed keys of each level, 16 to a page, as sealed receipts and steers). The tree of format 6 (leaves of 16 agents under 64-entry pages, message leaves of 64) is now the base the deltas apply to: a save folds them into it, rewriting the leaves of every agent changed since the last fold and the message leaves, when the deltas written since then are as many bytes as the tree's leaves (`JournalCache::folds`), when it is the writer's first save, or when the message log was rewritten rather than appended to. So a restore reads at most twice the tree (`load_deltas` reads the delta pages level by level and `load_leaves` lets the newest record of an agent win), and folding costs at most what the deltas did. An agent whose record is byte for byte the one last written is left out of a delta (`CachedAgent::record`): every `Sessions::get_mut` gives an agent a new journal generation, and 6,093 of the 15,610 agent records 1,000 children wrote were copies of the one before. Why: an agent's record is about 700 bytes and changes 9 to 15 times in its life (started, each nested call's evidence, its accepted result, its settle, its completion's delivery), and each change rewrote its leaf of 16 (9.5 to 11 KB) and the 64-entry page above; each new message rewrote the unfilled leaf of up to 64. Versions 1 to 6 still load, a journal restored from them is written as 7 at its first save, and binaries before this refuse 7. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger, `swarm run sqlite <db> 'SWARM flat 1000'`: the journal wrote 12.0 to 12.7 MB of records in 4,423 to 4,430 records and 2.0 MB of payloads, instead of 35.9 MB in 8,619 and 0.6 MB (7.1 MB of it deltas, 2.0 MB folded leaves, 1.5 MB the children's first checkpoints, 0.3 MB profiles). `swarm resume` after a run of 1,000 and of 3,000 children (whose runtime was shut down, which writes no final journal, so the 3,000's payload still named deltas) restored every agent as completed; a swarm of 600 killed at 300 finished (`KILL_AT_FINISHED`, `LATENCY_MS=1000`) resumed its 305 running children and settled, and so did one killed under the build before this, whose journal was format 6.
- A running operation's steers are sealed records, not part of its head (state format 9, 2026-11-09). Each steer an operation accepts is written once as a record of its own (`{message_id, input, input_key, accepted_after_model_call_index}`), and the operation keeps a `steered` log in the head: how many it sealed, the unsealed page keys of each level (16 to a page, as sealed receipts, `seal_page`), the slots that were withdrawn in the order they were, and, only on the wire, which model call each run of steers is bound to (`bound`, runs of `[through steer index, model call]`). In memory an operation's `steers` and `steer_receipts` are what they were, so the state machine, its checks and every reader are unchanged: `OperationState::wire` leaves them out of the head while the log holds them, and `DurableState::hydrate` and `hydrate_read` read the log back level by level (`JournalState::steer_pages`, `steer_page_children`, `absorb_steers`) wherever they read sealed receipts. A change to an operation whose log was not read is refused (`was changed before its sealed steers were loaded`), and a head that was not hydrated writes itself back unchanged. An operation that settles drops its log and carries its receipts in its receipt page, as before, so settled operations read as they did; an operation whose first steer an earlier build accepted keeps its steers inline until it settles, and binaries before this refuse format 9. Why: steers and their receipts lived in the head (about 510 bytes a steer: the message ID twice, the input key twice) and every commit rewrote the head, so the n steers of one operation cost n²/2 x 510 bytes. A root's subagents report through steers while its turn runs: 300 children steered 155 to 177 times into one root turn, whose head reached 85 KB and was rewritten by each. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger. `swarm run sqlite <db> 'SWARM flat 300'`: the root session wrote 2.78 MB of heads instead of 50.2 MB (and 1.93 MB instead of 21.6 MB with `LATENCY_MS=1500`, where the children report into the root's notice turns); `SWARM flat 1000` wrote 116 MB to `SqlStore` instead of 686 MB, of which the root's heads were 11.2 MB instead of 581 MB, and 108 KB a subagent to JSONL instead of 549 KB. `statecheck 300 600` drives `JournalState` through 134,092 random valid transitions (74,602 steers, 14,736 withdrawn, binds, retirements, completions) and after each one reloads the head it wrote from the records it staged: steers, receipts and statuses equal the live state's every time, the reloaded state writes the same head, and the largest head was 2,940 bytes. A root killed with 85 sealed steers in its pending turn (`KILL_AT_FINISHED=300`, 600 children) reopened and settled, and so did one killed under the build before this, with 41 steers inline. What a commit of a steered turn still rewrites is the operation's inline continuation, 4.3 to 4.7 KB for a Claude root.
- A native session's Code Mode cell journal (agent-host's `DurableCellJournals`, state `<session>:cells`, format 2, 2026-11-09) stores each update as a content-addressed record that names the update before it, and the state's payload names each live cell's newest update (`{"version":2,"cells":{"<cell>":"b3:…"}}`); a header's tool catalog is a record of its own (`catalog`, chunked like any payload past 16 KiB), written once per session and process and named by every header after it. Until then an update was the record `<cell>/<seq>` with the catalog inline in every header. Two things were wrong with that. Bytes: the catalog was 14.8 KB of a 15.2 KB header with no host tools at all, so a cell that printed 2 KB wrote 16.7 KB of journal. And `SqlStore` and `RowStore` keep one record per key for the whole database (`ON CONFLICT (key) DO NOTHING`), so every session's `1/0` was the first session's: after 1,000 subagents each ran one cell on `SqlStore`, the database held 1,004 such records for 892 `:cells` states, all the root's. A payload written by the earlier format (a bare map of cell to seqs) still loads, its cells are read by their numbered keys, and it is kept beside the new cells (`numbered`) until each is replaced or forgotten; binaries before this refuse format 2. Forgetting a cell still blanks its records, which only JSONL's compaction reclaims. Checked on 2026-11-09 with output/durability-ledger (its own workspace; a `StateStore` wrapper that counts every commit's payload, record and event bytes by state, over `JsonlStore` and over `SqlStore` and `RowStore` on a SQLite `Sql` host kept in the harness, with an in-process Messages mock). `turns memory '' 6 3 2000` (6 turns of 3 cells that each print 2,000 bytes): the `:cells` state wrote 68,615 bytes for 18 cells instead of 300,639, and a turn 72 KB instead of 110 KB. `cellresume` (output/native-resume's check that kills the process 1 s into a resumable MCP call and reopens the session) resumed the call on JSONL, `SqlStore` and `RowStore`, and with `EARLIER=1`, which first runs another session's cell to its end in the same store, on all three, where `SqlStore` before this answered "Tool execution interrupted by a host crash; outcome unknown" because the journal it read was the earlier session's; a journal written by the build before this (`CHILD_EXE`) resumed under this one on JSONL and `SqlStore`. A subagent that runs one cell still writes its own copy of the catalog on JSONL, whose files share nothing.
- Every gateway is offered at once, and `/model` groups the models by gateway (2026-11-09, asked for by Georgios: "allow easy merging of gateways … all the models grouped by gateway and easy way of switching across all the gateways incl models and filtering when i search for a model i wanna see all the gateways that offer it"). The CLI keeps a list of gateways (`gateways.rs`): `chatgpt` and `claude` (the logins, or the clients a host supplies), `tempo` (Tempo's MPP proxies), `tempo-gateway` (`https://gateway.tempo-dev.workers.dev`, whose `/v1/models` lists Workers AI, Vercel, OpenRouter and Boundless models) and `boundless` (`https://api.inference.boundless.network/mpp`), the last two paid from the Tempo wallet and listed in the background at start, then `openrouter`, `vercel`, `cloudflare` and `cloudflare-gateway` when their keys are in the environment, and `--gateway tempo --gateway-base-url`'s endpoint, named by its host (the URL of a built-in endpoint is that endpoint). `--gateway` (new values `tempo-gateway`, `boundless`) now names the gateway to prefer, not the only one. `-m` takes any gateway's model, a listed ID waiting for the listings when no catalog names it, so `nanocode -m claude-fable-5.2 -t high` runs on Boundless without `--gateway`; it resolves to the preferred gateway when that serves the model, else the first that does, and refuses a model the preferred gateway's harness offers but it does not list, as before. `/model` shows one group per gateway headed `<name> · <about>`, and its filter keeps every gateway's matching models under that gateway's header and all of a gateway's models when the header matches, so typing `fable` shows each gateway that serves a Fable model (`nanocode-tui`'s picker); the footer names a listing that is loading or failed. `/model <model> [gateway]` picks one by name. Picking a model on a gateway chooses that gateway for the model (`Routes::choose`), and a model on another gateway than the current agent's builds a new agent instead of switching in place. The status line says `via <gateway>` for any gateway but the logins, `/status` describes it, compaction is refused per gateway, and `/reload` keeps the main session's gateway. Each agent runs on one gateway: `CodexHarness::client_for` and `ClaudeHarness::client_for` (additive) let a host pick the client from the `HarnessRequest`, and the CLI records the gateway of each session it builds, so a session keeps its gateway, a subagent runs on its parent's when that serves the subagent's model, and otherwise on the one chosen for the model, the preferred one or the first that serves it. Resuming a session from another run takes the same order, since the store keeps no gateway. Tempo's gateways share one `Payments` (one spend panel, one paying proxy, one reqwest client), and an endpoint's wallet opens on its first payment with the endpoint's chain, currency, fee token and deposit (`Terms`), where `--gateway-base-url` opened it at start and refused to start without a wallet; `--tempo-currency`, `--tempo-fee-token` and `--tempo-close-session` apply to every MPP endpoint. `--no-tempo-tools` now leaves out the tools and paying proxy also with `--gateway tempo`. Checked on 2026-11-09 with `output/gateways` (a throwaway `HOME`, no proxy variables): `nanocode models` printed the seven gateways, Boundless's 26 models and the Tempo gateway's 17; against a mock endpoint (`mock.py`) `exec --gateway tempo --gateway-base-url http://127.0.0.1:18731 --tempo-network mainnet` answered from the mock with `-m mock-model` and with its first listed model by default, refused `-m gpt-6.1-sol` naming the endpoint's models, and a root that called `spawn_agent` for `mock-sub` had the subagent's request reach the mock (`subagent.sh`); `exec -m claude-fable-5.2 -t high` with no gateway flags reached Boundless and stopped at "sign in to a Tempo wallet with /login tempo to pay api.inference.boundless.network". In tmux (`picker.sh`, `picker2.sh`) `/model` grouped the models, `fable` showed the claude, tempo, tempo-gateway and boundless groups, `boundless` showed all of Boundless's, `/model claude-fable-5-2 tempo` and then `… claude` each started a new session (`via tempo`, then none), and `/model gpt-6.1-sol 127.0.0.1` was refused. Startup to the first request (debug builds, `bench.py`, interleaved, 20 runs each, `exec -m gpt-6.1-sol` failing at the missing ChatGPT login): 40.2 ms before and 46.4 ms after with `--no-tempo-tools`, 346.7 and 354.2 ms with the tools (network-bound); `nc.gateways.build` (building every gateway's client) took 2.3 to 3.9 ms, where `nc.host.build` had built one OpenAI client in about 2.2 ms, and sharing one reqwest client among the MPP layers left the difference within noise. In a shell behind nanocode's own paying proxy the Tempo gateway's listing fails, since the proxy answers its paid origin with a certificate reqwest does not trust. `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-backend-codex -p nanocode-backend-claude -p nanocode-tui --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` and clippy on those crates and `nanocode-cli` are clean.
- `--gateway tempo --gateway-base-url` keeps its session open on exit, Ctrl-C included, so the next run reuses the channel instead of opening another; `--tempo-close-session` (`NANOCODE_TEMPO_CLOSE_SESSION`) closes it cooperatively on exit, returning the unspent deposit at once, as every exit did before. Each process still pays from the lowest of the 64 slots no other process holds (`session_slot`), so concurrent processes keep a channel each and later runs reuse them, as Tempo's proxies already did. Boundless suggests a 10 USDC deposit for Claude Fable 5.2, so every run locked 10 MACH, paid the open's gas and got the rest back on exit. `--tempo-keep-session`, now the default, is still accepted, hidden and conflicting with `--tempo-close-session`. Checked 2026-11-09 with `--tempo-currency mach --tempo-fee-token usdt0 --tempo-session reuse-check exec -m deepseek/deepseek-v4.1-flash`: the first run opened a 1 MACH channel and left it open, the second paid $0.000165 on that channel without opening one, and a third with `--tempo-close-session` paid on it and closed it cooperatively.
- `--tempo-fee-token TOKEN` (`NANOCODE_TEMPO_FEE_TOKEN`) pays the gas of `--gateway tempo --gateway-base-url`'s session opens and top-ups in the token the caller names (usdc.e or usdt0 on mainnet, pathusd or a token address; MACH and OUSD are refused, as neither can pay gas), through mpp's `with_session_fee_token`, instead of the `feeToken` the endpoint's challenge names. Boundless (`https://api.inference.boundless.network/mpp`) names USDC.e and sponsors no fees, so a wallet paying in MACH needed USDC.e for gas: on 2026-11-09 a wallet holding 0.000041 USDC.e, 9.67 USDT0 and 86.1 MACH failed every Claude Fable 5.2 request with "gas estimation failed: … out of gas: gas exhausted during precompiled contract execution: 68333", the gas that balance buys at Tempo's 600,000,000 gas price (41 × 10^12 / 6 × 10^8). USDT0 can pay fees (`FeeAMM.getPool` held 39.3 USDC.e and 42.8 pathUSD against it) but the DEX had no liquidity to swap it into USDC.e (`tempo wallet swap 1 USDT0 USDC.e --dry-run`: InsufficientLiquidity), and MACH cannot be swapped, so the gas had to be paid in USDT0 itself. With `--tempo-currency mach --tempo-fee-token usdt0`, `nanocode exec -m claude-fable-5.2 -t high "Reply with exactly: ok"` opened a 10 MACH session, answered "ok" for $0.174296 and closed the session cooperatively; the open's gas cost 0.000061 USDT0.
- Subagent budgets (2026-11-05, asked for by Georgios for tempoxyz Swarms: "each subagent can also have a budget … so that we can fund sub-populations of a given swarm or redirect our funding from a given sub-agent swarm"). `SpawnPolicy::budgets`, off by default, offers `spawn_agent` a required, nullable `budget_usd`: the most, in USD, the child and its descendants may spend together. The SDK keeps it on `AgentDescriptor::budget_usd`, in the subagent journal (a journal without it reads as none), and refuses a budget of 0, any budget while the policy is off, and one above the smallest budget in the spawning agent's lineage (`budget_usd $0.05 exceeds the $0.02 that agent 1 and its subagents may spend together`). The assignment tells the child its budget and that requests past it are refused, ending its turn. `SubagentControl::budgets(session_id)` answers the budgets along a session's lineage, the agent's own first, for a host's request context; `SubagentControl::set_budget(root, id, budget)` sets or clears one, persists it and announces the descriptor again (an unknown id is `unknown agent_id`); `AgentSummary`, `AgentDirectoryEntry` and `spawn_agent`'s report carry `budget_usd`. The SDK only records budgets: the host meters what each request costs and refuses requests once a lineage's budget is spent, because only it sees prices, refunds and paid tools (tempoxyz/gateway does, from the lineage gakonst/nanocode-ts sends with each request). Checked on 2026-11-05: `cargo check --locked --lib -p nanocode-subagents -p nanocode`, clippy on `nanocode-subagents` natively and for wasm32-unknown-unknown, `cargo check -p nanocode-cli`, and gakonst/nanocode-ts's WASM build against this commit. End to end through a local pack of nanocode-ts and tempoxyz/gateway under `vp dev` (DeepSeek V4.1 Flash on Codex, paid from a throwaway wallet's key): a child spawned with `budget_usd` 0.01 ran a loop of paid tool calls until the gateway refused it at $0.0105 and its turn failed; after `set_budget` to 0.03 a message started it again and it completed at $0.0156; a child given 0.02 had its own `spawn_agent` with 0.05 refused with the message above, and a grandchild given 0.005 stopped at $0.0054 while its parent's subtree counted it.
- A Code Mode cell sends a call to a nested tool missing from its catalog to the host instead of rejecting it in the cell (`code-tools.mjs`), and the host asks its providers to find it before answering. `DynamicToolProvider::resolve(name)` (by default `false`) lets a provider register a tool it learned after the cell started, such as one read from an MCP server in the background, and return `true`; the registry then runs the call through that provider's `execute` like any other. `ToolRegistry::execute_nested` asks each provider in turn and stops at the first `true`, before the call's span and nested admission, so a slow resolve holds no admission slot and a name nobody resolves (any string a script wrote) opens no span; a name whose input names another attached `hand` goes to that hand without resolving. A name no provider resolves fails with the same `TOOL_NOT_AVAILABLE` message the cell raised before, with `code` and `tool` on the cell's error (`embedded::tool_not_available`), and so does a missing name in `LocalCells`. Object's own names and `toJSON`, which `String(tools)` and `JSON.stringify(tools)` call, still reject in the cell. A cell fixes its tool names when it starts, so before this a tool a provider registered after that failed its first call with nothing sent while the next cell's call worked (tempoxyz/pyramid's first-call gap for an app whose tools it reads in the background), and the host could not count calls to names it did not have. `exec`'s description and the cell's catalog are unchanged, so prompts stay cached; the description's line that a missing nested tool "rejects locally" now holds for every name the host can't resolve. A cell journaled before this that called a missing name diverges if it is replayed after it, since the call now reaches the host, and stops with the usual could-not-be-resumed note.
- Codex requests can ask for a Cyber access program, as Codex's `--cyber-access-program` (openai/codex `codex-rs/core/src/cyber_access_program.rs`): `OpenAiBuilder::cyber_access_program(Some(CyberAccessProgram::DaybreakRed))` (`ModelConfig::cyber_access_program`; `Standard`, `DaybreakBlue`, `DaybreakRed`) sends `"access_programs": {"cyber": "daybreak_red"}` on every Responses request, and `OpenAiBuilder::model_cyber_access_program(model, program)` (`ModelConfig::model_cyber_access_programs`, resolved per request by `cyber_access_program_for(model)`) asks for a program on one model's requests only, over the client's, because a host such as tempoxyz/pyramid runs several models on one client (subagents, a retry on Daybreak Blue); with neither (the default) no field is sent, leaving it to the server, which decides entitlement and which models accept it. `nanocode` and `nanocode exec` take `--cyber-access-program <standard|daybreak_blue|daybreak_red>` for the process, and refuse it with `--gateway`, since only OpenAI reads it. Checked on 2026-11-05 with `nanocode exec` against a mock Responses server (`NANOCODE_OPENAI_BASE_URL`): with `--cyber-access-program daybreak_red` the request carried `{"cyber":"daybreak_red"}`, without it no `access_programs`; with `--gateway openrouter` it is refused before any request. A unit test checks the per-model lookup. Not checked against the live API.
- nanocode-computer: a driver can start without named environment variables (`Install::without_env`, `Driver::without_env`), and an app is leased by its bundle where the backend has bundles. tempoxyz/pyramid-core's Hand marks every process it starts (`PYRAMID_HAND_PID`) and terminates the marked processes that escaped its process group when it pauses or stops (`shell::terminate_marked`); an external cua-driver inherited the mark, left the group and was killed on every pause, so core starts it `without_env("PYRAMID_HAND_PID")`, as it already starts Pyramid.app's provider (tempoxyz/pyramid main #2160 kept a list of helper executables instead). `Computer::execute` used to lease the app by the name it was asked for, so "Notes" and "com.apple.Notes" were two leases and two threads could drive one app; now the provider resolves the name first (`Desktop::lease_key`: on macOS, `Backend::keys_by_bundle`, the running app that `engine::find` names, else the installed one, without launching it) and leases its bundle identifier, falling back to the name when nothing resolves; other backends and providers keep the name, and the refusal names the app as asked. Checked on 2026-11-05 on dev-georgios: `cargo test -p nanocode-computer` 43 passed (new: an engine with bundles resolves a running app by name, path or bundle and an installed one without launching it, and an unresolved name or a backend without bundles gives none; a Computer leases "Notes", "com.apple.Notes" and "/Applications/Notes.app" as one app for another session while a desktop without bundles keeps names apart); an out-of-repo harness starts a script as the driver and sees `PYRAMID_HAND_PID` removed and other variables kept, through `Install` and `Driver`. The macOS backend's one-line `keys_by_bundle` isn't compiled here (no Apple SDK on Linux).
- `SessionStore::cancel_pending(id, to)` settles the operations a session left pending (a turn its process stopped mid-attempt, or a handed-off turn its claimer couldn't resume): it acquires the session as an agent, readmits and attempts each pending operation, and cancels it to a checkpoint chosen by `CancelTo`: `Latest` (the session's newest), `Checkpoint(payload)` (a base the host kept; if it doesn't load, the newest, with the reason in `CancelledPending::unresolved`), or `Fresh` (none). It answers `None` when there is no checkpoint to settle to, leaving the operations pending, and otherwise how many it cancelled and the checkpoint used. Ported from pyramid-core's runtime (`durable::settle_pending`), which now calls it. Checked on 2026-11-05 with an out-of-repo harness on dev-georgios that builds the same crashed session twice in a `MemoryStore` (a checkpoint, a completed operation with a second checkpoint, then an attempted operation left pending) and settles one copy with pyramid-core's own function and the other with this. Six cases (latest, a kept base, a base that doesn't load, fresh, no checkpoint at all, nothing pending) gave the same answers and the same turn statuses each time. pyramid-core's runtime-harness smoke, laterpass, subhandoff and crash (58 points, 0 failed) gave the same results with and without the change.
- `Recent::get` and `get_if` run a read in a task of its own (`tokio::spawn`), started when the shared read is first awaited, so a read whose callers all went away (a cancelled turn, a dropped request, a timeout) still runs to its end and lets go of what it holds; a read that panics, or is cancelled as the runtime stops, panics in its callers as before, and the API is unchanged. Before, the read was a `Shared` future kept in the map and polled only by its callers: once all of them dropped it, a pool acquire inside it kept its semaphore permit until the entry was evicted, which an entry keyed per turn never is. In tempoxyz/pyramid-core with Postgres stopped for 90 s under 8 turns, the hot pool stayed at 0 free permits for 90+ s after the database came back, with 12 waits woken and never polled again (fix-db's repro, 2026-11-05); pyramid-core spawned the read in a wrapper of its own until this. Checked on 2026-11-05 with an out-of-repo harness on dev-georgios: a read whose only caller is aborted after 10 ms runs to its end, releases its semaphore permit and is kept for the next caller (at master the read never finished and kept the permit); 8 concurrent callers share one read; a panicking read panics its caller; an error is returned as before. With the harness's other 10 cases, 12 of 12 in each of 5 runs.
- Claude Bash gives a call from a Code Mode cell (its call ID holds `/code-`) 128 KiB of stdout and of stderr (`NESTED_STREAM_BYTES`), where the model's own call keeps Claude Code's 30,000 bytes (`MAX_STREAM_BYTES`), because a nested call's output goes to the cell's script and the model sees only what the cell prints. Why: gakonst/opentempi's Temini runs every shell command inside cells, and of 1,457 nested Bash results in 25 of its recorded research turns on 2026-11-05, 65 were cut at 30,000 bytes, 57 of them under 120 KiB (the 99th percentile of all 1,457 was 105 KB), and scripts that parsed or split the cut output failed with `JSON.parse` errors or used the cut text as file paths. Checked on 2026-11-05 in opentempi's local Worker with gakonst/nanocode-ts built against this tree: a cell's `tools.Bash` of `rg -l` over 6,632 archive files returned 131,072 bytes of paths with `truncated: true`, and the session log kept 10,947 characters of each nested result.
- `ServedTools::redacting(redactions)` scrubs what a served shell call returns (its text, content, metadata and structured result) of each value the host's `Redactions` lists, replaced by its placeholder, without exporting anything to the command. `served::proxy_redactions(secrets)` lists the credentials of the proxy URLs in the process environment and the secrets file (`HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY` and their lowercase names) in every form a command can print: as written, percent-decoded, and as the Basic auth `curl -v` shows, for passwords of at least 8 bytes, longest first, each as `[secret <NAME>]`. `file_secrets` also passes `http_proxy`, `https_proxy`, `no_proxy` and `all_proxy`, which curl and Python read; other lowercase names stay out. Why: a tempoxyz/pyramid hosted Hand's egress login reaches its commands through the environment (tempoxyz/pyramid #1876, pyramid-core #237), and pyramid-core's kernel-hand kept its own served tools, read_file and proxy redactor (about 330 lines) for this. Checked on 2026-11-05 with an out-of-repo harness on dev-georgios that builds the tools as kernel-hand does and ports tempoxyz/pyramid main's `hands/src/machine.rs` tests (secrets reach commands and are redacted; lowercase proxies reach commands and every form of their credentials is redacted; the Hand's own percent-encoded egress login from its environment; short or missing passwords redact nothing; workdirs outside the served directories are refused; read_file reads, refuses `..` and reports links and missing files): 6 of 6.
- nanocode-sync has `Lines`, `Coalesce` and `Refreshed`, from tempoxyz/pyramid (its backend's `core/src/sync.rs`, `api/src/coalesce.rs` and `api/src/refresh.rs`), so Pyramid and pyramid-core take them from here. `Lines` is a `Sequencer` per key, held weakly: work on a key waits for that key's earlier work and never for another key's, and a key's line goes once no `Turn` holds or awaits it (`enter`, `try_enter`). `Coalesce<V, E, C>` shares a read among requests that arrive together: while one read for a key runs, later requests for it wait for the next read, which starts when that one ends and answers them all, so none sees older state than it would alone and a burst costs at most two reads. Each read runs in a task of its own, so a request that goes away strands nobody, with the context `C` of the first request it answers (Pyramid passes its span, route and GitHub caller, so the read's work shows in that request's trace and metrics); a read that panics or is cancelled answers its requests and those queued behind it with `E::from(Stopped)` and frees the key. `Refreshed<T>` answers with a key's newest value at once and refreshes it in the background when it is older than `fresh`, or when `now_unless`'s `outdated` says it no longer describes what it was read from; one refresh runs per key at a time, and a read that fails, panics or passes `max_read` keeps the old value until the next refresh. `Coalesce` and `Refreshed` are native only, like `Recent`. Checked on 2026-11-05 with an out-of-repo harness on dev-georgios (tempoxyz/pyramid main 83c59243b's coalesce and refresh tests ported to these types, plus a hung refresh, keys that don't wait for each other, a lead request that goes away, and `Lines`' arrival order, independence and weak holding): 10 of 10 in each of 5 runs.
- A model call whose response stream is cut part way is retried, and the retried attempt's text replaces what the cut attempt showed instead of appearing twice. Every reqwest error classifier (`nanocode_http::retryable`, which Codex's HTTPS and Chat Completions transports, the Claude client's transport and `reqwest_error` now share) counts an error while reading the response body as retryable: reqwest 0.13's `bytes_stream` reports a connection reset, a close in the middle of a chunk or a read timeout as `Kind::Decode` (it maps body errors through `error::decode`), never `is_body`, so a reset or FIN after the stream's first event failed the call on its first attempt. Claude retries a transient failure after it streamed text, which it refused before; it shows every attempt of a call under the first attempt's message ID (a retry's `message_start` keeps it) and records the completed response under that ID, so a replay after a handoff rewrites the same message, and its `model.attempt.retrying` payload numbers the call as its deltas do (`model_call_index` from 1, it was the 0-based index) and carries `error_class` (`retry_class`: `http_rate_limit`, `http_overloaded`, `http_server`, `transport`, `transport_timeout`, `stream_overloaded`, `incomplete_stream`, ...), as Codex's does. `AssistantText` follows `model.attempt.retrying`: the messages the cut attempt showed restart empty, their first update after the retry has `delta: None` (a host that appends deltas takes that text whole), and the next attempt's messages take their keys in order, so a host writes the retried text over the cut attempt's message; it forgets a call's attempts at its `model.call.completed` or `model.call.failed`. A host must read a pass's late events with the same `AssistantText` it streamed with, or the keys a retry took over are lost. A tool call whose input was cut is never dispatched: calls still run only from a completed response. Why: in tempoxyz/pyramid a turn whose model stream was cut (a dying sidecar at a deploy, a reset, a proxy's error page) failed with "Turn failed before it could reply", Claude never retried once text was shown, and where Codex did retry (a clean close before `response.completed`, a stall, an `error` event) the partial text stayed beside the full reply as a second message. Checked on 2026-11-05 on dev-georgios with tempoxyz/pyramid#2061's backend (bdf3c4357, this repository by path) against a raw HTTP/1.1 mock of the Responses and Messages APIs that cuts streams on purpose (out of repo: /home/ubuntu/perf/a50/harness, `cuts`), 17 cases for each of Codex and Claude: a TCP reset, a FIN mid-chunk, a clean end before completion, a proxy's error page, a 20 s stall past a 6 s read timeout, the provider's overloaded and rate-limit events mid-stream, 502 after a reset, 503, 529 and 429 with `retry-after` before the stream, a cut in a tool call's input, after its input completed and after its command ran, a cut after a whole message, and resets on every attempt. Before: the reset, FIN and reset-then-502 cases and every tool-call cut failed the turn on both; Claude failed 13 of the 17 cases, every one whose stream had begun, and Codex failed 8 and left the partial text as a second message in the other 5 it retried. After: every case but the last succeeded with one message holding the whole reply, each tool command ran once (Pyramid's Hand ledger: 1 command, none duplicated), and resets on every attempt failed the call after 5 attempts (Codex 15.9 s, Claude 9.6 s) with the partial text kept. The WASM targets were not built (no wasm32 target on the machine); clippy of nanocode-types-core, nanocode-http, nanocode-backend-codex-client and nanocode-backend-claude is clean.
- Computer use is one crate, nanocode-computer (crates/integrations/computer), replacing nanocode-cua and nanocode-cua-tools and taking in tempoxyz/pyramid-core's own macOS engine (kernel-computer's native.rs, from tempoxyz/pyramid's hands/src/desktop, after Vercel Labs' agent-desktop). Old paths keep working: `nanocode::cua` re-exports it, the Sky-compatible cua_repl surface (`ComputerConfig`, `ComputerTools`, `browser`, `browser_socket`, `embedded_driver`, the catalogs and `cua_driver.mjs`, now under `src/repl/`) is at its root, `driver` (the cua-driver client, with `Leases` and `app_key`) and `embedded` (macOS) keep their items, and `nanocode-cua-driver` is the same executable. New is the `computer` tool, `Computer`: one schema on every platform (`definition(Capabilities)`; the description names no platform but in its examples of app identifiers and keys and states only what the default provider can do, the pointer, screenshots, change diffs and whether a policy refuses apps, worded so that a model reading the diffs text and given whole states reads them right; with all four it is the same on every platform and byte for byte tempoxyz/pyramid's pinned definition, `tool/pinned.json`, which Linux Hands also present. Until 2026-11-05 it opened with the owner's Mac on macOS, with AT-SPI on Linux and with its refusal on Windows, which the Windows engine's `ready` still gives) over `Provider`s: `Native` (this platform's engine), `Driver` (cua-driver, built in or external), `Mcp` (an MCP server that serves the actions as tools, such as Pyramid.app's `--computer mcp`; `rename` maps argument names and `_scope` carries the turn for its diffs) or `Missing(reason)`; `Computer::or(name, provider)` adds one that a call's `_provider` picks. The tool enforces for every provider the action list, arguments of at most 32 KiB, one session per app until its turn ends (`end_turn`) or after 180 s idle (`Computer::refusal` words the refusal), a 45 s step and 10 s start deadline (`Limits`), state clipped at 192 KiB on a character boundary, a screenshot of at most 480 KiB of base64 or `screenshot_omitted`, and a step line that never holds typed text. Platform engines implement `Backend` (state: `read`; act: `press`, `set_value`, `select`, `scroll`, `perform`; keys: `type_text`, `press_key`; `screenshot`; apps: `apps`, `installed`, `launch`; an optional `pointer`), which is synchronous and runs in `spawn_blocking`, and the generic `Engine` holds the safety model so no backend can skip it: the host's `Policy` (`Fn(AppIdentity) -> Result<(), String>`, given the app's name and each of its identities in turn, `App::id`, the most specific, then `App::aliases`, so an app is refused when any of them is) before an app is read, acted on or launched, with `SENSITIVE`/`sensitive` as a starting list (terminals, password managers and authentication agents on macOS, Linux and Windows, and on Linux also shells, system settings, keyring and polkit prompts and privileged tools such as package managers and `pkexec`, by .desktop id and executable name); elements only by index from the app's latest state, checked alive first; the app's whole new state after every action, so an index is valid only within the state that showed it; secure fields (`Node::secure`) rendered as `••••`; typing and keys refused unless the focused element is still the one the latest state showed; the pointer (coordinates, drags, middle and multiple clicks) only when the backend has one and the host allows it (`Native::pointer(true)`); selections as UTF-16 ranges for AX (`Selection::utf16`) and character offsets for AT-SPI (`Selection::chars`). Backends: macOS (`macos`: AX, CoreGraphics and ImageIO, keys posted to the app's pid, no pointer; `macos/ax.rs` is the crate's only `unsafe` code, so the crate sets its own lints with `unsafe_code = "deny"` and that module allows it, where the workspace forbids it), Linux (`linux`: AT-SPI over D-Bus through zbus and atspi-proxies, read breadth-first with up to 64 calls in flight, 1 s per call and 5 s per tree, 3,000 nodes; actions through Action, EditableText, Text, Value, Selection and Component, a primary click on a text field focusing it rather than running its `activate`; with `DISPLAY` set, keys and the pointer through XTEST, only into the X window that has keyboard focus and belongs to the app, and screenshots through GetImage of the app's window; under Wayland without XWayland those refuse and the accessibility actions still work; passwords are never read; an app's id is its .desktop id, from `GIO_LAUNCHED_DESKTOP_FILE` (when `GIO_LAUNCHED_DESKTOP_FILE_PID` is absent or its own PID) or `BAMF_DESKTOP_FILE_HINT` in /proc/<pid>/environ, else `_GTK_APPLICATION_ID` or `WM_CLASS` of its X windows matched to an entry's `StartupWMClass` or file stem, else the entry whose Exec runs its executable, or the script its interpreter runs, with the process's arguments beginning with the entry's (the longest such entry), else its script's name (the first argument that isn't an option, for python, perl, ruby, node, java, the shells and other interpreters), else its executable's name, and the names after it are its aliases, so a Python app is no longer `python3.12` and a terminal behind a harmless desktop id is still refused; an app found for launch through an entry has the entry's id and the same aliases, and starts as GIO starts one, with `GIO_LAUNCHED_DESKTOP_FILE` and its own PID (exported by `/bin/sh` before it execs the program), so its children, which inherit them, do not claim the entry; entries are read from at most 32 data directories (XDG_DATA_HOME and XDG_DATA_DIRS name 2 to 8 on a desktop), 4,096 entries each (a full desktop has a few hundred) of at most 1 MiB (entries are a few KiB), and kept until a directory's modification time changes, and /proc files are read up to 2 MiB, Linux's bound on a process's arguments and environment together with the default 8 MiB stack), Windows (`windows`: a skeleton that advertises nothing and refuses every call; UI Automation is designed, not built, as the member chose on 2026-11-05) and none elsewhere. Platform code is gated by `cfg(target_os)` only. Checked on 2026-11-05: `cargo test -p nanocode-computer` on Linux (34 pass) and on the member's Mac (macOS, 29 pass, among them the pinned definition, installed apps read without permissions and refusal without Accessibility), the ignored live tests with the Mac's terminal already trusted (`live_desktop_reads_and_operates_calculator`: reads Calculator, clicks 7 by index, refuses coordinates, drags, double and middle clicks, a stale index and Terminal, presses Escape, and quits Calculator when it was not running) and in Docker on Linux (Ubuntu 24.04, Xvfb, dbus, at-spi-bus-launcher and a GTK3 fixture, through `Computer` and `Provider::native`: 1,015-line state with a JPEG in 285-313 ms, click and re-read 320-327 ms in a debug build, set_value, type_text, select_text, masked password, checkbox, scale, scroll, press_key with a borrowed keycode for €, stale indexes, pointer refused by default and working with `pointer(true)`, launch from a .desktop entry, keys refused once the app lost focus; without a desktop the step refuses cleanly), and `cargo check` and clippy for `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu` (mingw in Docker), `aarch64-apple-ios` and `wasm32-unknown-unknown`. App identities and the shared description were checked on 2026-11-05 on Linux only (macOS was not rebuilt; its one edit is the new `aliases` field): `cargo test -p nanocode-computer` (41 pass, the pinned definition among them), clippy with `-D warnings` on Linux, `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu` (mingw in Docker) and `wasm32-unknown-unknown`, and in Docker (Ubuntu 24.04, Xvfb, at-spi-bus-launcher, GTK3 fixtures and xterm; the ignored `apps_are_known_by_desktop_id_script_or_program`): fixtures launched through `org.example.Third.desktop` and `second.desktop` read as `org.example.Third` (its environment held the entry and its own PID) and `second`, running ones as `org.example.Fourth` (`WM_CLASS` against `StartupWMClass`) and `org.example.Fifth` (its Exec), a script without an entry as `fixture.py`, and a policy on `sensitive` refused XTerm and UXTerm by desktop id, an entry `org.example.Harmless` that runs xterm by its alias before anything launched, and a running app with a harmless desktop id whose script is `ssh-askpass`; list_apps over six apps took 2.3-10.2 ms, a refusal 3.3-5.1 ms and launching and reading an entry 1.0-1.5 s in a debug build at load average 19 to 50, and the earlier live test still passed in its own harness.
- `SubagentControl::discard_restored` closes only the agents restored as interrupted that the host chose not to resume, each through `close` (`Registry::close_batch`), which journals the closure, and leaves the root's scope open. It used to call `close_all`, the root's shutdown, which froze the scope after saving the journal and before marking the agents closed: the root then refused every spawn (`subagent scope is closing and cannot spawn children`) for the rest of the process, and since the frozen scope writes no more journal, the agents stayed `running` in it, so every later turn restored them, discarded them and froze the scope again. Why: a tempoxyz/pyramid thread whose backend crashed with subagents running could never spawn one again in any later turn (Pyramid calls `discard_restored` at the start of each turn that moved no workers). Checked on 2026-11-04 with a test in tempoxyz/pyramid's engine (`handoff_tests.rs`, `discarded_restored_workers_close_durably_and_spawning_stays_open`; a real Codex SDK root, a memory journal holding two running workers with checkpoints, manual resume) against 6127b8b61, Pyramid's pin, through a path patch: before, `start_agent` after the discard failed with that error and a new registry restoring the journal deferred both workers again; after, the spawn succeeded, both workers were `Closed` and the journal restored none. Pyramid's other four handoff tests passed both ways. All five also passed with the fix on 1a762d4f9.
- `Runtime::shutdown` stops the subagent registry's subagents and lets go of its session handles (`SubagentControl::shutdown`, which replaces `release_sessions`), before it shuts down the host and the agents. The registry keeps each root's `AgentHandle` and runs each subagent's harness, event forwarder and the root's journal writer until `close_all` for that root, and their tools hold the registry, so a runtime shut down without `close_all` kept them all, and everything those tools held, for good. Each root's scope is frozen first and its subagents closed as `close_all` closes them (their harnesses, then their tasks, within 30 s), but no journal is written, final or background: a host that moves running subagents to another process (as tempoxyz/pyramid does at a deploy, after `persist`) keeps them as last saved, and after such a handoff nothing is written to the store the claiming process owns. Until 2026-11-05 shutdown only released the handles (#1125), and tempoxyz/pyramid and pyramid-core each called `close_all` before it, whose final journal write a handed-off turn's claimer then fenced. Why: on pyramid-core's turn runtime every turn that spawned subagents left about 14 tasks behind. Checked on 2026-11-05 with pyramid-core's lifetimes harness (pyramid-core main f4c8a64 against this tree by path, 10 turns of each kind on dev-georgios): live tokio tasks went 53 → 1,293 over plain, fan-out, unwaited fan-out, cancelled, steered, interrupted, revoked, failed and handed-off turns at master 3f8067f8, and stayed at 11 (21 in the handed-off pass, which runs a second backend) with this, as with pyramid-core's own `close_all` workaround; every handed-off turn was claimed and its 60 subagent rows succeeded on the claimer in both, with no fenced journal write.
- A subagent restored mid-turn is always resumed: its resume message (`durable::resume_message`: the restart notice, the evidence of its in-flight calls and its delegated task) stays within the 64 KiB message limit (`MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES`) instead of failing to send and leaving the agent interrupted. A message that fits whole is sent byte for byte as before; otherwise each in-flight call quotes at most 2 KiB of its arguments and the task at most 16 KiB (its history begins with the task whole), then the oldest calls count as omitted, and last the task is cut to what is left. The crate runs its unit tests again (`cargo test -p nanocode-subagents`). Why: in tempoxyz/pyramid, which hands a running turn to the next build at every deploy, 124 moved subagents in 23 turns failed to resume in the 7 days to 2026-11-04 with `message exceeds the 65536-byte limit` (an in-flight call's arguments, such as a cell that writes a file, are quoted whole), and their turns started their work again in new subagents. Checked on 2026-11-04 with unit tests: a small resume is unchanged, and 100 in-flight calls of 64 KiB each with a 128 KiB task fit, keeping the latest calls, counting the dropped ones and cutting the task.
- `Tools::with_text_pins(pins)` (and `ToolsBuilder::text_pins`) lets a host keep an earlier wording of what the runtime itself writes for the model (`TextPins::pin(key, contract, text)`): the description of each tool added after it through `add_shared_tool` (`spawn_agent` and the other subagent tools, `submit_result`; key `tool:<name>`), and Code Mode's `exec` and `wait` on the native runtime (keys `exec`, `wait`). Each text comes with its contract, a blake3 of everything it is rendered from but the runtime's own wording: a tool's definition without its description, and for `exec` that plus every nested definition (as sent, after pins), the provider summaries and the flags. A host keeps an earlier text only for the same contract, so only prose is pinned. tempoxyz/pyramid keeps a warm thread's wording across a Nanocodex bump this way, as it does its own tools' (`with_descriptions`). The embedded (WASM) runtime pins added tools but not `exec` yet.
- A Code Mode cell encodes its nested tools once per catalog, not once per cell. `CodeModeToolset::shared_nested_tool_metadata()` (by default `nested_tool_metadata()` in an `Arc`) lets a toolset hand out the same catalog while it is unchanged: the tool host's `ToolRegistry` keeps the metadata it last built with the definitions it built it from, and returns it while every nested definition has the same kind, name, description and schemas (compared by pointer first, since definitions share their text, then by value); it used to copy every description and schema into new JSON for each cell. `CodeModeRuntime` keeps the last catalog's `nanocode_code_mode_engine::CellTools` (the catalog as the bootstrap reads it, `CellTools::encode`) and reuses it for a cell whose metadata is the same `Arc` or equal, `EmbeddedHost::start_cell` takes that `Arc<CellTools>` on both targets, and a cell's journal record shares the metadata instead of copying it. The bootstrap parses the tools' descriptions only when a cell reads one from `ALL_TOOLS` (each entry's `description` is an enumerable getter over the list, parsed once per cell, as `toolSchema` already parsed schemas), so a cell that only calls tools parses their names and kinds. A registry's first cell still builds and encodes its catalog. Why: in tempoxyz/pyramid's production traces (Tempo, 3 h to 2026-11-04 13:30 UTC) a `code_mode.cell` that made one `exec_command` call took p50 7.1 ms and p90 21 ms beyond the call (n=1,122; about 280k cells a day), of which 3.2 ms p50 (p90 12.7 ms) passed between the cell's span and its actor's, where the metadata was built. Measured on 2026-11-04 (Linux, 32 cores, load average 5 to 13, release build; example not committed) with cells 20 ms apart that await one instant nested call and print its output, over catalogs of tools with a 1.5 KB description and an 8-property schema each: time beyond the nested call p50 1.17-1.36 ms before and 0.40-0.44 ms after at 60 tools, 2.99-3.13 ms and 0.70-0.81 ms at 150, 0.25-0.31 ms and 0.22-0.26 ms with none; back to back at 150 tools 2.87-2.89 ms and 0.37-0.65 ms. A cell printing `ALL_TOOLS`, a `toolSchema` and `Object.keys(tools)` printed the same before and after. The WASM target was not built (rquickjs-sys needs a wasi-sdk toolchain the machine lacked).
- `Tools::with_descriptions(&map)` (and `ToolsBuilder::with_descriptions`) gives each registered tool the map names the description it maps that name to (`ToolDefinition::with_description`), keeping its schema, exposure, place in the catalog and handler; Code Mode's `exec` renders the replaced text for a nested tool. tempoxyz/pyramid pins a warm thread's tool prose this way across a deploy that rewords a description, which otherwise had the thread's next turn write its whole prompt cache again (43% of first calls missed 5-15 min after such a deploy, 64% at 15-60 min, 2026-11-04). The tool-host crate runs its unit tests again (`cargo test -p nanocode-tool-host`).
- A Claude model step that completed replays across a Nanocodex bump that changed the request's host prefix. A step's durable input (`PreparedWire::durable_input`) now records, beside the whole body's blake3, a `conversation` digest (the blake3 of the request's messages as sent, without their `cache_control` markers, since where a host puts its breakpoints and their TTL don't change what the model reads; only a key can follow `{` or `,` with an unescaped quote, so a message quoting a marker keeps it) and a `prefix` digest (everything around the messages: tools, system prompt, settings). When a recovering run's request for a recorded step differs, the Claude durability policy (`earlier_input`) still tries the earlier builds' formats first (the whole body's blake3 alone, `blake3_durable_input`, then its sha256, then the body itself); after that, only for a `Completed` step, it reads the recorded input and, when `PreparedWire::same_conversation` finds the same wire and conversation digest, begins the step with that recorded input, so it replays the recorded response, and emits a `nanocode_claude` warning and `monotonic_counter.nc.claude.steps_replayed_across_prefix` on `nc_metrics`. A step whose effect is still pending, a changed conversation, and a step recorded before the conversation had its own digest still fail with `step … changed definition`. Codex model steps record no request (their input is `()`), so they have no such check. Why: tempoxyz/pyramid hands a running turn to the next build at every deploy, and when 5f163c932 put a cache breakpoint on every request's last tool, every turn handed off across that bump failed on the replay of its last completed model step (tempoxyz/pyramid#1895, which now restarts such a pass from its messages, losing its reasoning). Checked on 2026-11-04 with a mock Messages server and a Claude agent on a JSONL store whose Code Mode tool exits the process while the model step that called it is recorded (`examples/replay_check.rs`, not committed), resuming in another process: with a build that sends another system prompt, the pending turn replayed the step without a request and finished with one more (the build before failed with `step … changed definition` on `model-1`); with one that changes the first message, it failed as before; a step recorded by the build before replayed in this one with the same request and failed with another system prompt; and `same_conversation` held for another tools list and an added or changed cache marker and not for another message, a message quoting a marker, or an input of the build before.
- A Claude request carries a cache breakpoint at the end of its tools as well as at the end of its system prompt (`MessagesRequest::cache_system_prefix_with`, which also marks the last tool the prompt carries, not a deferred one, with the request's cache control; `ToolDefinition::cache_control`; server tools take it in their options), unless the host placed a tool breakpoint itself or the request would then have more than three, which keeps room for the subscription wire's identity block under the API's four. The tools come first in the cached prefix and depend only on the host's catalog, while the system prompt after them often differs between sessions that share it (a member's memory, a project's instructions): a session whose system prompt nobody sent before now reads the tools another session wrote, where it wrote them again. In tempoxyz/pyramid's traces of 2026-11-03 (254 Claude sessions), `prompt.system` took 216 values and `prompt.tools` 49, and 89 of the 106 root first calls that read nothing had tools an earlier session had sent; the tools are most of such a prompt (a Pyramid root's first call of about 66k tokens carries about 6k of static instructions). Checked on 2026-11-04 by building two requests with the same tools and different system prompts: the same `prompt.tools` fingerprint, both ending tools and system with a 1h breakpoint, four breakpoints on the subscription wire, and a request whose host already placed three system breakpoints left without a tool breakpoint.
- A remote command queue (`code_mode::remote::serve_queue`) polls at once when a wakeup arrives or a released command is to be retried. Before, it set the next poll to now and slept until then, and tokio's millisecond timer rounded that up to the next tick, about 1.2 ms on every admitted call. The early poll happens once per wakeup, so a `QueueOptions` with a zero `poll` or `busy_poll` still reaches the `select!` between polls. Checked on 2026-11-04 with tempoxyz/pyramid-core's broker-e2e (its broker at b67f800, which pins this repository at 093ebd423; a real `pyramid-hand` on loopback; ParadeDB 0.26.1-pg18; `exec_command true`; 300 serial calls; 4 interleaved runs at load 4-9), against 093ebd423 itself through the same path patch: serial round trip p50 3.09-3.90 → 1.94-2.12 ms, the broker's overhead beyond the command 2.04-2.52 → 0.88-1.00 ms, a call through `HandTool` 3.31-4.07 → 2.06-2.31 ms.
- A Codex agent's compactions can run at a lower reasoning effort than its turns: `AgentHandle::set_compaction_thinking(Some(effort))` (`ModelConfig::compaction_thinking`; the driver hands it to the run before each turn and manual compaction, so it applies from the next one on, and is not persisted) appends `{"type":"configuration_update","reasoning":{"effort":…}}` right before the `compaction_trigger` of that request only, leaving the request-level `reasoning.effort` at the context's baseline, so the compaction still reads the cached prefix (OpenAI's reasoning guide, "Change reasoning mid-conversation": an update between responses keeps the prompt prefix, and an explicit `compaction_trigger` may follow a history with updates; only automatic compaction and `/responses/compact` refuse them). The update never enters the conversation: installing a compaction replays the next request in full at the selected effort, and a failed compaction leaves the history as it was. It applies only where `supports_reasoning_effort_updates` (Sol, Terra, Astra and Daybreak Blue, not through a gateway) and the model accepts the effort, and only when it differs from the context's current effort (its latest authored update, else its baseline); elsewhere a compaction runs as before, byte for byte, because changing the request-level effort instead would make it read its whole context uncached. The `model.compaction` span now carries `reasoning.effort`, the effort it ran at. Other backends refuse the setting. Why: tempoxyz/pyramid's GPT compactions took p50 91.5 s at xhigh and 50.5 s at medium (Tempo, 24 h to 2026-11-04, ~240k tokens 99-100% cached, ~4.2k output tokens), all mid-turn, so members waited on them inside running turns. Checked on 2026-11-04 with a mock Responses server (Astra at xhigh, manual compaction twice; example not committed): with `medium` each compaction request ended in `configuration_update:medium`, `compaction_trigger` with `reasoning.effort` `xhigh`, its input began with every item of the request before it (item hashes equal) with the same instructions, tools, reasoning and cache key, and the turns after it carried no `medium` update; with none or `xhigh`, and on Luna with `medium`, the requests were as before. Not checked against the live API.
- A Claude model call's span (`model.call`, exported as `chat <model>`) says what decides whether the provider's prompt cache serves it, so a call that wrote its whole prompt to the cache again can be told apart from the session's previous call without the request: `prompt.tools`, `prompt.system` and `prompt.first_message` are fingerprints (`nanocode_backend_claude_client::fingerprint`, the xxh64 of the JSON, never the text) of the request's tools, system prompt and first message as the agent sends them, before the subscription wire adds its per-request billing block, in the order the cache reads them, so the first that changed is where the cache stopped matching; `request.speed` (each speed has its own cache), `request.cache_ttl` (`5m` or `1h`, the automatic breakpoint's, which `cache_system_prefix` also gives the system prompt's) and `request.wire` (`api` or `subscription`, whose cache is the member's account's) say which cache it asked for. Added 2026-11-04 after tempoxyz/pyramid's prd traces showed a fifth of turns' first calls reading nothing from the cache (`cached_input_tokens` 0) with no way to tell whether the tools, the system prompt or the conversation had changed.
- nanocode-chatgpt-extension serves a browser extension of the host's own as a tool and chooses which browser's ChatGPT extension to proxy, which tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand did itself. `ExtensionLink::new(name, absent)` holds one live extension connection at a time: `serve(socket)` binds a 0600 Unix socket (at most 8 connections at once), greets the first connection with `{"type":"hello","hand":name}` and answers any other with `{"type":"status","connection":"other_browser"}`; `connected()` says whether one is live, and `set_unavailable(reason)` appends a reason to the `absent` text a call gets with no connection. `ExtensionTool::new(link, definition)` is the `Tool`: it refuses arguments that are not an object or are over 32 KiB, sends each call as `{"type":"call",id,session,turn,input}`, waits 45 s for the `result` with that `id` (`ok`, `summary`, `result` or `error`), and sends `{"type":"turn_ended",session,turn}` from `end_turn`. `relay_host(claimed, socket)` is the native-messaging host the extension starts: it forwards its length-prefixed stdin frames to the socket as lines and the socket's lines back as frames, reports `{"type":"status","connection":"unclaimed"}` or `"hand_offline"` when it changes, retries every 2 s, and exits when the browser closes stdin. `Bridge::choose(preferred, default)` proxies, in the default browser first, the first browser with the ChatGPT extension that has not declined it, unless one of the `preferred` extension IDs is on in any browser or a browser was proxied explicitly, and releases every other automatic proxy, returning `Choice::Proxied`, `Released` or `Failed` by browser name; `Bridge::proxy(browser)` installs an explicit proxy and forgets a decline, `Bridge::decline(browser)` releases it and writes the `chatgpt-<key>.off` marker (`declined_path`), `Bridge::roots()` lists the existing browser directories once each by canonical path, and `Bridge::not_started()` words `launches` for a member ("<browser> has not started <owner>'s ChatGPT connector since it was registered …"). `cached_default_browser(home)` runs `default_browser` again only when LaunchServices' plist changes size or modification time. The texts are the Hand's, moved unchanged; checked with this crate's unit tests (`choice.rs`: default first, a preferred extension anywhere proxies nothing, a declined browser is skipped).
- Hosts that serve listed directories, such as tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand, get their placement from the Codex backend instead of their own wrappers (unix, `builtin_tools::served`). `ServedDirectories::new(file, home, refusal, report)` reads `file`, a JSON array of absolute paths, again only when the open file's size or modification time changes, it is unlinked, or (checked at most once a second, by one caller at a time) the path names another inode; `report(message, error)` is called for each failure it turns into one of its messages and `refusal` is the text of a workdir that is neither absolute nor one of those directories or `home` (a listed path that is a real directory matches without `canonicalize`). `ServedTools::new(directories, runtime, read_file, chunk)` is a `ToolSource` adding `exec_command` and `write_stdin` through the `WorkspaceToolRuntime`, the first refusing such a workdir and dropping `sandbox_permissions`, `justification` and `prefix_rule`, and `read_file` with the host's `read_file` definition: a path relative to a served workdir without `.`, `..` or empty components, no symbolic link followed (a last one answers `{exists, link}` with its target), opened with `O_NOFOLLOW` and refused when its device and inode differ from the walk's, at most `chunk` bytes from `offset` as base64 `data` with `exists`, `size`, `modified_ns` and `offset`. In nanocode-process, `file_secrets(file)` is a `SecretEnvironment` reading a JSON object of names and values on every command and keeping names of `A-Z`, `0-9` and `_` not starting with a digit; `ShellSessions::idle_for()` is zero while a command runs (and restarts its clock then) and otherwise the time since a call ended or a command was last seen running; `ShellSessions::quiet()` returns once no command runs, woken by each command's exit and each session's removal instead of polling `running()`; and `terminate_marked(variable, value, grace)` (unix) sends SIGTERM to every process whose environment holds `variable=value` (`/proc` on Linux, `ps -E` elsewhere) other than this process and its process group, waits up to `grace` for them to go, SIGKILLs those left in up to five rounds and returns how many it signalled. All of these were tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand's own code (`machine.rs`, `control.rs`), moved with their messages; checked on 2026-11-03 only with `cargo check` and clippy.
- `nanocode_home::service` (Unix) installs a program as a per-user service that starts at login and restarts when it fails: a launchd LaunchAgent in `~/Library/LaunchAgents/<name>.plist` on macOS (`launchctl bootstrap` in `gui/<uid>` after a quiet `bootout`, `kickstart -k` to restart, `KeepAlive` only on an unsuccessful exit, so exit 0 stops it) and a systemd user unit in `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/systemd/user/<name>` (or `~/.config`) elsewhere (`enable --now`, restarted when it was already active, `Restart=on-failure`). `UserService { name, home }` names it (the launchd label or the unit's file name) and has `definition_path`, `installed`, `install(&ServiceProgram)`, `restart` (false when it is not installed or not running), `uninstall` (false when there was nothing to remove) and `state` (`loaded`/`not loaded`, or `systemctl --user is-active`'s answer); `ServiceProgram` is the program, its arguments and environment, the unit's description, the launchd log file, the restart delay, launchd's `Interactive` process type and optional comments written into the definition, and renders either definition with `launchd_plist(label)` and `systemd_unit()`. Every value must be UTF-8 without control characters and a comment cannot contain `--`; failures are `io::Error`s naming the command and its stderr. It is tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand's service code (`crates/hand/src/service.rs`) with Pyramid's label, unit, texts and hints left to the caller: for Pyramid's values both definitions are byte-identical to what the Hand wrote (18 combinations of executable and `PYRAMID_HAND_HOME` paths with spaces, quotes, `&<>`, `%`, `$` and backslashes compared on 2026-11-03).
- A zsh shell server started by a process without a controlling terminal (CI, `nc exec` in a pipe, Docker, systemd) gets a pty of its own as its stdin (`own_terminal` in `server.rs`, macOS and Linux). zsh refuses `setopt monitor` without a terminal, so every command it forked stayed in the server's process group: cancelling a command `killpg`s its PID, which found no group (`ESRCH`, read as already exited), and the command ran on and wrote into the next command of its slot. zsh takes its terminal from fd 0 when it starts, so a pty that is only its stdin, never its controlling terminal, is enough for job control: background jobs get their own process groups again, while a command that opens `/dev/tty` (`git credential fill`, `sudo`, `ssh`) still fails at once, where making the pty the controlling terminal (`setsid -c`) left such commands stopped for good by SIGTTIN and a large write to `/dev/tty` blocked on the unread pty. Nothing reads or writes the pty, the server keeps its master open while it runs, and a process that has a terminal still gives zsh `/dev/null` and leaves job control to that terminal. The bootstrap prints `$options[monitor]` after its names, and `read_ready` refuses a server without job control (`Stop::Unready`), so commands start their own shells rather than run where they cannot be cancelled. A command's `Lease` now owns its process-group guard until `Pending::spawned` takes it: a call dropped after the server sent it the lease and before it took it dropped the lease, which returned the slot's pipes and left the command running, with a terminal as well (6 to 16 of 900 calls cancelled at 0 ms on master). Without a terminal, the process that started the servers dying now ends their running commands too (zsh HUPs its jobs when it exits with monitor on), as it already did with one. Codex starts a shell per command and Claude Code sources a shell snapshot in one; on this machine a fresh `zsh -lc` took 2.0 ms, sourcing Claude Code's 40 KB snapshot 4.7 ms, a served command 0.8 to 1.0 ms and `posix_spawn` of `/bin/true` 0.4 ms. On 2026-11-03 on Linux 6.8 (zsh 5.9, 12 CPUs, load average under 1; harness over `ShellSessions::execute` not committed, two interleaved rounds per build), 900 calls cancelled after 0 to 24 ms and 900 cancelled at 0 ms each followed by an `echo` that had to print only its own marker: on master without a terminal 896 to 899 and 830 to 846 of them still ran to the end and 158 to 161 outputs leaked, with a terminal 0 to 1 and 6 to 16; now 0 of 900 in every run, with and without a terminal, and 20 of 20 commands ran in the server in their own process group. `/dev/tty` reads, `stty`, `git credential fill` and `sudo -k -v` failed in 1 to 8 ms without a terminal before and after. One command at a time took 0.75 to 0.99 ms p50 against 0.78 to 1.02 on master, and 512 at once 8,400 to 11,000 calls/s against 8,300 to 10,900. On macOS the same harness, run once per case, gave 899 and 876 of 900 on master without a terminal (173 outputs leaked) and 2 and 9 with one, and 0 of 900 in every case after; `/dev/tty` reads, `stty` and `git credential fill` failed in 1 to 54 ms without a terminal before and after, one command at a time took 0.82 to 0.83 ms p50 against 0.79 to 0.80, and 512 at once 4,000 to 5,200 calls/s against 4,100 to 5,600.
- Claude agents can send their conversation in a message thread (beta, `message-threads-2026-08-12`), so each model request after the first sends only the messages after the previous response and the API skips validating and tokenizing the rest: `ClaudeBuilder::message_threads(true)`, off by default because the API holds a thread's conversation in memory for up to an hour and threads are not eligible for zero data retention. The client crate's `thread` module is sans-IO: `MessageThread::plan(head, messages, now)` returns a `ThreadTurn` that continues the stored thread only when the conversation begins with exactly the messages it holds (a blake3 digest of their encodings; the reply is hashed as the `Message` the agent appends, through the same encoding as the history, so the two cannot drift apart), the create-only and fixed fields (`model`, `system`, `tools`, `tool_choice`, `thinking`, `context_management`, `output_config`, top-level `cache_control`, and `speed`, which selects a beta) are unchanged, and less than `THREAD_IDLE_EXPIRY` (5 minutes, documented) has passed since the last response completed; otherwise it creates one. `ClaudeClient::attach_thread` makes a `PreparedWire` send that turn (a continue leaves out `system` and `tools`) while the wire keeps its full body for durable inputs, so a recovered step's digest does not depend on whether the API still held the thread. `MessageThread::complete` anchors on a response unless it is a refusal or ends in a tool call cut off by `max_tokens`, which the API does not store; `recover` reads `error.details.error_code`: `thread_not_found`, `thread_already_continued`, `thread_continue_in_flight` and `thread_fingerprint_mismatch` on a continue resend at once as a create, which is not counted as an attempt, and `thread_unsupported_request`, or `thread_not_found` on the first continue after such a create, sends the conversation without a thread from then on. Other errors leave the thread as it was, so a retried continue whose attempt the API consumed fails with a thread error and is replayed. Compaction, image eviction or any other edit of the history starts a new thread. `ThreadTurn::mode()` says whether a request continues, creates (with a `CreateReason`: `first`, `not_stored`, `lost`, `consumed`, `expired`, `fields_changed` or `history_diverged`) or goes unthreaded; the agent logs each create's reason at debug level and adds `message_thread` (`mode`, `create_reason`) to `model.call.completed`, so a host can watch its continue rate, and `history_diverged` on turns that only appended messages flags an encoding drift that would otherwise only cost time. Only the conversation's own steps use it (not compaction or side requests), and never the subscription wire. Checked on 2026-11-02 with a mock Messages server that enforces the documented rules (`examples` harness not committed): Code Mode turns continued with one message per request and no `system` or `tools`, a lost thread (404) replayed the 9-message conversation once and continued after it, 5 minutes idle created a new thread without a 404, and `thread_unsupported_request` went unthreaded for good; not yet checked against the live API.
- A native hand can charge for its operations: `Hand::pricing(HandPricing)` (`nanocode_tool_runtime::remote`, re-exported as `nanocode::hand::HandPricing` and `PricedOperation`), and `nc hand --pricing <file>` with `{"payee": "0x…", "describe": "…", "rules": [{"paths": ["data/**"], "ops": ["read"], "usd": 0.01}]}` (`HandPricing::rules`, unknown fields refused). An optional `"currency"` (`HandPricing::currency`, `OUSD` by default or `USDC.e`, matched without case or punctuation, anything else refused at startup; added 2026-10-31) names the token the payee is paid in: tempoxyz/gateway's hub has agents pay the payee over MPP session channels in that token, and MACH cannot be offered because it reaches only whitelisted merchants. `HandPricing::new(payee, price)` takes any `Fn(&PricedOperation) -> Result<Option<f64>, String>`, where the operation is the request as it arrives: `kind` (the tool name, `execute` or `wait`), `access` (`read`, `write`, `run`, `cell`, `tool`), `input`, `command`, `paths` (the input's path fields, an `apply_patch` patch's files and the command's words after the first, relative to the hand's directory), `source`, `session`, and the `agent` and `max` (`max_usd`) a billing brain stamps. A rule matches when one of its `ops` is the kind or the access (`read` also matches a `run` whose command names a path) and one of its `paths` globs (`*`, `**`, `?`) matches a path or, for a command, the path is `.` or a directory above the pattern; the first match wins and no match is free. The connection layer (`serve_link`, so the hand core is unchanged) appends `pricing: { payee, describe, currency }` to the catalog (`currency` only when set), adds a `pricing` capability (`Priced` wraps the hand's device), refuses a priced request before the core receives it when it carries no `max_usd` or costs more, and appends `charge: { usd, item }` to that request's update frames and Ok reply, keeping a resumable request's quote until an `Ack` names it so a reply sent again on resume carries it again. `--tools-only` makes the CLI hand run no cells, so each tool call reaches the rules one by one; on a hand that runs cells only the `Execute` can be priced. The frame fields match gakonst/nanocode-ts `Hand.connect`'s `pricing`, and brains that do not bill ignore them. Checked on 2026-10-31 through tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker against Tempo mainnet: `nc hand --pricing` with the rule above and `--tools-only` charged $0.01 once for `cat data/secret.txt` and nothing for `cat notes.txt`, refused the read under a $0.005 `max_usd` before running it, and attached straight to an agent session (no billing hub) refused it as `the brain that sent it does not bill hand charges`; a payee that is not an address and a rule with an unknown field fail at startup.
- A host transport can tell which agent made a model request. `nanocode::http::origin` holds an `Origin` with `context`, the opaque origin `current()` returns as before, and `session`, the session ID of the agent whose model call is polled: `origin::session()` reads it, `origin::captured()` reads both, `Origin::scope(future)` sets both, and `origin::scope(context, future)` still sets the context alone. The Claude backend scopes the session around each model response, Codex around a turn's and a compaction's task and around side questions, and `ResponsesAttempt` captures the whole `Origin` for the Responses connection owner. `SubagentControl::lineage(session_id)` answers a subagent session's agent ID and its ancestors' (the subagent first, its top-level ancestor, the child of the root its branch starts from, last) and nothing for a root or unknown session. A child's host context is still the one it was spawned with, so hosts that authorize requests by origin are unchanged. `AgentUpdate::Event` carries the child's `parent` (None for a child of the root), which session events and stored session-log events write beside `id`, so a host can filter a log into per-subagent and per-branch feeds without the directory. MCP requests leave the calling agent's poll through the MCP session's outbound channel and carry no session; a host reads the caller from a `tools/call`'s `_meta["x-codex-turn-metadata"].session_id`. Roots that adopt a child journal (`AgentHandle::child_journal`, as gakonst/nanocode-ts' do) keep `DEFAULT_MAX_RESIDENT_SUBAGENTS` (16), because only `Registry::set_store` lowers it to `DURABLE_MAX_RESIDENT_SUBAGENTS`, and eviction takes only idle children, so the limit never holds back a running one.
