Checked on 2026-10-30 through tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker with nanocode-ts' new subagent routes (DeepSeek V4.1 Flash): every model request of a Codex root, its children and a grandchild, and of a Claude root's child, reached the egress with the right subagent and top-level ancestor (a grandchild under child 1 as 2 and 1, the root's own with none), stored child events carried `"parent":null` or the parent's ID, and four children of one root ran at once with the resident limit set to 2.
- A Codex turn whose model response has neither a final message nor an `exec` or `wait` call appends one `<empty_response>` user message (`model/input.rs`, `empty_response`) and samples again instead of failing; a second such response in the same turn fails as before (`malformed Responses API event: model completed without a final message or exec call`), and a response with a message, even an empty one, still ends the turn. On 2026-10-30 two DeepSeek V4.1 Flash subagents behind tempoxyz/gateway each ended a model call of over two minutes with neither, and both failed for good. Checked with a scripted Responses provider in Node (gakonst/nanocode-ts `output/empty-reply.ts`): a reasoning-only response followed by a message completed with the message, the second request carrying the nudge; two reasoning-only responses failed after the one nudge; the build before failed on the first.
- Code Mode cells on WASM survive their host's restart. The engine replays a journal on WASM too (`start_cell` runs the cell with the journal's inputs through `replay_inputs`, with an interrupt budget scaled by the inputs replayed), and `CellJournals::with_sink(sink)` makes every cell write its journal ahead to a `CellJournalSink` as ordered `CellJournalUpdate`s (one `Sequencer` per cell, so a later update never lands before an earlier one): before a nested call is dispatched (the update carries the entries up to its `Call` entry), once the worker has consumed a nested call's result and has nothing left to run (`RuntimeEvent::Waiting`, sent only by cells that journal), when an observer begins (its call ID, and `content_returned` from the observation before, which the agent has committed by then) and when a cell yields; a held cell's result, which the worker does not consume, is written as `Completed` at once. A result reaches the journal as its `Result` entry, so the script can use it before it is saved: whatever it does next that leaves the cell (a nested call, a yield) writes first, and a crash before that replays the call as running, as a crash just before the result arrived would; an update that would carry nothing new (no entries, no call state, observer or returned content the sink does not already hold) is not written. Until 2026-11-04 each result was saved as `Completed` before the script got it, so a sequential loop of 20 nested calls wrote 40 updates and now writes 20, and 10 parallel calls 20 and now 12 (`output/hand-wiring` `sinkreplay`, which also crashes a cell mid-loop, while it waits on a timer after a result and while one of two parallel calls runs, and resumes each without running a call twice and with every result the script had used); gakonst/nanocode-ts, which writes a row per update and deletes them when the cell retires, wrote 108.3 rows for a turn whose cell makes 20 sequential calls instead of 148.3 and 70.3 for 10 parallel calls instead of 88.3. nothing is written for a cell that finishes in its first observation without nested calls, and a cell's journal is forgotten when it retires (observed to its end, terminated or diverged). An update with a `header` replaces the cell's stored journal; an adopted cell writes nothing until its replay ends, so a partly replayed journal never replaces a whole one. `CellJournal::fold(updates)` rebuilds a journal from the stored updates (contiguous by `seq`; a call whose `Result` entry is in the journal is dropped from `calls`). A replayed call that was running gets its outcome from `CodeModeToolset::recover_nested` before falling back to the unknown outcome: `LocalTools` sends a call that named a `hand` to `HandCalls::recover`, which `CodeModeExecutors` answers with that hand's `CodeModeHost::recover_call` (`RemoteCodeModeHost::recover_call`, so `RemoteTransport::recover`). `LocalCells::journals(journals)` gives each session's runtime the journals, `CodeModeHost::adopt(context, tools)` (default none) adopts restored cells with the cell tools, which `EmbeddedToolRuntime` calls before `exec`, `wait` and `recover_code` on code-only hosts and `CodeModeExecutors` sends to every executor that runs cells, `LocalCells::recover` answers `recover_code` through `recover_with_updates`, and `CodeModeExecutors` tries its fallback executor after the placement for `recover` and `wait`, because after a restart the executor that ran a cell is not recorded. gakonst/nanocode-ts persists the journals in the session's SQL store and restores them when a runtime opens. Checked on 2026-10-30 through tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker, restarting it (every Durable Object reset, every socket closed) during a Codex turn: a cell printing a line every 5 s, observed by a `wait` when the restart came, replayed and finished with its four lines once each instead of reporting the `wait` as interrupted by a host crash; and a cell in the agent whose `exec_command` on a tools-only hand was running finished with the command's real output after 20.06 s of wall time, the command having run once, the nested call matched in the journal instead of dispatched again and its result recovered from the hand.
- On WASM a host's prepared context (`prepare_later`, made 20 ms after each cell) is cleared before it drops, as the native worker's is: dropping an `Engine` (`EmbeddedHost::terminate` and `Drop`), replacing a prepared context whose prelude no longer matches and a preparation that fails each `clear_pending` the state first, because the natives hold the state and the state holds the bootstrap's `uncaught` handler, a cycle QuickJS's collector cannot see. Before, terminating an idle host aborted the module on `JS_FreeRuntime`'s leak assertion (`RuntimeError: unreachable` from `__assert_fail`, with no panic message). The cell actor terminates the host a finished cell displaces from the shared slot, so a second cell that ran and finished while a first was still running, such as an `exec` while a yielded cell waits, brought down the runtime when the first one finished; tempoxyz/gateway's agents then recovered the turn from its checkpoint, reporting the in-flight `wait` as interrupted by a host crash and the cell as not found. On 2026-10-30 through the gateway's local Worker (nanocode-ts build-58e8696 with the module rebuilt with `strip = "none"` so the trap's frames had names), a Codex turn that yielded a 30 s cell, ran `echo hi` in a second cell and then waited on the first trapped in 4 of 4 runs before and returned `done` in 2 of 2 after, and a single cell yielded and waited on alone finished in the build before.
- A subagent's thinking is not bounded by its parent's unless the host opts in: `SpawnPolicy::bounded_by_parent_thinking` (default false; a missing field deserializes as false) refuses `spawn_agent` with `SpawnPolicyError::ThinkingExceedsParent` ("<child> thinking exceeds the spawning agent's own <parent> thinking") when the child's resolved thinking ranks above its spawning agent's, the root and registered subagents alike, and with `ParentThinkingUnknown` when the spawning agent's thinking is not recorded; it also applies when a child's settings are re-admitted from its checkpoint, and adds "A subagent cannot request more effort than its spawning agent's own." to `spawn_agent`'s `thinking` description. Until ee0f8ba1e the bound was on by default and applied to an unregistered parent even when off; now nothing bounds a child by its parent when it is off, while `min_thinking` and `max_thinking` bound every child either way. With the bound on, a harness switch with `thinking: null` resolves to that harness's default effort (medium for Claude and Sol) and is refused under a parent at low: on 2026-10-29 tempoxyz/gateway's agents (nanocode-ts's Cloudflare app, GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash at low) failed their first such spawn and retried with `thinking: low`, which is why it is off by default.
- JSON numbers parse to the exact double (2026-11-09): the workspace's `serde_json` has `float_roundtrip`. Its default parser is best-effort: of 1,000,000 random doubles in [0, 1) written as JSON and read back, 105,566 came back as another double (`output/floatcheck`, serde_json 1.0.150, comparing bits), and none with the feature. That broke replay. A cell journal is JSON text, so a restored `random` entry gave the script a different `Math.random()` than the run that recorded it, and a script that put the number into a string argument, such as `"job-" + Math.random()`, then reached a nested call whose input did not match the journal's and ended as `Script failed` ("This cell moved to another host and could not be resumed (at journal entry 1 the script reached call note (nested call 1) where the journal recorded call note (nested call 1))"). In nanocode-ts's `output/blog/replay-id.mjs` (Node, `node:sqlite`, a scripted Responses model, a cell that calls a tool with such an ID, sleeps 2.5 s and calls it again, process 1 killed 1.5 s into the sleep and process 2 opened on the same file) 6 of 80 cells failed that way on the module of nanocode-ts build-07238e5 and 0 of 60 on the same tree with only this change. The same parser read every tool argument: a cell that passed `0.09596104461529853` to a tool delivered `0.09596104461529852` to the host, and now delivers the number it wrote. The cost is in parsing floats only: 20 parses of an array of 100,000 17-digit floats took 71 to 72 ms instead of 55 ms, 20 parses of 100,000 text objects 596 to 616 ms either way, and nanocode-ts's `scripts/measure-node.ts` over five alternating runs opened a Claude runtime in 33 to 50 ms against 32 to 37 ms and ran a warm turn in 16 to 19 ms against 17 to 20 ms, within its run-to-run spread; its module grew from 12,110,113 to 12,140,549 bytes.
- Cell journaling takes no locks (2026-11-01): the QuickJS worker records a cell's journal into a `JournalWriter` it owns and publishes what it recorded as one immutable page (`SharedJournal`, an `Atom` holding the newest page, each page linking the one before) right before it sends any event to the host, so the host reads a consistent prefix that ends at an event (`entries_from(n)` walks back only over the pages after `n`, `entries()` the whole chain, `overflowed()` the flag); a page chain drops iteratively, so a journal of many pages cannot overflow the stack when it goes. `CellJournals` keeps its cells, held sessions and restored journals in `Atom`s, and a cell's `CellStatus` is an `Atom` too, read and changed through closures, whose `life` is one `CellLife` (`Active { replay: Replaying | Live, hold: Running | Held }`, `Finished`, `Retired`) where five booleans (held, replaying, finished, retired and the snapshot's `held || finished`) allowed a retired cell to count as held. These were the workspace's last `std::sync::Mutex`es (in `replay.rs`, `mod.rs` and the engine's `journal.rs`; a `chatgpt-extension` unit test used one too and now reads a channel). On 2026-11-01 tempoxyz/pyramid's five cell replay checks (`cell_replay_tests.rs`, ported to output/hand-wiring's `cellreplay`) passed, and a cell making 400,000 journaled `Date.now()` and `Math.random()` reads took 69-70 ms p50 before and after (`journalbench`, 7 cells, load average 27-49).
- Code Mode cells can move between hosts by replay (`code_mode::CellJournals`, `code_mode/replay.rs`, the engine's `journal.rs`). A runtime given a `CellJournals` handle (`Tools::builder().cell_journals(handle)`, `CodeModeRuntime::with_journals`) journals each cell's inputs in its QuickJS worker, in the order the script consumed them: every nested call it made (id, tool, arguments, host flag), every result delivered to it, every timer it set (with the wall time it was set) and fired, and every value it read from `Date`/`new Date()`, `performance.now()`, `Math.random()` and `bytes.random()` (the bootstrap wraps those globals; `Date` keeps its prototype, statics and `instanceof`). `hold(session)` makes a session's cells stop dispatching nested calls (the worker pauses timers and results until `release`; calls the script makes meanwhile are queued, results that arrive are kept) and `state(session)` says when every cell is held with no nested call running; `journals(session)` then snapshots each cell as a serializable `CellJournal` (the script, tool metadata, stored values, the journal, how many content events its observers already returned, and its undelivered calls as queued, running or completed). Another runtime's `restore(session, journals, fallback, outcomes)` adopts them on the session's next `exec`, `wait` or `recover_code` (`CodeModeRuntime::adopt`, under admission, keeping cell IDs): each cell re-runs its script with the journal, where a nested call matching the next entry returns nothing new (its recorded result arrives when the journal does), timers fire and values read in recorded order, and content already returned and notifications are not sent again; at the journal's end the worker sends `RuntimeEvent::Replayed`, timers left are rescheduled to their recorded due time, kept results are delivered, queued calls are dispatched, and calls that were running get `outcomes[call_id]` or an `outcome: unknown` failure, so a recorded call is never sent twice. A call, timer or value that doesn't match the next entry stops the replay (`RuntimeEvent::Diverged`) before anything is dispatched, and the cell ends as `Script failed` with the host's `fallback` text. `NativeToolRuntime::recover_code` now answers a local placement too: the agent's durable recovery of an `exec` or `wait` step that was in flight finds the restored cell by its origin or last observer call ID and observes it as that call would have. A journal over `JOURNAL_LIMIT_BYTES` (32 MiB) is dropped and the cell reports itself not replayable. WASM hosts record but can't hold or replay. Checked on 2026-10-30 from tempoxyz/pyramid's engine tests over `ToolRuntime` (`cell_replay_tests.rs`): `Promise.all`/`Promise.race` resolving out of order with a call completing while held, a polling loop with `setTimeout`, `Date.now()` and `Math.random()`, a yielded cell resumed by `wait` after the move, a cell moved twice, and a corrupted journal that falls back, each counting that no nested call ran twice; the WASM target was not built (rquickjs-sys needs a wasi-sdk toolchain that hand lacked).
- On macOS the CLI links neither Security.framework nor CoreFoundation, so launch no longer pays for their 401 dyld initializers (362 of them Network.framework's). The workspace patches `rustls-platform-verifier` 0.7.0 and `iana-time-zone` 0.1.65 with copies in `crates/patches` (`[patch.crates-io]`, excluded from the workspace; crates that depend on nanocode from elsewhere keep the published ones, so `nanocode-tls` uses only the upstream API). On macOS the patched verifier reads `NANOCODE_TLS_TRUST` once per verifier: `files`, the default and what fx (Zig's std) does, trusts every certificate in `/System/Library/Keychains/SystemRootCertificates.keychain` and `/Library/Keychains/System.keychain` and verifies chains with rustls' webpki verifier; `system` runs `/usr/bin/security verify-cert -p ssl -n <host>` on each chain it is given (DER files in a 0700 temporary directory), which is the macOS trust evaluation; any other value fails building the client with the variable's name. `files` reads the keychain file format as Zig does (each `X509_CERTIFICATE` table's record slots after its 28-byte header, each certificate 60 bytes into its record), but over `record_numbers_count` slots, where Zig reads `record_count` and missed one of this Mac's 12 System.keychain certificates; it does not apply admin trust settings, Apple's distrust list or certificate transparency, and it trusts every certificate in System.keychain as an anchor, so certificates only the login keychain or trust settings make trusted need `system`, which costs one `security` process per new TLS connection (50-200 ms; resumed sessions skip it). iOS keeps upstream's Security verifier. On macOS `nanocode-tls` loads `SSL_CERT_FILE` and `SSL_CERT_DIR` itself (PEM files, every regular file of each directory, as rustls-native-certs does) and depends on `rustls-native-certs` only elsewhere; tokio-tungstenite is built with `__rustls-tls`, since every `wss` dial passes `nanocode_tls::native_client_config`; the patched iana-time-zone reads the `/etc/localtime` link on macOS. On 2026-10-29 (dist builds, M1 Max, load average 100 to 340) `nc --version` took 21.7M instructions instead of 31.9M (fx 16.8M), 310 page reclaims instead of 592 (fx 279), 2.7 MB of RSS instead of 6.9, and ran 2 initializers instead of 401 (fx 2); a `files` verifier takes 0.75 to 2.8 ms to build. output/tlscheck checked reqwest's default client and `native_client_config` in every mode: api.openai.com, api.anthropic.com and https://1.1.1.1 connect, and wrong.host, expired, self-signed and untrusted-root.badssl.com are refused (`system` reports `NotValidForName` for a host mismatch and security's `Cert Verify Result` otherwise).
- Simple external commands of the zsh shell server start from the Hand without a shell once the server is known to give them the same process (`integrations/process/src/server/direct.rs`). The server's bootstrap reports its plain-named aliases and functions, builtins and reserved words, global aliases and suffix aliases; when the first server of a pool is ready, a background probe runs `/usr/bin/env -0` through it (the environment a command it execs gets, startup files included) and `/bin/sh -c 'umask; ulimit -a; ulimit -H -a'` both through it and directly, and only when those agree does the dispatcher hand out the snapshot, which goes when a startup file changes. A command whose words pass the same check as the server's `direct` prelude (`server.rs`) is then offered the snapshot instead of a slot: the Hand checks the names, builds the environment the prelude would (the snapshot with the command's differences from the server's launch environment exported or unset, every secret exported, `PWD` and `OLDPWD` the logical directory when the command's `PWD` is the same file as the workdir and the physical one otherwise, `_` the absolute path zsh would exec), resolves the program on that `PATH` as `whence -p` does (relative entries and paths against `PWD`), and spawns it on the blocking pool with `arg0` as typed, its own process group, `/dev/null` as stdin and two FIFOs opened with `O_CLOEXEC` as stdout and stderr, so its output descriptors cannot leak into another child as std's pipes can on macOS; a name, a resolution or a spawn that fails (a script without a shebang, a directory, a missing command) sends the command to the server as before, so error texts and statuses are zsh's. At most `available_parallelism() * DEPTH` direct commands run at once, the servers' own capacity; more queue on the servers, because a burst opens every command's descriptors at once (with the 256 descriptors a CLI process starts with, 200 `cat`s at once failed 36 to 94 times with `Too many open files` through this path and 120 to 124 through the servers; the CLI does not raise its limit, hands do). The command is the Hand's child, so `getppid()` is the Hand and its status comes from `waitpid` rather than the server's `w<pid>` round trip. output/hand-wiring's `directcheck` ran 18 commands through the servers and then directly with identical output and status (sorted `env` included, `ZDOTDIR` startup files with aliases, functions, global and suffix aliases and `PATH` changes, logical, physical and symlinked workdirs, `zsh:1: command not found`, `permission denied` for a directory, a script without a shebang), and a `.zshenv` with `umask 077` kept every command on the server. On 2026-10-29 `cat /etc/hosts` took the same 3.2 to 4.5 ms p50 as through the server (3.3 to 3.8) on a quiet moment, close to a bare tokio spawn (3.0 to 4.0), and 7.5 to 9.2 ms against 13.1 to 17.9 with 20 extra busy loops on the 10 cores (150 calls each, interleaved); a 300-step long tool turn of `cat` reads ran in 2.33 s instead of 3.35 (5.7 ms per step instead of 8.0; 4 interleaved runs at load average 100 to 135, with this build's `panic = "abort"` also changed), and its spans put `tool.execute` at 3.7 ms a step instead of 5.4.
- `[profile.dist]` sets `panic = "abort"`, as the published binaries should: the CLI binary went from 32.9 MB to 25.7 MB on 2026-10-29 (unwind tables and landing pads). In dist builds a panic ends the process instead of failing the tool call or task that panicked (the SDK's `catch_unwind` around tool dispatch has nothing to catch; the TUI's panic hook still restores the terminal before the abort). `release`, `dev` and `profiling` keep unwinding.
- The zsh shell server execs a single simple command whose command word is an external program instead of forking it again: the forked subshell `source`s the command file, which never execs its last command, and its EXIT trap needs it to outlive the command, so every external command cost two forks. When the script is one line of words made only of ASCII letters, digits and `_-./,:+=%` (none starting with `=`, the first neither an assignment nor starting with `-` or `%`), the prelude (`direct` in `server.rs`, on the first line so `zsh:1:` messages keep their line numbers) checks in the subshell that the command word has no alias, function, builtin or reserved word, that no word is a global alias and no suffix alias matches, and that `whence -p` finds it (or, for a path, that it is an executable file), and then runs `\builtin exec` with the same words; otherwise the command runs as before. zsh runs no EXIT trap on exec, so the status comes from the server's `wait` (`w<pid>`, the path an exec in a command already took), which costs one round trip to the server, and if the server dies while such a command runs its status is lost as it was for any command before the EXIT trap. Checking `${+commands[name]}` instead of `whence -p` rehashed every `PATH` directory after the bootstrap's `hash -r`, 17 ms with 27 entries. On 2026-10-29 at load average 135, `cat /etc/hosts` through `ShellSessions::execute` took 24 to 27 ms p50 against 30 to 41 ms for `cat /etc/hosts; :` (the old path) in the same build, 200 calls each, and a 300-step long tool turn of `cat` calls ran in 12.3 s instead of 14.1 at load average 150 (p50 of 4 interleaved runs).
- `JsonlStore::open_home_in_background(home)` returns the store at once while its worker opens the sessions directory (moving sessions out of a legacy `sessions.jsonl` first, see the one-file-per-session entry); operations queue behind the open (the job channel already ordered them), and an open that fails makes that error, not `JSONL durability worker stopped`, the result of every operation. The CLI opens its store this way (`host::open_store`), so the TUI's first frame no longer waits for the index; `nc exec` and `nc sessions` wait at their first store operation as before. On 2026-10-29, on an APFS clone of the user's store (24.7 GB journal, 90 MB index), main to the TUI's first frame took 13.5 ms p50 instead of 18.0 (6 interleaved runs at load average 125, from the process's OTLP spans).
- Published CLI binaries build with the `dist` profile (`release` with `lto = "fat"` and `codegen-units = 1`); `release` keeps `lto = "off"` for the edit-to-install loop. `scripts/release-cli.sh` builds both macOS targets with `--target` and `--profile dist` and the Linux ones with `--profile dist`, and builds `nanocode-cua-driver` with a cargo invocation of its own, because it is now its own package (`bin/cua-driver`, the former second binary of nanocode-cli, unchanged): cargo unifies features over every package a build selects, so while nanocode-cli depended on nanocode-cua for the helper, cua-driver's dependencies turned on features such as `image`'s default codecs (tiff, with a second JPEG decoder) in the main binary too. `cargo build -p nanocode-cli` alone therefore no longer builds the helper; build `-p nanocode-cua-driver` separately for computer use on macOS. On 2026-10-29 the main binary took 47.2 MB without the helper's features (47.7 before) and 32.9 MB as `dist`, with 48,823 dyld fixups instead of 69,957, while launch moved little (`--version` 33M instructions against 34M, 595 page faults against 620): two thirds of nanocode's launch above fx's is CoreFoundation and Security, which rustls-platform-verifier links and whose initializers (401, 362 of them Network.framework's) cost an empty C program 10M instructions and 285 page faults. Streaming through `nc exec` at load average 130 to 220, `dist` used 180M instructions and 103 ms of CPU for 5,000 tokens at 1,000 tok/s against 211M and 133 ms, and 763M and 257 ms against 1,012M and 384 ms for 50,000 at 10,000 tok/s (p50 of 6 interleaved runs). A `dist` build took 27 min of wall time and 17 min of CPU at load average 300.
- The TUI draws its first frame before the session exists: it starts the session in the background (`App::connect`, as it already did while waiting for a replacement hand), shows `connecting…` and queues anything submitted meanwhile, and removes a reload's state file once the session is ready, where it used to wait for `Host::start` and the agent's construction before drawing anything. `nc exec` drops the session's `AgentEvents` stream instead of draining it in a task of its own, so each event reaches one consumer (the turn's stream) instead of also waking a second task, and its printer reads only the payload fields it prints (borrowed, through a typed struct) instead of parsing every event into a `serde_json::Value`, skips kinds it does not print before parsing, and remembers an answer's item once per block instead of allocating and hashing its key for every delta; its output is byte-identical. On 2026-10-29, with the machine at load average 185 to 290, the TUI's first frame came 93 ms after spawn instead of 124 (p50 of 12 interleaved runs), and `nc exec` streaming 5,000 tokens at 1,000 tok/s used 134 ms of CPU and 242M instructions instead of 152 ms and 262M (p50 of 8 interleaved runs).
- `nc exec` writes streamed text at most once per display frame (`app::DISPLAY_FRAME`, the TUI's 60 Hz): `stream_turn` prints into a `BufWriter` and the printer buffers reasoning and notices for stderr, takes every event already delivered (`Turn::try_next`, which returns one without waiting), and flushes once nothing is waiting and a frame has passed since the last flush, so the first text after a pause still appears at once; a change between stdout and stderr blocks flushes the one it leaves, so their order holds. It used to write and flush every delta, and with output in a file `write` was half the process's samples while streaming. Against a local Responses mock (2026-11-04, load average 140 to 160) a reply of 8,000 deltas paced over 10 s took 465M instructions and 0.07 s of system CPU instead of 598M and 0.14 s, a burst of 64 paragraphs (38 KB) 88M instead of 110M and one of 8 paragraphs 58.6M instead of 61M, with byte-identical output. Without streaming, a one-turn `nc exec` (`--ephemeral`, `--telemetry off`) is 55M instructions, of which `nanocode --version` is 22M (dyld and process start), agent telemetry adds 12M and the durable store 5 to 8M; the installed and freshly built binaries ran the same 22M instructions and 335 page reclaims for `--version`, so the 58 ms against 9 ms measured on 2026-11-03 was the machine's load or a first launch's code-signature check, not the build.
- Subagent resume can be left to the host: `SubagentControl::set_manual_resume(true)` makes a root's restore keep the subagents its journal shows mid-turn as `Interrupted` instead of resuming them at once, and `deferred_resume(root)` lists them; `resume_restored(root)` resumes them as automatic restore does (`RESUME_MESSAGE`, `resuming` in `list_agents` and `wait_agent` until they run), and `discard_restored(root)` closes them. Hosts that commit a root's subagent journal only to move a running turn to another process, such as tempoxyz/pyramid handing a turn off during a deploy, resume children only on the run that claims that turn and close them on every other open, so a crash or a later turn never brings back subagents still running an old task. Automatic resume stays the default.
- The CLI no longer links the built-in cua-driver: it runs as a second executable of nanocode-cli, `nanocode-cua-driver` (`mcp` serves `nanocode_computer::embedded::serve_stdio`, `permissions` and `request-permissions` print what `embedded::permissions` and `request_permissions` report), which `release-cli.sh` builds and uploads as `nanocode-cua-driver-<target>.gz` for both macOS targets and `install` puts beside `nanocode` when the release has one (its checksum in `.nanocode-cua-driver.sha256`). Everything of nanocode-cua except `driver` and `embedded` moved to a new crate, nanocode-cua-tools (`ComputerConfig`, `ComputerTools`, `ProviderTool`, `browser`, the cua_repl provider and its catalogs), which nanocode-cua re-exports (`pub use nanocode_cua_tools::*`), so every `nanocode_computer::` path, `driver::Driver::detect` and `embedded` included, is unchanged for hosts such as tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand; the facade's and nanocode-agent-host's `cua` features now depend on nanocode-cua-tools, so `nanocode::cua` has no `driver` or `embedded` and nothing that links `nanocode` pulls in cua-driver-sdk. On macOS `ComputerConfig::cua_driver()` sets `CUA_DRIVER_PATH` to `nanocode-cua-driver` beside the running executable (`embedded_driver()`), leaving `CUA_DRIVER_ARGS` unset so the provider passes `mcp`, and only without it to the running executable with `cua driver-mcp`, as before, for hosts that build the driver into themselves; `nanocode cua install` and `status` ask the helper for permissions and the hidden `nanocode cua driver-mcp` runs `nanocode-cua-driver mcp`, and without the helper they say it is missing and to reinstall. macOS attributes Accessibility and Screen Recording as before, to the app that runs nanocode, since the driver was already a child process of it. With the driver's 22 frameworks and 25 Swift libraries no longer linked, dyld ran their initializers on every launch: on 2026-10-29 the `nanocode` binary linked 4 libraries instead of 49 (Security, CoreFoundation, libiconv, libSystem) and took 47.7 MB instead of 67.0 (the helper 23.9 MB), `nanocode --version` took 6.8 ms instead of 9.6 (hyperfine, 300 runs; fx 3.7) and 7.1 MiB instead of 9.7 at 34.0M instructions instead of 47.0M, and a new session's `nc exec` over 1,000 sessions used 16.7 ms of CPU instead of 18.4 (p50 of 40 interleaved runs); `nanocode cua status` reported both permissions through the helper, its MCP server listed the running apps, and the cua_repl provider ran `cua.listApps()` through it.
- The legacy shared journal (`sessions.jsonl`, which `JsonlStore` now opens only to move its sessions out) saves its index sidecar by appending to a copy of the file it loaded instead of rewriting it: `std::fs::copy` clones the file (`fclonefileat` on APFS, `copy_file_range` on Linux), and only if the copy still ends with the header this process loaded (same length and header checksum) does it append the record blocks of states whose sorted encoding differs from the block they were loaded from (or that are new), a summaries block if those changed, and a new header and trailer, then rename the copy over the index as before, so a save writes what changed plus the header instead of copying and hashing every deferred block. Record blocks and the summaries block are now encoded in key order so an unchanged state encodes to the block it was loaded from; the first save after loading an older index appends each loaded state once. When the file would exceed twice the bytes its new header references, when another process replaced the index, or when there is no current-format index to copy, it rewrites the whole file as before, so a rewrite's cost is spread over at least as many appended bytes. Older builds read appended files, since they find the header through the trailer and every block precedes it. On 2026-10-29, on an APFS clone of the user's store (24.7 GB journal, 90 MB index of 458 states), a samply profile of one `nc exec` put 67% of its 150 ms of CPU in the exit save (reading 33%, SHA-256 21%, writing 12%); a new session's `nc exec` there took 19 to 21 ms of CPU and 39 to 51 ms instead of 90 to 95 ms and 110 to 385 ms, each save appending about 270 KB (the header is 265 KB). Over 1,000 one-turn sessions (header 556 KB) a new session took 21.5 ms of CPU instead of 29.5 and `nc exec -c` 34.3 instead of 42.6 (p50 of 40 and 30 interleaved runs), and the appended index and one rebuilt from the journal read the same 18,097 records (62.7 MB).
- `JsonlStore` stores a record that another state already holds once (since 2026-11-03 only among the states that share a session file: a root, its `:subagents` journal and its `:process-routes` state; a session file never names another): a fresh record whose key another state holds with the same value is written as a stand-in, `\u{1}` and the holder's state ID, and reads resolve it to the holder's value, so the instructions and tool definitions every session's first checkpoint carries (about 57 KB of chunks) are no longer copied into every session. A literal value that starts with `\u{1}` is stored with a second `\u{1}` in front, so no stored value is ambiguous; a value an older build stored that starts with `\u{1}` would read as a stand-in, which no SDK payload does, since JSON text escapes control characters and chunks are slices of it. The holder is found among other states newest first, each holder's value compared byte for byte; a state whose record block is still deferred in the index sidecar costs its block's size to search, and the search stops once that would exceed the bytes of the commit's fresh records still unshared, so a commit never decodes more index than it writes, and it stops when every record has a holder. A stand-in always names a state that holds the literal value; JSONL deletes no record and compaction keeps every indexed record, so holders outlive their stand-ins. Builds before this one cannot read a session that holds stand-ins (`invalid durability state: invalid payload record`). On 2026-10-29 (output/bench-vs `gen-nc.mjs`, one-turn `nc exec` sessions, 4 at a time) 1,000 sessions took a 13.7 MB journal and a 2.8 MB index instead of 70.7 and 3.4 MB (13.7 KB a session; fx 4.7 KB on disk), 400 sessions 16 at a time shared every repeated record, a new session's `nc exec` on that store used 29.6 ms of CPU against 29.5 (p50 of 40 interleaved runs at load average 217), and sessions resumed before and after `compact`. Most of the user's own 24.7 GB journal is not repeated records (307 MB repeat across states), but 18.7 GB of heads of two version 1 subagent journals written on 2026-10-19, which `compact` would blank.
- `SessionStore::list` reads the summaries the store remembers instead of reading every session. `StateStore::list_summaries(limit)` (default `None`, which keeps the old path) lists states newest first, each with `ListedSummary::Listed(StateSummary)`, `Unlisted` or `Unknown` for its current revision, and `remember_summaries(Vec<KnownSummary>)` (default no-op) keeps summaries computed at a revision; `SharedStore` and `CachedStore` forward both. `list` builds a summary only for `Unknown` states, as it built every summary before (peek, hydrate, first prompt), and hands those back to the store. `JsonlStore` keeps them in `sessions/.summaries.json`, each valid only while its session file still has the identity and length it had at that revision, so any append, from any process, makes that session's summary unknown again (until 2026-11-03 they were a block of the shared journal's index sidecar). Listing used to make three store calls per session through the JSONL worker (3,015 for 1,000 sessions, 73% of the CPU parking and waking threads). On 2026-10-29 over 1,000 one-turn sessions `nc sessions` took 17 ms instead of 125 (the same output; fx's `fx sessions --json` 12.5 ms) and `nc exec -c` 39 ms instead of 146 (fx 48), once a listing had remembered the summaries; the listing that remembers them costs what a listing cost before.
- The TUI shows the first streamed token of a reply at once instead of up to a frame later. `RenderScheduler` paces streamed text at `DISPLAY_FRAME` (60 Hz) from the last frame that showed streamed text, where it used to pace from the last frame of any kind, and a run start, an accepted input and a terminal run event redraw immediately rather than as streamed text, so a spinner or run-start frame just before the first delta no longer holds it back; later deltas still render at most 60 times a second. On 2026-10-29 (output/bench-vs `sweep.mjs --mode=tui`, a PTY of 120x40 replayed through a VT emulator, 3 runs per rate) the first token reached the screen 0.2 to 0.5 ms after the fixture wrote it at 10, 100, 1,000 and 10,000 tok/s, against 12 to 17 ms before (pi 6 ms, fx 20 to 3,900 ms because it prints a paragraph at a time), with per-token lag and CPU unchanged.
- The TUI no longer redraws in a loop after a status flash. A full frame set its next redraw at the earlier of the next label tick and the flashed status's expiry (`App::status`, shown for `STATUS_VISIBLE`, 4 s), and `status` stayed set after it expired, so once anything had flashed (a queued follow-up, `/copy`), every frame while `animating()` (a turn running, a subagent shown active, a pane busy) or `panel_refresh` asked for a redraw in the past and the event loop drew again at once; the frame then found nothing to write and skipped. The loop now clears an expired status before it schedules the next frame. In a 120x40 tmux pane against a local Responses mock (`output/hand-wiring/tui-mock.mjs`, 8 paragraphs and two code blocks a reply, 150 turns 1.2 s apart, 2026-11-04) the TUI drew 290,365 frames in about 3 minutes (1,600 a second, 1,991 of them written) and used 12.5 s of CPU for the 150 turns, 0.59 s a turn once 150 turns were on screen and 0.96 s of every 20 s idle; it now uses 1.18 s for the 150 turns, 18 ms a turn at 150 and none idle. The user's four long-lived `nc` sessions on that machine each held a core at 84 to 91% this way, their main threads at 8 to 9.5 hours of user CPU after one to one and a half days.
- `nanocode exec` and `nanocode sessions` run on a Tokio runtime with one worker thread, and their work runs as a task on that worker instead of in `block_on` on the main thread. With the default multi-thread runtime every streamed event woke a sibling worker or the parked main thread: in a samply profile of `nc exec` streaming 50,000 tokens at 10,000 tok/s, 92.6% of the process's CPU was kernel time parking and waking threads (the I/O driver's kevent 42%, worker condvars 25%, the main thread's `block_on` 18%), against under 8% decoding and printing, at 2.6 to 3.4 context switches per token. On 2026-10-29 (output/bench-vs `ab-exec.mjs`: a local fixture streaming for 5 s, medians of 3 interleaved runs, load average 14 to 29) the process used 146 ms of CPU instead of 235 at 1,000 tok/s and 965 ms instead of 1,925 at 10,000 tok/s, with a third of the context switches, and `nc sessions` over 1,000 sessions took 100 ms instead of 128. Two or four workers measured like the default in the same harness over the Rust SDK, so the gain needs one worker; nothing in the workspace uses `block_in_place`. The TUI keeps the multi-thread runtime.
- A ChatGPT-extension request (nanocode-chatgpt-extension, `Extension::rpc`) left unanswered for 15 s fails with which request it was and what to check, not std's `timed out waiting on channel`: "The ChatGPT extension did not answer <owner>'s <method> request within 15 seconds; whether it took effect is unknown", then to open the extension in that browser, check the user is signed in to ChatGPT there and that it may use the browser and try again, and "; or to <text>" when the host set `Bridge::alternative(text)`. An extension that disconnects first says so and that the outcome is unknown, not `channel is empty and sending half is closed`. Ported from tempoxyz/pyramid#659, whose Hand offers the Pyramid extension as the alternative.
- Bash commands start in a shell that is already running (`integrations/process/src/warm.rs`, macOS and Linux): Claude's `Bash` (`run_bash`) and an `exec_command` that runs bash without a PTY (the default shell on most Linux machines, or a model-provided bash) take an idle `bash -c LOADER` (or `-lc`) started earlier with the same shell, login flag, workdir and environment, write the command to its stdin and use its pipes, where each used to start a new bash. The loader closes every inherited descriptor above 2, prints a NUL once it is ready, reads the session variables (`SessionEnvironment::VARIABLES`, exported before the command, so sessions share processes) and then the command, each up to a NUL, takes `/dev/null` as stdin, resets `SECONDS`, sets `$_` to `$BASH` and `eval`s the command from `BASH_EXECUTION_STRING`, so `$0`, `$$`, the environment, the cwd, exit codes, traps, timeouts and process-group kills are a fresh `bash -c`'s; what differs is that syntax errors read `bash: eval: line N:` (as in Claude Code, which also `eval`s commands) and exit 1 under bash 3.2, and `$_` names the shell rather than an inherited `_`. Each process runs one command. A command over 4 KiB (bash reads a pipe a byte at a time, 0.4 µs per byte on an M1 Max, which passes a bash start's 4-8 ms near 12 KB), or with a NUL, starts its own shell, as do commands for which no idle process is left; an idle process whose startup files (for login shells `/etc/profile`, `~/.bash_profile`, `~/.profile`, `~/.bashrc` and the like, plus `BASH_ENV`; `/etc/paths` on macOS) or workdir changed, or that died, is discarded. Each pool (one per shell, login flag, workdir and environment) keeps as many idle processes as it last needed: one after its first command, one more whenever it hands out a process that had not finished starting, one fewer when it hands out a ready one while another ready one waits, refilled one at a time on a blocking thread after each take; all pools together keep at most one idle process per CPU and evict the least recently used pool's. An idle process exits when its stdin closes, so they go with the process that started them. On 2026-10-27 in nanocode-ts's `bench/hands.mjs` (WASM brain, native hand, medians of 10 turns, load average about 20 from other builds on an M1 Max with nix-darwin's bash) a cell with one `Bash` call took 6.0 ms instead of 12.4 and 50 sequential calls 137 ms instead of 333, and 50 at once took 76-100 ms against 70-91 (the same within that load: the build with the pool switched off measured 83-95); in a Debian bullseye arm64 container (bash 5.1, OrbStack) one call took 5.3 ms instead of 7.1, 50 sequential `Bash` calls 34 ms instead of 46, 50 sequential `exec_command`s (bash -lc) 39 ms instead of 94 and 50 at once the same (23 ms). Starting it exposed a stall that older builds have too: on macOS, std creates a child's pipes with `pipe` and then sets close-on-exec, so a child spawned at that moment on another thread inherits the write end of another command's output pipe, and a child that lives long (a warm bash, or a zsh shell server below) keeps that command's output open: its `Bash` or `exec_command` call never saw EOF and its cell came back only at its yield, after 11 s, in about one of 20 turns of 100 mixed `Bash` and `exec_command` calls at once, with the build before this too. The warm loader closes every inherited descriptor above 2, and the zsh shell server's bootstrap now first closes every inherited pipe and socket above 2 (zsh's own descriptors, 10 and 11, are a terminal and `/dev/null`); 140 turns of those bursts after the change showed no stall.
- The ChatGPT extension's connector (nanocode-chatgpt-extension) survives what used to strand a thread's browser. (1) It reads messages from the browser up to 64 MiB, where it used to drop the whole connection on any message over 8 MiB ("disconnected before answering ... executeCdp"): every step ends with `Accessibility.getFullAXTree`, which was 17.7 MB on html.spec.whatwg.org's parsing page on 2026-10-28. A larger answer to one of its own requests fails that request with its size, and a larger message for OpenAI's host is dropped; the connection stays. (2) A step naming a tab the connection does not know asks the extension with `getTabs` and takes the tab back when the extension still holds it for the session: the extension keeps a session's tab leases across reconnects, but each connection starts a new host process whose `Tabs` begin empty, so every tab a turn had opened was refused ("tab is not one of this session's <owner> tabs; open one first"). (3) The extension runs a session's `createTab`, `attach`, `detach` and turn changes one at a time in one queue per session ID, and some of them await Chrome without a timeout (`chrome.debugger.attach`, the viewport command `attach` sends), so one that never settles left every later `createTab` unanswered for the rest of the session (seen as repeated 15 s `createTab` timeouts, tempoxyz/pyramid, 2026-10-28). A step whose request goes unanswered (the `Unanswered` error) now moves that thread to a fresh extension session (`<namespace>:<session>:<n>`), drops its known tabs and says to open a new tab; the move lasts for the connection, so a new connection tries the original session first. The crate's unit tests run against `Extension::fake`, which answers each request from a closure, or leaves it unanswered, within 200 ms.
- `ShellSessions` runs an `exec_command` of the default shell, when that is zsh and the call asks for neither a PTY nor a `shell`, in a forked copy of a long-lived zsh instead of a shell started for it (`integrations/process/src/server.rs`, macOS and Linux). A dispatcher per login flag starts up to one server per CPU (`zsh -lc` or `-c` with the first command's environment and workdir; the startup files are read once) and hands each server at most 2 unanswered starts (`DEPTH`), so a server's status queries never wait behind a backlog of forks and a burst spreads over servers as they start: 2,048 commands at once took 0.73 to 0.96 s in a release build instead of 5.6 to 8.9 s with a queue per server. The server forks one subshell per command as a background job, so each command is its own process group; the subshell opens its own stdout and stderr (FIFOs in a `0700` `$TMPDIR/nanocode-shell-<pid>-<random>` directory, reused once both reached EOF), takes `/dev/null` as stdin, `cd`s to the workdir (to `$PWD` when that is the same directory, as a new shell keeps it, else `-P`), exports the command's environment where it differs from the server's (every secret, always; a read-only or non-identifier name makes the command start its own shell; an environment equal to the server's, in the same order, is not compared name by name), unsets what the command lacks, sets `OLDPWD`, reseeds `RANDOM` and resets `SECONDS`, and sources the command from a file named `zsh` with that prelude on its first line, so `$0`, `$LINENO`, `return` and messages such as `zsh:3: command not found: x` read as in `zsh -c`. The server runs with `bg_nice` off, so commands are not niced (zsh's default ran every served command at nice 5), and `notify` on, so zsh drops each job once it reaped it rather than at a prompt or a `wait` (with it off, a non-interactive zsh that started 1,100 background jobs and waited for none aborted at the 998th); the prelude restores both for the command. The caller gets the command once the server names its PID (a command the server never forked starts its own shell), and the Hand learns its exit from kqueue `NOTE_EXIT` (a pidfd on Linux). The status comes from the command itself: the subshell's EXIT trap prints it on a close-on-exec copy of the server's output (zsh/system's `sysopen`, unloaded again unless a startup file loaded it, so programs the command runs see no extra descriptor), which the Hand reads before it takes an exit; the server's `wait` gives it only for a command that ended without the trap (exec, a signal, a fatal shell error, or an EXIT trap of its own), and the PIDs that reported ride along with the next request in a `c` line so zsh forgets their statuses. That is so a server's death costs no command: zsh exits whenever a fork of a non-interactive shell fails (`fatal:` in exec.c, `exit(1)` after "fork failed", as at the user's process limit, and also for "pipe failed" and a full job table). The dispatcher hears of it at once, the server's last error (its `log`) is logged, and the commands it was running go on and report their status; only one that ends without the trap fails with "failed to wait for shell command: the shell server stopped". With `ulimit -u` 390 above the user's process count, 4,000 calls of `sleep 0.2; echo $RANDOM` 200 at a time through tempoxyz/pyramid's `exec_bench` lost the status of 930 to 3,800 calls before and 2 to 3 after (commands whose own fork failed, so their shell exited without its trap); the previous build also panicked re-awaiting the server watcher's oneshot (`called after complete`, 27 times in those runs), which left the dispatcher a dead server and hung one run. The Hand then stops the command's process group as before, with a single `killpg` when the group is already empty (SIGKILL used to follow a SIGSTOP that found no process); Ctrl-C (`write_stdin` of `\u{3}`), timeouts, cancellation and `terminate_all` signal that group, and `terminate_all` also stops the servers. A command starts its own shell as before when no server is ready yet (a server starts in the background), when its text has `$$`, `${$}`, `$!` or `${!}` (a subshell keeps the server's values), when its workdir is not a directory, when a server reached zsh's job table limit (997 jobs ran at once on zsh 5.9, the 998th aborted the shell), and while startup files print anything (that output then reaches every command, as before); a server that failed to start does not stop later ones, while one whose startup files printed or ended it does until they change. A server is replaced when one of the startup files it read changes (dev, inode, size, mtime and ctime of `/etc/zshenv`, `/etc/zprofile`, `/etc/zlogin`, `.zshenv`, `.zprofile` and `.zlogin` in `$ZDOTDIR` or `~`, and on macOS `/etc/paths` and `/etc/paths.d`; files those source are not tracked) or it dies. Each batch of commands checks those files, on macOS only after a kqueue watch on each file and every directory its path and link targets resolve through saw a change (the stats were 5 to 7% of the Hand's busy samples at 512 calls at once). kqueues are polled with kevent64 and `KEVENT_FLAG_IMMEDIATE`: on an empty queue that takes 0.23 µs, a zero timeout 11.5 µs with either call, and the exit queue is read again only once tokio sees it readable. Differences a command can see: `$ZSH_SUBSHELL` is 1, `$zsh_eval_context` is `cmdarg file`, `getppid()` is the server, startup files run once per server rather than once per command, and `trap` lists the EXIT trap that reports the status. macOS kqueue never reports a FIFO's last writer closing, so once a command exited its output drains read every 10 ms (`REAP_POLL_INTERVAL`) until EOF. Until a command exits, the owner holds a write end of each of its slot's FIFOs (`Busy::keeper`, opened before the slot is sent), so a drain's read of 0 bytes is always the end, and before each command the slot's readers are registered with tokio again, because tokio keeps a pipe readable for good once its writers all closed: on Linux a reused FIFO reads 0 bytes and stays readable before zsh opens it, and the drains used to spin on it, which with `nanocode exec`'s single worker kept the runtime's I/O driver from ever reporting the exit, and each read now spends tokio's coop budget so a pipe that stays ready cannot starve it again (checked on 2026-11-01 on Linux 6.8 with a harness of 13 commands on 1 worker, many workers and a current-thread runtime: the second command hung before, all 13 pass now in 0.02 s of user CPU, and 6,000 commands take 0.85 to 1.6 ms p50 as before). The command's file keeps its length and is padded with spaces after the prelude, because truncating it on every command was 5% of the Hand's CPU under load; a command with secrets has its file overwritten with spaces once it exits. Commands that still start their own shell now spawn it on the blocking pool: with 2,048 commands at once about 1,000 of them did, and while `posix_spawn` blocked the async workers 3 to 23 commands per run lost their output, their drains woken on a blocked worker past the 2 s drain grace. `prune` returns before sorting when every session is in a call (all 512 were, on every insert). Sessions are kept in `MAX_SESSIONS` shards (Atom::update copies the whole map, and copying and dropping one map of every session were 5.4% of the Hand's busy samples at 512 calls at once), chunk IDs and `RANDOM` seeds come from splitmix64 seeded once by the OS (a getentropy call each was 1.5 to 2%), sensitive names are matched without allocating, and an output drain keeps its buffer on the heap so spawning it copies no 8 KiB future. On 2026-10-27 on an M1 Max (fish login shell, so `/bin/zsh`; load average 4 to 12; three runs of each, alternating with the previous build), tempoxyz/pyramid's `exec_bench` (`echo` through its Hand's socket) took 1.24 to 1.29 ms p50 one at a time instead of 1.35 to 1.39, ran 3,830 to 3,930 calls/s 64 at a time instead of 3,420 to 3,590 (p95 26 to 27 ms instead of 40 to 43) and 3,800 to 3,950 calls/s 512 at a time instead of 3,430 to 3,480 (p50 126 to 128 ms instead of 134 to 136, p95 142 to 162 ms instead of 229 to 248), and a call cost the Hand and client 0.31 to 0.32, 0.22 and 0.215 ms of CPU instead of 0.37 to 0.39, 0.35 to 0.37 and 0.34 (one, 64 and 512 at a time), with the commands' 1.0 to 1.5 ms unchanged; before the shell server, 512 at a time ran 578 to 955 calls/s at 4.17 to 5.29 ms of command CPU a call. Most of what remains is the fork: a forked zsh subshell that exits and is waited for cost about 0.65 ms of CPU there, 0.5 ms of it in the kernel.
- On macOS a served command's output drain also reads its FIFO when kqueue reports nothing (`Reading` in `server.rs`: `Watching` the pipe's readiness, `Unreported` 50 ms after the last wakeup, or 10 ms when the last unreported read found output, until a read finds the FIFO empty, and `Exited` once the command exited, reading every 10 ms until EOF): macOS raises no EVFILT_READ, edge or level triggered, when one blocking `write()` is larger than the FIFO's free 8 KiB, so `cat` of a 10 KiB file or Python flushing a large print blocked in `write()` until the call yielded, with no output. Linux only waits for readiness, as epoll reports every write. Ported from tempoxyz/pyramid#850, whose Hand vendored nanocode-process for it. On 2026-11-01 output/hand-wiring's `execcheck` (one worker) ran `cat` of a 10 KiB file and `python3 -c 'print("x"*20000)'` twice with no exit code and no output after each call's 10 s yield (40 s for the run), and now with exit 0 and all 10,240 and 20,001 bytes, the run with two more commands taking 0.28 s.
- A ChatGPT-extension `Connection` stays owned (`owned`, and so `live`) while its registration record exists, whatever the browser's `com.openai.codexextension.json` names meanwhile: an app that rewrites that manifest (OpenAI's installer, the ChatGPT app, a second host; a running host restores it within seconds) used to end the relay for good while the connector kept forwarding to OpenAI's host, so the host had no browser until the extension reconnected. Releasing a registration, `uninstall_all` and a host's own disable remove the record and still revoke at once. The connection's handshake is one `Handshake` (`Controls`, `Refused` for an official extension that did not answer `getInfo` with its ID, `Forwards` for any other origin) instead of two booleans whose fourth combination meant nothing. Ported from tempoxyz/pyramid#937, whose Hand vendored the crate for it.
- On macOS, shell commands of a user whose login shell runs none (fish, nushell) run in `/bin/zsh`, and only without it in the first `zsh` on `PATH` (`nanocode_home::shell::default_user_shell`); a login shell of zsh or bash is still used as it is, and a model-provided `shell` still resolves on `PATH`. A Nix or Homebrew zsh loads its libraries from outside the dyld shared cache, so each command started later and cost more CPU. On 2026-10-26, on an M1 Max with fish as the login shell, nix-darwin's zsh first on `PATH` and other builds loading the machine (load average 33 to 37), tempoxyz/pyramid's `exec_bench` (an `exec_command` of `echo 1` through its Hand's socket) took 6.6 to 8.3 ms p50 one at a time before and 4.8 to 7.5 ms after, the commands' CPU fell from 4.7 to 5.7 ms a call to 2.9 to 4.1 ms, and 512 calls at once ran at 1,261 to 1,290 calls/s instead of 865 to 914 (p50 358 to 378 ms instead of 504 to 592, p95 580 to 649 ms instead of 776 to 932): with 512 shells starting at once a Hand is bound by their CPU.
- `Bridge::connect` (nanocode-chatgpt-extension) records each time the browser starts the native-host launcher next to its registration (`chatgpt-<key>.started`): empty while the connection runs, or why it stopped, including an official extension that did not answer the `getInfo` handshake. `Bridge::launches` returns each registration's manifest, when it was written and the last start since then, so a host can tell a browser that never started the launcher (the extension is off, the browser was not restarted, or it loads hosts from another folder) from one whose launcher failed; releasing a registration removes the marker. Pyramid's Hand reports both to its agent when no browser connects (tempoxyz/pyramid#464).
- Codex agents on WASM hosts get `view_image` and `image_gen__imagegen` like native ones. `codex_tools::host_tools` and `host_file_tools` register `view_image` over the host's `FileHost` (relative paths resolve against the workspace, same definition, output and errors as the native handler, no wire limit). `codex_tools::image_generation_tool(api_base_url, transport, files, workspace)` is `image_gen__imagegen` for hosts, built like `web_search_tool`: it POSTs the native handler's `gpt-image-2` requests to `{api_base_url}/images/generations` or `/images/edits` through `HostTransport::http` (no bearer token; the host adds credentials and routing), reads `referenced_image_paths` with `FileHost::read_bytes` as `data:` URLs with a sniffed MIME type and no resizing, and saves results with `FileHost::write_bytes` at the native `generated_images/<session>/<call>.png` path; the host transport exposes no response headers, so its results carry no `imagegen_request_id`. Native and host share `image_generation/mod.rs` (arguments, requests, history images, response handling, artifact paths). `FileHost::read_bytes` and `write_bytes` have default impls over `read`/`write` (UTF-8 only, `write_bytes` errors on other bytes), so hosts that store binary files must override both.
- On the embedded (WASM) runtime the `exec` description defers every `mcp__*` nested tool by default (left out of its sections and named on one "Deferred nested tools" line; full description in `ALL_TOOLS`, schema in `toolSchema(name)`) and briefs `send_agent_message`, `list_agents`, `close_agent` and `interrupt_agent` unless the host passes its own `brief_code_tools`, and its compact preamble says cells run in QuickJS, not Node, and that `WebAssembly` exports are async. Georgios asked on 2026-10-25 that nanocode-ts and its demos send only this SDK's native prompts and tool text, so these moved here from host-supplied lists and instructions; with every host instruction removed, a local GLM-5.3 sweep through the Cloudflare demo Worker passed 12 of 12.
- The embedded (WASM) runtime's `exec` description uses a compact preamble (`EXEC_DESCRIPTION_COMPACT`) and compact shared MCP types (`ImageContent`, `ContentBlock`, `CallToolResult` only), keeping every rule: `tools` naming and argument shapes, raw source rather than JSON or fences, the `// @exec:` pragma, awaiting promises, and each global helper. Native runtimes keep the full text (`exec_spec(.., compact: false)`). On 2026-10-25 this took 773 o200k tokens off every request of gakonst/nanocode-ts's Cloudflare demo (Claude-shaped 7,576 to 6,803, Codex 8,304 to 7,531), and a local GLM-5.3 sweep of 12 prompts, 5 with subagents, passed 12 of 12 with no failed tool calls.
- `ToolsBuilder::brief_code_tools(names)` (and `Tools::brief_code_tools`) leaves the listed nested tools out of the `exec` description's full sections on the embedded (WASM) runtime and lists each as one line under "Additional runtime-provided nested tools": its first sentence and argument names, with `ALL_TOOLS` (full description) and `toolSchema(name)` (input schema) for the rest. The tools stay callable. gakonst/nanocode-ts briefs `send_agent_message`, `list_agents`, `close_agent` and `interrupt_agent` on Cloudflare (-432 o200k tokens per request, 2026-10-25). Do not brief `spawn_agent`: in a local GLM-5.3 sweep on 2026-10-25, with its TypeScript declaration gone the model guessed `output_contract` as a string or as JSON Schema `properties` and needed 8 to 15 model calls and up to 12 failed tool calls per subagent turn, against 3 to 8 calls with it.
- In Code Mode cells, `tools.mcp__<server>__<tool>` for a tool that is not in the catalog calls `tools.mcp__<server>__call_tool({ name: <tool>, arguments: input })` when that server exposes one (`code-tools.mjs`), instead of rejecting with `TOOL_NOT_AVAILABLE`. Hosts that defer MCP tools behind `call_tool`, such as gakonst/nanocode-ts's `deferMcp`, name every deferred tool in its description, and models call those names directly: on 2026-10-24 Luna created a gateway sandbox and called `tools.mcp__gateway__compute_terminate`, which failed and left the sandbox running out its paid lease. With this the host can list only `describe_tools` and `call_tool` per server.
- `ChatGateway` (custom models such as GLM-5.3 on Workers AI and OpenRouter, over Chat Completions) folds a second output for a call it already answered into that call's `tool` message instead of rejecting the request with `invalid_history`: Codex logs a Code Mode cell's `notify()` texts as extra `custom_tool_call_output` items with the cell's `call_id` after its output, and Chat Completions takes one tool message per call. On the Cloudflare demo on 2026-10-25 every GLM-5.3 turn whose cell called `notify()` failed at its next model request with "tool output has no matching call in full history". Workers AI requests also carry `x-session-affinity` set to the request's `prompt_cache_key`, which Cloudflare uses to route requests to a replica that holds the prefix in its cache: on 2026-10-25, six repeats of a 7,857-token GLM-5.3 prompt through AI Gateway hit the cache 6 of 6 times with the header and 4 of 10 without, and a cached call cost 191 neurons instead of 1,000. A 36-turn sweep of the demo before this cached 65% of GLM-5.3 input against 98-99% for Claude and Codex.
- The Claude runtime sets `code_only` on WASM whenever the session has a Code Mode host (in-host cells or placed hands), as native runtimes always do. Before, WASM Claude agents ran Code Mode catalogs (`exec` and `wait`) without `code_only`, so a durable cursor saved with direct tools, such as a background child journaled on 2026-10-19 by SDK b651367 and resumed by a Code Mode host, kept offering `submit_result` and the other direct tools while no direct handler was registered: every call answered "Tool is not available in the admitted catalog" and the child failed with "subagent turn ended without a valid submit_result call". Now the cursor upgrades to Code Mode on recovery with its notice, and nanocode-cloudflare-durable's measure-faults legacy scenario completes the child (2026-10-25, 1,433 checks on two seeds). Codex already resumed it.
- `InboxHost::live_deltas()` (default false) lets a host take streamed deltas out of the session log. `TurnInbox`'s sink then publishes root `assistant.delta` and `tool.input.delta` events through `InboxHost::publish_live` as `LoggedEvent`s with `seq` 0, each with `offset`, the UTF-16 length of the item's text before it, and does not append them: `assistant.message` and `tool.call` carry the full text and arguments, so the log keeps those. A run that ends, fails, errors, is steered or whose model call or attempt fails first appends each item's unfinished text as one merged delta with its `offset`, so no text is lost. Reasoning summaries and subagent events are logged as before. Each 100 ms coalesce window of a streamed item was a log row: on 2026-10-25, with 20 chunks 50 ms apart per model call, a turn of 3 Code Mode cells and a reply wrote 89.7 rows (Claude) and 95.3 (Codex) through gakonst/nanocode-ts, and 49.7 and 52.7 with live deltas. `InboxHost::live_progress()` (default false, 2026-11-04) does the same for `model.call.started` and `model.attempt.started`: they reach `publish_live` and are not appended, since `model.call.completed`, `model.attempt.failed` and `model.attempt.retrying`, which stay logged, carry what a later reader needs and no transcript is built from them (the subagent runtime reads them from the live agent stream). Through gakonst/nanocode-ts's Node harness (`output/vs-agents/rows-nc.ts`, which sets it) that took two rows off every model call: a text turn wrote 17.1 rows instead of 19.1 and a turn with one cell and one tool 27.3 instead of 31.3.
- `Hand::activity()` returns a cloneable `HandActivity` for a host process that must drain before replacing its own binary (its bookkeeping is O(1) per request: the executor, its only writer, pushes each request's work onto a list readers walk without locks, marks it ended when it settles, and rebuilds the list only once ended entries outnumber live ones; it used to republish a snapshot of every running and retained request on every frame, the hottest code in a 64 x 64-call profile): `busy()` is true while the hand has a cell running or yielded, a callback awaiting its result, a request in flight, or a result the brain has not acknowledged (retained for resume), `idle()` resolves once none is left, and `drain()` makes the hand refuse every new `Execute` and `Call` with the reply the brain reads as never dispatched (`NOT_RECEIVED`, so the brain's call is unsent and can be retried elsewhere) while `Wait`s for cells it already admitted, cancels and turn bookkeeping still run, so yielded cells can finish. A listening hand counts all its connections; a hand whose brain never returns for unacknowledged results stays busy, so hosts bound `idle()` with a timeout. `undrain()` admits new `Execute`s and `Call`s again, so a host whose drain gave up keeps the same hand instead of replacing it, which would end the commands its tools still run. `items()` says what keeps the hand busy, for hosts that decide whether quiet work may be ended (Pact pauses a cloud machine only once nothing but long-silent work is left): one `BusyItem` per piece of work with its `BusyKind`, `id`, a short `label`, the brain `session_id` it belongs to and `silent_for`, the time since its last output or input when `items()` was called (a monotonic clock, so it is unaffected by wall-clock changes). `Cell` is a Code Mode cell running or yielded, by its `exec` call ID, labelled with its first source line after any `// @exec:` pragma, active at its start and at each nested call start or end and `notify()`; `Call` is a direct hand tool call in flight, by call ID, labelled with the tool's name; `Request` is any other brain request in flight (wait, cancel, turn bookkeeping) and `Reply` a result the brain has not acknowledged, both by request ID and silent since they arrived or finished; `Task` is a command a hand tool started that is still running, whether its call still waits on it or it moved to the background: a Claude `Bash` command by its task ID (labelled with its `description`, else its command, with no `session_id` because the hand's Claude tools are shared by its sessions) and a Codex `exec_command` process by its process ID (labelled with its command, in the session whose runtime owns it), active at each chunk of output and, for Codex, each `write_stdin` that sends characters. A task leaves the list when its command exits, however it ended: a command killed from outside leaves `TaskOutput` answering at once with its final status, `TaskStop` saying it is not running and `write_stdin` reporting the exit and then an unknown process. `busy()` and `idle()` cover the same work as the items other than `Task`s (a callback awaiting the brain belongs to the cell or call that made it), so drains that should not wait for background commands keep their meaning; tools report running commands through `Tool::running_tasks()` (default none) and runtimes through `ToolRuntime::running_tasks()` (their workspace tools). `HandActivity` holds the hand's tools weakly, so stopping a hand still ends their commands while a host keeps its activity. Checked on 2026-10-25 with a hand from `nanocode::hand::connect` and a `SocketTransport` brain on loopback: a cell and a yielded cell, a background `Bash` and a tty `exec_command` with their silences reset by output and input, both killed with SIGKILL from outside, and a stopped hand whose commands ended and whose task and process IDs a new hand rejected at once.
- `Hand::run` raises the process's soft open-file limit to its hard limit first (`nanocode_process::raise_open_file_limit()`, public for other hosts; on Unix it asks for the hard limit and, when the kernel refuses, as macOS does above `kern.maxfilesperproc` when the hard limit is unlimited, halves from 2^20 until it is accepted, never below the current limit; off Unix it does nothing). Every command a hand runs holds pipe or PTY descriptors, and macOS starts processes with a soft limit of 256: the `load` binary with 16 sessions of 2 cells each running 16 `exec_command` calls at once (`output/hand-wiring`, `TOOL=exec`) failed 23 to 32 of 32 cells with `Too many open files (os error 24)` before, and 0 of 32 after, as 32 sessions of 32 calls did.
- The hand side of remote Code Mode is one portable core on both targets, `nanocode_code_mode::remote::HandCore` (`remote/hand.rs`): it decodes the brain's frames, runs each request, keeps resumable replies until `Ack` and answers `Resume`, holds brain callbacks while disconnected (`ExecutorOptions::callback_hold`), buffers up to 1,024 detached frames, caches brain tool lists by `local_tools_key` across reconnects, cancels Calls per session, turn and call, refuses `Execute` and `Call` while its `HandActivity` drains, and publishes `HandActivity` items. `HandCore::new(host, options, activity)` returns the core and a driver future the host spawns for the hand's lifetime; a connection is `catalog(max_frame_bytes)` (the first frame to write), `connect(wire, max_frame_bytes)` (its `failed` resolves when the core ends the connection), `receive(connection, frame)` per brain frame and `closed(connection)`, which resets the core unless a request was resumable. A host supplies a `HandHost` (its tool definitions, workspace, whether it can filter tools by `selected_tools`, `open(session, excluded, brain)` returning a `HandSession`, `end_turn` and `spawn`) and the `HandSession` methods (execute, wait, call, cancel, cancel_turn, detach_turn, begin_turn, preempt, settle_preempt, running_tasks and close). `HandBrain` gives a session the brain's tools of an origin (`tools(call_id)`, or `definitions` and `execute` behind a `DynamicToolProvider`) and `hand_calls()` for cross-hand calls. integrations/tool-runtime's `Hand` and `serve_*` run it with `NativeHost` (a `ToolRuntime` per session over the hand's `Tools`, tasks on `tokio::spawn`) and keep only the sockets, and gakonst/nanocode-ts's WASM hand (`src/wasm/hand.rs`) runs the same core over `LocalCells`, so a browser or Node hand resumes across dropped sockets, caches brain tools and cancels calls as `nanocode hand` does, where its own port of the frame loop cancelled everything on a drop. A WASM hand also publishes the providers' workspace tools as `nanocode hand` does (`codex_tools::host_tools` and `claude::host_tools` over its `FileHost` and shell, without `update_plan`): `Tools::registered_tools()` is available on WASM for that, and on 2026-10-27 a Node WASM hand's catalog listed `apply_patch`, `view_image`, `exec_command`, `write_stdin`, `Read`, `Edit`, `Write`, `Bash`, `TaskOutput` and `TaskStop`, and a cell from the `brain` example (Luna) ran `tools.exec_command` on its simulated shell (`Linux`, `/workspace`). `HandActivity` (`HandActivity::new(tools)` is public), `BusyItem`, `BusyKind` and `ExecutorOptions` moved to the core and keep their paths through integrations/tool-runtime. `SocketTransport::accept`, `serve_accept` and listening hands also take the token as the `bearer` WebSocket subprotocol (`Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: bearer, <token>`, answered with `bearer`), as `RunnerPool` already did, because a browser cannot set `Authorization`; nanocode-ts's hands, relay and hand routes use the same convention. A browser also cannot read the HTTP status of a refused upgrade, so when a credential that came as that subprotocol is wrong, `SocketTransport::accept`, listening hands and `RunnerPool` complete the upgrade and close with 1008 `invalid runner credential` instead of answering 401, and a nanocode-ts hand stops on that close; `Authorization` callers still get 401 at the upgrade. On 2026-10-27 a Node WASM hand dialing the `brain` example as a browser does, with a wrong token, closed with that reason at once instead of redialing. Checked on 2026-10-27 (nanocode-ts `output/relay-test` harness, `wrangler dev` relay, scripted Claude model unless noted): `nanocode hand` on the core ran cells for a WASM brain through the relay (turns of 82, 20 and 20 ms against 47 and 22 ms before), kept a 4 s cell across a relay restart (output 5.3 s after the turn started), ran a turn for the production Cloudflare demo and connected to the `brain` example (Rust brain, real Luna); the nanocode-ts WASM hand in Node ran cells for the relay brain (78 and 17 ms), kept a 4 s cell across a relay restart (5.5 s), and ran a cell for the `brain` example with the token as the bearer subprotocol, as a browser sends it.
- A brain tells a hand at once that it took the connection: `RemoteClient::new` (links that do not resume) sends an empty `Ack` as its first frame, as resuming clients already sent an empty `Resume`, and nanocode-ts's hand routes and relay send the same `{"type":"ack","ids":[]}` when they accept a hand. A hand reports itself attached on that first frame (the native hand's `HandEvent::Connected` and nanocode-ts's `attached` status), so a hand refused after the upgrade, as browsers are, is never reported attached, and a `SocketTransport` brain no longer leaves its hand unconnected until the first ping 20 s later. `ExecutionEnvironment::local()` (native) is this machine's date and time zone, as a Codex session detects them by default, for hosts that describe another machine's working directory or shell with it, and `SocketTransport::workspace()` is the directory the hand or runner advertised; the `brain` example now describes that directory to both backends (`ExecutionEnvironment::local().working_directory` and `ClaudeEnvironment::working_directory`) when it runs cells remotely, instead of its own working directory and AGENTS.md. Before, on 2026-10-27, Luna passed the brain's checkout as `workdir` to `exec_command` on a hand in `/tmp` and its output named the brain's directory; afterwards the same prompt ran in `/tmp`.
- A hand describes its machine and what it offers in its catalog: `environment` (`HandEnvironment`: `platform`, `os_version` and `shell`, from `HandHost::environment`; the native hand reports `darwin`, `linux` or `win32`, the OS version and `$SHELL`'s file name, and `ExecutorOptions::environment`, native `Hand::environment(HandEnvironment)`, replaces it for a host that knows better, such as gakonst/nanocode-swift on an iPhone), `capabilities` (`HandCapability`: `name`, `description`, the `tools` it gates and a `permission` of `granted`, `prompt` or `denied`) and `tools_only` for a hand that runs no cells (`ExecutorOptions::tools_only` or `HandHost::runs_cells`, native `Hand::tools_only()`): it refuses Execute, and `select_prompt_hand` never places a session on it. Capabilities are `HandHost::capabilities` plus those of the `HandCapabilities` a host passes as `ExecutorOptions::device` (native `Hand::capabilities(Arc<dyn HandCapabilities>)`), which answers `capabilities()` and `request(name, reason)`. The brain reads live permissions with `RemoteOperation::Capabilities` and asks for one with `RemoteOperation::RequestCapability { name, reason }`, sent only to hands whose catalog lists capabilities, so older hands never receive them, and older brains ignore the new catalog fields (checked on 2026-10-28: a new TS hand against the deployed site, Vercel and machines brains). `RemoteCodeModeHost::described(&catalog)` keeps the description, which `CodeModeHost::describe`, `capabilities`, `request_capability` and `runs_cells` expose; the blanket `impl CodeModeHost for Arc<T>` forwards each explicitly, because a trait default there silently answers for every `Arc`ed host. The `hands` tool adds `about` to its result, each described hand's `HandDescription` (workspace, environment, tool names, capabilities with their live permissions, `tools_only`), and takes `{ request: { hand, capability, reason } }`, which asks that hand's user, showing the reason, and answers `{ hand, capability, permission }`; a capability's tools may still ask on their first call. gakonst/nanocode-ts's `Hand.connect` takes `environment`, `capabilities` and `toolsOnly`, and its `Capabilities.browser()` offers a tab's location, notifications and clipboard; the shape follows tempoxyz/pyramid's `request_hand`/`inspect_hand` and gakonst/nanocode-swift's permission-gated device tools. On 2026-10-28 a `nc exec` brain with a TS hand named phone, offering `location` (gating `where_am_i`, permission prompt), called `hands`, requested the permission with its reason, which the hand logged, then called `where_am_i` and answered Lisbon.
- Code Mode cells reach tools that only a hand offers: a call to a tool missing from the cell's catalog runs on the named hand when its input carries a string `hand` (`code-tools.mjs` dispatches it and `bootstrap.js` invokes undeclared names) instead of rejecting with `TOOL_NOT_AVAILABLE`. So a brain whose cells run on its own tools, such as just-bash in a Worker or the CLI's local machine, also uses a phone's or a browser tab's tools from the same cell. `LocalCells` routes `hand` calls through the `HandCalls` that `CodeModeExecutors::insert` gives it (`CodeModeHost::route_hands`) as the native registry does: `hand` is taken out of the input, a call naming the executor the cells run under stays local, and a `hand` that is not a string fails without dispatching.
