Before, a WASM brain's `LocalCells` passed `hand` to its own tool, or failed with `unsupported nested tool call` for a tool it lacked. `CodeModeExecutors::set_fallback(name)` names the executor that runs a session's cells when neither the session, its root nor the default selects one, and `select_prompt_hand` does not count it as the only attached hand, so a host keeps its own cells beside hands: they run on the hand that sent the prompt or the only other attached hand that runs cells, and otherwise on the fallback, from where they call tools-only hands; gakonst/nanocode-ts installs `LocalCells` that way for hands sessions. On 2026-10-28 one cell of a nanocode-ts brain with `LocalCells` and a tools-only TS phone hand listed the hands, requested `location`, ran `where_am_i({ hand: 'phone' })` on the phone and `Bash` on its own just-bash shell (`Linux`, `/workspace`); the CLI brain in alongside mode did the same with cells on the Mac.
- A replacement executor that has not connected yet makes cells wait for it instead of failing: `RunnerPool::listen` and `keep_connected` with `RemoteMode::Replace` call `CodeModeExecutors::await_replacement(true)` (stopping the listener clears it), and `CodeModeExecutors::ready_placement`, which native `execute_routed` and `CodeModeExecutors`'s own cells use, waits until the default executor connects for a session without a selection of its own or of its root; `replacement()` waits for that executor and `RunnerPool::replacement_hand()` returns its name and `HandDescription`. The CLI's `Host::start` and `Host::open`, which `nc exec` and the TUI both call, wait for that hand and give agents its machine: Claude's `ClaudeEnvironment` and Codex's `ExecutionEnvironment` take the hand's workspace, platform, shell and OS version through each harness's `configure`, so the model's environment section names the hand's machine instead of the brain's, and `host::listener_notice` is the one listener message both print. The TUI opens at once in that case (`RunnerPool::awaiting_replacement`): it shows the listener's token and command and "waiting for a hand to connect", queues the launch prompt and starts the session in the background once the hand connects, and quits while waiting; it used to draw nothing until a session started, so it hid the token a hand needs. A default executor that runs no cells, such as a tools-only browser tab or phone, never places a cell (`State::resolve` skips it), so cells run on the brain and call its tools with `hand`, and the CLI keeps describing the brain's machine for it. Before, `nc exec --runner-host … --runner-mode replace` failed its cells with "replacement runner `incoming replacement runner` is unavailable" until a hand connected, and described the brain's machine. On 2026-10-28, with the brain started 8 s before its hand, `nc exec` ran its cells on a native hand (its environment section: darwin, Darwin 25.3.0, /private/tmp) and on a TS hand dialling browser-style (bash, /workspace, no platform). A real Chrome tab (headless, through Playwright) attached as a tools-only hand with nanocode-ts's `Capabilities.browser()`: with geolocation granted, `nc exec` in replace mode waited for the tab, ran its cells on the Mac, called the tab's `location` and answered Lisbon; with it not granted, the model asked through `hands({ request })` with its reason, Chrome answered denied, and the model reported that without retrying or guessing.
- `nanocode --tab` and `nanocode exec --tab` make a browser tab the session's hand without a terminal on the other side: the CLI draws a 256-bit token (`getrandom`), dials the nanocode.xyz hand relay as the brain (`wss://nanocode.xyz/api/hands?role=brain&wait`, the Cloudflare demo's `HandRelay` behind tempoxyz/nanocode-site) in replace mode, and prints `https://nanocode.xyz/hand#url=wss://nanocode.xyz/api/hands&token=<token>`, which opens tempoxyz/nanocode-site's `/hand` page with that command filled in; clicking Attach makes the tab the hand, and the session starts on it as for any replacement hand. A URL a brain listens on (`--runner-host`) cannot serve this, because Chrome refuses a public page's WebSocket to a loopback address (`net::ERR_BLOCKED_BY_LOCAL_NETWORK_ACCESS_CHECKS`, headless Chrome 156, 2026-10-28). `--tab` conflicts with `--runner-connect` and `--runner-host`. On 2026-10-28 `nanocode exec --tab` and the TUI with `--tab`, each with headless Chrome opening the printed link and clicking Attach, ran `uname -a` and `pwd` in the tab's just-bash 3 to 4 s after the link opened, and the model named its machine a browser tab.
- `Hand::header(name, value)` adds an HTTP header to the WebSocket upgrade request of every dial of a `Hand::connect`ed hand, redials included, next to the bearer token; repeating a name sends each value. A name or value that is not a valid HTTP header, or a name the upgrade or credential owns (`Authorization`, `Host`, `Connection`, `Upgrade`, `Sec-WebSocket-*`), fails `Hand::run` before it dials, as an error and not a panic, and listening hands ignore them. Hosts such as Pact announce their build, protocol generations and capabilities on every dial this way while the socket's first frame stays the catalog. A brain that serves none of the hand's protocols answers the upgrade with 426, which ends the hand with `HandEvent::Rejected { status: 426 }` like a refused credential instead of redialing, so the host updates itself rather than looping.
- `RemoteTransport::recover(context)` lets a durable host finish a remote operation that was in flight when its process restarted, instead of reporting an unknown outcome. A tool call whose durable step began but never completed is admitted as interrupted; Claude and Codex now try to recover it before finalizing it. `exec` and `wait` go to `ToolRuntime::recover_code` and `CodeModeHost::recover`, which answers `Ok(None)` unless a host overrides it, so `LocalCells`, local Code Mode and every other host never run anything. Any other tool goes to the `ToolRecovery` a host sets with `ToolsBuilder::tool_recovery`: `recover(name, context)` returns the call's output or `None`. `RemoteCodeModeHost::recover` (cells) and `RemoteCodeModeHost::recover_call` (a hand tool's `RemoteOperation::Call`, decoded as `call` decodes its reply) build the `RemoteContext` from the call's own `ToolContext`, with the same `session_id`, `turn_id` and `call_id` as the dispatch (for Codex the Responses `call_id` and a turn ID of the form `<session>:<n>`, for Claude the `tool_use` ID and the request ID), and call `RemoteTransport::recover`, whose default is `None`. A transport that keeps a table of dispatched operations, such as Pact's Durable Object beside `RemoteClient::resuming`, returns `Some(future)` resolving to the stored `Reply` result for that key, in the form `ResumedReply` yields (`Ok(None)` when it has none); a host's `ToolRecovery` for its hand tools (Bash, Read, Edit, Write, TaskOutput, TaskStop, `exec_command`, `write_stdin`, `apply_patch`) passes the context to `recover_call`. The result is journaled like any completed call. Recovery never dispatches: it reaches only `recover`, `recover_call` and the host's `ToolRecovery`, never `request`, `call` or a tool's own handler. `None`, `Ok(None)`, an error, a cancelled turn or a reply that does not decode finalize the call as before (`Tool execution interrupted by a host crash; outcome unknown`, status `unknown`, `outcome_unknown` true), and tool hooks do not run for a recovered call. Native Claude runtimes offer only `exec` and `wait` at the top level (`code_only`), so `ToolRecovery` is reached natively by Codex with a direct host and on WASM by both backends. Callbacks into the brain's host tools from a cell that is still running are not restored: `RemoteClient` registers a cell's callbacks only when it dispatches, and the recovered reply's nested calls are published as results, not replayed. Hosts need it because a Durable Object can reset while its hand keeps running and already holds the real result.
- A hand holds the callbacks it awaits from its brain across a reconnect of the same runtime instead of failing them when the socket drops. Each pending `Frame::Callback` keeps its id, origin and reply channel, and when the redialed brain sends `Resume` the hand sends every held callback again as the identical frame, so its id, name, input and context (the brain's authorization of the origin) are unchanged and a brain that already ran it before the drop can deduplicate by callback id; a `CallbackResult` for a held id reaches the waiting cell as usual. Callbacks a cell makes while disconnected are held too. A callback that no `Resume` reaches within `ExecutorOptions::callback_hold` (`Hand::callback_hold(duration)`, 60 s by default) fails with the dropped-connection error as before, and a hand that is not resumable or is reset (a new runtime) fails them at once. `DetachedUpdate` and `DetachedComplete` frames for yielded cells used to be discarded while disconnected; the hand now keeps up to 1,024 of them (the oldest dropped first) and sends them in order after the `Resume`, before any later frame.
- Hosts that serve a hand's socket with `serve_queue` or route a call into a shared provider session get small accessors instead of their own adapters: `RemoteCatalog::publishes(name)` says whether a catalog publishes a tool, `RemoteCatalog::frame_limit(limit)` is the hand's `max_frame_bytes` capped at the host's own limit (the largest frame either side writes), `Settlement::outcome()` is the receipt a `CommandQueue` stores for a settlement in the attachment protocol's outcome form (`{"status":"completed","output":…}` or `{"status":"unavailable","message":…}`; none for `Unknown`), and `ToolContext::with_session_id(id)` is the same call's context under another session ID, as tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand sends every `computer` step to one nanocode-cua session.
- `nanocode_code_mode::remote::MessageLink::new(stream, writer, max_frame_bytes, resumes)` is a `RemoteLink` over a message connection, such as a server's WebSocket, for hosts that accept a hand's socket themselves and serve it with `serve_queue` or a `RemoteClient`: `stream` yields the peer's text messages (an error fails the link with its text; its end is `PEER_CLOSED`) and is read by one caller at a time through `nanocode_sync::Owned`, `writer` is a `MessageWriter<M>` taking each frame as any `M: From<String>`, and `close` writes nothing, so the host closes with a reason of its own. `MessageWriter::new(sink)` returns the writer and the future that owns the sink and writes every message in order (`MessageWriter::spawn(sink)` spawns it natively); clones of the writer share it, `send(message)` resolves once the message is written and flushed, and the sink closes when the last clone is dropped, so the link, its heartbeat and the host's own final Close share one sink without a lock. Both used to take the sink as `Arc<tokio::sync::Mutex<Si>>`, which made every host hold a Mutex and made pings and frames wait for each other's writes. tempoxyz/pyramid's broker maps axum's `Message` into it instead of implementing the link. `nanocode_chatgpt_extension::Connection::relay(socket, idle, title)` serves browser steps for a host process on a Unix socket of newline-delimited JSON (`hello`, then `call` answered by `result`, and `turn_ended`), naming a session's tab group after the step input field `title`, ending turns idle for `idle`, reconnecting while the connection is live and then waiting for the browser as `finish`; Pyramid's Hand used to carry this loop itself. `remote::websocket(stream, writer, max_frame_bytes, resumes, classify, ping, (interval, timeout))` (native) builds one over a WebSocket library's own messages, which `classify` maps to `SocketMessage`: it answers pings with the pong `classify` gives, fails on a read error or another message and ends on close, and returns a heartbeat future that writes `ping()` every `interval` and resolves with `heartbeat lost` after `timeout` without a pong or `socket write failed`, for `serve_queue`'s `failed`; Pyramid's broker passes axum's `Message` through it instead of tracking pongs itself.
- `nanocode_code_mode::remote::read_catalog(first, timeout, max_frame_bytes)` reads a hand's catalog from the first frame of a socket a host accepted itself (`first` resolves to `None` when the connection ended or failed first, `Some(None)` for a frame that is not text, `Some(Some(text))` otherwise) and fails with `CatalogError::NoCatalog` (none within `timeout`: transient, the hand should reconnect), `Outdated` (not text, or JSON without `definitions`, as `RunnerPool` tells protocols apart) or `Invalid` (a catalog that does not decode). `admit_runtime(previous, connected_before, live, advertised)` is the pure decision whether the runtime a catalog advertises takes the hand: `Fence` for a retired runtime (another runtime took the hand after it), even while its successor's socket is open; `Wait` while another runtime's socket still counts as open, since a host can't tell a live competitor from a dead or sleeping process's socket, so the new runtime reconnects rather than stopping for good; `Retry` for the same runtime while its previous socket on this host is still open; else `Take { replaces }`, where `replaces` retires the previous runtime and forgets its undelivered operations. tempoxyz/pyramid's broker keeps its storage, logging and close codes around both.
- `RemoteContext::new(session_id, call_id, turn_id)` is the context of a call a host queued without a model session, such as a row of tempoxyz/pyramid's `hand_commands` served with `serve_queue`: no model, history, instruction revision or host context, and an output token budget of JavaScript's largest safe integer, the attachment protocol's "no budget beyond the tool's own".
- Streamable HTTP MCP runs on both targets. `McpServer::max_response_bytes(bytes)` bounds what one response may make the client read: a JSON body, an error body, each SSE event and the whole of a request's SSE response stream (the standalone stream only per event, since its total grows for as long as it is open); past it the call fails with a transport error whose effect is unknown, as tempoxyz/pyramid's own client failed past 4 MiB. Without it nothing is bounded but SSE events at 16 MiB, as before. `McpServer::standalone_stream(false)` opens no standalone GET stream, which a client making one call per session, as Pyramid's integrations do, never reads. `McpServer::reuse_connections(true)` keeps idle connections in the client's pool: the client otherwise opens a connection for each request (initialize, initialized, tools/list, the call and the session's DELETE), so 20 one-call sessions against a mock server opened 101 connections without it and 21 with it, each one a TCP and TLS handshake against a real server; it stays off by default, since a request on a connection the server closed while it idled may be one whose outcome is unknown. Both reach the transport as `streamable_http::HttpOptions` (`spawn_with_options`, `spawn_with_sender_options`; `spawn` and `spawn_with_sender` keep the defaults). Checked on 2026-10-26 against a mock server: with a 1 MiB cap a 100 KB result passed and a 5 MB one failed as unknown, and without the stream the server saw no GET. `streamable_http::spawn_with_sender(url, sender, headers, auth, outgoing, inbound)` drives the same protocol code as the native reqwest `spawn` (session and protocol-version headers, JSON and SSE replies, SSE resumption, 404 re-initialization, bearer tokens and the `BearerSource` 401 retry) over any `nanocode_http::layer::Sender`, with `nanocode_platform::{spawn_local, sleep, timeout}` for its background work on WASM. On WASM `nanocode::tools::mcp` is `nanocode_mcp_tools` for HTTP servers only: `McpBuilder::http_sender` supplies the sender, and stdio servers, OAuth, reloads and the deferred tool-search provider stay native. `PreparedMcpTool` implements `Tool`, so a host awaits `Mcp::prepared_servers` and registers each tool with `ToolsBuilder::tool` on either target, with the catalog's names, schemas and result conversion; gakonst/nanocode-ts does so for its `mcp_servers`.
- MCP transport failures are typed: `nanocode_mcp::Error::Transport` carries a `TransportError` whose `is_unsent()` says the request certainly never reached the server (a connect or request-builder error, a body or parameters that failed to encode, an OAuth or `BearerSource` token that could not be obtained, or a request made after its session closed, such as once a stdio server exited, which `Session` refuses before queueing it), and whose `status()` is the HTTP status of a non-2xx response that failed it: a 4xx without a JSON-RPC error body (401 and 403 challenges included, and 404 when an expired session could not be reopened, so the request was not resent) or any 5xx, whose JSON-RPC error body, if any, stays in the message because a server that failed may have acted. A 4xx JSON-RPC error, such as a 402 payment request, still arrives as `Error::Rpc`, with the reply's status in `RpcError::status`; one sent with a 2xx or over SSE has none. Anything else, such as a stream that broke after a 2xx, has neither and its outcome is unknown, as has `Error::Closed`, which now means the session closed after the request was queued; a request refused by a closed session used to be `Error::Closed` too. `message()` and `Display` keep the earlier text. Hosts that record calls before sending, such as Pyramid, use these to report a call as not done instead of fencing it as unknown.
- `nanocode_mcp_tools::tool_output(result, server, tool)` is the conversion `PreparedMcpTool` uses from a `CallToolResult` to the model's `ToolOutput`, public so hosts that call MCP tools themselves, such as tempoxyz/pyramid's integrations, show results the same way and leave image limits to the runtime's image preparation (unsupported, undecodable or oversized images become placeholders before a model request). A result with image, audio or encrypted content is shown as that content even when it also has `structuredContent`, which stays the structured result; before, `structuredContent` won and the images were dropped. `value_output(result, server, tool)` does the same for a result held as JSON and shows a value that is no `CallToolResult` as JSON.
- `McpCallError::effect` says what is known of a failed MCP tool call's effects, so hosts that record calls before sending them, such as tempoxyz/pyramid, don't each classify the error: `Refused(message)` when the server or the host refused it before anything ran (a JSON-RPC error carried by a 4xx reply or whose code says the request was rejected before it ran: parse error, invalid request, method not found, invalid params or -32042 payment required, with its message; a 4xx other than 401 without one, as `HTTP <status>`; or, as `Refused(None)`, an `OAuthRuntime` refresh that failed before the request), `Unsent` when the request never reached the server (`TransportError::is_unsent`, a bearer source that could not give a token included, since the host that implements it knows why), `Unauthorized` for a 401 the transport could not resolve with a fresh token, and `Unknown` for anything that may have run: any other JSON-RPC error, such as an internal or implementation-defined error a server answers with a 2xx or at the end of an SSE stream after its handler started, a 5xx, a broken stream, a timeout or a payment failure. Every JSON-RPC error used to count as refused.
- `nanocode_mcp_tools::stdio::spawn(command, outgoing, inbound)` runs a newline-delimited JSON-RPC MCP server as a child process for any `nanocode_mcp::Session`: it pipes the child's stdin and stdout, starts it in its own process group with kill-on-drop, returns its stderr for the caller to drain or log, and stops the process group when the session's last handle is dropped. Hosts that drive one MCP stdio server themselves, such as Pyramid's Hand for its computer-use provider, use it with `Session::new` instead of a JSON-RPC loop of their own.
- MCP OAuth serves web backends as well as the native loopback login. `nanocode_mcp_http::oauth::discover_server` discovers an MCP server's authorization (an unauthenticated `initialize` POST first, because its 401 challenge carries the scopes to request and some servers answer a GET with 404 or 405, then the well-known probes) and `discover_issuer` an authorization server without an MCP server, each returning an `OAuthServer` with its metadata, resource indicator (none for an issuer) and scopes. `select_scopes` follows the MCP spec's order: the challenge's scopes, else the protected resource's, else none, so the scope parameter is omitted and the authorization server applies its defaults, plus `offline_access` where offered and at least one scope is requested (alone it would replace the server's default scopes; a host that adds scopes of its own afterwards, such as Pyramid's catalog scopes, calls `AuthorizationMetadata::add_offline_access` again); it used to request the union of the first two. `register_web_client` registers a confidential client (RFC 7591) with the first of `client_secret_basic`, `client_secret_post` and `none` the server supports, and honors the method the server assigns. `WebLogin::start` takes the host's redirect URI, scopes, extra authorization parameters and a state prefix and returns the authorization URL and a serializable `PendingWebLogin` the host keeps under its `state`; `PendingWebLogin::complete` checks the callback's state, client and `iss` (RFC 9207) and exchanges the code. The native `begin_login` runs on the same two steps. An empty resource omits the `resource` parameter from authorization and token requests. `ClientAuthentication::with_method` and `method` map RFC 7591 method names, `revocation_request` and `revoke_token` revoke a token (RFC 7009), and `token_endpoint_request` is public for refreshes a host schedules itself. `parse_token_response` accepts a missing `token_type`, a string `expires_in`, Slack's `authed_user.scope` when `scope` is absent, and a 200 that carries an OAuth `error` (GitHub); `invalid_grant`, `invalid_client`, `unauthorized_client`, GitHub's `bad_refresh_token` and a 401 without an error body are `TokenError::Rejected`, meaning sign in again. `McpOAuthCredentials::client` keeps a confidential client's secret and method, so `OAuthRuntime` refreshes such clients, and the native login stores the client it registered. tempoxyz/pyramid runs its integrations' sign-in and revocation on these, and their refreshes on `OAuthRuntime` over its own `McpOAuthStore` (sealed per-member token sets in Postgres, an advisory lock across backends). `oauth::http_client(user_agent, https_only, timeout)` builds the `HttpClient` a web backend passes these functions, with the rustls provider installed and no redirects, so no token follows one to another host.
- `discover_server_with(http, server_url, policy)` and `discover_issuer_with(http, issuer, policy)` run the same discovery under a host's `DiscoveryPolicy`, whose `Default` is exactly what `discover_server` and `discover_issuer` do. `client_name` names the client of the unauthenticated `initialize` probe (`Nanocode`). `check_url`, a `UrlCheck` (`Arc<dyn Fn(&Url) -> Result<(), String> + Send + Sync>`, none by default), vets the server URL or issuer before any request, skips each authorization server candidate it refuses, and fails discovery when it refuses any of `OAuthServer::endpoints`; it is for hosts whose HTTP client cannot tell which origins it may reach, such as tempoxyz/pyramid, which allows plain HTTP only on a tool's own service-mesh origin. `any_authorization_origin` accepts an authorization endpoint on any origin, where the default refuses one on another origin than the issuer's and the token endpoint's without issuer-bound callbacks (Box, Clay and Close sign in at `app.` or `account.` beside `api.`). `scopes_without_resource_metadata` takes a 401 challenge's `scope` also when the challenge names no resource metadata. `offline_access_alone` requests `offline_access` where the authorization server offers it also when no other scope is selected (Semgrep's resource lists none, and only `offline_access` gets it a refresh token). `legacy_fallback: false` fails discovery that found no authorization server metadata instead of using `AuthorizationMetadata::legacy`. tempoxyz/pyramid's integrations discover through these instead of their own copy.
- Web backends that run MCP OAuth themselves get its generic pieces: `OAuthServer::issuer` (the metadata's issuer without a trailing slash, which keys registered clients and token sets) and `OAuthServer::endpoints` (authorization, token, and registration and revocation when offered, for hosts that vet them, such as HTTPS only), `revoke_sign_in(http, endpoint, client, access_token, refresh_token)`, which revokes the refresh token when there is one, since that ends the grant, else the access token, and `PreparedMcpTool::listed`, the tool as `tools/list` gives it (remote name, description, input schema, readOnlyHint). `McpOAuthStore::authorization_required` is an `async fn` like the trait's other methods. tempoxyz/pyramid's integrations use them instead of their own copies.
- `OAuthRuntime` serves stored sign-ins without rediscovery and APIs other than MCP servers. `McpOAuthCredentials::token_endpoint` names the token endpoint the sign-in found, and refreshes go there without discovering the server's authorization again (and without checking its issuer against current metadata); `resource` sets the resource indicator refreshes send instead of the server URL, empty for none, as for an authorization server found by `discover_issuer`. Refreshes keep both. `OAuthRuntime::stored(server_name, server_url, http, store, credentials)` builds a runtime for such credentials, whose `server_url` only names it to the store, and `transport_from_credentials` uses it for them; `access_token` and the now public `refresh_after_rejection` give a host's own API calls the same refresh as MCP requests. `with_access_token(call, refused)` wraps one such call: it calls with the current token and, when `refused` says the API refused it, refreshes after the rejection and calls once more, calling `refused_after_refresh` when the refreshed token is refused too; tempoxyz/pyramid serves its Google Workspace tools through it. `McpOAuthStore::authorization_required` (a no-op by default) hears when only authorizing again helps: the authorization server rejected the refresh token (`TokenError::Rejected`), none is stored, or a server refused the token just refreshed after its rejection (`refused_after_refresh`, which the Streamable HTTP transport calls); transient failures don't call it, and a refresh that fails transiently before the token expires keeps using it. Refreshes join in process per store as well as server name and URL, since a host with a store per user, such as Pyramid, reuses server names; a store is its instance unless `McpOAuthStore::refresh_key` names it, which hosts that build a store per call (Pyramid) return, such as the member's ID, so their refreshes join too.
- Hosts that keep their own per-user credentials, such as Pyramid with each member's tokens in Postgres, authenticate Streamable HTTP MCP with `HttpAuth::Source` (`McpServer::bearer_source`): the transport asks the `BearerSource` for a token before every request, and calls `rejected(token)` with every token a server refuses with a 401 challenge. The first refusal of a request is retried once with the token `rejected` returns when it differs; a refusal of that retry is reported to `rejected` too and its result ignored, so the source can tell a refreshed token that is still refused (the sign-in is gone) from an expired one. A `token` error fails the request as unsent. `Mcp::prepared_servers` waits for discovery and gives each configured server its callable tools or its error (a server still pending after the discovery timeout, twice the longest startup timeout unless `McpBuilder::discovery_timeout` sets it, fails as timed out), so a host can offer the servers that answered and say which did not. `PreparedMcpTool::call` sends one `tools/call` within the tool timeout and returns the `CallToolResult` or a typed `McpCallError` (`Session` keeps the `nanocode_mcp::Error` with its unsent flag and status, `Timeout` means the outcome is unknown), so hosts that record calls before sending fence only unknown outcomes; `read_only()` is the tool's readOnlyHint. `PreparedMcpTool::listed` gives a tool as its server's `tools/list` did, with its `outputSchema` when the server declared one (read back from the definition, where it types `structuredContent`), so a host that caches or shows listings can type the tool's result.
- `nanocode_process::set_command_environment(variables)` sets variables that every command in the process gets, such as an app's `GH_TOKEN`: `exec_command` sessions, Claude's foreground `Bash` and hands, after the process environment and before a call's own variables, for hosts whose users connect accounts once and expect every session's commands to use them. Values of sensitive names (`is_sensitive_name`, at least 8 bytes) are redacted from those commands' output as the process environment's are, and Claude's `Bash` now redacts its output and what it streams too (`foreground.rs`), byte by byte since a read can end inside a UTF-8 character; a secret split across two streamed reads still streams, but the final output is redacted whole. On 2026-10-28 `echo $GH_TOKEN` printed `[REDACTED]` through `run_bash` and through `ShellSessions::execute` with and without a login shell. Moved from NanoSwift's patched copy of this crate.
- `ShellSessions::with_secrets(environment, secrets)` takes a `SecretEnvironment`, a function the sessions call on every `exec_command` for variables a host keeps outside the process environment and may change between calls, such as Pyramid's per-thread secrets on a hosted Hand. Each command gets them in its environment, never in its command line, after the fixed environment, and every value of at least 4 bytes (`MIN_REDACTED_SECRET_BYTES`; shorter ones such as `1` or `on` would garble all output) is redacted from that session's output as sensitive names' values are, whatever the variable's name. Hosts used to write them to a script, prefix each command with sourcing it and redact the output themselves.
- Shell commands and stdin writes carry the calling agent session (`ExecCommand::from_caller`, `WriteStdin::from_caller`; Codex's `exec_command` and `write_stdin` handlers set it from `ToolContext::session_id`). `ShellSessions::per_caller()` makes a process answer only the session that started it: `write_stdin` from any other session fails without touching the process. Hosts whose one shell runtime serves many sessions, such as Pyramid's Hand serving several threads, use it instead of keeping their own owner map; the CLI leaves it off so subagents may poll a process the root started.
- `ShellSessions::running()` lists the processes its sessions run right now as `RunningProcess` values, by session ID: the calling session from `ExecCommand::from_caller`, the command as written, the resolved workdir, the wall-clock start, `in_call` while an `exec_command` or `write_stdin` call is waiting on it, and `silent_for`, the time since its last output or the last `write_stdin` that sent characters. `RunningProcess` lives in nanocode-process-contracts (re-exported by nanocode-process) and `ShellExecutor::running()` (default none) reaches it through a shell behind the trait, as Codex's `exec_command` does for `HandActivity::items()`. A process is listed from its spawn, through its `exec_command` call and after it as a numeric session, and leaves the list the moment the sessions reap it, even when nobody polls it again, though its session stays until a poll collects the exit; termination and pruning remove it too. Hosts that show what a machine runs, such as the status socket of Pyramid's native Hand, read this instead of recording calls themselves and guessing which child process belongs to which session from the process table.
- `RuntimeBuilder::without_runners()` builds a runtime with no remote runners: its agents get no `hands` tool and no runner routing, runner management is off, and any configured runners or runner servers are dropped. Hosts that run every command on machines of their own through their own tools, such as Pyramid's backend (which also passes `without_cua()` and its own tools), use it so the model sees no runner surface it cannot use; the CLI keeps runners.
- `RuntimeBuilder::model_instructions(model, instructions)` gives one model its own system prompt in place of the runtime's or its harness's `instructions`, for the root and for every agent spawned on that model: aliases resolve to the catalog ID, the runtime resolves a root or cross-harness child through `Settings::model_instructions`, and each harness passes the map to `ClaudeBuilder::model_instructions` and `CodexBuilder::model_instructions`, which a same-harness child spawned with that model takes instead of inheriting its parent's prompt. Instructions a spawn sets itself (`SpawnBuilder::instructions`) still win, and a child that keeps its parent's model keeps its parent's prompt. Hosts tailor prompts to a model, and before, a same-harness child on another model was built from its parent's recipe and always got the root model's prompt.
- Project instruction discovery is shared by local and remote hosts: `PROJECT_INSTRUCTION_FILES` in nanocode-home lists the file names in precedence order (`AGENTS.override.md` hides `AGENTS.md` in the same directory), `instruction_scopes(paths)` turns workspace-relative paths into the directories whose instructions apply, the root `.` first and then each ancestor of each path, root to leaf without repeats, and `instruction_candidates(scope)` names the files to try there in order. `ProjectHome` reads them from the local disk; hosts that read a workspace elsewhere, such as Pyramid reading a Hand's files through its own tool, use the same functions and their own reader.
- `nanocode::http::origin` (`nanocode_http::layer::origin`) tells a host transport which work a model request continues. While an agent's model call is polled, `origin::current()` returns its opaque origin: the agent's host context, or for a root without one the durable operation of its turn, which is the prompt's request ID, a subagent completion turn's `subagent-completion:` ID or a compaction's operation ID, the same value its tool calls carry in `ToolContext::host_context`. Claude root tool calls now fall back to that operation as Codex roots already did, so a child spawned from either names its parent turn. A host's `Sender::send` and Codex `HostTransport` calls read it; it never enters a request's body, headers or logs. `origin::scope` holds it in a thread-local only while the scoped future is polled, so it follows that future across threads, never reaches other tasks, and is restored if a poll panics; Codex `ResponsesAttempt`s capture it when they are built and the Responses service restores it in its connection-owner task, which sends both HTTPS and WebSocket requests. Every model request of a turn carries it, including those after a steer, a child's requests carry the host context it was spawned with, side questions the agent's own host context, and a socket an agent opens before any request (preconnect) carries none, while each WebSocket send carries its request's. `SubagentControl::completion_origin(request_id)` maps a completion turn's request ID to the host context its child was spawned with, reading the child session ID up to the first `:` after the prefix so a suffix after it does not matter, and returns None for other IDs. Hosts that authorize each model request, socket send and tool call by the turn whose work it continues, such as a multi-user service built on nanocode-ts, use these: a host cannot tell turns apart from endpoint, headers and body, and a scope of its own around a turn does not reach the Codex service's owner task.
- A Claude `assistant.message` event names its model call with the one-based `model_call_index` that its `assistant.delta` events and `model.call.started` use; it was one lower, so hosts that attribute a reply to the model call that streamed it matched the wrong call.
- `ComputerConfig::cua_driver()` is a computer-use backend built on trycua's `cua-driver` and nanocode's own browser extension, with nothing from ChatGPT.app or Codex Computer Use, meant to be Sky-compatible so a model that learned Codex's `cua_repl` uses it unchanged: a `cua_repl` MCP provider (`crates/integrations/computer/src/repl/cua_driver.mjs`; `cua_driver()` substitutes `cua_driver_catalog.json` for `__CATALOG__` and writes the source to `$NANOCODE_HOME/cua/cua-driver-<hash>.mjs`, run as `node --expose-internals <file>`, because the source outgrew the 128 KiB a single `--eval` argument may have on Linux) with Sky's tool names and schemas (`js`, `js_add_node_module_dir`, `js_reset` and the hidden `turn_ended`) and Sky's globals: `cua` (`getState` with `errors`, `getApp` by name, bundle ID, path or `{ windowId }`, `listApps`, `listWindows`, `getBrowser`, `createBrowserTab`, `getTab` by ID or `{ url }`, `listBrowsers`, `listTabs`, `rewriteDocumentation`, `initialize`, `cua.driver.call` for raw cua-driver tools, and `cua.computer`, Sky's snake_case native API: `target`, `list_apps`, `launch_app`, `get_app_state` returning `{ app, text, screenshot }` with a `file:` URL, `click`, `drag`, `perform_secondary_action`, `press_key`, `scroll`, `select_text`, `set_value`, `type_text`) and `agent` (`agent.browsers.list/get`, `agent.documentation.get` for `browser-troubleshooting`, `local-web-development`, `file-uploads`, `chrome-file-upload-troubleshooting` and `screenshots`). Native apps go through cua-driver's MCP tools with background delivery (on macOS served by nanocode itself, see below): element index N is `<snapshot_id>:N` of the window's latest `get_window_state`, diffs are its `since:"latest"` reads, the window ID is cached and re-resolved with `list_windows` only when a read fails, and a read after an action waits `CUA_REPL_NATIVE_READ_MS` (60 ms) and reads again once, up to `CUA_REPL_SETTLE_MS` (400 ms) after the action, only when the first read saw no change (it used to sleep 400 ms and list windows before every read); a name several apps share (or a partial name matching several) is an ambiguity error naming their bundle IDs unless exactly one runs; native `selectText` calls cua-driver's `select_text` (element token, text, prefix, suffix, selection type) and only on a driver without that tool clicks the field and moves the caret with arrow keys through `run_steps` from the value in the latest tree (refused where the toolkit takes no background keys, such as GTK 4); on macOS actions pass `post_action_wait: "none"` when the driver's schema offers it (paste keeps the default wait before restoring the clipboard), because the provider ignores action results and its next read settles; the cua-driver session label carries the provider's pid because the daemon refuses a label whose session ended. Browser tabs go through the MV3 extension in `crates/integrations/computer/src/repl/browser/` (ID `hifgmffkihloigeaannkmimoglgminee`, fixed by its manifest `key`; protocol 2, version 0.2.0; `debugger`, `tabs`, `tabGroups`, `nativeMessaging`, `alarms`, `history`, `downloads`): it connects to native host `xyz.nanocode.browser`, whose launcher runs `nanocode cua browser-host`, `nanocode_computer::browser::serve_host`, a pump between Chrome's length-prefixed messages and the provider's `browser_socket` (`$NANOCODE_HOME/browser/browser.sock`, newline-delimited JSON) that waits for the provider and tells the extension `disconnected` when it goes. The provider accepts every browser's connection, sends each `hello` and reads back `{type:"hello",protocol,browser:{name,version},extension}` (the name from `navigator.userAgentData` brands: Chrome, Brave, Edge, Chromium, ...), gives browsers stable IDs "1", "2" by name, resolves `createBrowserTab`/`getBrowser` IDs by ID, name or `"chrome"` (Chrome or the only browser), refuses `"iab"` and `"mcpapps"`, and treats a connection that never answers `hello` as an outdated extension it does not use. Then `call` steps answered by `result`: `open` (`title`; attaches at once), `cdp` (commands run in order, each optionally in an iframe target's `session`, where `{"$from":{"command":N,"pointer":P}}` takes a value from an earlier result, with optional `before` and `after` settles), `state`, `close`, `keep` (`markDeliverable`/`markHandoff`), `logs`, `frames`, `fileChooser`, `userTabs`, `claim`, `history`, `nameSession`, `viewport`, `waitDownload` and `download`. On attach the extension enables Page, lifecycle events, Runtime, Log and Network, auto-attaches iframe targets (out-of-process frames), adds a `Runtime.addBinding` that a MutationObserver calls, and buffers per tab console calls, exceptions and log entries (1,000), the open JavaScript dialog, main-frame loading and navigations, in-flight requests and file choosers; a settle is `{minMs, quietMs, load, networkIdle, timeoutMs}` evaluated against those events in the service worker every 15 ms (no page timers, which background tabs throttle to 1 Hz), and a command that opens a dialog returns instead of blocking until it closes, after which only dialog-safe commands run. Each tab has `playwright` (a locator engine injected into the page by `Runtime.evaluate` resolves role and accessible name, text, label, placeholder, alt, title, test ID, CSS with `:has-text()`/`:text()`/`:visible`, XPath, `text=`/`role=`/`internal:` selectors and `>>` chains through open shadow roots and same-origin frames, with `filter`/`has`/`hasText`/`nth`/`first`/`last`/`and`/`or`; actions wait in the page on mutations until the single match is visible, enabled, editable or hit-testable, scroll it into view and send trusted `Input` events at its center in the same relay step, with Playwright's strict-mode error listing the matches; `domSnapshot()` is a Playwright-style YAML outline with iframe bodies, out-of-process ones read in their own target; `evaluate`, `waitForURL`, `waitForLoadState`, `expectNavigation`, `waitForEvent` for downloads, file choosers and dialogs, `keyboard`, `mouse`), `dev.logs`, `clipboard` (the system clipboard through cua-driver), `content.exportGsuite`, `capabilities` (`pageAssets`), `title()`, `url()`, `screenshot({fullPage, clip})`, `getJsDialog()`, and Sky's indexed `typeText`/`pressKey`/`paste(elementIndex | null, ...)`; browsers have `tabs.new/get/list/selected`, `user.openTabs()`/`claimTab()`, `history()`, `nameSession()` and the `viewport` capability (`Emulation.setDeviceMetricsOverride` on the session's tabs). Clicks send no `mouseMoved`, which background tabs answer about a second late, and screenshots use `captureBeyondViewport` and send another capture every 700 ms until one returns, because a hidden tab renders a frame only when asked again. `turn_ended` closes the tabs the turn opened that are not kept, and the extension detaches kept and claimed tabs; claimed user tabs are never closed; the extension opens tabs with `active:false` in the last focused window and groups each session's tabs under its `sessionName` in a color of their own. `nanocode cua install` asks macOS for the built-in driver's Accessibility (`AXIsProcessTrustedWithOptions` with its prompt) and Screen Recording (`CGRequestScreenCaptureAccess`) and elsewhere installs cua-driver from its pinned release when missing, writes the extension to `$NANOCODE_HOME/browser/extension` and the host manifest into every Chromium user data dir found (or `--browser NAME|DIR`); loading the extension stays one manual Load unpacked in `chrome://extensions` (and Reload after an update), because branded Chrome ignores `--load-extension` (also with `--disable-features=DisableLoadExtensionCommandLineSwitch` on 156) and off-store external extensions on macOS; `nanocode cua status` and `uninstall` report and undo it. `ComputerConfig::detect()` takes `NANOCODE_CUA` (`cua-driver`, the default, or `sky`); the agent host and `nanocode hand --cua` use it. On 2026-10-25 gpt-6.1-sol `nanocode exec` runs on macOS (headless Chrome for Testing with `--load-extension`) searched Wikipedia with `getByRole`/`fill`/`press` and read Alan Turing's birth date, filled and submitted a form with `getByLabel`/`selectOption`/`check` and read its console errors with `dev.logs`, claimed a tab opened outside the session and clicked in it (the tab survived the turn), and computed 12×12 in Calculator, with the front app unchanged; against the previous provider on the same machine a tab opened in 0.19 s instead of 0.64–1.17 s, a browser click took 0.11 s instead of 0.17 s, `goto` 0.17 s instead of 0.32 s, `back` 0.02 s instead of 0.32 s, and a native click followed by `getAXState` 0.8 s instead of 1.15 s; on Linux (Chrome for Testing on Xvfb, cua-driver 0.38) repeated clicks took 0.11 s instead of 1 s. On macOS cua-driver is built into nanocode: `nanocode-cua` links gakonst/cua's `cua-driver-sdk` (rev pinned in the workspace `Cargo.toml`, from the fork's `nanocode` branch that merges trycua/cua PRs 5921-5923, 5971-5973 and 5976-5978) and `nanocode_computer::embedded` runs its runtime in the process (one per process: `CuaDriver::try_create_service_for_host` with the cursor overlay off and the host owning permission prompts) and answers MCP through `cua_driver_core::server::handle_request_with_transport_session`, the handler `cua-driver mcp --direct` uses. `cua_driver()` points `CUA_DRIVER_PATH` at the running executable with `CUA_DRIVER_ARGS=cua driver-mcp`, the CLI's hidden subcommand that serves `embedded::serve_stdio` (another host that uses `cua_driver()` needs that subcommand or sets `CUA_DRIVER_PATH`), so macOS attributes Accessibility and Screen Recording to the app that runs nanocode (a terminal such as Ghostty, or Pyramid.app), never CuaDriver.app, and `nanocode cua status` reports `AXIsProcessTrusted` and `CGPreflightScreenCaptureAccess` for it; `CUA_DRIVER_PATH` still selects an external driver. ScreenCaptureKit's Swift bridge links `libswift_Concurrency`, which a binary deployed below macOS 12 loads through `@rpath` (dyld then fails with "Library not loaded: @rpath/libswift_Concurrency.dylib"), so `.cargo/config.toml` sets `MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET=12.3`; other workspaces that link nanocode-cua on macOS need a deployment target of 12 or newer too, or `-Wl,-rpath,/usr/lib/swift` on every binary and test. Linux and Windows keep spawning upstream's cua-driver, because embedding it on Linux links libX11, libXi and libXtst into every nanocode build. Drop the fork once those PRs land upstream.
- `nanocode_computer::driver` (native) is a host-neutral Rust client for trycua's `cua-driver`, in process on macOS and over MCP stdio elsewhere, for hosts that serve Sky's computer-use actions themselves instead of through the `cua_repl` provider, such as tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand for its `computer` tool. `Driver::embedded()` (macOS) uses the built-in runtime through `embedded::Server` (its own transport session per driver) and `Driver::detect()` is that on macOS and `Install::installed()` elsewhere; `Install::installed()` finds an external driver (CuaDriver.app, `~/.local/bin`, `/usr/local/bin` and `/opt/homebrew/bin`, run `mcp`); `Install::new(path, direct)` takes a host's own override and `command()` is its `tokio::process::Command`. `McpClient::start(command, name, version)` runs any newline-delimited MCP stdio server through `nanocode_mcp_tools::stdio::spawn` and `nanocode_mcp::Session` (initialize, then `initialized`; stderr drained), with `request` and `call_tool` returning raw JSON results or `McpError` (`nanocode_mcp::Error`, re-exported). `Driver::new(install)` with `.client(name, version)` and `.policy(|name, bundle_id| Result<(), String>)` (the host's refused apps and its refusal text; none by default) keeps one driver session (an external driver's process) and each app's window, snapshot and rows across calls: `step(action, input, scope, max_image)` takes Sky's actions and arguments (`list_apps`, `get_app_state` with `full` and `screenshot`, `click` by `element_index` or `x`/`y` with `mouse_button` and `click_count`, `drag`, `type_text`, `press_key`, `set_value`, `select_text` through the driver's `select_text` or else `run_steps`, `scroll` by pages, `perform_secondary_action`) and returns a `Step` with the state text (the full tree on a scope's first read or with `full`, else the `since:"latest"` diff, re-read once up to 400 ms after an action that showed no change) and a screenshot (PNG, or JPEG at `.jpeg(quality)` when `get_window_state` offers `screenshot_format`; `screenshot_mime` names it) shrunk until its base64 fits `max_image` (`screenshot_omitted` when none did); element N is `<snapshot_id>:N`, so indexes are valid only after a read in the same scope, and on macOS actions whose schema offers `post_action_wait` pass `none`, because every step reads afterwards. A `Failure`'s `sent` says whether the driver may have acted; a transport failure drops the process and `reset()` drops it too, as after a host's step timeout. `find_app` resolves a name, bundle ID or app path with Sky's ambiguity error unless exactly one candidate runs, `chord` maps keys such as `cmd+shift+z` or `Page_Down` to cua-driver's key and modifiers, and `ax_action` maps `AXShowMenu`-style names. `Leases::new(idle)` gives an app (keyed by `app_key`, which folds display names, bundle IDs and `.app` paths of common browsers together) to one session: `take(app, session, turn, now)` refuses another session until `end(session, turn)` for the holder's latest turn or `idle` passes since its last step.
- A completed subagent that a coordination message starts again (`send_agent_message` from the root or another agent, any purpose but `delegate`) keeps its accepted result when that turn ends without a new `submit_result`: `harness_turn_started` carries the previous `last_output` into the turn (`follow_up_output`) unless the turn's message delegated a new task (`delegated_turn`, set by `begin_delegation` and cleared by its rollback), and `harness_turn_finished` completes with a newly submitted output, else that one. Before, starting the turn cleared the accepted result and a plain final answer to the follow-up failed the child with "subagent turn ended without a valid submit_result call", although `last_output` still held its valid result; on 2026-10-28 a tempoxyz/pyramid research turn (gpt-6-astra) lost a finished subagent this way after an urgent coordination message. The root's message prompt now also says to call `submit_result` again with the updated result, as a user's message already did, instead of only "Return any response through your required structured result". A delegated task still fails without a new result, and a follow-up's own submission replaces the old one.
- A subagent's queued messages start one turn together, and a follow-up turn of peer messages does not ask the child to submit an unchanged result again. Messages that reach a busy child queue in its harness; when its turn ends, `start_pending` delivers every queued message up to the next `delegate` message (which still starts a turn of its own) as one prompt, urgent ones first and each in arrival order, and records each `Delivered { Started }`, where it started one turn per message. When every message of a turn comes from another agent and none delegates, and the child holds an accepted result that the turn keeps (`harness_turn_started` returns `StartedTurn { revision, keeps_result }`, `keeps_result` being the follow-up output above), the prompt ends with `follow_up_reminder` ("Before finishing, call `submit_result` again only if this changes your result; otherwise your accepted result stands, so finish with a brief final assistant message.") instead of `completion_reminder`; messages from the root or the user still ask for a new result. A follow-up turn holds back the parent's notice, because a running child's status does not wake its parent, so each queued message delayed the result by a whole turn. In the telemetry of 2026-10-28 to 2026-11-03 (1,675 Claude child turns), 981 child turns started within 2 s of the previous one's end, for a queued message: 13% of child turn time (96,738 s and 5,095 model calls), holding the parent's notice back p50 157 s and p90 1,114 s per chain. 941 of them called `submit_result` again, the model call that wrote it averaging 2,763 output tokens and 21 s, and the children read mostly said the message changed nothing. Of the children whose messages could be matched to them (179), 93 turn ends had two or more deferred messages queued, whose 457 follow-up turns would have been 93. Per model call, children were as fast as roots (p50 6.0 s against 6.8 s, time to first event 2.0 s against 2.4 s, about 125 output tokens a second either way, also with 13 or more Claude calls in flight), and the time outside model calls and cells was 0.2% of a child's turn; model calls took 51% of it and cells 49% (their Bash 34%, TaskOutput 9%). Checked on 2026-11-03 with `nc exec --ephemeral -m claude-opus-5-5 -t low` and a child running `sleep 45` while two peers each send it a deferred `finding`: the build before ran the two messages as two turns that each called `submit_result` again (4 model calls), this build as one turn of one model call without it. The CLI's `subagent_prompt_display` shows each message of such a prompt.
- An accepted `submit_result` receipt carries `next`: "Your parent receives this result, not your final message. End the turn now with one short sentence and no tool calls." A child's turn still ends with a final assistant message, as the tool description and assignment ask, but nothing reads it (the parent's notice and `wait_agent` carry only the structured result), and it is on the result's path, since a running child does not wake its parent. In the telemetry of 2026-10-28 to 2026-11-03, 1,444 child turns submitted a result; all but 4 then made exactly one model call and no tool call, which took p50 5.6 s and p90 13.9 s and wrote p50 396 and p90 1,351 output tokens, often a full report. Checked on 2026-11-03 with `nc exec --ephemeral -m claude-opus-5-5 -t low`, three children reading a small module each and submitting a summary and a function list: at `high` the final call wrote 172 to 582 tokens in 3.1 to 6.1 s with the build before and 36 to 55 tokens in 1.5 to 1.6 s with this one, and at `xhigh` 629 to 775 tokens in 6.8 to 7.9 s against 44 to 64 tokens in 1.6 to 1.9 s.
- Do not add code comments, doc comments, or comment headers.
- Do not create or edit README files. The user maintains them; put development guidance in AGENTS.md.
- Do not add project Cargo features or `cfg(feature = ...)` branches. Use crate boundaries and native/WASM target gates. The one feature is `local-cells` on `nanocode-code-mode`, which `nanocode` forwards and enables by default: on WASM, `nanocode-code-mode` builds `nanocode-code-mode-engine`, `CodeModeRuntime` and `LocalCells` only with it, and without it `code_mode` keeps only the specs and embedded types, as it did before the engine ran on WASM. A WASM brain whose cells run on remote hands, such as Pact's Worker, depends on `nanocode` with `default-features = false` and builds without wasi-sdk and without QuickJS in its module. Native builds always include the engine.
- Follow [the crate layout](crates/README.md): active orchestration in `runtime/`, provider code in `backends/`, shared contracts in `core/`, and host adapters in `integrations/`. Keep each backend's API definitions and tools in its own provider-owned crates: `backends/<provider>/client` owns the provider wire protocol, authentication and transport without runtime, tool-host, Code Mode or durability dependencies, so it compiles alongside the runtime; `backends/claude/tools` owns independent Claude tool definitions and handlers without client or runtime dependencies; `backends/<provider>` owns the agent driver, tool assembly and durability and re-exports the client and tool paths.
- Keep runtime crates backend-generic. Code Mode owns only exec/wait contracts and cell orchestration. Keep generic tool selection and execution in runtime/tool-host, macro-free invocation contracts in core/tool-contracts, and MCP, remote transports and machine adapters in integrations/tool-runtime. Each backend owns its tool names, descriptions, schemas, formats and handlers, and supplies them to the host layer.
- `FunctionTool` in runtime/tool-host is the adapter for tools a host declares at run time, such as a JavaScript host's: each has a name, description and JSON Schema parameters, and its calls run through an `Arc<dyn FunctionHandler>` whose async `call` receives the tool name, the decoded JSON arguments and the `ToolContext`. An `Ok` string becomes the tool's text output, as harness-neutral host tools such as durable-harness's `HostTool` return their results, any other `Ok` value the JSON tool output and structured result, and an `Err` text a tool error; parallel calls stay off unless `parallel_tool_calls(true)`. Arguments that do not decode reach `FunctionHandler::invalid_input` with the decode error before any call: its `Some` answer is the tool's output or error, and the default `None` keeps the tool error the runtime reports for undecodable input, so a host can answer malformed arguments in its tools' own error shape. Register it with `ToolsBuilder::tool` on either backend and target: it is offered directly and inside Code Mode, and the backends keep its receipt like any tool's, so an interrupted call reports an unknown outcome instead of running again. gakonst/nanocode-ts registers its hosts' tools this way.
- `TypedHandler<Args, Error>` is `FunctionTool` for a host's stateful tools with typed arguments: `FunctionTool::typed(name, description, handler)` offers the JSON Schema of `Args` (`DeserializeOwned + JsonSchema`, through `schema_for`) as the parameters, and `typed_with_parameters` takes parameters the host builds at run time, such as enums of names it only knows per turn. `call` gets the decoded `Args` and the `ToolContext` and returns a JSON result or the host's `Error`; `failed` turns every `Error`, and arguments that don't decode (`Error: From<serde_json::Error>`, before any call), into a `Failure`: `Failure::Error` is the tool's error text and `Failure::Result` a result such as a structured error the model acts on. Hosts implement it with an `async fn` (`MaybeSend` is `Send` natively and nothing on WASM) and need no `FunctionHandler` adapter of their own; tempoxyz/pyramid's tools use it with fixed failure texts so provider details never reach the model.
- Each backend's web search tool runs on both targets in its upstream shape. `nanocode_backend_claude::web_search_tool(client, max_tokens)` is Claude Code's `WebSearch` as a `FunctionTool` (upstream name, description and schema), answered by a nested Messages request with Anthropic's `web_search_20250305` server tool on the calling agent's model (`ToolContext::model`) through that `ClaudeClient`, as `ClaudeBuilder::nested_web_search` does. `builtin_tools::web_search_tool(api_base_url, model_prefix, transport)` (on both targets; native hosts whose model requests go through a paying or authenticating layer, such as gakonst/nanocode-swift's machine payments, give it a `HostTransport` over that layer, since the native `WebSearchHandler` answers unavailable through gateways) is Codex's `web__run`, sent without the `x-openai-internal-codex-responses-lite` header (`HostConnectRequest::without_responses_lite`), which OpenAI accepts only on requests whose `reasoning.context` is `all_turns`: through the MPP proxy, which forwards headers to OpenAI, every search failed with that 400 before, (the native handler's name, description and `web.run` schema) answered the way Claude's is: a nested non-streaming Responses request to `<api base>/responses` over the `HostTransport`'s `http`, without a bearer token, with OpenAI's `web_search` tool on the calling agent's model (prefixed with `model_prefix`, such as `ResponsesGateway::vercel().model_id_prefix()`), the commands' JSON as `input` and `search_query` domains as `filters.allowed_domains`, returning the answer and up to 8 sources and retrying once after a reconnectable failure. OpenAI's `web_search` tool only works on OpenAI's models, so an agent on another vendor's model (`Model::Glm53`, `Kimi`, `Mimo`, or a custom ID with a vendor prefix other than `openai/`) searches on `Model::Luna`, the cheapest OpenAI model, under the same `model_prefix` (`openai/gpt-6-luna` on Vercel), and `web_search_tool_with_model` lets a host pick that model; OpenAI agents' requests are unchanged. Before, such a session's search asked Vercel for `openai/kimi-k3` or `@cf/zai-org/glm-5.3` and failed with 404 (shown as `web search failed: WebSocket handshake was rejected with HTTP 404`, `HostError`'s text for any rejected request), and on 2026-10-25 a direct `POST https://ai-gateway.vercel.sh/v1/responses` with `web_search` returned `web_search_call` items for `openai/gpt-6-luna` but only reasoning and a message, without searching, for `zai/glm-5.3` and `moonshotai/kimi-k3`. The native `WebSearchHandler` calls OpenAI's `/alpha/search` and answers unavailable through any gateway, a `ChatGateway` (OpenRouter, Workers AI) as well as a Responses gateway, instead of posting to the gateway's base URL. Gateways add their key to Responses requests as to any Codex turn, while on 2026-10-24 Cloudflare AI Gateway forwarded `/alpha/search` without it (OpenAI answered 401), so `web__run` works wherever the session's Codex turns do. Hosts such as gakonst/nanocode-ts add them as shared tools, so Code Mode cells reach them too.
- Hosts without a machine, such as gakonst/nanocode-ts over its simulated bash, give sessions each provider' + "'" + 's own workspace tools on both targets through the portable contracts in `nanocode-process-contracts`: `FileHost` (read a file' + "'" + 's text or None, write it creating parents, remove it) and the existing `ForegroundExecutor` and `ShellExecutor`. `nanocode_backend_claude::host_tools(workspace, files, shell)` registers Claude' + "'" + 's `Bash`, `Read`, `Edit` and `Write` (the read pagination, edit matching and field checks are `tools::text_files`, shared with the native `ClaudeWorkspaceFiles`), plus `TaskOutput` and `TaskStop` for Bash's `run_in_background`: a background command runs as the host executor's foreground future spawned on the runtime (`tokio::spawn` natively, `nanocode_platform::spawn_local` on WASM) with the background `timeout` (600 s by default, 7,200 s at most) and answers its task ID at once, and `TaskStop` drops that future, which cancels the host call, so a host whose `ForegroundExecutor` honors cancellation, such as nanocode-ts's `shell_run`, needs nothing more. Nothing notifies the agent when it ends and its output is not streamed, so the agent polls `TaskOutput` (blocking up to its `timeout`), and the command lives as long as the runtime that spawned it and `builtin_tools::host_tools(workspace, files, shell)` Codex' + "'" + 's `apply_patch` (the same parse, plan and summary as the native handler, with reads, writes and deletes through `FileHost`), `exec_command`, `write_stdin` and `update_plan`, each with its backend' + "'" + 's Code Mode policy. `BashExecutor` futures are `Send` only natively (`BashFuture`), and `effective_token_limit` and `redact_and_limit` moved from integrations/process to the contracts so hosts limit command output as native sessions do; `redact_and_limit` skips empty secrets and replaces longer secrets first, so a host can pass its own secret list as given. `host_file_tools(workspace, files)` in both backends registers only the file tools (Claude's `Read`, `Edit` and `Write`, Codex's `apply_patch`) with the same Code Mode policy, for a Code Mode host whose cells already reach the same shell.
- `submit_result` is installed on every session again, the root's too (`shared_tools`), so a subagent's tools, and with them the cached prefix of its first request, are its root's; `Registry::submit_result` still refuses a root (`NotChild`, "submit_result is only available to subagents", "Return root answers as assistant text."). From 2026-10-25 only children had it, which took about 235 o200k tokens off a root's requests but made every child's tool list differ from its root's, and the tools come first in the prompt: in Pyramid's traces of 2026-11-03 (Tempo, `chat claude-*` spans with `prompt.tools`, `prompt.system` and token counts), none of 46 Claude subagent sessions had a root's `prompt.tools`, though all but one had a root's `prompt.system`, and four subagents of one Claude Opus 5.5 root started within 32 ms each read 0 cached tokens and wrote about 80.7k on their first call (one traced took 3.1 s to its first event and 5.1 s in all), where a sibling started later read 65.8k. A root's cached 235 tokens cost far less than a subagent's prefix written again at 2x input.
- `FunctionTool::content_results(true)` lets a host tool answer with text and images and gives it an MCP tool's shape in Code Mode. An object whose `content` is a list of tool output items (`{"type":"input_text","text"}` and `{"type":"input_image","image_url","detail"}` with a data URL and a required detail) becomes the tool's content output, an optional `structured_result` its structured value, and any other field or an invalid item a tool error naming the tool; strings and other JSON keep their text and JSON outputs. A Code Mode cell receives every result of such a tool as an MCP `CallToolResult`: `content` blocks with `{type: "image", data, mimeType}` taken from each data URL, and the structured value as `structuredContent`, and the tool declares the MCP output schema, so `exec` declares it as returning `CallToolResult` as it does MCP tools. Models write image-returning tool calls for that shape, as upstream's `exec` description teaches with `image(result.content[0])`: when the cell received the items as a list, Codex Luna looked for `r.content` blocks of type image, showed nothing and answered that it could not see the image, and Claude Haiku 5.5 took three cells to find it. Tools without the option are unchanged.
- A nested tool call that fails inside a Code Mode cell rejects with an `Error` (`bootstrap.js` `toolFailure`), on both backends and targets: its `message` is the failure's text (a string failure itself, else the `text` of an MCP `CallToolResult`'s content blocks, else a `message`, `error` or `detail` string, else the JSON), enumerable so `JSON.stringify` of a `Promise.allSettled` result keeps it, `tool` names the tool, an object failure's fields stay on the error (so `error.content` still reads an MCP result) and the whole value on the non-enumerable `detail`. Cells used to receive the parsed failure itself, so an uncaught MCP failure (such as nanocode-ts's `tempo_wallet_required` result) ended the cell with `Script error: [object Object]`, `error.message` was undefined for host-tool failures, and `JSON.stringify` of settled results printed `{}` for them; checked on 2026-10-24 through nanocode-ts in Node against mock Claude and Codex providers with a failing host tool and an MCP server answering `isError: true`.
- `nanocode::ImageHistory::new(n)` caps the images a model request carries inline (default `DEFAULT_INLINE_IMAGES`, 8), set with `RuntimeBuilder::images` (`Settings::images`, which reaches roots, recipe children and same-harness forks and spawns), `CodexBuilder::images` or `ClaudeBuilder::images`. Before every Codex Responses and Claude Messages request (turns, compactions, side questions) the backend counts message images, `input_image_file` references and images inside function, custom-tool and `tool_result` outputs, and replaces all but the newest in place with `[image omitted from this request, which keeps only the newest N images: <media type, file ID or URL>]`, so order, roles, tool pairing, `cache_control` and non-empty content stay valid; history is unchanged. Eviction goes in batches of ceil(N/2), so a request carries floor(N/2)+1 to N images and its cached prefix changes once per ceil(N/2) new images; a Codex continuation keeps `previous_response_id` only while the server's prefix keeps its evicted batch, otherwise it replays in full. Keep N at or below 20: Claude rejects images over 2000 px once a request has more than 20 (https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/vision), and 8 screenshots of about 1.46 MB of base64 each stay under Bedrock's 20 MB request limit.
- Text-only prompts and Code Mode shutdown stay off Tokio's blocking pool. `prepare_user_input` (both backends, every prompt) used `spawn_blocking` even for a prompt without images, and `EmbeddedHost::terminate` (every session's shutdown, a stopped turn's cells and a displaced prewarmed host) used it to join the QuickJS worker thread; every such call takes the blocking pool's mutex and wakes or starts a pool thread. Now a prompt goes to the pool only when it has an `Image` or `LocalImage` input, and `terminate` awaits a oneshot the worker sends after its runtime and channels are dropped, as the join waited for. With 200 concurrent sessions against a local Responses mock (`output/hand-wiring`, `pool` binary: build an agent, one turn, shut down; 5,000 sessions per run, 4 interleaved runs at load average 57-96 on 2026-10-26), a run took 2.6-3.8 s instead of 4.5-7.2 s, first-turn p50 70-107 ms instead of 120-185 ms, and 1.08-1.22 s of system CPU instead of 1.44-1.63 s. In a `sample` profile of the old build the blocking-pool mutex (`parking_lot` `lock_slow` under `Spawner::spawn_task`) was the largest SDK-side cost of a session.
- A session no longer asks the OS for the user's login shell and, on macOS, the timezone each time. `user_shell_path` (nanocode-home) reads the passwd entry once per process: `ToolRuntime` and `ShellSessions` each called it per session, and on macOS `getpwuid_r` goes through Open Directory behind a lock (`lock_slow` under `User::from_uid`). The Codex `<environment_context>` timezone read and scanned the 107 KB `/var/db/timezone/zoneinfo/tzdata.zi` on every capture; it now keeps the name it found for the `/etc/localtime` link and reads the index again only when the link changes, so a changed system timezone is still seen. The `pool` binary (5,000 sessions, 200 at a time, 4 interleaved runs on 2026-10-26) used 4.38-4.49 s user and 0.74-0.77 s system CPU instead of 4.68-4.83 s and 1.00-1.01 s, and first-turn p50 fell from 25.8-37.2 ms to 21.8-24.4 ms.
- `JsonSchema` and `JsonValue` (types-core, the tool schemas of every `ToolDefinition` and the JSON a `ResponseItem` carries, such as an `additional_tools` item's tools) hold their `serde_json::Value` behind an `Arc`, so cloning a definition or an item shares the tree instead of copying it; the API is unchanged except that `as_value` is no longer `const`. The tool host clones definitions several times a session (the registry, the model contract, Code Mode's nested definitions and summaries): 10,000 one-turn Codex sessions 200 at a time (`pool` binary) used 5.46-5.61 s of user CPU instead of 6.55-6.79 s, first-turn p50 12.8-13.3 ms instead of 16.4-17.2 ms. Codex's built-in tool definitions (`StandardTool::definition`, `web__run` and `image_gen__imagegen`) are built once per process and cloned, so every session shares their schemas: a live Codex session holds 152 KB of heap after one turn instead of 236 KB (139 retained 512-1,024-byte allocations, the `BTreeMap` nodes of JSON objects, fell to 16; `heap` binary, Claude 30 KB), and the `pool` binary used 4.75-5.06 s of user CPU instead of 5.14-5.41 s.
- Three per-session derivations from a session's tools and instructions keep their last result process-wide and reuse it when the next session's inputs are equal (`ToolDefinition`, `CustomToolFormat`, `JsonSchema` and `JsonValue` now implement `PartialEq`, the JSON ones comparing their `Arc` pointer before the value): Code Mode's `exec_spec` (the rendered `exec` description), Codex's prompt-cache prefix digest (`prompt_cache::prefix_key`, the SHA-256 of model, tools and instructions; a custom model's per-session bucket is still derived each time) and the Responses-lite request prefix (`responses_lite_request_prefix`, the tools and instructions items with their UUIDv5 IDs over the serialized tools). Each is a pure function of its inputs, so a session whose inputs differ computes and stores its own. They were 18%, 16% and 12% of a one-turn session's CPU in a `sample` profile of the `pool` binary; 10,000 such sessions 200 at a time used 3.07-3.16 s of user CPU instead of 5.05-5.36 s and first-turn p50 11.6-13.1 ms instead of 17.1-24.5 ms, and the first request of two sessions in a row was byte-identical to the previous build's once session UUIDs and dates were masked, prefix IDs and `prompt_cache_key` included.
- `default_user_shell()` is resolved once per process (it also searches `PATH` when the login shell is not zsh, bash, sh or PowerShell, such as fish), so a process that changes `PATH` or the user's shell later keeps the first answer; `get_shell_by_model_provided_path` and `bash_shell` still look each time. `nanocode_platform::uuid_v7()` natively no longer calls `Uuid::now_v7`, whose shared counter sits behind a `std::sync::Mutex` that every session ID, turn ID and response item ID took (`lock_slow` under `generate_timestamp_sequence` in a profile of 200 concurrent sessions): a process-wide `AtomicU64` holds the last `millis << 12 | counter`, each ID takes the larger of the clock and the last value plus one by compare-and-swap, the 12 counter bits fill `rand_a` (RFC 9562 method 1) and `getrandom` fills `rand_b`, so IDs stay unique and increase in the process, a counter that passes 4,095 in one millisecond borrows the next one as RFC 9562 allows, and only the random syscall remains per ID. 32 threads making 200,000 IDs each took 457 ns per ID instead of 804 (`output/hand-wiring`, `uuid` binary), and the `pool` binary used 0.59-0.62 s of system CPU instead of 0.77-0.78 s.
- A Claude session no longer spawns `uname -sr` for the `OS Version:` line of its `# Environment` section: `nanocode_home::shell::os_version()` reads `uname(2)` through `nix` once per process (the same `<sysname> <release>` text, such as `Darwin 25.3.0`; none off Unix, where `uname` did not exist either). The spawn ran synchronously on a Tokio worker for every session's first request, and macOS serializes process creation in the kernel: 5,000 Claude sessions 200 at a time against a local Messages mock (`output/hand-wiring`, `claude_pool` binary, one turn each) took 7.0 s with first-turn p50 253 ms (10,000 used 57 s of system CPU, `__posix_spawn` the top frame of a `sample` profile), and now take 0.47-0.50 s with p50 14.4-14.8 ms and 0.28-0.29 s of system CPU.
- `ImageHistory::with_blob_store(store)` (`SharedBlobStore`, `Arc<dyn BlobStore + Send + Sync>`, the type `SessionLog::blobs` takes, so a host shares one store) keeps image bytes out of SDK state. Prompt and steer images are stored at submit, route, steer and steer-receipt time, before durable admission; tool-output images are prepared and stored before their receipt, `tool.result` event and history entry (Claude receipts written by older builds are prepared on replay). History, checkpoints, receipts, subagent forks and `input.accepted` events carry `nanocode-blob:<media type>;b3:<hex>` (Claude: a `url` image source). A prompt image that is already a BlobUrl is loaded, prepared and stored again; without a store it becomes a text placeholder. Only images inside the request window are loaded, when a request is actually sent, never for a replayed model step, and a missing blob fails the request with the Reopen disposition. Blob puts finish before any record that names them and are content-addressed, so identical images are stored once; orphans from a crash between put and commit are the host's to collect with the session. Code Mode nested calls' Codex `tool.result` events still carry the raw data URL, because the cell observer is synchronous.
- Maintain one Rust crate workspace with reusable crates that compile individually for native and WASM targets. Keep language bindings, separately packaged WASM SDKs, managed services, and apps outside this repo. Do not add wasm-bindgen glue.
- The SDK builds for iOS (`aarch64-apple-ios`, `aarch64-apple-ios-sim`), for gakonst/nanocode-swift's XCFramework. rquickjs-sys ships no iOS bindings, so `nanocode-code-mode-engine` turns on rquickjs's `bindgen` there, as on WASM (bindgen finds Xcode's libclang). `nanocode-tls` uses `rustls-platform-verifier` on iOS as on macOS, because `rustls-native-certs` reads certificate files iOS does not have, and every hand, runner and attachment WebSocket dial (`remote::native::dial`, `RunnerPool`'s dial, `RemoteRunner` and the attachment driver) passes that TLS configuration to tokio-tungstenite through `attachment::driver::tls_connector` instead of the crate's own native-roots configuration, so a hand on iOS dials `wss://` brains. iOS apps may not spawn processes, so an iOS host offers file tools and Code Mode cells rather than shell tools.
- Preserve agreed public APIs. Use upstream Claude/Codex prompts and tools; share Code Mode and CUA across both backends.
- Prioritize the Rust/WASM SDK. Enable Claude and Codex together, with in-place tool registration and dynamic remote execution through shared host contracts. Keep nanocode a thin public re-export surface; generic orchestration belongs in runtime/agent and backend construction belongs in each backend.
- Keep embedded Code Mode and on-disk durability as the native CLI defaults. An explicitly configured Code Mode host must replace the local evaluator without starting local machine tools. Keep inference, subagents and durability in the brain; execute remote Code Mode on the user's configured machine.
- The native QuickJS worker prepares the next cell's context as soon as a cell has ended and its result is sent: a fresh `Context::full` with the bootstrap module evaluated, its `run_cell` saved as a `Persistent` and a per-execution state bound to the natives, so a `Start` only sets the execution ID and stored values (`begin_execution`) and runs; a prelude that differs from the one prepared for prepares anew. Every cell still runs in a context no code ran in. Context creation, the bootstrap's bytecode load and the collection after a cell were 80% of a `text('ok')` cell's CPU, and the collection still runs per cell, now with the preparation off the reply's path. A prepared state is cleared before it drops, also when `terminate` interrupts a preparation, because the natives hold the state and the state holds bootstrap functions, a cycle QuickJS's collector cannot see, which otherwise left objects for `JS_FreeRuntime` to assert on (the hand-wiring queue load and cells suite hit it before the fix); `Prepared` drops its `Persistent` before its context. WASM keeps its own delayed preparation (`prepare_later`). A sequential Execute of `text('ok')` through output/hand-wiring's `rtt` went from p50 340 to 241 µs, and after 2 ms idle from 406-414 to 254-261 µs; the cells suite's output is unchanged.
