- Code Mode's embedded evaluator builds on both targets. `nanocode-code-mode-engine` keeps the QuickJS cell core in `cell.rs`; native runs it on a worker thread with blocking receives (`EmbeddedHost::spawn` returns as soon as the thread starts: commands queue until QuickJS is ready and a failed startup is reported by the first `start_cell` or `read_event`; it used to block its caller until QuickJS had a runtime and a context and had collected garbage, which `CodeModeRuntime::new`, and so every new session, did on an async worker: on 2026-10-26 200 `ToolRuntime::new_with_tools` blocked p50 0.25-0.34 ms and p90 0.6 ms before, 0.02-0.03 and 0.03-0.07 ms after), and WASM (`wasm.rs`) runs each step (start, a nested call's result, a pass of due timers) on the host's single thread from `read_event`, keeps each QuickJS runtime in a thread-local and stops one synchronous run after `set_interrupt_checks` (`DEFAULT_INTERRUPT_CHECKS`) interrupt checks, since a JavaScript host cannot preempt it and Workers freeze the clock during CPU work. `CodeModeRuntime::with_prelude(source)` adds host globals to each cell: the source is a function body called with the cell's `tools` and `call(name, input)`, which calls any nested tool by name, and returns an object of globals. It runs as strict-mode code: it is compiled once per process as the body of a module's default export (`export default function (tools, call) {…}`, `nanocode:prelude`) and each cell's context loads that bytecode through the runtime's loader (see the cell speed entry below). `LocalCells::host_tools` registers tools that only `call` reaches: they stay out of `tools`, `ALL_TOOLS` and the model's tool list, so a prelude global such as `fetch` has no second spelling for the model to confuse with it (on 2026-10-23 gpt-6-luna wrote `tools.bash.writeFile` when the shell was both a nested tool and a global). Each nested call says whether the prelude's `call` made it, and `CodeModeToolset::execute_host_nested` receives those, defaulting to `execute_nested`: in `LocalCells`, `tools.<name>` and `ALL_TOOLS` reach only the model-visible tools, while `call(name)` prefers a host tool of that name and otherwise reaches the model-visible one, so a host tool that shares a model tool's name no longer silently answers the model's `tools.<name>`, which it did before. `code_mode::LocalCells` is a `CodeModeHost` over one `CodeModeRuntime` per session that runs cells in this process with the callback tools each Execute passes as `tools.*`, so a WASM brain without a machine installs it in `CodeModeExecutors` instead of reimplementing cells. WASM builds compile QuickJS's C against wasi-libc and generate its bindings with bindgen: set `CC_wasm32_unknown_unknown` and `AR_wasm32_unknown_unknown` to wasi-sdk's `clang` and `ar`, `CFLAGS_wasm32_unknown_unknown` to `--target=wasm32-wasip1 --sysroot=<sysroot> -DEMSCRIPTEN=1 -DFE_DOWNWARD=0 -DFE_UPWARD=0 -include <sdk>/crates/runtime/code-mode-engine/wasm/quickjs-wasi.h` (it includes the C headers QuickJS needs, undefines `__wasi__`, which would turn off QuickJS's stack limit, and redefines `__builtin_frame_address` so QuickJS's stack check also covers the host's native stack. Each WASM frame takes native stack that the shadow stack does not show (about 200 bytes against 14 of shadow stack per `JSON.stringify` level, 1,640 against 650 per nested `String(array)`), and how much native stack is left depends on the host (Node's `--stack-size`, workerd, the host frames below the module) and on the tier (Liftoff or TurboFan), so no fixed charge fits every host: the previous charge of 192 bytes per frame plus twice the shadow stack cut plain recursion to 858 calls and still trapped the instance in `JSON.stringify`, `String(array)` and getters under `node --stack-size=500` on 2026-10-24, and the 512 KiB shadow limit before it trapped Node 26 and workerd at 4,800 nested objects. The header keeps the shadow addresses of the frames that check the stack and asks the host through the import `host.stack_room(want, entry)` how much native stack is left: nanocode-ts nests `Reflect.apply` calls of 2,048 arguments (16 KiB each, which V8 refuses before pushing when they do not fit) up to `want` bytes and returns how many fit. It assumes 4 KiB per frame (the largest steps measured in Node 24 with Liftoff only and TurboFan only were 1.7 to 2.6 KiB, and a few frames reached 8.7 KiB): an answer of R bytes allows (R - 48 KiB) / 4 KiB more frames from where it was asked and, since every frame below is one of those frames, that many frames from the outermost one for every later path in the same entry, so repeated recursion within that depth asks again only after the entry ends. It first asks after 16 frames for 128 KiB and doubles each answer up to 1 MiB, so shallow cells pay one 128 KiB probe (about 10 us) per entry, and makes QuickJS throw `RangeError` once less than 64 KiB is left, enough to build the error. The host counts its entries into the module in the word at `entry`, and a changed count discards every answer, because each entry starts at a different native depth. Cargo does not rebuild `rquickjs-sys` when the header changes, so a host build must change `CFLAGS_wasm32_unknown_unknown` with it (nanocode-ts adds the header's hash). Rust places the 1 MiB WASM stack first, so the engine sizes QuickJS's own limit as the stack below the spawning frame minus 192 KiB for host frames, which only guards the shadow stack.
- Each cell's fresh QuickJS context loads the bootstrap (`bootstrap.js`, `code-tools.mjs` and `code-values.mjs`, 29 KB) as precompiled bytecode instead of parsing it. The first runtime of a process compiles one ES module, `nanocode:bootstrap` (`export const bootstrap = <bootstrap.js>`, then `code-tools.mjs` and the value helpers, so bootstrap frames keep their line numbers), with `Module::write`, leaks its 88 KB of bytecode once, and every runtime installs it with rquickjs's `Bundle` loader (the `loader` feature), whose safe `Loader` impl reads only this bytecode, which this process wrote; `prepare_execution` imports it with `Module::import` and `Promise::finish`. `BootstrapResolver` resolves that one name only while `prepare_execution` imports it (a thread-local flag, since the import and its jobs run synchronously on the cell's thread on both targets), so a cell's `import()` of it or of anything else still fails, now as `Error resolving module '<name>' from '<input>'` instead of `could not load module '<name>'`, and internal stack frames read `nanocode:bootstrap:<line>` instead of `eval_script:<line>`. The bytecode is written with `strip_source` (19 KB less to read per context, 24 KB instead of 88 KB): a trivial cell's p50 fell from 0.285-0.291 ms to 0.250-0.256 ms, and `Function.prototype.toString` of a bootstrap function, such as a `tools.*` facade, no longer returns its source. Debug information stays: without it (`strip_debug`) those frames read `runCell (<null>:0:1)`. The import is strict-mode module code; 33 cells covering console formatting, errors, rejections, timers, tools, `store`/`load`, `exit`, `yield_control`, images, sloppy-mode `with`, octal literals and undeclared assignments in cell source gave the same output as before apart from those two texts (`output/hand-wiring`, `cells` binary). Preparing a context took 1.14 ms parsing and 0.10 ms from bytecode (`output/qjs-bytecode`), a trivial cell 1.28 ms and 0.285 ms (p50 of 3,000 on one runtime, `cell` binary), and the `load` binary in queue mode with 64 sessions of 20 one-call cells ran 8,461-9,611 cells/s with cell p50 5.7-6.5 ms instead of 3,450-4,477 and 13.5-17.1 ms (3 interleaved runs, 2026-10-26). The WASM engine builds with wasi-sdk 34 when bindgen also gets `BINDGEN_EXTRA_CLANG_ARGS_wasm32_unknown_unknown=-fvisibility=default`; without it bindgen drops `JS_ToCStringLen2` and rquickjs-sys fails to compile. A native worker no longer builds and drops a context before its first cell (it did since the engine was embedded, with no recorded reason): the first cell's p50 stayed 0.40-0.43 ms against 0.40-0.42 ms with it (300 runtimes, 3 runs), and the `pool` binary, whose sessions run no cell, used 3.87-3.94 s of user CPU per 5,000 sessions instead of 4.32-4.45 s. A `CodeModeRuntime` also no longer starts its QuickJS worker when it is built: the first cell starts one, and a finished cell's worker waited in the runtime for the next (since 2026-11-02 cells take runtimes from a worker pool instead, below). A session that never runs a cell holds no thread: 500 live Codex sessions after a turn without cells held 0 extra threads and 284 KB each instead of 500 threads and 355 KB each (`resident` binary), 10,000 such sessions 200 at a time used 6.30-6.48 s of user and 0.76-0.80 s of system CPU instead of 6.87-7.02 s and 1.26-1.37 s, and the first cell of a runtime takes about 0.07 ms longer than the next (p50 0.49-0.53 ms against 0.43-0.45 ms).
- Embedded Code Mode cells start, log and wait on timers faster on both targets (2026-10-27). A host prelude was parsed with `new Function` in every cell's fresh context, 2.7 ms of WASM CPU per cell for nanocode-ts's 30 KB prelude; `install_prelude` now compiles it once per process into module bytecode (cached by source, its bundle leaked once per distinct prelude like the bootstrap's) and sets the runtime's loader to serve it beside the bootstrap, and `import_prelude` loads it into each context. `text()` and `console`'s methods hand their string to `__nanocodeText` instead of JSON-encoding `{type, text}` for `__nanocodeContent` to decode, `stringify` returns a string at once and `format` returns a single string argument as it is. Due timers run in passes: `next_timeout_wait` and `next_due_timeout` read an ordered `(deadline, id)` set instead of scanning every pending timer, and each wake runs every timer due at its start, in deadline order with microtasks drained after each, so 10,000 `setTimeout(0)` started in one turn need one pass instead of one host sleep each (a host `setTimeout`, at least 1 ms in Node). Timers a pass adds wait for the next pass, so a cell that keeps scheduling zero-delay timers still yields to the host between passes, and on WASM a pass ends early once its callbacks have used one `set_interrupt_checks` budget, each callback still getting the full budget as before. In gakonst/nanocode-ts's `bench/hands.mjs` (Node 22, WASM brain, scripted Claude model, local cells as the Cloudflare and Vercel demos run them, medians of 5 turns) an empty cell's turn took 5.35 ms instead of 9.53, 10,000 zero-delay timers 34.7 ms instead of 11,037 (native hand: 20 ms instead of about 700, measured beside another run), 100,000 `console.log` lines 76.8 ms instead of 460 (native hand 55 instead of 260) and 10,000 lines 12.8 instead of 54.6; `bench/overhead.mjs` measured an empty cell at 2.82 ms instead of 6.11.
- Code Mode cells (`bootstrap.js`) have Node's `console`: `log`, `info`, `warn`, `error` and `debug` with `%s`, `%d`, `%i`, `%f`, `%j`, `%o`, `%O` and `%c`, `table` (Node's box table), `dir` (depth 2 unless `options.depth` says otherwise), `time`, `timeLog`, `timeEnd`, `count`, `countReset`, `group`, `groupCollapsed`, `groupEnd` (two spaces per level), `assert` and `trace`. Objects print as JSON, with `[Circular]`, `1n`, `[Function: f]`, Maps as objects and Sets as arrays; a cell's `console` used to have only the five logging methods. An error prints its `[cause]` chain and an `AggregateError` its `[errors]`, as Node does, and a thrown non-`Error` fails the cell as `Uncaught <JSON>` (a thrown non-empty string stays the message) instead of `[object Object]`. The cell's `setTimeout` passes extra arguments to its callback, and a callback that throws fails the cell, where it used to fail the whole host. A rejection still unhandled after the microtask queue drains fails the cell with `Unhandled promise rejection: <reason>`, as Node exits, also after a yield, which `wait` then reports: the runtime's rejection tracker records rejected promises until a handler arrives, and `drain_jobs` passes those left to `runCell.uncaught`, which ends the cell. Such rejections used to vanish. `tools.*` promises stay observed, as upstream, so an unawaited tool failure stays silent, and `exit()` in a timer or an unawaited async function ends the cell as done.
- When a host gives `LocalCells` a prelude, the embedded engine installs `__nanocodeBytes` for it (`code-mode-engine/src/bytes.rs`): `put(string | ArrayBuffer, offset?, length?)` stores UTF-8 or bytes under a numeric ID, `take(id)` returns them as an `ArrayBuffer` that owns the Rust buffer (no copy), `text(id, fatal?)` decodes a stored buffer straight into a JS string without an `ArrayBuffer`, `discard(id)` drops one, and `decode`, `encode`, `toBase64` and `fromBase64` (lenient: URL-safe letters, no padding, other characters skipped) run natively, as do `digest(name, buffer, offset?, length?)` (SHA-1, SHA-256, SHA-384 or SHA-512 over the range, hashed 1 MiB at a time) and `random(length)` (an `ArrayBuffer` filled by `nanocode_platform::fill_random`), which back the prelude's `crypto.subtle.digest`, HMAC, `getRandomValues` and `randomUUID`; on 2026-10-28 a native cell hashed 26 MB with SHA-256 in 293 ms, which the prelude's JavaScript SHA-256 took 17 s for in gakonst/nanocode-swift's shell. `nanocode_code_mode::code_mode::bytes::{put, take}` reach the same store from host tools, so a prelude passes IDs in tool inputs and results instead of base64 inside JSON. The store is process-wide and lock-free (an `Atom` of `Arc`'d `Slot`s: `take` moves the buffer out of its slot without copying it), since native cells run on a worker thread, and `put` drops entries unclaimed for 10 minutes, such as a result whose cell stopped before it took it. The prelude removes the global; without a prelude it is not installed. gakonst/nanocode-ts moves fetch bodies and `bash.readFile`/`writeFile` this way: on 2026-10-24 an 8 MiB fetch, write and read in one cell grew its module memory to 20.6 MiB instead of 651 MiB. `put` of a string encodes it in 1 MiB slices (cut before a high surrogate), each through `CString`, which borrows an ASCII string's own bytes, and turns a lone surrogate into U+FFFD as `TextEncoder` does, so it never holds a whole C-string copy beside the result. An `ArrayBuffer` is still copied through 1 MiB latin-1 strings: rquickjs 0.14 reads `ArrayBuffer` and typed-array bytes only through the unsafe `as_bytes`, `as_slice` and `as_raw`, and its safe `Class` needs an unsafe `JsLifetime` impl.
- A cell's source first runs as a global script with top-level await (QuickJS `JS_EVAL_FLAG_ASYNC` through the bootstrap's `__nanocodeScript`), with the cell globals (`tools`, `text`, `store`, ..., and the host prelude's names such as nanocode-ts's `fetch`, `bash`, `wasm` and `WebAssembly`) defined as configurable properties of `globalThis`, so its top-level `const`, `let`, `class` and `function` declarations are script globals: indirect eval (`(0,eval)('name')`, `globalThis.eval`) and later code in the cell see them, and a top-level `const` or `let` may still shadow a cell global. Each cell gets a fresh QuickJS context on both targets, so those globals never reach another cell or session. A source that does not parse as a script, such as one with a top-level `return`, runs as before inside an async arrow function within the `AsyncFunction` whose parameters are the cell globals, on the same first line so error line numbers do not move. Declaring a cell global's name in that function form used to fail the whole cell with `SyntaxError: invalid redefinition of parameter name` (GPT-6 Luna, `const wasm = ...`, nanocode.xyz/vercel, 2026-10-24).
- `CodeModeExecutors` stops a session's cells on its touched hosts and also on the host its placement selects (`stoppable`), because a host counts as touched only once a cell it ran returned: before 2026-10-24 a Stop during a session's first cell never reached `LocalCells`, so on the Cloudflare and Vercel demos the cell kept running after the turn was cancelled and its nested calls finished later (found with a mock Codex turn in Node: `touched` was empty at cancel time). Adding the host to `touched` when a cell starts instead made the next steps route through it and trapped the WASM module, so only the stop paths use the placement.
- On WASM, `EmbeddedHost::terminate` (and `Drop` while a cell is running) closes the stopped cell's context and then frees its QuickJS runtime, as it does after a cell that finished. From 59c5cd9 until later on 2026-10-24 it leaked the runtime with `mem::forget` instead, because freeing a runtime whose cell was stopped while suspended in an `await` trapped the module (`memory access out of bounds` in `JS_FreeRuntime`; natively a debug build asserted `p->ref_count > 0` in `gc_decref_child` in 3 to 7 of 10 runs, depending on the order the collector met the objects). The cause was quickjs-ng 0.15 (rquickjs 0.12): open var_refs into a suspended coroutine's frame were invisible to the cycle collector, so freeing the frame detached a var_ref whose value the same collection then freed (fixed upstream in quickjs-ng 7955cfd49, shipped in 0.16, which rquickjs 0.14 bundles). With the leak, 50 stop/run cycles in Node 26 (mock Codex turns, each stopped cell holding 20,000 small objects) grew the module's memory from 3.0 to 130.6 MiB, about 2.4 MiB per stopped cell, so a 128 MB Worker isolate ran out after about 50 Stops; with rquickjs 0.14 and the runtime freed they end at 9.8 MiB, as do stopped cells waiting on timers, tool calls or a promise that never settles.
- A running cell streams its `text()`/`console` output: `observe_updates` sends the text items not yet streamed as one `CodeModeUpdate::Notification` on the cell's origin call (a delta, items joined by newlines), once the observation is 500 ms old and then whenever the cell's queued updates drain, so a cell that finishes quickly logs no extra events and a tight loop's lines arrive as one notification; at most 64 KiB is streamed per observation, the rest arriving with the result. These notifications are not added to the model's result. A text item that arrives while the last buffered item is also unstreamed text is appended to it after a newline instead of becoming its own item, so the result carries one text block where a loop logged many (the same text the streamed delta and truncation, which join items with newlines, already showed), and the Claude backend's `bound_tool_result` drops text blocks once its 12,000-token budget is spent and ends with one `[N more text blocks omitted]` block. The engine merges at the source too: `__nanocodeContent` appends text to the execution state's `pending_text` and sends it as one `RuntimeEvent::Content` once it reaches 64 KiB, before any other event (`ExecutionState::send`), when the cell's jobs drain and when a step settles, so a synchronous loop no longer queues one channel message per line (merging only in `observe_updates` left the 200,000-line cell at 58 MB). When a turn is stopped, `terminate_turn` reports each terminated cell's output since it last returned (its last 16 KiB) through `CodeModeObserver::cell_terminated`, and the Codex and Claude backends append it to the stopped `exec` or `wait` call's result (`aborted by user after Ns; output before it stopped:` / `Tool execution stopped by the user ... Output before it stopped:`), so the model knows what ran.
- Cells get a `console` parameter whose `log`, `info`, `warn`, `error` and `debug` append their arguments joined by spaces as one `text()` item (strings as they are, errors as their stack, other values as `text` stringifies them). It is a cell parameter like `text`, not a property of `globalThis`, which still has no `console`, as upstream Codex's runtime deletes it (`v8_console_is_not_exposed_on_global_this`); the `exec` description keeps upstream's "no console" text, and a cell may still declare its own `console`.
- Use the same remote Code Mode protocol whether the brain initiates a connection or accepts a runner connection, on loopback or across machines. Keep socket and storage adapters replaceable for WASM hosts such as Workers; in-memory storage is not persistent durability. Preserve cancellation and report uncertain remote outcomes without silently replaying dispatched work.
- Remote Code Mode links write in batches, encode each request once and decode frames without buffering. `MessageWriter` drains every queued write before its one flush, and the hand's writer task writes every queued frame under one deadline (`write_all`), so replies and requests that queue together share a syscall. `RemoteClient` keeps the text it encodes to check a request against the frame limit and passes it to `RemoteLink::send_encoded`, whose default calls `send` (which encodes again) and which `MessageLink` and the native `SocketLink` use as is; requests used to be serialized twice, which for an Execute includes its source and brain tool definitions. A resumable client acknowledges replies in the next batch it sends, and only after `ACK_DELAY` (10 ms) when nothing else goes out, instead of writing an `Ack` for every batch it reads: in a loop of sequential calls the next request follows a reply within tens of microseconds. `RemoteFrame` deserializes through `remote/frame.rs`: it reads the `type` key first, as every encoder writes it, and deserializes the rest of the object straight into the variant (a frame whose `type` comes later takes a slower path through `Value`); serde's derive for internally tagged enums buffered every frame into `Content` first, 10-16% of a call's CPU. The hand publishes the work `HandActivity::items()` lists at most every 10 ms (`WORK_PUBLISH_DELAY`) instead of on every request and reply, because an `Atom` store makes the writer pay the read debts of every thread that used arc-swap (about 1-2 µs, 10% of a call's CPU at 16 concurrent calls); a call finishing still shows at once through its `ended` flag. Shared admissions of calls outside a cell (no origin) count on an atomic in the admission gate unless an exclusive call holds or waits for it (`CLOSER`), instead of cloning and swapping the gate state twice per call; the gate stress test with direct calls mixed in (output/hand-wiring `gate2`, 1,800 rounds with and without cancellations) found no violation or stuck gate. Native sockets read with a 16 KiB buffer (`READ_BUFFER_BYTES`), because tungstenite zero-fills its whole read buffer (128 KiB by default) before every read, which cost 7% of a call's CPU; 16 KiB measured as fast as 4 KiB for small frames and faster than 4 and 128 KiB for 64 KiB ones. A resumable hand encodes a retained reply once when its live form is the same. Measured with output/hand-wiring's `rtt` (a `RemoteClient` over `MessageLink` and a `Hand::connect` hand with a noop tool in one process on loopback, interleaved A/B on a shared laptop, 2026-10-26): a sequential Call went from p50 65 to 45 µs and 78 to 50 µs of CPU, 16 concurrent callers from 59.9k to 83.1k calls/s (53 to 39 µs of CPU each), a 64 KiB Call from 252 to 186 µs, a callback round trip from 481 to 392 µs; the Worker demo's measure-hand-load (4 sessions of 40 pong turns, 20 ms polls) went from 86 to 105 turns/s and 12.4 to 10.6 ms of workerd CPU per turn with the durability changes. After 2 ms idle a Call takes about 140 µs here, the same with a single tokio thread for both ends, so idle starts pay cache and clock warm-up rather than thread hand-offs, and shrink only with the work per call.
- `nanocode_code_mode::remote::RemoteClient` owns the brain side of the remote Code Mode protocol on every target: pending replies, brain and hand callbacks, detached cells and their turn generations, cancellation, operation deadlines and unknown outcomes after a disconnect. The pending replies belong to the driver alone: a caller sends its reply channel in the same channel item as its frame, so no reply can arrive before its waiter, and a dropped caller sends a forget; `RemoteClient::calls(Vec<(id, operation)>)` sends a batch of Calls under caller-chosen IDs as one item, so the link gets them in one `send`. The map used to be an `Atom<HashMap>` that every request copied on insert and on removal, O(n) per request with n in flight: through a loopback hand, 64 sessions running cells on the hand with 64 nested calls each went from 12,091 to 51,913 calls/s. `RemoteClient::new` runs it over any `RemoteLink`, which only sends and receives frame batches and closes the connection, and returns a driver future the host spawns. `SocketTransport` in integrations/tool-runtime is the native WebSocket link with dialing, accepting, heartbeats, liveness and close frames; WASM hosts implement `RemoteLink` over their own sockets. Only frames whose effect the brain must have recorded first are `RemoteDelivery::Durable`, so a host makes its state durable before sending a batch that contains one: `Execute` and `Cancel` requests and brain callback results. Turn bookkeeping (`BeginTurn`, `DetachTurn`, `SettlePreempt`, `EndTurn`), `Wait`, `CancelTurn`, `Preempt`, `CancelCall`, relayed hand calls and their results are `Immediate`: none starts work on the hand, and a brain that loses its state after sending one reports the cells it finds gone as unknown outcomes rather than replaying them. On a deployed Cloudflare Durable Object (nanocode-ts syncs storage before every durable send, about 20-35 ms each) this took two confirmation waits out of every Code Mode turn: a mock-model turn with one cell on a hand went from p50 317 to 280 ms inside the object, and to 102 ms when nanocode-ts also stops syncing before model requests, which have no side effect to protect (2026-10-26). `RemoteFrame::dispatches` marks the requests that start or continue work on the peer (Execute, Wait and Call), so a host can refuse to send them, for example once a turn's cancellation is requested, without decoding frames itself. `RemoteCatalog::decode` reads the catalog a hand or runner publishes as its first frame, with its tool definitions and advertised name, and rejects any other first frame with `NO_CATALOG`; the native `SocketTransport` and WASM hosts use it alike, so a host that stores the catalog across hibernation keeps it as opaque text. `RemoteLink::changed` runs after each received batch and finished callback, `RemoteActivity::busy` reports running callbacks and detached cells for host idle checks, and `RemoteActivity::is_alive` reports whether its client still exists. A link that wrote none of a batch, such as a host refusing it for a revoked hand or a cancelled turn, fails `send` with `CodeModeHostError::unsent`; when the connection ends, operations whose request no link accepted, or a link refused that way, report that they were not dispatched, and only requests a link may have written report unknown outcomes. A link that refuses only some request frames of a batch, such as a host that shares one link among turns and stops one turn's dispatches, writes the other frames and fails `send` with `CodeModeHostError::refused(reason, ids)`: the client counts the rest of the batch as written, fails only those requests' operations as not dispatched and keeps the link and its other operations running; refusing an ID that is not a request of the batch fails the link like any other send error. `RemoteLink::max_frame_bytes` is the largest frame both ends accept: `SocketTransport` and the WASM package's link take the smaller of their own policy and the `max_frame_bytes` a hand or runner publishes in its catalog, and every request carries it, so the hand learns the brain's limit. No side writes a larger frame: the brain fails an oversized request as not dispatched and replaces an oversized callback result with the limit error; a hand replaces the inputs and outputs of an oversized result's nested calls, and then its output, with the limit error while keeping its cell, sends oversized nested-call updates without their payloads and fails an oversized cross-hand call without dispatching it. Before this, one 1.5 MB `text()` or cross-hand `Write` on a Worker hand closed the connection and failed every pending call with an unknown outcome. Execute, Wait and Call deadlines add the operation's requested `yield_time_ms` (a Call's from its input, such as a `write_stdin` poll of up to 300 s) to the operation timeout, because a model-chosen yield longer than the host's timeout otherwise expired the deadline and cancelled the turn's cells or reported the call's outcome unknown. Each client starts its request IDs at a random offset below 2^52, so when a host hands a connection to a new client, as the Worker does for a runtime reopened after a failure, a late reply to the previous client's request cannot reach the new client's request with the same ID. A client knows only the cells its own requests started: when a host hands a live connection to a new client, as the Worker does after a forced Durable Object restart while a hand stays attached, the hand keeps running the previous client's cells, and the new client drops their `DetachedUpdate` and `DetachedComplete` frames, so their completion notices are lost, answers their brain callbacks as unavailable, never answers a callback the previous client was still running, which then waits for the hand's callback deadline (`nanocode hand` sets none), and sends turn controls such as CancelTurn only to hands it has used itself; a Wait on such a cell's ID still reaches the hand through the session's placement. Reporting or cancelling those cells needs the hand to learn that its client changed, or durable records of each session's hands and cells.
- `RemoteOperation::EndTurn { session_id, turn_id, hook_event_name }` tells a remote hand that a turn ended, so its tools release what the turn held: the native hand (`Hand::connect`) calls `Tool::end_turn` on every registered tool, whether or not it runs cells for the session, logs a tool's failure without retrying it, and answers `Ok(None)`; it dispatches nothing, so links that send only tool calls pass it. It is the remote counterpart of the attachment protocol's `EndpointFrame::TurnEnded`. Hosts send it with `RemoteClient::request` once they know a turn ended; tempoxyz/pyramid's broker sends it (`hook_event_name` `Stop`) for turns that ran browser steps, so the Hand's ChatGPT-extension bridge closes their tabs. A hand built before it fails to decode the frame, so send it only to hands built since.
- A hand whose catalog carries a `runtime_id` keeps its work across reconnects when its brain opts in: its catalog says `resumable: true`, and a brain whose `RemoteLink::resumes` returns that runtime ID marks every request `resumable`. `Hand::connect` owns one executor for its whole run, so once a resumable request arrives a dropped socket no longer cancels the session runtimes, their processes or running cells, and a brain that wants a session gone ends it with Cancel. The hand keeps each resumable request's reply until the brain's `Ack`, which a client built with `RemoteClient::new` sends for every batch of replies it receives, and answers requests that finished while it was away on the next connection. A resumable link survives reconnects itself, stays open and passes each later catalog to `RemoteClient`, which sends `Resume` with the requests it wrote and still waits for: the hand resends their replies in full form, because compact replies may name nested-call updates lost with the old socket, leaves running ones to reply when they finish, answers ones it never received with `NOT_RECEIVED` (not dispatched) and forgets the rest; a catalog with another runtime ID fails the client with `HAND_RESTARTED`, so only that runtime's outcomes are unknown. A new client sends an empty `Resume` first, so a hand forgets replies no client waits for, unless it is built with `RemoteClient::resuming`. Cancel and CancelTurn also end a session's in-flight Call requests, which reply with `CALL_CANCELLED` (unknown outcome), because a Call runs one nested tool outside any cell and nothing else could stop it; a process it started may outlive it. A `CancelTurn` with `turn_id` ends only the Calls whose context names that turn and leaves the session's cells alone; without one (the field is omitted on the wire, as before) it cancels the session's turn as it did. A 410 at the WebSocket upgrade rejects a hand's credential for good, like 401 and 403, so a revoked hand stops instead of redialing; `Hand::run` reports it as `HandEvent::Rejected { status }` before returning the error, so hosts match the event instead of the error's text. A policy close (1008) other than revocation or `HAND_REPLACED` fences only the runtime it closed: the hand ends that runtime's sessions, mints a new runtime ID and redials at the longest backoff (5 s), reporting `HandEvent::Disconnected`, because a backend from before a protocol change, or one a deploy rolled back to, closes a newer hand with 1008 and stopping for good stranded every such hand until it was claimed again (6dfd20726 had made any 1008 terminal; tempoxyz/pyramid's PR 219 review rated that its highest risk). On loopback a hand closed with 1008 redialed 5.0 s later under a new runtime ID. Callbacks in flight when the socket drops fail with an unknown outcome, and updates sent while it is down are lost. Brains that do not opt in, the native `SocketTransport` and pool-managed executors among them, see no change: their hands still cancel every session at disconnect. On loopback (a brain whose link outlives sockets and a `nanocode hand` with workspace tools), dropping the socket 1 s into a 3 s `exec_command` Call, a cell whose nested `exec_command` takes 2 s and a background process polled afterwards: before, the hand cancelled every session and the brain failed with `unexpected runner frame` and unknown outcomes; after, the hand reconnected in 100 ms, the Call and the cell returned their real outputs 2.0 s after the drop, and the background process kept running and printed its remaining output.
- A `Hand::connect`ed hand redials at once, with its backoff reset, after a connection the brain served and closed with 1012 (a brain asking it to reconnect, such as one moving its sockets to a newer brain behind the same endpoint) or after any connection that lasted 10 s or more (`FRESH_CONNECTION`); only quick failures in a row (a refused dial, a socket closed before the brain served it, a 1012 the brain sent before serving) back off, from 100 ms doubling to 5 s, and a policy close (1008) still waits the 5 s cap. It used to wait out the backoff after every end and reset it only after a 30 s connection, so a hand cut again soon after reconnecting (a deploy that moved it twice) waited up to 5 s. A served socket is one whose brain sent a frame or a ping (`serve`'s `heard`).
- A `Hand::connect`ed hand moves between brains without a gap. Its catalog lists the `move` capability (`MOVE_CAPABILITY`, `RemoteCatalog::moves`; `HandDescription` leaves it out, as it is no device capability), and a brain may then send it `{"type":"move"}` (`RemoteFrame::Move`) instead of closing with 1012. The hand dials again with the same runtime while the connection it was sent on serves on, as `HandEvent::Moving`; the new connection is a standby (`HandCore::standby`): replies keep going on the old one until the brain first sends a frame or a ping on the new one, which makes it current (`HandCore::promote`, as a reconnect does: callbacks held, `Resume` answered there), and the old one then closes with a close frame. A standby the brain refuses (a draining brain's 1012) is dialed again with the usual backoff while the old connection serves; a brain that never sends the frame, and hands of other hosts (`serve_connect`, listeners), are unchanged. Each connection is served in a task of its own (`Serving` in `remote/hand.rs`). A brain must send the frame only to a hand whose catalog lists the capability: an older hand fails its connection on an unknown frame type. `dial` now resolves, connects, runs the TLS handshake (tokio-rustls) and upgrades in separate steps and times each (`DialStages`: `trust` loading the TLS configuration, `dns`, `tcp`, `tls` with the chain's trust evaluation, `upgrade`), logged at debug (`dialed the brain`) and carried by `HandEvent::Connected { dialed }`, so a host can tell where a slow reconnect spends its time.
- A brain that outlives its process, such as a server whose hands reconnect to whichever replica holds their socket, resumes its predecessor's requests with `RemoteClient::resuming(link, timeout, pending)`: its link's `send` sees every Request frame with its ID and the host's `RemoteContext::call_id` before the write, so the host persists that pair there, and after a restart it passes the IDs still unsettled for a link whose `resumes` names the same runtime. The client sends them in its first `Resume` instead of an empty one and returns one `ResumedReply` per ID: the hand's retained reply in full form, the reply when a running request finishes, or `NOT_RECEIVED`, which every reply path surfaces as `CodeModeHostError::unsent`, so a write lost with a dying socket is known not to have run without a separate delivery acknowledgement. Such a client sends no `Ack` itself: the host calls `RemoteClient::ack` with a request's ID once it has durably settled the reply, so a successor resumes every ID whose outcome its predecessor had not recorded and gets the retained reply, never `NOT_RECEIVED` for a request that ran. A link without `resumes` answers each ID with `HAND_RESTARTED` (unknown outcome). A reply's value has its nested calls expanded from the compact field forms, as `request` expands them, while updates are ignored because the full form names none and a successor never saw the updates its predecessor received, so an Execute or Wait reply deserializes as `CodeModeExecution` and a Call reply decodes with `output_from_wire`, as `call`'s value does; before, it was the raw compact value, and on loopback a resumed cell whose nested call returned JSON failed to deserialize with `missing field output`. Only one client may hold a runtime's unsettled IDs, because each `Resume` makes the hand forget replies it does not name. On loopback, a brain dropped 0.7 s into a 2 s Bash Call and a new client built with `resuming` on the hand's reconnect got the Call's real output 2.05 s after it started, and an ID the hand never received came back unsent.
- `nanocode_code_mode::remote::serve_queue(link, queue, wakeups, options, failed)` serves one hand socket from a host's durable command queue, for brains such as tempoxyz/pyramid's broker whose calls are queued in a database and may be sent by whichever replica holds the socket, and is lock-free by design: the host persists nothing before a write and every host call covers a batch. A command's request ID is `QueuedCall::request`, the ID a host's earlier build sent it under, or else derived from its call ID (`QueuedCall::request_id()`, `request_id(call_id)`: FNV-1a folded to 52 bits with bit 52 set, so derived IDs stay below 2^53, JavaScript's largest safe integer, and never meet the `RemoteClient` counter's IDs, which start below 2^52), and a hand treats a request ID as an idempotency key: a Request whose ID is running or retained for the same call attaches to it (a retained reply is sent again) instead of running again, and one whose ID belongs to another call is answered `REQUEST_IN_USE`, which counts as not dispatched. So a command runs at most once per hand runtime however often it is sent, and nothing records "attempted" before a write. A host whose fleet still runs a build that resumes only the request IDs it recorded (Pyramid's `request` column) keeps writing the derived ID there from `admit`, one conditional update per batch, and passes recorded IDs back as `request`, so neither build sends the other's commands under a new ID. The host implements `CommandQueue` (methods return `impl Future + Send`, so a host writes them as `async fn`): `poll` returns every unsettled command for the hand's runtime, sent or not (`QueuedCall` with a host `Origin`; `serve_queue` skips those in flight), `stop` (commands that must not run, with a reason), `cancel` (turns to cancel) and `watch` (turns to tell when they end); `admit` (optional, default `Admit::Send` for all) is one fence over the batch about to be written, answering `Send`, `Wait` (poll again) or `Refuse(reason)` per call; `settle(&[(command, Settlement)])` stores a batch of receipts; `turns_ended(&[(session, turn)])` answers for every watched turn at once; and `unsettled(&[command], error)` hears a failed `settle`, whose commands stay pending. `wakeups` is a stream the function owns (a new command, say), so hosts need no Mutex around a receiver as `CommandQueue::changed(&self)` made them hold. On connect it polls once and resumes every unsettled command through `RemoteClient::resuming`: a retained reply or a finished running one settles, `NOT_RECEIVED` makes the command eligible to send, and a link without `resumes` answers `HAND_RESTARTED`, so those commands settle `Unknown` rather than running twice. It then sends the calls of each poll as one frame batch (`RemoteClient::calls(Vec<(id, operation)>)`, one channel item and so one `link.send`), and one settler task drains every finished reply at once: a reply is `Completed` with its full wire output and acknowledged after `settle` stored it, `NOT_RECEIVED` is sent again unless the command was stopped (then `Unavailable`), another unsent failure (a frame over the limit, a refusal) is `Unavailable`, a failure while the connection is gone is left for the next socket, and anything else is `Unknown`, never retried. A failed `settle` releases its commands, so the next poll sends them again and the hand answers with the retained reply; a `NOT_RECEIVED` for a call this socket sent (a draining hand refuses it) releases it to the next scheduled `busy_poll` rather than to an immediate poll, which made a draining hand and its host loop as fast as they could answer, while one for a command resumed at connect is sent at once; a hand does not retain such a refusal, so `HandActivity::idle()` resolves while it drains (it used to wait for the full drain timeout: on loopback a drained hand went idle and the brain retried once a second, and after `undrain()` the command ran once); a stopped command never sent settles `Unavailable`, and a sent one gets `CancelCall` when `QueueOptions::cancels_calls` (the catalog's `cancels_calls`) is set. It sends one `CancelTurn` naming the turn per session and turn and one `EndTurn` (`Stop`) per watched turn that ended, checking at `QueueOptions::end_check`, polls every `poll` (or `busy_poll` while anything is unsettled or watched) and at once on a wakeup or a released command, and returns the driver's reason when the connection ends or the host's error when `poll`, `admit` or the `failed` future (such as a heartbeat) fails. Before 2026-10-26 the contract was per call: `admit` wrote each request ID before its write (all or none for the batch, so one stale call refused the rest), `recorded` read each command back before settling it, `settle` took one command, and the hand would run a request ID sent twice twice. tempoxyz/pyramid takes one deployment-wide advisory lock (`WRITE_LOCK`) in every write transaction, on a pool of one connection that its reads share, so each of those was a turn through that lock: through a loopback hand (`output/hand-wiring`), 8 parallel calls made 8 `admit`s, 8 `recorded` reads and 8 `settle`s, and now make 1 `admit` and 5 `settle` batches and no reads; a socket dropped 0.5 s into a 2 s command, a command queued while the hand was away and a `settle` that failed once each ran exactly once and settled with the command's real output. The loop owns its in-flight and stopped maps (the settler reports back over a channel, and only retries, `NOT_RECEIVED` or a failed `settle`, poll again at once), and the client's driver runs as its own task, so a slow host `poll` or `admit` never stalls the socket. Under load (`output/hand-wiring`'s `load`: sessions each running Code Mode cells in the brain whose `Promise.all` fans out N nested `noop` calls through `serve_queue` to a loopback hand, 4 cells per session, single runs on a laptop at load average 30-60), 64 sessions x 64 calls went from 3,896 to 25,245 calls/s with cell p50 from 1,001 to 140 ms, 16 x 64 from 10,097 to 24,831, and 64 x 16 behind a host whose calls take 2 ms from 4,752 to 12,638; behind a host that serializes those 2 ms calls under one lock, as Pyramid's write lock does, it stays at about 285 calls/s, bounded by the host's own per-call insert.
- Remote Code Mode payloads cross the connection once. Brains mark their Request frames and hands their Callback frames with `compact: true`, and only answers to a marked frame use the compact forms, so peers built before them keep sending and receiving the full ones: a tool output omits `output` when it is the JSON text of its `structured_result`, as `ToolOutput::from_json` makes it, and the receiver encodes the value again; a nested call omits `structured_result` when it equals `output` and `output` when it is the JSON text of `structured_result`; a completed update omits the input that the call's start update on the same request carried; and a reply names each nested call whose completed update the same request delivered as `{"call_id","delivered":true}`. `SentCalls` writes them on the hand and `ReceivedCalls` in `RemoteClient` restores them before an observer or the execution sees them. A tool output whose `structured_result` equals its `output` crosses with a null `structured_result` to every peer, because every build derives the structured result from the output then. A value holding floats is compared with its JSON text after a parse and a second encoding, because serde_json's default float parsing does not round-trip every value. Requests and callbacks that also carry `output_prefix: true` (RemoteClient's requests and a native hand's callbacks do) accept a text output that ends with its structured result's `output` string as `output_prefix`, the text before it, without `output` (`encode_output(output, compact, output_prefix)`, `SentCalls::output_prefix`); `output_from_wire` and `ReceivedCalls` rebuild the text byte for byte. Codex `exec_command` and `write_stdin` results are such text (a header, then the command output, which `structured_result.output` repeats), so their output crossed twice: on 2026-10-26, through `serve_queue` and a loopback `Hand::connect` hand with a 1 MiB frame limit as tempoxyz/pyramid's broker sets it (`output/hand-wiring`), a 64 KB output's reply went from 141,271 to 70,876 bytes and a 400 KB one's from 880,475 to 440,482, and outputs of 480 KB to 768 KB, which exceeded the frame and settled with unknown outcomes, completed. A hand retains a Call reply for resumes without `output_prefix` (and Execute and Wait replies' full form never used it), because a brain built before it that resumes the request would read the missing `output` as the structured result's JSON; detached cell updates, which can reach another brain after a reconnect, don't use it either. `serve_queue` receipts (`Settlement::Completed`) stay the full form. A brain's own answers to a hand's callbacks don't cross back either: `RemoteClient` marks its requests `answers: true`, a hand then marks its callbacks `answers: true` and records each answer it decodes (`Answers`, by nested call ID), the brain records the same answer under the session and call ID before it sends the `CallbackResult`, and the hand's completed update for that call, when its output, structured result and metadata are still exactly the answer, carries `answered: true` instead of them; `ReceivedCalls::answered` restores them from the brain's record (an `answered` call the brain did not record fails the reply). Records go when used, when their cell's origin finishes (hand `CallbackCatalog::forget`, brain request completion and `DetachedComplete`) and when a session is cancelled or the connection ends, and resumable replies' full form never uses the marker. Brains and hands built before it neither send nor expect the flags, so they keep the full form both ways. On 2026-10-27 in nanocode-ts's `bench/hands.mjs` a cell calling a brain tool with a 1 MiB argument that it echoes back sent 3,073 KB from the hand per turn and then 2,049 KB, and its turn took 37.6 ms instead of 44 on a native hand and 52 ms instead of 63 on a TypeScript hand. The argument still crossed twice, in the call's start update and in its callback, which leave on different tasks. Since 2026-10-28 `RemoteClient` also marks its requests `asks: true`; a hand then marks its callbacks `asked: true` and, once a callback's frame is queued on the socket, records its input under `asked_key(call_id)` (with `hand` added back for a call routed to another hand, which `take_hand` removed), the brain keeps the same input when it reads the callback, and a start update the hand writes after that whose input still equals the record carries `asked: true` instead of the input, which `ReceivedCalls` restores (an `asked` start update that no callback carried fails). The socket is one ordered queue, so the callback always reaches the brain first; a start update written before its callback keeps its input, and both sides drop an unused record when the call completes or its origin finishes. On 2026-10-28 the same cell sent 1,025 KB from the hand per turn on native and TypeScript hands, and 2,049 KB with a hand or brain built before it, which keep the full form.
- A native hand caches the brain callback tools an Execute names (`local_tools`), and a brain sends each list once instead of in every Execute. The hand's catalog says `caches_local_tools: true` (`RemoteCatalog::caches_local_tools`, `RemoteLink::caches_local_tools`, which the native `SocketTransport` and nanocode-ts's Worker link read from it); `RemoteClient` then sends each Execute with non-empty `local_tools` as `local_tools: []` and `local_tools_key`, the BLAKE3 hex of the list's JSON (`local_tools_key`). A hand that holds that key runs the cell with the cached list; one that does not answers `LOCAL_TOOLS_UNKNOWN` before admitting anything, and the client sends the Execute again, under a new request ID, with the list and the key, which the hand stores. The hand keeps the lists its current connection used and, when it reconnects, those its previous connection used (a list moves to the current connection when used again), so a reconnect, such as the redial every `maxDuration` of a Vercel Function, does not send them again, and lists no recent connection used are dropped. So the first Execute of each tool list costs one extra round trip, and every later one, from this brain or a brain that took over the same connection (as each drive of nanocode-ts's Vercel adapter does), carries only its source and context. Hands that don't advertise it, such as nanocode-ts's WASM hand, keep receiving full lists. A hand also no longer sends `DetachedComplete` for an origin that an Execute or Wait reply settles (the brain removes its callbacks on that reply), only for detached cells and origins dropped without a reply, so a cell's reply is its only frame. On 2026-10-27 a nanocode-ts Vercel session's Execute was 53,303 bytes (its MCP, subagent and host tool definitions), and through a Vercel Function's WebSocket (`upgradeWebSocket`) to a `nanocode hand` in San Francisco, 79 ms round trip for small messages, it took 213-224 ms against 83-92 ms for the Cancel frames beside it; with the cache, the exec tool call (one World commit before the Execute plus the round trip) took a median 196-309 ms over four interleaved 7- and 8-turn runs, against 379-412 ms without it.
- `RemoteClient` answers a `CancelTurn` without a `turn_id` or a `Cancel` itself when the session has nothing on the hand: no Execute, Wait or Call of this client still waiting for its reply, no cell it knows to be running (a callback origin) and no brain callback running for it. It still writes the frame, as `Immediate` rather than `Durable`, so the hand still ends anything another client left there, but replies `Ok(None)` to the caller once the frame is written instead of after the hand's round trip. Both backends cancel the turn's cells before a turn completes (Claude's `settle_code_turn`, Codex's `terminate_turn_cells`) and the session's runtime cancels its cells when it stops, so every turn with a remote hand waited one round trip at its end and every stop one durable write and a round trip. On a Vercel preview with a hand 79 ms away on 2026-10-27, the time from the exec call's result to the turn's completion went from a median 84-118 ms to 45-46 ms; a busy `CancelTurn` still waits for the hand (loopback: 1.9 ms with a yielded cell, which the hand then reports as not found).
- gakonst/nanocode's `nanocode-cli` prerelease is the build the demos install: `nanocode-<target>.gz` for `aarch64-apple-darwin`, `x86_64-apple-darwin`, `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` and `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, with their `SHA256SUMS`. `install` at the repository root, which https://nanocodex.paradigm.xyz redirects to (raw.githubusercontent.com/gakonst/nanocodex/master/install) and which is uploaded to the same release as `install.sh` and served at https://nanocode.xyz/install by tempoxyz/nanocode-site, downloads the one for the machine, checks it, installs `nanocode` and `nc` to `~/.nanocode/bin`, adds that to the PATH in the shell's profile (`.zshrc`, `.bashrc` or fish's `conf.d`; `NANOCODE_NO_MODIFY_PATH` skips it) and runs `nanocode` with its arguments, so `curl -L https://nanocodex.paradigm.xyz | bash` (the nanocodexyz organization's README) and `curl -fsSL https://nanocode.xyz/install | sh` install the CLI and the demos' chats offer visitors `curl -fsSL https://nanocode.xyz/install | sh -s -- hand '<url>' --token <token>`, which brings people to the CLI. It skips the download when `~/.nanocode/bin/.nanocode.sha256` already holds the release's checksum. `scripts/release-cli.sh` builds all four: the macOS ones on the host (`MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET` 12.3 from `.cargo/config.toml`) and the Linux ones in `rust:1.97-bullseye` containers, so they need glibc 2.30 or newer (Debian 11, Ubuntu 20.04); `--publish` moves the `nanocode-cli` tag to `HEAD` and replaces the release's assets and `install.sh`. On 2026-10-27 each Linux build took 6 minutes under OrbStack, the x86_64 one ran `nanocode --version` and the aarch64 one `nanocode hand --help` in `ubuntu:22.04`, and a Mac without nanocode installed from the release with that command, connected to a nanocode.xyz/vercel session through the site's WebSocket proxy and ran the session's cell.
- integrations/process stops a command's process group with SIGSTOP before it sends SIGKILL. macOS delivers a group signal to one member at a time, so with SIGKILL alone a cancelled shell could see its killed child exit and run its next command, such as a redirect that creates a file, before its own signal arrived: `/bin/zsh -lc` scripts killed 2 ms after they started did so in up to 18 of 400 rounds per run on a loaded laptop on 2026-10-19, and in none of 2,800 rounds with SIGSTOP first.
- `RemoteCodeModeHost::with_tool_selection(Arc<dyn HandToolSelection>)` lets a host select advertised hand tools by session ID, backend family and canonical tool name: each backend binds roots and children through `Tools::for_backend`, and `CodeModeExecutors` binds a selected or cross-call target immediately before use, so a late-attached hand sees the child's own family. The selection filters the model contract and travels as optional `selected_tools` on Execute and Call; a native hand fixes it for the session, filters ALL_TOOLS and refuses excluded names before hand routing or tool admission. Brain callback tools keep their existing origin catalog. Policy changes within a live hand session fail, and hosts keep the policy stable for that session. The catalog's default-false `tool_selection` capability is true only for hands without dynamic providers or a runner pool, whose later discoveries are outside their initial catalog; a configured policy refuses unsupported peers before dispatch and raw restricted requests to dynamic-provider hands also fail. Without a policy the fields stay absent and old peers keep their behavior. Mixed-provider desktop, IDE and hosted-agent hosts can share one stock hand while keeping each backend's native tool shapes.
- Brains without a local evaluator install `CodeModeExecutors` as their Code Mode host, call `CodeModeExecutors::select_prompt_hand` for each prompt, which selects the hand that sent it, or the only attached hand when no default executor is set, and otherwise clears the session's selection, and add `PLACEMENT_INSTRUCTIONS` to the agent's instructions.
- A session's Code Mode placement is its own selection, then its root session's (linked through `Tools::for_session`), then the replacement default, then this machine. A selected executor that is not connected fails the cell without dispatching anything and never falls back to another machine, and executor failures, including unknown outcomes after a dropped connection, are failed cell outputs rather than host errors. Wait goes to the most recent placement with a running cell from that session, turn and cancellation controls reach every executor the session used, and model-visible tool definitions do not change with placement. Hands are chosen per call: a nested call whose arguments include `hand` (freeform tools take `{ input, hand }`) runs on that hand through `HandCalls` unless it names the machine running the script (then `hand` is dropped and the call runs here), the runtime has no `HandCalls` at all, or the tool's own input schema has a top-level `hand` property (`embedded::declares_hand`; `embedded::take_hand` is the one rule `LocalCells` and the tool registry share), in which cases `hand` stays an ordinary argument of the tool: an embedder's own tools may take one (Pyramid's `inspect_hand` and `request_hand` do), and every Code Mode call of theirs failed with "this session has no hands" while it was taken for routing, as tempoxyz/gateway's MCP `compute_create` did until it renamed its `hand` to `attach` (gateway 5c99d6e, 2026-10-30); a routed call skips the local admission gate, passes the target's and fails on its own, so `Promise.all` across hands settles each call separately.
- Code Mode nested calls on one tool runtime pass an admission gate, as Codex's parallel_execution lock gates its tool calls: tools a backend's `CodeModeToolPolicy` marks exclusive (Claude Write and Edit, Codex apply_patch) wait for running calls and block new ones, and every other call shares the gate. A shared call gives up its admission when its cell yields to the model (`CodeModeToolset::cell_observed`), as a Codex exec_command releases parallel_execution at its yield time, because an Edit had waited 272 s and 394 s behind shell commands whose cells had yielded; exclusive calls keep theirs until they finish. The gate's state counts shared admissions per origin (the cell's call ID) rather than keeping one entry per call: each origin holds its count and an epoch from a counter that never repeats, a yield moves the origin to a new epoch and drops its count from the total, and a release decrements only when its admission's epoch is still current, so calls admitted before a yield never count again, as before. A call allocates a `Signal` only when it has to queue, and a queued shared call that the gate admits records its epoch under its token. Every admit and release used to clone a map holding every in-flight call, its origin `String` included. A stress of 300 rounds of 48 tasks making exclusive and shared calls with yields and cancellations found no overlap and no deadlock, as with the previous gate (`output/hand-wiring`, `gate` and `gate_old` binaries), and the `load` binary in queue mode (64 sessions of 8 cells calling 64 tools at once) ran 50,959-59,893 calls/s on 2.16-2.32 s of user CPU instead of 45,281-53,921 on 2.52-2.67 s.
- A local Code Mode cell (`LocalCells`, the embedded host) reports at most `LOGGED_NESTED_CALLS_PER_TOOL` (100) nested calls of each tool name to its observer and in `CodeModeExecution::nested_calls`; later calls of that name still run and keep their receipts (an interrupted one is still carried as an unknown outcome), and when the cell ends the observer gets one `Notification` per tool saying how many more calls ran without being logged one by one, which the model's output does not include.
- Both backends log a nested Code Mode call's `tool.result` bounded, while the cell itself still receives the whole result and the model's `exec` output is unchanged: when the call's output, its structured result (unless it repeats the output) and its metadata exceed 32 KiB, Claude (`nested_event_result`, `EVENT_RESULT_BYTES`) and Codex (`logged_nested_result` in model/run/tool_calls.rs, `LOGGED_RESULT_BYTES`) log the first 16 KiB of its text (Codex keeps image and other non-text items of a content result), the structured result reduced to its `image_url` or `file_id`, metadata only up to 4 KiB, `truncated: true` and `original_bytes`. 32 KiB is the bound Claude already used; gakonst/nanocode-ts's chat keeps at most 64 KiB of a whole cell's input and output, so a longer nested result is never shown. A nested call's `tool.call` arguments over 32 KiB are logged the same way (`nested_event_arguments`, `logged_arguments`): every string is cut to one common length so the strings total 16 KiB less the rest of the encoding, so short fields such as a path stay whole, with `truncated: true` and `original_bytes`, or null when the non-string part alone passes 16 KiB. A top-level call's `tool.result` logs the result the model receives: Claude's cut to `TOOL_RESULT_TOKEN_LIMIT` (12,000 estimated tokens, head and tail around `…N tokens truncated…`) by `bound_tool_result`, which also returns the original text size, and Codex's output cut as `ContextManager::record_items` cuts it into history (`TOOL_OUTPUT_TOKEN_LIMIT`, 12,000 tokens of 4 bytes; `visible_output` in model/run/tool_calls.rs), with `truncated` and `original_bytes` when it was cut; Codex also logs a structured result over 32 KiB as its image reference or null and drops metadata over 32 KiB. Until 2026-10-28 both kept up to 256 KiB of text (`EVENT_TOP_LEVEL_TEXT_BYTES`, `LOGGED_TOP_LEVEL_TEXT_BYTES`), five times what the model had seen and more than the chat shows (64 KiB of a cell). On 2026-10-27 in gakonst/nanocode-ts's `bench/hands.mjs` (WASM brain in Node, scripted model), a cell writing 6 MiB of text logged 6.2 MB of events (Claude) and 12.3 MB (Codex, which logged the text as result and structured result) and now 311 KB, and its turn took 40 ms instead of 65 (Claude) and 56 instead of 105 (Codex); a nested call with a 1 MiB argument logged 1,065 KB and now 57 KB. On 2026-10-28 in the same bench a cell writing 1 MiB of text committed 311 KB of SQL per turn (Claude and Codex) and now 102 KB, one logging 100,000 lines 313 KB and now 84 KB, while the model's requests are unchanged.
- Remote Code Mode sockets derive liveness from `SocketPolicy.request_timeout`: each side pings every half of it and closes only when a ping sent after the last inbound message stays unanswered for another half, after draining buffered traffic, so a stalled local scheduler cannot fault a live peer and a frozen peer is detected within request_timeout of its last traffic. Pending operations then report unknown outcomes; operation deadlines never close the connection. Pool-managed hand sockets and `nanocode hand` use `SocketPolicy::runner()`: tungstenite's default 64 MiB message limit and twice the attachment heartbeat interval, so both sides ping every 20 s like attachment heartbeats, and calls have no deadline unless the caller passes `call_timeout_ms`.
- The attachment protocol's frames are public in `nanocode_tool_runtime::attachment`: `RunnerFrame` (catalog, diagnostic, result, status and drain, which runners send; borrowed `Cow` fields so the runner serializes without copying and other hosts deserialize owned values) and `EndpointFrame` (ready, call as `AttachmentCall`, turn_ended, cancel, ack, recover and draining; `EndpointFrame::parse` applies the runner's identifier, budget and recovery-batch checks), with `CommandStatus`, `DiagnosticStage` and `ReceiptTiming`. `AttachmentMachine` deserializes through `AttachmentMachine::new`, so a catalog naming an invalid machine fails to decode. Hosts that broker attachment runners themselves, like Pyramid, use these types instead of copies of the JSON.
- `RunnerPool` accepts tool runners and remote Code Mode executors on the same connect and listen paths, and the first frame decides: a catalog with `definitions` registers an executor in the pool's `CodeModeExecutors`, anything else continues as an attachment runner on the open socket. `RunnerPool::keep_connected` redials startup runners with the attachment backoff, connects through the runners tool and /runner fail fast, an executor reconnecting with the same advertised runtime id replaces its registration, and a different live executor with that name is rejected with a policy close. Disconnect permanently revokes a registration and resolves its pending calls, sent ones as uncertain and unsent ones as unavailable, before awaiting socket teardown; pool shutdown closes executors without revocation so they reconnect when the brain returns. `remote::Hand` is the hand side: `Hand::connect` dials and reconnects until the brain revokes it or closes it with `HAND_REPLACED` (`HandEvent::Replaced`), which the Worker sends to the older of two hand processes attaching under one name so they do not displace each other in a loop, `Hand::listen` accepts brains on a loopback address, its name defaults to `Hand::machine_name()`, and its directory is only where cells start.
- A Codex agent with an `ExecutionEnvironment` treats its workspace as a label: a resumed checkpoint keeps the workspace it was saved with, and a configured workspace that differs does not fail it with `WorkspaceChanged`, as it still does without one. Hosts that build agents through a runtime, which always configures an absolute workspace, otherwise could not resume checkpoints their earlier builds saved with the environment's default `.`: tempoxyz/pyramid's threads saved before it moved to `AgentRuntime` failed every turn with "an active agent session cannot change workspace from . to /tmp", and resumed after this.
- A Codex model ID outside the catalog is a `Model::Custom`, not an error: `Model::from_str` returns the catalog variant for known IDs and aliases (`Model::known`) and `Custom` for any other non-empty ID, interned once into a `&'static str` so `Model` stays `Copy`, and serialized as the bare ID. The interned set is a lock-free `nanocode_sync::Atom`, and each distinct custom ID stays allocated for the life of the process, so only a Codex-family selection interns (parsing or deserializing a `Model`, `ModelSelection::as_codex` on a Codex backend); `ModelSelection::from_str` keeps an unknown ID as a Codex `ModelSelection` without interning it, and the Claude agent and `spawn_agent`'s harness inference never probe a name as a Codex model. Hosts that bring their own Responses endpoint, such as nanocode-ts consumers on Workers AI or the Tempo gateway, run any model that endpoint serves without a catalog change. Custom models take the GLM path: HTTPS with full history replay, no stored responses or WebSocket warmup, the GLM system prompt naming the model, low default thinking, every thinking level, no Pro mode and no price estimate. `ModelSelection::from_str` and the CLI's `parse_model` still try catalog Codex models, then Claude models, so Claude names never become custom Codex models; the CLI keeps rejecting unknown names. A `spawn_agent` call with a model and no `harness` picks Codex for a catalog Codex model, Claude for a catalog Claude model and its parent's family for any other ID, so a Codex parent asking for a Claude model gets a Claude child, a Claude parent keeps a gateway ID on Claude, and only a Codex parent or an explicit Codex harness starts a custom Codex child.
- Preserve authentic, version-pinned upstream provider system prompts and tool shapes, including names, descriptions, schemas and formats. Keep host, project and Code Mode additions separate. A provider version header is not prompt provenance.
- Claude provenance: `prompts/claude-code-system.json` keeps the exact identity and core text blocks, `prompts/upstream.json` their CLI version and package integrity, and `tools/src/tools/upstream.json` the exact tool names, descriptions and schemas that `upstream::tool_definition` selects per capture profile. `python3 scripts/verify-claude-upstream.py --capture` checks fresh requests of the pinned official CLI against a local stub with dummy credentials, `--write` refreshes the artifacts after review and `--offline` compares the ignored captures in output/claude-upstream-parity; never copy captured paths, Git state, credentials or user configuration into provider assets. TaskOutput, TodoWrite, Skill, Code Mode and delegation are SDK additions, not captured contracts; unsupported upstream tools are omitted and retained descriptions stay unchanged, including references to omitted tools. The capture pins Claude Code 2.1.304 and runs it with `ENABLE_TOOL_SEARCH=false`, because 2.1.304 otherwise defers TaskStop, WebFetch, WebSearch and the task tools behind ToolSearch and leaves their definitions out of the request; deferral does not change a definition. The subscription wire's headers, billing block and gzip behaviour stay 2.1.283's, so `upstream::VERSION` names the capture separately; since 2026-11-09 the wire claims 2.1.304 (`subscription_wire::CLAUDE_CODE_VERSION`, in its user agent and the billing block's `cc_version` and fingerprint), because a Claude plan serves Fable 5.2 only to Claude Code 2.1.290 and later (below). 2.1.304's first-party requests, captured through a local TLS proxy on 2026-10-20, add `cc_prompt_id`, `cc_turn_origin`, `cc_prompt_index` and `cc_turn_index` to the billing block, the `x-claude-code-prompt-id`, `x-client-request-id` and `x-claude-code-request-class: main` headers, a `diagnostics` field, SDK 0.128.0 and a different beta list; the fingerprint still matches, but the `cch` of both 2.1.285 and 2.1.304 requests does not reproduce as xxh64 of the placeholder body, although the binary still loads seed 0x4d659218e32a3268 there, so the rest of the wire, its `cch` included, stays 2.1.283's until that checksum input is known. WebFetch's `offset` reaches `ApprovedWebProvider` as `WebFetchRequest::offset`, a character position in the page text. `web_fetch_with_source` offers the captured WebFetch definition and answers as 2.1.304 does: "Fetched <url> (HTTP <code> <reason>, <content type>, <n> characters …)." with its untrusted-content note and reporting rules, the page text verbatim inside `<fetched-web-content>` with that tag escaped, at most 48,000 characters of result, and past that the text cut 8,000 characters short with a Haiku summary of up to 100,000 more characters for the prompt and the offset to read on; `ApprovedPage` carries the status and content type for it.
- Claude Read follows Claude Code 2.1.304 for text files: a read without `limit` of a file over 256 KiB (`MAX_READ_BYTES`, its `maxSizeBytes`) fails with "File content (<size>) exceeds maximum allowed size (256KB). …", a ranged read selecting over 25,000 × 128 bytes fails with its range text, and content over 25,000 tokens (`MAX_READ_TOKENS`, estimated at 4 bytes per token) fails for a ranged read and is paginated for a whole-file read, with its `<system-reminder>[Truncated: PARTIAL view — <path>: showing lines 1-<n> of <total> total …]</system-reminder>` after the lines (or its very-long-lines excerpt). `allow_large` raises the limits to the call's `output_token_budget` and three times its bytes, as Claude Code raises them to the context left in the turn; when that budget is unbounded, as in Code Mode, Claude Code's "allow_large is not available where this read runs" applies. Empty files and offsets past the end return its system reminders, a leading BOM is dropped, and lines are `<n>\t<line>` joined by newlines.
- Claude's host `Read` (`nanocode_backend_claude::host_tools` and `host_file_tools`, the WASM hosts' file tools) returns a `.png`, `.jpg`, `.jpeg`, `.gif` or `.webp` file as an image, as Claude Code and the native `Read` do: it reads the bytes with `FileHost::read_bytes`, names the media type from the bytes (`image::guess_format`; other formats or bytes that are no image fail with the native messages), refuses `offset`, `limit` and `pages` with the native texts, and answers one `input_image` data URL with metadata `{"kind":"image","bytes":n}`, which the backend's tool-image preparation resizes before the model request. A Code Mode cell gets `[{image_url, detail}]`, so `image(result[0])` shows it. Before, it read the file as text: on 2026-10-27 on nanocode.xyz/vercel a Claude turn asked to show a 160 KB matplotlib PNG failed with "File content (274.6KB) exceeds maximum allowed size (256KB)", then got the PNG back as escaped text and gave up after four cells. Checked through gakonst/nanocode-ts in Node with a scripted Claude model: a direct `Read` of a PNG answered an `image/png` tool result, and a cell's `image(r[0])` reached the model as an image block.
- Claude Bash follows Claude Code 2.1.304's background commands. With `run_in_background`, `timeout` is how long the command may run in the background: 600,000 ms by default and at most 7,200,000 (`DEFAULT_BACKGROUND_TIMEOUT_MS`, `MAX_BACKGROUND_TIMEOUT_MS`, as its captured description states); foreground commands keep 120,000 and 600,000. A foreground command still running at its `timeout` moves to the background with a 600,000 ms deadline counted from then, except a command whose first word is `sleep`, which is stopped there, as Claude Code's auto-backgrounding excludes it. Every command's stdout and stderr stream through `ForegroundCommand::output` into `<temp>/nanocode/<session>/tasks/<task id>.output` (directories 0700), which is deleted when the command finishes in the foreground. A background command's result is Claude Code's text, for example "Command running in background with ID: <id>. Output is being written to: <path>. You will be notified when it completes. To check interim output, use Read on that file path.", or "Command did not complete within its <n>s timeout and was moved to the background (ID: <id>). …" followed by "If it is still running after 10m in the background, it will be stopped and you will be notified."; the structured result keeps `task_id` and adds `output_file` and `message` for Code Mode. TaskStop answers `{"message":"Successfully stopped task: <id> (<command>)","task_id","task_type":"local_bash","command"}` and refuses a finished task with "Task <id> is not running (status: <status>)". When a root agent's background command ends on its own or at its deadline, the agent gets Claude Code's notification as a prompt routed like a subagent completion: steered into a running turn, or a new turn when idle. It is `<task-notification>` with `<task-id>`, `<tool-use-id>`, `<output-file>`, `<status>` (completed for exit code 0, failed otherwise, killed at the deadline) and `<summary>Background command "<description or command>" completed (exit code 0)</summary>`, `failed with exit code N` or `was stopped after reaching its background time limit` with Claude Code's restart `<note>`. Like Claude Code it also checks the output file every 5 s, and when the file has not grown for 45 s and the last line of its final 1,024 bytes looks like a prompt (y/n, yes/no, "Do you …?", "Press Enter", "Continue?", "Overwrite?"), sends once that the command "appears to be waiting for interactive input" with the last output. A command stopped by TaskStop or shutdown sends nothing, and none is sent when TaskOutput is waiting on the task or has already returned its result, as Claude Code drops a notification that TaskOutput delivered. Subagents and `nc exec` (`ClaudeHarness::headless`) follow Claude Code's `backgroundEndsWithFinalResponse`, which it applies to synchronous subagents and to headless sessions that do not wait for background commands: their notifications are only steered into a running turn (through the `Steer` handle the builder gives `agent_bound` hooks), a turn they did not ask for never starts, their background commands are stopped when the turn ends (an `adapter_cleanup` hook), and their result says "If it exits while you are still working you will be notified, but it is terminated when you give your final response …". Hands get no notifications, so their result says Claude Code's "Nothing notifies you when the command finishes …" instead. As in Claude Code, an output file stops growing at 5 GiB with " [output truncated: exceeded 5GB disk cap] " and the command is stopped. Claude Code's reaping of idle sessions' background commands under critical memory pressure is not implemented: it needs the session's idle time, running turns and delegated agents, which the Bash tool does not see. Codex never notifies: its processes are polled with write_stdin, and like codex-rs `MAX_UNIFIED_EXEC_PROCESSES` a workspace keeps at most 64 shell sessions, closing, when a new one starts, the least recently used one outside the 8 most recent that no call is using, preferring one whose process has exited (`ProcessChild::exited`), as codex-rs does.
- Codex provenance: `client/prompts/upstream.json` pins the prompt bytes and their extraction and `upstream/tools.json` the reviewed standard tool sources, artifacts and option profile. `python3 scripts/verify-codex-upstream.py` checks upstream hashes, prompt extraction, artifact integrity and patch grammar, `--offline` uses the ignored output/codex-upstream-parity cache, and `--serialize` runs the pinned upstream tool builders in an ignored consumer, with `--consumer-dump` comparing a public SDK dump. The immutable provider prefix stays on `ModelConfig::base_system_prompt` with runtime identity in developer context, output schemas stay host metadata, and local web.run, image_gen, attachment and delegation functions are SDK extensions rather than upstream server tools.
- `nanocode-chatgpt-extension` (integrations/chatgpt-extension, Unix only, an empty library elsewhere) lets a native host drive Chromium tabs through the official ChatGPT extension (`EXTENSION_ID`, `EDGE_EXTENSION_ID`) by taking over its native-messaging host `com.openai.codexextension` (`HOST_NAME`) and proxying OpenAI's own traffic to the original host. `config_base(home)` and `browser_dirs(base)` name the per-user Chromium data directories (macOS Application Support or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME`/`~/.config`), `profiles`, `chatgpt_installed`, `extension_enabled(root, id)` (Secure Preferences or Preferences settings, else an Extensions folder) and `default_browser(home)` (LaunchServices' https, then http, handler through `default_from_handlers`) read them. `Bridge::new(base, state)` owns registrations in the host's `state` directory: `chatgpt-<sha256 of the canonical browser root>.json` holds a `Registration` (`root`, the exact `original` OpenAI manifest or None, and the host's `automatic` flag stored as `auto`), `.sh` the launcher script, and `chatgpt.lock` an flock that serializes `install`, `Guard::release`, `reconcile_all` and `uninstall_all` across processes (`Bridge::lock` returns the `Guard`). `Bridge::launcher(Launcher)`, which `install` and `reconcile_all` need, sets the script the browser starts: `Launcher::new(program).env(NAME, path).arg(word)`
