renders `#!/bin/sh\nNAME='path' exec 'program' word '<manifest>' '<root>' "$@"`, quoting every path and any argument outside `[A-Za-z0-9-_./=:,@%+]`. `description`, `owner` and `namespace` set the manifest description that marks the proxy as the host's, the name in its errors, and the prefix of extension request IDs, CDP `session_id`s (`<namespace>:<session>`), the `<namespace>-no-turn` turn and temporary manifest names. `install(root, label, automatic)` works only on macOS, needs the extension installed, accepts only an OpenAI manifest that `official` verifies (stdio, an official origin, an executable that on macOS carries OpenAI's TeamIdentifier 2DC432GLL2 and Identifier extension-host), saves it once, and an explicit install clears `automatic`; `reconcile_all` rewrites the proxy after an OpenAI update, saving the new original, and leaves foreign hosts alone with an error; `release` and `uninstall_all` restore the saved original, or Chrome's when the browser had none, only while the proxy manifest is still the host's. A browser without an OpenAI registration is proxied to Chrome's and, with none anywhere, the host is the extension's only one and answers its `ping` itself (`standalone_reply`). `Bridge::connect(manifest, browser, args)` is the launcher's entry: it checks the registration and origin, starts the original host with the browser's arguments, forwards its frames both ways (`read_body`, `write_body`; 8 MiB in, 1 MiB for the host's own frames) through one stdout writer thread, and for the official origins sends `getInfo`; `Connection::controls` says the extension answered with its ID, `owned` that the registration is still the host's, `step(&mut Tabs, session, turn, input)` runs one browser action (`open` with `createTab`, `snapshot`, `click`, `type`, `press`, `scroll`, `navigate`, `close`) over `executeCdp` with a 15 s reply deadline and returns a summary and `{tab,url,title,snapshot,screenshot?}`, and `finish` waits for the browser to disconnect so OpenAI's host keeps serving it. Each tab remembers the turn that last used it, and `Connection::name_session(&mut Tabs, session, turn, title)`, `end_turn(&mut Tabs, session, turn)` and `end_idle(&mut Tabs, idle)` manage a session's tabs over their lifetime: `name_session` sends the extension `nameSession` with `group_title(title)` (one line, cut to `GROUP_TITLE_MAX` = 32 characters with an ellipsis, because Chrome shows a group's whole title on its chip; none for a blank title, when the extension keeps its own "ChatGPT") whenever it changed, so the session's tab group is named after it, `end_turn` sends `turnEnded`, which closes the tabs that turn used last as the extension does after ChatGPT's own turns, and forgets them, and `end_idle` ends every turn without a step for the host's `idle` policy, for turns whose end the host may never hear about. tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand names a thread's group after its title, relays its broker's turn ends and ends turns idle for 10 minutes; before, the extension kept every tab open under "ChatGPT". `snapshot(nodes, offset)` renders a CDP accessibility tree as an indented outline of at most 30,000 characters with `[ref=eN]` on actionable or focusable nodes, which `click`, `type` and `scroll` resolve per tab and session, and `[url=…]` (500 characters at most) on nodes with a `url` property, whose value is left out when it repeats the URL. It starts after the first `offset` lines, which every action that reads the tab takes from the input's `offset` (default 0), so a long page reads in parts: refs are numbered over the whole page, so an element keeps its ref in every part, and only the refs shown resolve; a first line says how many lines are above, or that the offset is past the end and how many lines the snapshot has, and the cut note names the line it stopped after and the offset that reads on. It used to take only the nodes and always start at the top. tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand keeps its own policy on top: which browser it opts in automatically, `pyramid-hand chatgpt proxy|disable` and its browser socket. Any native-messaging host uses the same pieces: `NativeHost::new(name, launcher).description(text).extension(id)` builds a stdio manifest (`manifest()`) that allows `extension_origin(id)` (`chrome-extension://<id>/`) for each extension, `register(base)` writes it to `manifest_path(root, name)` (`<root>/NativeMessagingHosts/<name>.json`) in every `browser_dirs(base)` directory that exists and returns those browsers' names, and `unregister(base)` removes only the manifests whose `path` is still that launcher (`owns`), so a registration another installation wrote since stays; `host_path(root)` is `manifest_path(root, HOST_NAME)`. `read_body` and `write_body` frame messages with the native-endian 4-byte length Chrome specifies (they used little-endian, the same bytes on every supported target). tempoxyz/pyramid's Hand registers its own `xyz.tempo.pyramid_hand` host for the Pyramid extension and relays its stdio frames with these.
- `nanocode-sandbox` (integrations/sandbox, Linux only, an empty library elsewhere) runs a host's command confined to read-only paths, one writable scratch directory and network only through a per-call egress proxy, the plugin sandbox tempoxyz/pyramid's organization tools use. `egress::Egress::tcp(allowlist)` (loopback port) or `Egress::unix(path, allowlist)` (a socket a namespace sandbox mounts, removed on drop) serves HTTP `CONNECT` tunnels and absolute-form plain HTTP only to `Allowlist::hosts` (exact or `*.domain`, `host_matches`), answering any other host with 403 and the body `Allowlist::refusal(host)` returns, and records refused and unreachable hosts (`denied()`, `unreachable()`). It serves at most 1,024 connections at once and answers any more with 503 rather than queueing them behind open tunnels, and drops a client that sends no complete request head within 30 s, outside a tunnel or inside an intercepted one. A host with an `Injection` (header name a token, value without CR, LF or NUL; invalid ones are dropped) is intercepted: the proxy terminates TLS with a leaf from its in-memory CA (`egress::Tls`, whose `ca_pem` the command must trust), opens verified TLS to the real host and sets the header on the first request with `Connection: close`; plain HTTP never gets injections. With `Allowlist::tailnet`, `.ts.net` hosts are dialled through that SOCKS5 server. Any other host is resolved once and dialled only at an address `egress::is_public` accepts (not unspecified, loopback, private, unique local, link-local, multicast or broadcast; 100.64.0.0/10 stays reachable for Tailscale), so an allowlisted name or IP literal that leads to 127.0.0.1, the cloud metadata service at 169.254.169.254 or a private network gets 403 and is recorded as unreachable, and DNS rebinding cannot change the checked address; `Allowlist::private_hosts` names the hosts that may reach such addresses. `bubblewrap::Bubblewrap` assembles `bwrap --unshare-all --die-with-parent --new-session` with `/proc`, `/dev`, a tmpfs `/tmp`, the `SYSTEM` directories read-only, the scratch directory bound and used as `HOME` and working directory, and `read`/`bind` mounts; the command forwards a loopback port to the mounted proxy socket itself. `confine::Policy { read, write, port }` is for hosts without user namespaces: `Policy::command(launcher, argument, program)` starts the host's own binary as `<argument> <policy JSON> <program>` in its own process group with a clear environment, and the host calls `confine::launch_if_requested(argument)` first thing in `main`, which applies Landlock ABI 6 (read and execute `SYSTEM` and `read`, list any directory, read its own `/proc/<pid>`, the safe devices, full access to `write`, TCP connect only to `port`, no bind, scoped signals and abstract sockets) and a seccomp filter (only plain IPv4/IPv6 TCP sockets, no TCP Fast Open, `io_uring`, `listen`, `accept`, `setsid`, `setpgid` or x32 calls), then execs the program or exits 126; `confine::check()` fails when the kernel lacks ABI 6. `PROXY_VARIABLES` names the proxy environment variables to point at the egress.
- The default CUA provider is ChatGPT.app's `@oai/cua-repl` launcher with `CUA_REPL_ENABLED_SURFACES=browser,computer`. Its tools/list catalog is pinned in integrations/computer/src/repl/catalog.json, so its tools and prompt prefixes exist at session build and stay identical across sessions, and a session whose live catalog differs fails the call before dispatching it; regenerate the file from a live tools/list when the provider changes. Form elicitations get the answer upstream Codex gives under danger-full-access with the never approval policy (codex-rs/core/src/session/mcp.rs): accept with empty content when no fields are requested, decline otherwise. Every call sends `_meta["openai/confirmation_policies"]` with "No confirmation policy applies." for computer_use and browser_use, which replaces the upstream Confirmations Policy document; upstream restores it for blank text, so the override must stay non-blank.
- Hosts with an MCP computer provider of their own configure `ComputerConfig::mcp(executable)` (its `args` and `environment` on top of the inherited `PROVIDER_ENVIRONMENT`). `ComputerConfig::catalog(tools, check)` pins its catalog, so `ComputerTools::connect` starts no process and each session starts the provider on its first call; `CatalogCheck::Exact` (as `installed` pins the ChatGPT.app catalog) refuses calls while the live tools/list differs, and `CatalogCheck::Names` only requires every pinned name, for hosts that show the model a tool of their own and call `ComputerTools::tool(name)`. Without a pinned catalog `connect` still starts the provider once to discover it. `startup_timeout` bounds start, initialize and discovery (120 s by default; nothing is dispatched when it runs over), and `step_timeout` bounds each `tools/call` (unlimited by default). A call that runs over it or whose transport fails after it was sent stops that session's provider and fails with `OutcomeUnknown` (`TimedOut` or `Transport`, boxed in the `ToolError` for hosts to downcast), and a provider that answers with a JSON-RPC error keeps serving the session. The session then refuses every call but `js_reset` until a reset succeeds, as before, unless `restart_after_failure()` is set, in which case its next call starts a new provider; Pyramid's native Hand uses it with `Pyramid --computer mcp` (a 10 s startup and 45 s step timeout under its broker's 60 s deadline), whose `computer` tool reports the unknown outcome to the model itself. The builder methods `args`, `env(name, value)` and `inherit_env_prefix(prefix)` (this process's variables whose names start with it, read when called) set them, and `ProviderTool::named(name)` is a catalog entry with only a name and an object input schema, for `CatalogCheck::Names`.
- Codex defaults to store: false for every authentication mode and gateway, since every mode supports it, Zero Data Retention organizations treat store as false anyway, and OpenAI keeps stored responses for 30 days in the organization that owns the key (Cloudflare's for AI Gateway Unified Billing). WebSocket sessions continue responses from the connection's memory and HTTPS sessions replay the full history. `.store(true)` opts an API-key session into HTTPS continuation with `previous_response_id` where the endpoint can continue stored responses: the OpenAI API, Vercel and Cloudflare's provider-native OpenAI gateway. The builder rejects it for ChatGPT authentication, which upstream Codex never stores (openai/codex ea01a5ffe2, 5775174ec2), for OpenRouter and for Cloudflare's unified Responses API, which replaces response IDs and rejects them as `previous_response_id`, and Chat Completions gateways stay stateless. A `previous_response_not_found` reply retries at once as a full replay. Storage is the slow part, not continuation: in a 12-round interleaved A/B of a 37 KB Sol request through AI Gateway on 2026-10-19 (`output/store-responses/binding-probe/ab2.js`), median first bytes were 790 ms for a store: false full replay, 1009 ms for the same replay stored, 892 ms for a stored continuation and 846 ms for a store: false continuation of a stored response; continuation saves request bytes but no tokens, since cached input tokens matched.
- `OpenAiBuilder::host_transport` routes Codex Responses requests through a host `HostTransport`, so external crates can own authorization, such as MPP payment or a Worker's private egress, without a native reqwest client; native host transports are HTTPS-only, and a `ChatGateway` sends its Chat Completions requests through the host transport when one is set. A host transport forwards requests the client already built: `HostConnectRequest::http_headers` and `socket_headers` carry the Responses and WebSocket handshake headers, with every gateway `headers()` entry on HTTPS and `Authorization: Bearer` only when `sends_bearer_token()` is true, `HostConnectionMetadata::from_response` and `HostError::rejected` read the upstream response, including Retry-After on a rejection, and `host::send_http` serves `HostTransport::http` through a `nanocode_http::layer::Sender`. `OpenAiAuth::gateway_managed()` accepts a host transport or an HTTP layer in place of an authenticating Cloudflare gateway, and `OpenAiAuth::host_managed_chatgpt()` is its ChatGPT-mode equivalent for transports that inject the subscription credential and account headers: it sends no token and reports a 401 that survives the host's own recovery as login required. Claude's `transport::SenderTransport` adapts the same `Sender` for `ClaudeClient::with_transport`, so a host that implements that one HTTP contract serves both backends.
- Codex HTTPS Responses requests fail with a retryable timeout when the response headers, a body chunk or an HTTP layer's pending stream-event work wait more than 300 s, upstream Codex's `stream_idle_timeout_ms` default (codex-rs `DEFAULT_STREAM_IDLE_TIMEOUT_MS`). The body reads of a Responses stream and of a `ChatGateway` stream share one `transport::idle::IdleTimer` per stream: each read computes its own deadline (the read's start plus the timeout, as `tokio::time::timeout` did), and the stream's single `Sleep` is reset only when it fires before that deadline, so a stream registers a timer about once per timeout instead of inserting and removing one in Tokio's timer wheel, behind a shard lock, for every chunk; `sample` showed that lock (`lock_slow` under `Handle::reregister` and `clear_entry`) as most of the contended time with 200 concurrent streams. With 200-delta replies (`pool` binary against the streaming mock, 4,000 sessions 200 at a time, 3 runs) user CPU fell from 4.77-5.04 s to 4.22-4.24 s. reqwest's own `read_timeout`, which `OpenAiBuilder::http_timeouts` and `ClaudeClient::messages_api` set, still resets its timer on every chunk. An `OpenAiBuilder::http_layer` also carries the standalone web search and image generation tools' requests, through `nanocode_http::execute`, so a paying or authenticating layer covers every OpenAI endpoint the agent reaches.
- An `OpenAi` built without `http_client` creates its default `reqwest::Client` on first use and shares it, like a given one, with every Responses service made from it or its clones: its sessions, subagents, side questions and the client a cancelled turn rebuilds. Each of those used to build its own client, so each opened its own connection: against a local Responses mock on 2026-10-26 (`output/hand-wiring`, `pool` binary), 20 sessions run one after another from one `OpenAi` opened 20 connections before and 1 after, and 64 sessions run 16 at a time opened 64 before and 11 to 13 after. A new connection costs DNS, TCP and TLS, which `curl` measured at 25 to 90 ms to api.openai.com and chatgpt.com from this machine. reqwest is built without its `http2` feature, so the shared pool holds one HTTP/1.1 connection per concurrent request and never queues requests behind an HTTP/2 stream limit. Hosts that build an `OpenAi` or `ClaudeClient` per turn, such as tempoxyz/pyramid's engine with `http_timeouts` and `messages_api`, still open a new pool each time and should build them once and clone them.
- Codex live settings validate the selected model's capabilities before changing its defaults, including service tiers while idle, so a refused update leaves subsequent turns and checkpoints unchanged. `set_thinking` affects later turns: direct Sol, Terra and Astra retain the surviving context's request-level reasoning baseline and append `configuration_update` items for effort changes; Luna and gateway requests carry the selected effort in `reasoning.effort`. Checkpoints retain both the selected effort and the baseline with its authored updates.
- Codex raw `api.event` events are off unless `OpenAiBuilder::raw_api_events(true)` asks for them. With them on, every Responses stream event is scanned for credentials, copied and published: a one-tool Codex Luna turn on 2026-10-22 delivered 43 `api.event` events beside its 35 others (`output/tool-input-20261022`). The TUI, gakonst/nanocode-ts and tempoxyz/xframes all turned them off, while tempoxyz/pyramid's engine received every one and discarded it.
- Each Codex agent opens its Responses WebSocket in the background when its driver starts, with a connect-only attempt that sends no request and is never retried; the first model call queues behind it on the connection actor and reuses the socket. A connect rejected with 426 activates the run's shared HTTPS fallback before it releases the connection actor, so queued calls start on HTTPS instead of opening another WebSocket; other connect failures leave the model call its full WebSocket retry budget.
- Codex WebSockets: check a cached socket's liveness before choosing incremental input, replace a closed one with a full replay without consuming a model retry, and replace a socket that closes during dispatch within the attempt only when the pump proves the request was not sent. Connections are renewed before dispatch at the documented 60-minute lifetime (`RESPONSES_WEBSOCKET_LIFETIME`, checked again before sending), and a `websocket_connection_limit_reached` error as the first response event renews within the attempt with a full replay and no retry or backoff (https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/websocket-mode); other errors keep ordinary failure handling. `websocket_warmup(true)` opts into the `generate: false` warmup, which on gpt-6-luna added 11.4K input tokens per session and moved first output from 2.05 s to 2.51 s, against 1.71 s with the background connect. Side questions send the latest request's effort and service tier from `ModelPromptCache`, because Responses prompt caching is partitioned by effort. ChatGPT-auth HTTPS bodies are zstd level 3, as upstream Codex compresses them, and WebSockets offer `permessage-deflate; client_no_context_takeover` through transport/deflate.rs, because tungstenite 0.30 has no extension support.
- Codex ChatGPT auth refreshes run through a `SingleFlight` whose spawned task outlives a cancelled caller, so a rotated refresh token is never discarded, and across processes under the auth file's `.lock` sidecar, which the blocking save releases only after the atomic rename and which is never unlinked. Managed state is an `ArcSwap` snapshot, so request paths decode no JWT and take no lock, and the stored file is reread after the token response so a concurrent external change is not overwritten.
- Responses gateways serve Sol, Terra, Luna and Astra, and `ResponsesGateway::vercel()` also serves GLM-5.3, Kimi K3 and MiMo V2.6 Pro under the Vercel AI Gateway's vendor slugs (`zai/glm-5.3`, `moonshotai/kimi-k3`, `xiaomi/mimo-v2.6-pro`); elsewhere route GLM, Kimi and MiMo through `ChatGateway`, which is exclusive with a Responses gateway and runs on both targets: natively over its own reqwest client, or through a configured host transport's `http` on native and WASM. Without a timer on WASM its stall timeout applies only natively, as the Responses host path has no idle timeout there. `ChatGateway::workers_ai(account_id)` serves GLM-5.3 as `@cf/zai-org/glm-5.3` at `https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/accounts/<id>/ai/v1/chat/completions`: on 2026-10-21 Workers AI's Messages endpoint answered "Anthropic Messages API is not supported" for that model and its Responses endpoint failed schema validation, while Chat Completions streamed tool calls whose continuation fragments carry null `id` and `name` and whose last chunk carries the cumulative usage, which the translator accepts unchanged; the Workers AI Durable Object demo (gakonst/nanocode-cloudflare-durable) ran a `charge` tool turn and a follow-up that recalled it through its AI Gateway binding's `workers-ai` provider. A gateway's model ID prefix follows the model: `ModelConfig::retarget`, which both builders' `model` and subagent and restore branches call, replaces a prefix the gateway assigned with the one it assigns the new model and keeps an explicit `model_id_prefix`, and `prefixed_model_id` names GLM-5.3 `glm-5.3` under a prefix, since its own ID is the Workers AI path. Under a Responses gateway the built-in web.run and image_gen adapters return an unsupported result before sending anything, and Codex inline compaction works only through Cloudflare's provider-native proxy. Under a Responses gateway, GLM, Kimi, MiMo and custom models (`ResponsesGateway::sends_custom_tools_as_functions`, the models that get the GLM system prompt) receive every freeform (`custom`) tool, Code Mode's `exec` included, as a function tool with one required string parameter `input` whose description adds the freeform hint and grammar Chat Completions gateways use (`transport/freeform.rs`, shared with `chat.rs`), in `AdditionalTools` items and inside namespaces; replayed `custom_tool_call` and `custom_tool_call_output` items go out as `function_call` (arguments `{"input":…}`) and `function_call_output`, and the stream turns those models' `function_call` items, `function_call_arguments` deltas (decoded from the partial JSON string) and done events and `response.completed` output for a declared freeform tool back into `custom_tool_call` events before decoding, so the harness sees the same `CustomToolCall` as with OpenAI models, whose requests stay unchanged. On 2026-10-25 the Vercel AI Gateway's `/v1/responses` silently dropped a `{"type":"custom","name":"exec","format":{"type":"text"}}` tool for `zai/glm-5.3` and `moonshotai/kimi-k3` (the model answered that it had no exec tool and called `wait` with invented cell IDs), while the same tool as a function with a required string parameter returned `function_call exec {"code":"console.log(42);"}` for both; `openai/gpt-6-luna` handled both forms.
- The Claude backend serves any non-empty model ID. `claude_selection` maps Claude model IDs and aliases to `ClaudeModel` and keeps any other ID as a Claude-family `ModelSelection` with the generic capabilities, so a host can send open-weight models through an Anthropic Messages gateway: on 2026-10-21 nanocode-ts's Vercel adapter ran `zai/glm-5.3` and `moonshotai/kimi-k3` through the Vercel AI Gateway's `/v1/messages` with the Claude harness, and turns calling `bash` and `read_file` completed. A model the SDK does not know needs an explicit `max_tokens` (`ClaudeBuilder::max_tokens`, or `ClaudeHarness::configure` in a runtime), since `model_max_tokens` lists only Claude limits; the Vercel AI Gateway's catalog lists 1,000,000 for GLM-5.3 and 131,072 for Kimi K3. The agent's own fast mode, service tier and restored-checkpoint checks read its model as a `ClaudeModel` and skip any other ID, as they did before `ModelSelection`'s parser turned unknown IDs into custom Codex models, which made `build`, `set_fast_mode` and checkpoint restores refuse fast mode on a gateway ID with Codex capabilities.
- Claude turns, side questions, subagent listings, `run.completed` and the `agent.turn` span carry list-price estimates as Codex ones do. `ClaudeModel::estimate_cost` prices one model call's `ClaudeTokens` at the rates on https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing (read 2026-10-22): base input and output, 5-minute cache writes at 1.25x input, 1-hour writes at 2x input and cache hits at 0.1x input (0.05x for Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5, 0.025x for Fable 5.1 and Mythos 5.1); Opus 5.5 fast mode at $8 and $40 per million with the caching multipliers on top, as the page states, and no estimate for fast mode on other models; and Haiku 5.5 at its long-prompt rates when a request's prompt (input, cache reads and cache writes) exceeds 100,000 tokens, the only model whose long prompts cost more. The backend prices each response in `State::response` from the request's model, speed and cache TTL, splitting cache writes by the response's `usage.cache_creation` and by the requested TTL when a gateway omits it, so the long-prompt rate applies per request rather than to a turn's sum, and `add_usage` sums the estimates into the turn's usage, which the durable cursor keeps. A provider-reported `usage.cost`, such as the MPP proxies send, still wins (`CostStatus::ReportedByProvider`); otherwise the turn reports `EstimatedFromUsage`, and a model outside `ClaudeModel`, such as GLM-5.3 or Kimi K3 through a Messages gateway, stays unpriced. `EstimatedUsdCost::new`, `UsdAmount::saturating_add` and `UsdAmount::saturating_mul` are public so hosts can price their own models the same way.
- `ClaudeModel::Fable55` (`claude-fable-5-5`, "Claude Fable 5.5") was added on 2026-10-27, before its release, as a copy of Fable 5.1: the same prices, 128,000 max output tokens, 1,000,000-token context window, 2576 px image resolution, thinking efforts and default, adaptive thinking with `block_binding` and adaptive-low context recovery. None of it comes from Anthropic's documentation, which did not list the model yet, so check each against its model and pricing pages once it ships. The `fable` alias resolved to Fable 5.5 until Fable 5.2 shipped.
- `ClaudeModel::Fable52` (`claude-fable-5-2`, "Claude Fable 5.2") and `ClaudeModel::Mythos52` (`claude-mythos-5-2`, "Claude Mythos 5.2", the same model for organizations verified through Anthropic's verification programs) follow their overview, what's-new, effort, fast mode and pricing pages, read on 2026-11-09, the day they shipped: 128,000 max output tokens, a 1,000,000-token context window, 2576 px image resolution, adaptive thinking always on with `block_binding` and adaptive-low context recovery, low through max effort with the API default high, per-message effort updates and fast mode. They cost $8 input, $40 output, $10 and $16 for 5-minute and 1-hour cache writes and $0.20 for cache reads per million tokens, and $16 and $80 in fast mode with the caching multipliers on top. The `fable` and `mythos` aliases resolve to them; `claude-fable-5-5` answered 404 `model: claude-fable-5-5` on 2026-11-09 and stays reachable only by its ID. Opus 5.5 stays the default. A Claude plan serves them only to Claude Code 2.1.290 and later: on 2026-11-09 `nanocode exec -m claude-fable-5-2` on a plan, with the wire then claiming 2.1.283, answered 404 `model: claude-fable-5-2`, with `PI_AI_CLAUDE_CODE_VERSION=2.1.289` 400 `claude_code_version_too_old` ("version 2.1.290 or newer is required"), and with 2.1.290, 2.1.304 and 2.1.321 the reply, while Fable 5.1 and Opus 5.5 answered at 2.1.283. The wire has claimed 2.1.304 since, and on it `-m fable`, Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1 answered on the plan without the override; that plan has no Mythos access, and both Mythos models answered 404 there at any version (`output/fable-5-2`).
- `Model::Terra` (`gpt-6.1-terra`, alias `terra`, "GPT-6.1 Terra") follows openai/codex's catalog (`codex-rs/models-manager/models.json` at 126a0ee92ed2, #57293) and OpenAI's model and pricing pages, read on 2026-10-31. Its `instructions_template` is byte for byte Astra's (SHA-256 35bd51b5…, 21,428 bytes), so it takes `prompts/astra.md`, and `prompts/upstream.json` stays pinned at the revision before Terra. Like Sol it is in `Model::ALL` with Codex's low default effort, `configuration_update` effort changes, fast mode, Pro and the 872,000-token ceiling; unlike Sol it accepts `none` (the API takes none through max) and has no ultrafast tier, which falls back to fast as Luna's does. Prices per million tokens are $1 input, $0.05 cached, $1.25 cache writes (30-minute retention) and $5 output, fast mode 2x, and over 272K input tokens 2x input and cache and 1.5x output. Codex's catalog gives Terra a 500,000-token default context window where its other models have 272,000; sessions here keep `CONTEXT_WINDOW_TOKENS`, since the SDK has no per-model default window.
- `ClaudeBuilder::environment(ClaudeEnvironment)` describes the machine a Claude agent's tools run on instead of the one building it: Claude Code's `# Environment` section with the working directory, git repository, platform, shell and OS version the host gives (each line only when given), today's date unless the host gives one, and project instructions in the `# AGENTS.md instructions` block, replacing the brain's `uname`, `$SHELL` and AGENTS.md discovery. It applies to the agent and, through the builder recipe, its subagents.
- `ExecutionEnvironment` describes the machine a Codex agent's tools run on, as `ClaudeBuilder::environment` does for Claude: `working_directory` and `shell` replace the brain's workspace path and `$SHELL` in the `<environment_context>` the model sees, `timezone` names the zone, and `ExecutionEnvironment::dated(timezone, clock)` takes the date from a host callback on every turn instead of the fixed string `ExecutionEnvironment::new` keeps, so a session that outlives a day reports the new date to the model as a context update. An empty working directory or shell is an invalid request, as an empty date or timezone already was, and a configured environment skips canonicalizing the workspace, which names a path on the host's machine.
- `nanocode::claude::default_context_window_tokens(model)` is the context window a Claude session assumes for a model ID (1,000,000 for the Claude 5 families, 200,000 otherwise), and `nanocode::codex::CONTEXT_WINDOW_TOKENS` with `Model::max_context_window_tokens` and `nanocode::codex::auto_compact_token_limit(model, window)` give a Codex session's default window (272,000, capped at the model's maximum), its ceiling and the token count at which it compacts on its own (90% of the window: 244,800 for Sol, Terra, Luna, Astra and Daybreak Blue, `None` for models without auto-compaction). Daybreak Blue had none until 2026-11-01, so its full-history replay grew until OpenAI refused every request, and tempoxyz/pyramid set Sol's limit on every Daybreak Blue turn (`docs/backend/modes.md`). Hosts that list models with their limits, such as nanocode-ts's `models()`, read them from the SDK instead of keeping copies that drift from what a session enforces.
- A Codex `SessionSnapshot` records its reasoning mode beside `thinking` and `service_tier`, and `SessionCheckpoint::codex` takes the checkpoint's mode from it. `CodexBuilder::resume_native_snapshot` adopts the snapshot's mode unless the host called `reasoning_mode` first, and `ChildState::validate` rejects a checkpoint whose mode differs from its conversation's, so a resumed session or a restored subagent child keeps Pro and the two records of one session cannot disagree. Snapshots written before the field existed have no mode and resume as Standard.
- Claude requests: build each Messages request once with `ClaudeClient::prepare_wire` and send those bytes on every attempt; subscription bodies stay byte-identical to `subscription_wire::body`, and history is Arc-shared `HistoryMessage` mutated only through `make_mut`. Native requests to api.anthropic.com gzip bodies of at least 4096 bytes at level 2 with 0-256 spaces or tabs of padding, as Claude Code 2.1.283 does, and a 400, 403 or 415 without an Anthropic `request-id` or `cf-ray` header, or a 400 reporting invalid JSON, resends uncompressed and turns gzip off for the client; level 2 took a 3.08 MB body to 819 KB in 19 ms (level 6: 725 KB in 82 ms). Host transports and WASM send identity bodies. Attempts back off min(500 ms × 2^(attempt-1), 32 s) plus up to 25% jitter derived from the call and attempt, as Claude Code 2.1.299 does; a longer Retry-After wins and a delay over 60 s stops retrying. An attempt that streamed only thinking is retried like one that streamed nothing; once it streams reply text the call fails instead, and a retryable failure leaves a durable turn pending for its host to resume (`Settlement::FailAttempt`), as Codex does, on every target. Before 2026-10-21 only Code Mode agents, which native builds always are, left it pending, so on WASM a stream that ended without message_stop failed its turn for good: an xframes Opus remix lost a 316 s thinking stream to a Durable Object code update, then failed when the replayed call's stream ended early after 115 s. Against a mock Messages endpoint that ends the first stream after thinking or after text, a local xframes Worker failed both turns with "Messages stream ended before message_stop" before and completed them after, the thinking drop retried in the same call 0.64 s later and the text drop on the host's recovery 3 s later. Root sessions on the subscription wire use the 1-hour cache TTL, as Claude Code 2.1.299 does, subagents and side questions keep the 5-minute default, and compaction keeps the turn's system prompt, tools and tool choice so the cached prefix survives.
- Claude durability: model step inputs are `{"wire","digest","bytes","conversation","prefix"}` digests of the identity body (since 2026-11-04; `PreparedWire::durable_input`), and steps recorded by older builds match, before replay, the input they would have recorded for this body: the whole body's blake3 with the same conversation and prefix fields (`body_durable_input`, the format until then), the whole body's blake3 alone, its sha256, and then the legacy full input. On the Messages API wire `conversation` is the blake3 of the messages' own digests, each the blake3 of a message's JSON without its `cache_control` members, which `HistoryMessage` computes once and keeps beside its cached JSON (`WireMessage::digests`, `MessageDigests`), so a request hashes 64 bytes per message plus the messages it has not sent before, however long the history; the subscription wire, whose last message may carry a cache marker the history lacks, and steps an older build recorded keep the blake3 of the messages' text without markers (`same_conversation` accepts either, and `stream_durable_input` is the format of 2026-11-04 until this change); `prefix` is the blake3 of the body outside the messages with the system prompt's and the tools' JSON replaced by their own blake3 and position, and `digest` the blake3 of the prefix, the conversation and the messages' digests as sent (with their markers). A request used to hash its whole body twice (once whole, once in the conversation and prefix digests), and then, from earlier on 2026-11-04, its messages once. `PreparedWire` holds its body as an `Arc<String>`, which the native transport sends without copying, where converting the built `String` into an `Arc<str>` copied it. With output/hand-wiring's `overhead` (one Claude agent against an in-process mock with no latency, an in-memory store, each round an `exec` cell making one nested call; 2026-11-04, interleaved, load average about 200), a round after a 4 MB prompt took 1.1 and 1.4 ms and 1.9 and 2.0 ms of CPU instead of 3.9 and 4.6 ms and 4.8 and 4.9 ms, and after a 1 MB prompt 0.64 and 0.84 ms instead of 1.4 and 1.8 ms; rounds with short histories cost what they did (about 1.1 ms and 1.2 ms of CPU at 1,000 rounds). A `HistoryMessage` also keeps how many images it holds and whether a tool result in it carries companion blocks to separate, so a request's image eviction, image inlining and companion separation read one cached value per message instead of walking every block (each block's image list was a `Vec`), and the agent's set of nested calls already reported (`LiveNestedStarts`, up to 4,096 call IDs, changed through an `Atom`) is a persistent `imbl` set and vector, where each nested call's start and receipt cloned all of it: at 3,000 rounds a round used 1.79 and 1.80 ms of CPU instead of 2.06 and 2.07 ms. The system prompt and tools are encoded once per distinct value: `WireCache` (`ClaudeClient::prepare_cached_wire`, which each agent passes its own) keeps the JSON, blake3 and xxh64 fingerprint of the tools and system prompt its agent sent last and finds a live encoding of an equal value that another agent made (a process-wide list of weak references, so agents with the same catalog share one encoding and none outlives the agents that use it); `model.call` spans read their `prompt.tools` and `prompt.system` fingerprints from it and `prompt.first_message` from the message's cached encoding, the same values as before, where each request serialized the tools and system prompt three times (body and two fingerprints) and the first message once more. At 5 x 2,000 children on SQLite (2026-11-04, two interleaved runs at load average 166 to 186) the swarm used 29.2 and 27.8 s of user CPU instead of 31.1 and 30.0 s; output/hand-wiring's `wirecheck` found cached and uncached bodies and inputs equal, with a container naming `"tools":` and system prompts as a string, as blocks and absent. A turn's `Journal` defers cursor advances and step completions into the next step admission through `commit_writes`, so flush it before any durable step outside the journal. When the turn ends, `run_locked` hands its remaining writes to `ClaudeExecutionPolicy::defer_writes` (doc(hidden); the default commits them, the SDK's policy applies them with `DurableOwner::advance_deferred` and `complete_step_deferred_encoded`), so they commit with the turn's settlement, as Codex defers its final model call: a crash between the final response and the settlement runs that model call again instead of replaying it. The final model call's completion used to be a commit of its own, so on 2026-10-28 a Code Mode turn in nanocode-ts's `bench/hands.ts` made 5 store transactions instead of 6, the Vercel demo's two-turn drill 12 replacements instead of 14, and the drill (a process killed mid-charge and recovered) and nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `measure-faults.mjs` (basic, evictModel, evictAfterTool, faults, chaos, cancelFinish, cancelRace and concurrent on both providers, 900 checks) passed. Cursors store `pending_base` plus the pending tail after the prefix shared with the snapshot.
- Claude requests that think adaptively, on turns and in Opus 5.5, Fable 5.1 and Mythos 5.1 context recovery, set `thinking.block_binding.prefix_mismatch_behavior` to `drop_block`, and `ClaudeClient` adds the `thinking-binding-controls-2026-08-01` beta to any request whose `thinking` carries `block_binding`, on every auth path and the subscription wire, because the API rejects the field without it. Fable 5.1, Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 bind each thinking block to the system prompt, tools and earlier messages it was produced under, and for accounts created on or after 2026-08-31 answer a replay under a different prefix with a 400 that no retry clears (`The block is bound to a different conversation`); a session forked into another store, a tool list that changes when a runner attaches or detaches, and changed per-model instructions all replay such blocks. With `drop_block` the API removes the failing block and every later thinking block and the turn continues without that reasoning, which the model may spend output tokens re-creating; on older accounts, which pass such blocks through unless the field is set, the same blocks are now dropped. Models that do not check the prefix, such as Mythos 5.1, accept the field. Haiku 5.5 rejects it with `disabled` thinking and Sonnet 5.5 with `between_tools`, so `disabled` requests and Sonnet 5.5's context recovery are sent without it. A turn resumes with the request template recorded when it started, so a turn an older build started keeps its requests unchanged.
- Follow Tact's Code Mode, recursive delegation and subagent business logic, using clabby/tact revision cb7e7997bdc895c5116e4fd8d49ff38e844b711f as the reference for this port. Retain this SDK's durable task tree, instruction revisions, accepted-result handling, cancellation, residency and checkpoint recovery.
- Default subagent admission requires at least Low reasoning effort and registered-parent effort bounds. Any agent may spawn any model the policy offers, whatever its own model or provider, as clabby/tact#292 decided: there is no model ladder. Apply explicit live runtime caps to new spawns without rewriting existing child settings.
- Authorize a requested Pro mode before resolving unsupported target models to Standard. Record the actual mode, bound descendants by that mode, and include it in spawn/list/wait/roster reports. Preserve mode across child creation and checkpoints; older records without a mode default to Standard and retain their Standard serialization.
- Append effective model, effort and mode context to each new child turn, including deferred and delegated follow-ups. Replay accepted durable prompts with their existing instruction revisions. AgentContext may also wrap root or auxiliary prompts without discarding transcript or media content.
- Keep existing spawn inheritance, typed output_contract and legacy output_schema entry points. Do not replace caller-controlled message, concurrency, residency or wait policies with Tact's fixed limits. Do not introduce new routing policy as part of copying Tact business logic.
- Preserve native and WASM parity for agents, subagents, durability, and shared runtime contracts, including existing spawn/events, cancellation, and checkpoint/resume behavior. Keep host dependencies behind Rust platform interfaces; support WASM-local futures. Validate affected crate builds on native and WASM targets.
- Keep the dev loop fast: build only what changed, reuse caches, and avoid redundant checks. Honor requests to skip builds or CI.
- Keep routine Cargo checks focused on the default SDK member. Compile TUI components and binaries explicitly when they change.
- Keep model, usage and error values in core/agent-types, independent of lifecycle code and event channels. Keep stable agent lifecycle contracts in core/agent, generic assembly and ownership in runtime/agent, and native defaults and runner lifecycle in integrations/agent-host. Keep public generic builders thin and delegate substantial work to concrete implementations.
- Hosts that drive durable turns by request ID classify a finished prompt with `AgentRuntime::durable_outcome`: a prompt that returned its message is `Completed`, and a failed one is classified from the configured store as `Completed` by replaying a turn the store records as completed to recover its final message, `Failed` or `Cancelled` once the store records it, `Waiting` while an execution policy asks to retry later, or `Interrupted` when the host must reopen the runtime from its checkpoint. `pending_turns` lists a session's pending request IDs, `durable_prompt` decodes a turn's stored input for resuming it, and `settle` reports a root quiescent with no pending turns after persisting its subagents, comparing activity revisions across the persist. A host that queues prompts before a runtime opens checks each with `admit_prompt`, which returns a `PromptRejection` for a request ID with the `COMPLETION_REQUEST_PREFIX` the subagent runtime uses for its own continuation turns and for a requested harness that differs from the session's recorded one.
- A turn keeps running when its caller drops the turn, the prompt call that admitted it or every handle to its agent: both backends finish and settle the running turn and the turns accepted behind it before the agent stops, so none stays pending until the session is reopened. `shutdown` instead stops the running turn, releases its claim and leaves it pending for the next owner to resume. It leaves the turns queued behind it that carry a request ID pending the same way, so the next owner resumes each of them once in accepted order, and cancels queued turns without a request ID and compactions. A subagent whose turn `shutdown` stops stays running in its root's subagent journal with its in-flight evidence, so the next owner resumes it from its latest checkpoint as after a crash instead of reporting it interrupted to its parent.
- A turn's Code Mode cells end with the turn on both backends: Claude terminates them in `settle_code_turn` after every turn, and Codex terminates them when a turn completes or is cancelled (`terminate_turn_cells`; a failed turn with no tool call in flight still detaches them, so a retry can wait on them). A cell that yielded keeps running while its turn lasts, so `wait` in the same turn collects it. Upstream Codex keeps a yielded cell after a completed turn but dispatches its tool calls only while a turn's worker runs, and terminates every active cell on interrupt.
- A user's cancel of a Claude turn (not a stop or shutdown) while an `exec` or `wait` call runs no longer drops that call: `call_tool` terminates the turn's cells (`stop_code_cells`, the cleanup `settle_code_turn` runs), publishes the terminated cell's nested results, and answers the call with `Tool execution stopped by the user. The cell was terminated and no nested tool call was left running.` and status `cancelled`, or, when a nested call was still in flight (its result is `CODE_MODE_CALL_INTERRUPTED` with an unknown outcome, or it never reported one), with a message saying that call's outcome is unknown. Other tools, and every call when the agent is stopped or closing, keep `Tool execution interrupted; outcome unknown.`.
- A manual compaction is session-level work, not a turn: `AgentHandle::compact` on Claude cancels every running turn, waits until none is left, compacts the history, and emits `model.compaction.started` then `model.compaction.completed` (or `model.compaction.failed`) on the session's event stream without a `turn_id`, so the record carries no request ID, as with Codex's manual compaction. Claude has no transport attempt for its summary call, so it emits no `model.attempt.*` events, and automatic compaction inside a turn emits only `model.compaction.completed`. Hosts that reset per-history state, such as image counts, at `model.compaction.completed` see a manual Claude compaction as they see an automatic one (the TypeScript host's log held no event for a manual Claude compaction before this).
- A turn left `TurnStatus::Pending` by a retryable failure or a crash blocks different prompts with a Retry `OperationBlocked` error, and admission refuses a new operation while an unclaimed turn is pending without recording anything; a turn claimed by a live attempt or a queued admission does not block. Agents opened through AgentRuntime resume pending prompt turns in accepted order before the first new prompt, and a prompt call dropped while it resumes them hands the turns it has not resubmitted to the next one. Replaying one with `PromptRequest::new(agent.decode_durable_prompt(turn.input)?).request_id(turn.id)` reports interrupted tool calls as unknown outcomes instead of repeating them, and `.cancel_on_admission()` records it Cancelled without a model request. When two hosts open one session, the latest opener fences the other, whose turns stop without settling, and resumes the pending turns.
- Model calls classify failures one way on both backends and every transport. `nanocode::http::retry` (`nanocode_http::layer::retry`) follows Anthropic's and OpenAI's official SDKs: a response's `x-should-retry: true` or `false` decides (`should_retry`), otherwise 408, 409, 429 and every 5xx are transient (`retryable_status`), and `retry-after-ms` wins over `Retry-After`. `ClaudeError::Http`, `ResponsesError::HttpRejected` and `HandshakeRejected` and `HostError::HandshakeRejected` carry `should_retry` from every transport path, and a `HostError` from a `layer::Error` is reconnectable when the error is retryable or a timeout. A transient failure is retried in the call within the backend's budget (Claude five attempts, three without tools, stopping at a server delay over 60 s; Codex `max_attempts`, five by default), and cancelling the turn ends the wait; once the budget is spent the turn settles with `Settlement::FailAttempt`, leaving its operation pending for the host to resume (`DurableOutcome::Interrupted`, with a new budget on each resume). Only a definite answer fails the operation durably: `x-should-retry: false`, another 4xx without the header, or a terminal provider error. A Codex auto-compaction that fails transiently records no step outcome, so the resumed turn compacts again. Hosts that refuse a model request for a reason that clears, such as Pact's egress before re-authorization, answer with `x-should-retry: true` and, when known, `retry-after-ms`; a host transport that fails without a response returns `layer::Error::retryable(true)`.
- AgentRuntime serializes open, open_loaded, open_session and durable resume per session id, and an open that finds a live agent returns it with a new `Agent::subscribe` stream instead of fencing it. An adopted agent whose prompt is refused before its turn started with AgentStopped, TurnStopped or a Reopen disposition, or whose store shows another owner, rebuilds its backend through `AgentOwner::reopen` under every existing handle, resumes the pending turns in accepted order and retries once; a prompt call dropped while it heals lets the next one heal, and explicit shutdown or disconnect turns healing off.
- `SpawnBuilder::session_id(id)` names a root session a durable runtime spawns, which is otherwise a new UUIDv7, so a host can key the session by its own identifier, such as tempoxyz/pyramid's thread ID, and reopen it later with `open_session(id)` without storing a generated ID; the session's record, lineage root and durable state are all that ID. `build` refuses it with `InvalidRequest` when the runtime has no durability, and spawning an ID whose session already exists fails with `InvalidRequest`: hosts open it instead. `SessionStore::prepare_session` creates only: its first acquisition fails with `Error::SessionExists` when the stored state already has a session record, checked with a non-fencing peek before acquiring and again inside the acquisition update, so of two concurrent spawns of one ID only one creates it, though on stores whose `acquire_with` acquires before running the update (the default, which `SqlStore` and `MemoryStore` use) the losing spawn of such a race still fences the winner, which then reopens, while the JSONL store's atomic acquisition leaves the winner its owner.
- `SessionEvents::start` merges a root's agent events and subagent updates into batches for an `EventSink` and returns a pump future the host spawns. It coalesces consecutive assistant and reasoning-summary deltas of the same agent, request and item, holds a batch that ends in a delta until the caller's window since the previous delivery elapses or a drain is requested, and `drained` answers once everything received has been delivered, or with the sink's error after a delivery failed. Drain requests reach the pump over a channel, so it holds no lock. `SessionEvent` implements `Serialize` as the hosts' wire format: `{"source":"agent","event","sdk_timing"}` for agent events and `{"source":"subagent","root_session_id","detach","update"}` with a typed `update` for subagent updates.
- `SessionLog::compact_events()` makes `TurnInbox` store agent events without the copies they carry, for hosts whose logs grow with tool results, such as Pact's Durable Object. A stored root event leaves out `protocol_version` when it is 1 and `request_id` when it is the root session's ID; a subagent update's event leaves out only `protocol_version`, since its `request_id` is the child's session ID. A `tool.result` payload stores its result once, and `"derived"` names the field it left out: `structured_result` when that equals the result text (`result`, or Claude's `result.text`), or `result` (Codex) or `result.text` (Claude, which keeps `result: {}`) when the result text is the exact JSON text of `structured_result`, as `FunctionTool` and other JSON tools return; the marker is needed because unknown-outcome and cancelled results have no `structured_result` of their own. `LoggedEvent::expanded(session_id)` restores what was left out, with the result text byte for byte and objects in sorted key order; other events, and every event of a log without the option, come back unchanged. `InboxHost::publish` still receives the whole event; only `page`, `turn_page` and their bounded forms return the compact form, so a host that enables the option expands what it reads. `submit` sizes the input.accepted event in its full form, an upper bound. Assistant deltas stay, because with a 0 ms coalescing window a reply's deltas and its `assistant.message` commit in different transactions and nanocode-ts rebuilds reply text from the deltas it pages; `model.call.*` fields stay because readers take each event alone.
- `AgentEvents::try_recv` returns an event already delivered without waiting. Every event of a turn is in its streams before the turn's result resolves, so a host that drains them after the result reads them all with it; Tokio's `try_recv` spends no cooperative budget, while polling the stream with `now_or_never` does, and once a task's budget of 128 polls ran out it read a backlog as empty: tempoxyz/pyramid's engine lost final messages that way until it wrapped each poll in `tokio::task::unconstrained`.
- Agents whose host asks for it, with `OpenAiBuilder::tool_input_deltas(true)` or `ClaudeBuilder::tool_input_deltas(true)` (through `ClaudeHarness::configure` in a runtime), stream each tool call's input as `tool.input.delta` events (`ToolEvent::InputDelta`: `model_call_index`, `call_id`, `tool` and `text`) while the model writes it: Codex from `response.function_call_arguments.delta` and `response.custom_tool_call_input.delta`, which carries Code Mode's `exec` source, naming the tool as `tool.call` does through `qualified_tool_name`, and Claude from the `input_json_delta` of `tool_use` blocks. Subagents inherit the setting through the builder recipe. They coalesce like assistant deltas, per network read in the event channel and within the caller's window in `SessionEvents`, and their concatenated text is the call's arguments. They are off by default because every `AgentEvents` reader and `TurnInbox` log would otherwise receive and store every tool input twice, and readers that record unknown events would keep them: tempoxyz/pyramid's engine traces every event kind it does not skip, and it learns the text a turn types into pages or processes from `tool.call` events and replaces it whole, which the fragments of a delta would escape.
- `model.call.started` names the prompt cache its request uses. A Codex call carries `prompt_cache_key`, the key its Responses request sends (see the next item), and leaves it out on a Chat Completions gateway such as Workers AI, which sends no key. A Claude call carries `cache_ttl`, `5m` or `1h`, when its request carries cache-control markers, since Claude caches by prompt prefix and has no key; a request without markers leaves it out. The typed `ModelCallStarted` reads both, and `previous_response_id`, which Codex already sent.
- Codex's default `prompt_cache_key` is `nanocode-prefix-` and 32 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the model ID, tool definitions and base instructions (`prompt_cache::prefix_key`), derived once per session from its first request profile and kept in its checkpoint, so sessions and subagents that share a prefix share a key and a child with other tools, instructions or model gets its own. It used to be the session ID (the durable state ID), and on gpt-6-luna a new session's first call read 0 of about 11k prefix tokens from cache, because on GPT-5.6 and later the key only separates cache accounting while OpenAI routes by prefix (https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/prompt-caching, read 2026-10-25). Custom models add `-<bucket>`, the session ID's SHA-256 modulo 4, since models before GPT-5.6 route by key and overflow above about 15 requests a minute per key; the built-in GPT-6 models get no bucket, which would only split the shared prefix into more cache writes. `CodexBuilder::prompt_cache_key` sets a key and `per_session_prompt_cache_key` restores the session-ID key. A restored session keeps the key its checkpoint holds, so sessions saved before the change keep their session-ID key.
- A Codex `RequestProfile` encodes its prefix items (the `additional_tools` item and the instructions, about 35 KB) once per combination of the request flags that change their JSON (image-detail stripping, freeform tools sent as functions, effort updates) and every request writes those bytes again (`encoded_prefix`, raw JSON per item in the input array), where each request serialized and escaped them; a profile whose prefix changes (`with_request_content`) starts a new cache. Against nanocode-ts's agents-vs-nanocode mock (bin/nanocode/output/agents-vs-nanocode `mock.mjs`, output/hand-wiring `vsagents`, a native Codex agent on an in-memory SQLite store, 2026-11-04, three interleaved runs of 200 turns) a text turn used 0.88 to 0.99 ms of CPU instead of 0.93 to 1.12 ms. A committed `ResponseHistory` segment (each turn's items once `commit_tail` seals them) keeps the same encoding of its items, so a full-replay request (HTTPS, `store: false`) encodes only the items of the turn in progress and copies the rest, and `RequestInput` writes the input array without counting it first (`serialize_seq(None)`, which serde_json does not need). Request bodies stayed byte-identical apart from IDs and times (50 requests of text, cell and 100 KB-output turns captured by `mock-dump.mjs`), and 1,500 rounds of text and of one-call cells in one session took 1.15 to 1.21 ms and 5.0 to 6.9 ms of CPU a turn instead of 1.35 to 1.40 ms and 7.1 to 7.5 ms. Each committed segment also caches the totals of the history through its end (encrypted reasoning tokens, image count, first image index), computed once and iteratively from the nearest segment that has them, so `ContextManager::active_context_tokens` (which needs the reasoning before the last user turn, and the tokens after the last model output, now counted from the end) and the image eviction and inlining before each request (`ResponseHistory::images_before`) read a few segments near the end instead of every item: at about 2,500 turns these walks were 26% of a one-cell turn's CPU. A 2,000-turn session of one-call cells (`vsagents` `SHORT=1 TURNS=2000`) retired 19.5G instructions instead of 31.1G (`/usr/bin/time -l`, two runs each, with the archive change above), 300 short sessions 20.9G against 21.0G, and 28 requests of a cell returning an image every turn, with 56 images evicted, were identical apart from IDs and dates (`mock-dump.mjs`, `dumpdiff.py`). `ResponseHistoryIter` collects the segments it walks only when it is first advanced and counts what is left as it goes, so an iterator built and not read (such as the two `reconcile_history_repair` gets every model call when no effort override exists) or asked only for its length costs nothing per segment: the same session retired 18.3G instructions instead of 19.6G, with requests identical as above.
- `SessionLog` and `TurnInbox` in runtime/agent host a session's turns and events for any host that runs SQL, like `SqlStore`: the SDK owns the schema and every statement and the host only runs `Sql::transaction`. `SessionLog` owns the `turns` table and the `events` table in the nanocode Worker's layout (`seq` as the plain integer primary key, a scope column for several logs in one database). `submit` records a queued turn and its `queued` event in one transaction, returns the existing turn for the same prompt and hand, which it compares by a BLAKE3 payload digest so finished turns keep no prompt, and reports a conflict otherwise; `turn`, `pending` and `page`/`turn_page` read status, unfinished turns in admission order and events by cursor or within a turn's own `first_seq`..`last_seq` range, without opening a session; `cancel` finishes a queued turn at once and marks a running one; `interrupted` notes a recovery on a running turn; `import` adopts turns a host recorded before. A turn's result is its `result` event at `last_seq`, and `TurnRecord::report` returns it with the request ID and status. Pending lookups scan the scope's turn rows instead of keeping an index: on Durable Objects a row read costs a thousandth of a written row, and an index entry would add two writes to every turn. `TurnInbox::open` loads or creates the session and starts `SessionEvents` with a sink that encodes each event, attributes it to its request (Codex turn IDs map to the request through the `input.accepted` event that names both), appends each batch in one transaction and hands the logged events to `InboxHost::publish`. `drive` runs one root turn at a time: SDK operations still pending, in accepted order, through `durable_prompt`, then the oldest queued turn, which the SDK accepts only when it starts so `InboxHost::place` can select that turn's hand, and it records the outcome from `durable_outcome` or returns `Waiting` or `Interrupted` for the host to retry or reopen. `TurnInbox::open` leaves the reopened session's interrupted turns to `drive` (`Agent::leave_interrupted_turns_to_host`, which also covers turns a reopen after an ownership loss finds), so a prompt or subagent completion the agent routes meanwhile does not resume them on its own. `TurnInbox::cancel` also cancels the live turn or makes the next drive cancel it on admission, `TurnInbox::steer(target, id, prompt)` steers the running root turn named by `target`, or with `None` whichever root turn runs, with an identified steer, which the backend accepts once per ID, and answers false when that turn is not running, its cancellation was requested or it no longer takes steering (`TurnNotSteerable`, `TurnStopped`), so a host submits such a message as a turn instead; a host that steers only a turn it saw running, such as one whose users share a session, names it, because a steer without a target lands in whichever turn started after the host's check, `settle` reports quiescence, and `InboxHost::finishing` adds host fields such as timings to a result. `SessionLog::retry` records why an unfinished turn waits on a host it cannot reach: the error, the host's kind for it, an attempt count kept in SQL and the next retry time sit on the turn row, its report shows them as `error`, `kind`, `attempts` and `retry_at` until the turn starts or finishes, and a `retrying` event is appended only when the error or kind changes, so retries add no events. Hosts end a turn they cannot run with `finish`, and `drive` cancels on admission, instead of running, an SDK-pending turn whose log record failed or was cancelled meanwhile. The `retry` column came after the first release; a database written before storage formats were versioned gets it when `SessionLog::migrate` adopts it (see the storage format entry). An agent event is stored as `{"source":"agent","event"}` without `sdk_timing`, the host's `annotate` fields or the top-level `timing` of its payload (1.4 KB of each `model.call.completed`), while `InboxHost::publish` receives the whole event under the same seq; subagent updates are stored whole. `TurnInbox`'s futures are `Send` natively, so a native host drives it from `tokio::spawn` as gakonst/nanocode-swift does; `prompt` used to hold a `&Turn` (whose result future is not `Sync`) across the cancel of a turn cancelled while it was admitted, which made `drive` not `Send`.
- `SessionLog` and `SqlStore` version their storage formats. Each owns an ordered, append-only list of `Migration`s per dialect and records the number it ran in the shared `nanocode_formats` table under its component name (`session_log`, `records`), so several components can keep tables in one database. `SessionLog::migrate()` and `SqlStore::migrate()` run the missing migrations, each with its version update in one host transaction, (`SqlStore::migrated()` skips the check on an instance whose host ran `migrate()` at startup: up to date it still sends `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS nanocode_formats` and a read, which a host that opens a store per turn, as Pyramid does on its single write connection, sends every turn) and fail with `StoreError::UnsupportedFormat { component, stored, known }` without touching the tables when the database records a format newer than the build's `FORMAT`; both still migrate on their first operation when the host did not call `migrate`. Hosts call it where they open storage, so a host that runs several builds against the same data, such as Pact's Durable Objects during a deploy (an object can be reached by the previous Worker version for a while, and threads are kept indefinitely and migrate forward), refuses storage a newer build wrote instead of reading a format it does not know, and can surface that refusal as a retryable error at a point it controls. A deployed migration is never edited or removed, and a change in what stored rows or JSON mean gets a migration too. `migrate_format_then(host, component, migrations, then)` runs the statements `then` in the transaction of the last migration it runs, or alone when nothing needs migrating, and returns their rows, so a store that reads right after migrating, as `SessionLog` does when its writer opens, sends no read after the migration's writes. Migration 1 is each store's existing schema; for `SessionLog` it also adopts a database written before versioning whose `turns` table lacks the `retry` column (`MissingColumn`), the only conditional ALTER, which replaces the per-process `pragma_table_info('turns')` check. `migrate_format(host, component, migrations)` is the shared runner.
- `SessionLog::replace_queued(request_id, edit_id, text)` edits a queued turn's prompt in place for hosts whose users revise a message waiting behind a running turn: the text replaces the prompt's first text input and drops its other text inputs, while its images and other inputs, their order, its transcript and hand, its request ID and its place in the queue stay. The update and its receipt, a `replaced` event naming `edit_id` and the digest of the edit's text, commit in one transaction, so `Replaced::Applied(Some(event))` comes back once and a retry with the same edit ID and text answers `Applied(None)` whatever state the turn reached since, without undoing a later edit; the same ID with other text is `Conflict`, a turn that is not queued and holds no receipt for the ID `NotQueued`, and an edit whose row, receipt or input.accepted event would exceed `logical_limit` `TooLarge`; an edited prompt above `value_limit` spills into `log_parts` in the edit's transaction as a submitted one does. The update is conditional on the prompt digest it read, and when another writer changed the prompt in between it reads the turn again and applies the edit to the new prompt. A submit retried with the original prompt after an edit reports `Conflict`, since the request ID now holds other content. `TurnInbox::move_queued(queued_id, target)` moves a queued prompt into the running turn as an identified steer under the queued turn's request ID and then dequeues the turn as cancelled with `steered: true` (`SessionLog::withdraw_steered`, conditional on the digest that was steered). The running turn's durable steer receipt is the only decision: `Agent::steer_receipt(id, prompt)` (`LifecycleBackend::steer_receipt`, `DurableSession::steer_receipt` for both backends' policies) answers `SteerReceiptStatus::Accepted`, `Absent`, `Different`, `Withdrawn` or `Unknown` from the session's identified steer receipts, `Absent` only when the driver holds every receipt (no terminal receipt limit, sealed receipt pages loaded, history never pruned) and `Unknown` otherwise or without a durable policy, and `drive` withdraws instead of running a queued turn whose ID already has an `Accepted` receipt for its prompt. So a crash between the steer and the dequeue neither loses nor repeats the prompt, and repeating the move answers `MovedQueued::Moved`; `NotRunning` leaves the prompt queued, `Conflict` means a steer under that ID carried other input or was withdrawn, and `Unknown` moves nothing. A moved prompt shares the running turn's outcome, and one edited after its steer was accepted stays queued and runs its new text.
- `SessionLog::value_limit` is the host's physical UTF-8 binding and row budget; `logical_limit` separately retains the optional prompt admission, event omission and result failure policy. Only owned event-body and prompt slots spill into immutable content-keyed `log_parts`, in the same transaction as their row; each part fits the physical budget including its key and SQLite record header, is verified against its BLAKE3 key when read, and is never replaced or deleted. Reference-shaped user bodies are stored as parts too, and only owned read columns are decoded. Imported prompts spill before JSON batching, whose bindings respect the physical limit; an imported row that cannot fit fails before writing. Small values keep the same row writes. `page_bounded(after, limit, max_bytes)` budgets decoded event JSON UTF-8 bytes before loading parts, excludes the crossing event except that the first event is always returned (also for zero bytes), and leaves the cursor on the last returned event; envelope bytes are outside the budget. Inline logs remain readable; binaries without this contract cannot read spilled values. Bounded pages require a positive event count.
- `SessionLog::logical_limit(bytes)` optionally bounds logical row and event sizes independently of physical storage. Rows are sized conservatively by SQLite's record format (https://www.sqlite.org/fileformat.html#record_format): one varint for the header size and one per column, at most 9 bytes each, the text columns' bytes and at most 8 bytes per integer column. `append` logs an event above this logical limit as `{"type":"omitted","request_id","source","event_type","bytes"}`, and `finish` records an oversized result as a failed one whose error names its status and size. `submit` answers `Submitted::TooLarge { bytes, limit }` without writing when the logical turn row or its input.accepted event would not fit; that event is sized as `TurnInbox` stores it, with the request ID, UUIDv7-length identifiers or the request ID where a resumed turn uses it as its turn ID, and its seq at u64::MAX. Notes and retry errors that hosts add to an unfinished turn are not reserved or spilled and remain subject to the host's physical SQL row limit.
- `SessionLog::blobs(store, threshold)` gives a log the host's `SharedBlobStore` (Send + Sync on WASM too, because `TurnInbox`'s sink is an `EventSink`), so no SQL row holds image bytes or a value above the threshold. `submit`, `replace_queued` and `import` store each base64 data-URL `UserInput::Image` of a prompt as a `BlobUrl` (the decoded bytes under `BlobKey::of`), found with `BlobUrl::from_data_url` before anything is stored, so a `TooLarge` answer puts nothing. The submission digest stays the BLAKE3 of the host's prompt, so a resubmitted data-URL prompt is the existing turn; an edit's digest is of the edited stored prompt. `TurnInbox` hands the runtime the stored prompt, whose BlobUrl images the runtime loads. An event body or prompt above `threshold` UTF-8 bytes, or above `value_limit`, becomes one blob referenced as `{"$nanocode_log":["b3:<hex>"],"bytes":N}` instead of `log_parts` parts, and `page`, `page_bounded`, `turn_page*`, `turn`, `pending` and `import_events`' comparison re-materialize it; BlobUrls inside prompts and event content come back as stored, for hosts to serve from their own store. Every blob is put before the transaction that references it, so a failed put commits nothing. The SDK never deletes blobs: the host owns a session's blob namespace, deletes it with the session and copies the referenced blobs before importing a fork. `log_parts` references written before keep reading, a log without a store behaves as before, and binaries without this contract cannot read blob references.
- `SessionLog` format 2 (2026-11-04) writes a turn's events into rows it writes anyway instead of a row per event. `log_turns` (the former `turns` columns plus `edits`, the edit receipts as a JSON map from edit ID to the edit's text digest, `result`, the result event's body, and `tail_first`, `tail_last` and `tail`) and `log_chunks` (`scope`, `seq`, `last`, `body`, keyed by scope and first seq) hold events as lines `[seq,created_at,request_id,body]` ending in a newline, the body being the event or its `log_parts` or blob reference. No two rows' seq ranges overlap: every write takes every buffered event, so lines go only to the newest row (the holder, a turn row whose `tail_last` is the scope's highest seq) until its tail would pass 16 KiB (`SEAL_BYTES`, as `RowStore`'s tail) or the value limit, and otherwise to new chunks of at most that size. Seqs are per scope and assigned in memory, starting 16,384 (`GAP`) above the highest stored seq when a log opens, so a seq published before a crash and lost with the buffer is never assigned again; the log refuses new seqs while 16,384 are assigned without a successful write, which a 16 KiB buffer of lines of at least 15 bytes cannot reach unless writes fail. `stage(entries)` assigns seqs and buffers events (writing once the buffer reaches 16 KiB), `flush()` writes the buffer and `append` does both. `submit` inserts the turn with the buffer and its `queued` line in its tail (one row) without reading first: a request ID the log already holds fails the insert, and only then does it read the row to answer `Existing` or `Conflict`; `start` only buffers its `running` line and keeps the turn running in memory, which `turn`, `pending` and every lifecycle call see, until the next write of that turn's row stores it; `finish`, `cancel`, `retry`, `interrupted`, `replace_queued` and `withdraw_steered` write the row once, with the buffer when the turn is the holder. The log keeps the rows of unfinished turns it read (`turn`, `pending`) or wrote in memory and updates them with its own writes, so these calls, `turn` and `start` read nothing; a finished turn's row leaves memory, and a turn the process has not seen is read once. Reads matter on Vercel, where the journal cuts a Workflow batch at every read that follows queued writes: a read before each finish put it in a batch of its own. A page reads the chunk holding `after` and the following chunks and turn tails in seq order, in doubling batches, until `limit` events (or `max_bytes`, a reference counted by its `bytes`) are known, plus the buffer once every row is read, so it shows events not yet written; turn tails have no index, so a page scans the scope's turn rows (reads, which Durable Objects bill at a thousandth of a write). One process writes a scope. Every write checks, in its own transaction, the turn row it updates and the holder whose tail it appends to, with a statement that fails when the row moved (`json()` of text that is not JSON on SQLite, a division by zero on PostgreSQL), so the transaction rolls back whole instead of committing the writes whose guards held; the error then says the log changed outside its writer, the buffer is kept, and the next call reads the log's state again (`logcheck`: a second log on the same scope that finished a turn the first had written to since failed with nothing written, and its retry succeeded). `TurnInbox`'s sink stages events, publishes them and flushes at a top-level `tool.call` (not a cell's nested calls, which carry `parent_call_id` and are durable through the cell journal; Codex's nested `tool.call` events carry it since this change, as Claude's did), a `run.steered`, and a `run.completed` or `run.failed` of a turn `drive` does not finish (its `finish` writes the buffer), and `settle` and `shutdown` flush, so a crash loses at most the events since the last of those, which no durable state depends on. Migration 2 copies `turns` into `log_turns` (edit receipts from its `replaced` events, results from the events at `last_seq`) and each `events` row into a chunk of its own, and drops both tables; builds before it cannot read format 2. On 2026-11-04, through nanocode-ts in Node with the mock Responses server (`rows-nc`, with `RowStore` and logged prompts below), rows written per turn went from 17.1 to 3.1 for a text turn, 27.3 to 8.3 with one cell and one tool call, 57.3 to 20.3 with ten parallel calls and 87.3 to 50.3 with twenty sequential ones, turn CPU unchanged within the noise of a machine at load average 24 to 27 (two interleaved `bench-nc` runs each); `output/hand-wiring`'s `logcheck` gave identical output on SQLite and PostgreSQL 15 for every call (pages of 1, 3 and 100 events, budgets of 0 to 100,000 bytes, turn pages, `import` and `import_events`, a reopened log) and for a converted format 1 log, and nanocode-ts's `journal-crash` killed at 1, 20, 60, 200, 360, 520 and 900 ms completed the turn with no tool call run twice. With the rows in memory, top-level flushes and the migration's read in one transaction, `rows-nc` wrote 3.1, 7.3, 19.3 and 31.3 rows per turn in 7, 11, 21 and 31 transactions (from 11, 16, 26 and 54), and gakonst/nanocode-vercel-durable's drill (with its scripted model calling `charge` through `exec`, which Code Mode sessions need) committed 11 Workflow batches and 59 events for Claude and Codex with durability on, against 12 and 62 before format 2 and 14 to 15 and 68 to 71 with format 2's first build, and 31 batches and 119 events with strict durability against 43 to 47 and 155 to 167.
- Erase private service and spawned-task future types before substantial driver orchestration and executor dispatch. Preserve public generic service factories, native Send bounds and WASM-local futures; measure compile-time gains before adding boxed future boundaries.
- Instantiate large generic dependencies with one type each in code that compiles for WASM. Decode images from `Cursor<&[u8]>`: every reader type instantiates all of `image`'s decoders again, and the three reader types the SDK used cost the Worker's WASM 247 KB.
- Keep provider assembly interfaces in runtime/agent-config so backends do not depend on runtime/agent or runtime/subagents implementations. Pass delegation tools through the assembly interfaces.
- Keep value-only image handling and Code Mode dependent on core/types-core instead of event channels. Keep value and event wire definitions in core/types-core independent of Tokio; core/types preserves public reexports and owns event channels. Keep store interfaces, payloads and deterministic state transitions in core/durability, parameterized independently of agent metadata. Keep session drivers and checkpoint catalogs in runtime/durability, and storage adapters dependent on the contracts only.
- Third-party storage implements one host interface and leaves every durability rule to core/durability. `KeyValueStore` runs over a linearizable `KeyValue` (get, put_values, compare_and_swap and an optional list_keys). `SqlStore` runs over a SQLite or PostgreSQL `Sql` host whose `transaction(operation, statements)` executes the SDK's statements with text parameters atomically and returns each statement's rows; Durable Objects, D1, libSQL and sqlite-wasm need nothing else.
