SqlStore owns the `states`, `owners`, `records` and `state_records` tables, which are created by `SqlStore::migrate` or on first use, and every statement: acquisition increments the fence in SQL, a replacement inserts its new records, the state's holds of them and its head only while the owner, fence and expected revision match, and only while the state holds every record it staged, and only when it replaced nothing reads the head in a second query to report Fenced or Conflict (it used to read the head in every replacement transaction, one statement and two row reads per commit that only a refused commit needed), and commit_many sends a group in one transaction with independent outcomes. Counters are decimal text so JavaScript hosts keep u64 revisions, and the fence stops at SQLite's INTEGER maximum. It reports no concurrent handles, so SharedStore groups concurrent commits into commit_many. `list_states` sends a limit above the signed 64-bit maximum as that maximum: PostgreSQL refused `usize::MAX` (`out of range for type bigint`), so `SessionStore::list` failed there. How records are stored and collected is the next entry.
- `SqlStore` stores each record once, by its content key, and notes which states hold it (format 2 of its `records` component): `records` has a row per key (an integer `id`, the unique `key` and the value) and `state_records` a row per record and state that holds it (primary key record and state ID, indexed by state), so the instructions and tool declarations every session of a host names, and any chunk two sessions share, are stored once. Format 3 (2026-11-04) folds a record's first hold into the record: `records.holder` names the state that stored it (indexed where not null), and `state_records` gets a row only for another state that holds an existing record, so a new record costs one row instead of two: a Codex tool turn through `output/hand-wiring`'s `vsagents` (in-memory SQLite, `total_changes()`, which counts table rows and not index entries) wrote 12.3 rows instead of 19.6 and a text turn 8.2 instead of 13.3. A state holds a record when it is the record's `holder` or a hold row names it: inserts set `holder` (the grouped path to the first committing state that stages the record, which at worst holds a record its commit did not name until that state is collected), hold statements skip records the state is holder of, a replacement checks either, `list_records` reads both, and a collection clears `holder` beside releasing hold rows (`UNHOLD`, under the same revision and fence guards) and deletes only records with neither. On PostgreSQL the upserts return the existing row's holder under the row lock they already take, so a collection that clears it still waits for, or is waited for by, the writer. Records stored before keep their hold rows. `holdcheck` (SQLite through `SqliteSql`, PostgreSQL 15 through `PostgresSql`) migrated a format 2 database and swept it, checked sharing between two states, and raced 8 to 12 writers, each staging only records it had not committed since acquiring, against 3 to 4 sweepers: every head named only records that existed and that its state held, no record was left unheld, a final sweep left only reachable records, and 136 to 369 commits per run were fenced and retried. A record used to be a row of `blobs` per state, keyed by a hash of state ID and key; migration 2 copies those (and SQLite's `WITHOUT ROWID` `records` of builds before 2026-10-21) into the new tables in one transaction and drops them, and keys no longer collide, so the row-id collision check is gone. A read looks the key up in `records` alone, since a state only reads keys its own payloads name and holds every record its revisions named; `list_records` lists what it holds. A replacement or `put_records` inserts each record whose key is not stored and the state's holds of all of them: on PostgreSQL in one statement whose insert, sorted by key, updates a stored row's key to itself (`ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`) so it returns the row's id and holds its lock until the transaction ends, so a collection cannot delete it meanwhile and two writers lock shared rows in one order; on SQLite one statement per record and one for the holds. A state holds what its revisions named until it is collected. `SqlStore::unreachable(state)` marks, without a lock, what the current revision reaches: the keys its payload names (`EncodedPayload::references`, each `b3:` and 64 hex digits anywhere in a JSON text, so text that only looks like a key keeps more, never less), followed through the records the state holds with the payload loader, manifests and chunks included, and returns the revision and the held records it does not reach; a state holding a key of an earlier build (neither `b3:` nor `c:`) is kept whole, and a missing or corrupt record fails the mark. `SqlStore::collect(state, revision, keys)` releases one batch in one host transaction: it locks the state's owner row (inserting an ownerless placeholder at fence 0 when the state has none, so a first acquisition waits for it), on PostgreSQL locks the batch's records in key order, and only while the head is still at `revision` releases the state's holds of them and raises its fence, answering `None` and releasing nothing otherwise; it then deletes each of them no state holds any more, in a statement that starts after those locks, so it sees every hold committed by a writer it waited for, and a writer that waits for it inserts the record again. Raising the fence is what makes collection safe without a commit checking every record it names: a writer of the collected state that knew a released record, from a load, an earlier commit or records it put ahead, is fenced, and a writer of any other state names only records its own state holds. `SqlStore::sweep(state, batch)` marks and collects in batches, and `nanocode_store_sqlx::unreachable` and `collect` run them on a host's connection, so a host can check its own state (that no turn runs) and hold its own locks in the transaction. Collect sessions no live driver owns, since a collection fences the owner, and only once readers of an older revision are done; tempoxyz/pyramid sweeps a thread's state a minute after its last turn. On 2026-10-27 against PostgreSQL 18, a copy of Pyramid's dev database (1,498 states) went from 30,972 `blobs` rows of 140.1 MB (94 MB on disk) to 13,598 records of 17.4 MB plus 30,972 holds (25.1 MB on disk with indexes), and `output/storage-check` passed with 8 writers of 150 commits each on states sharing records, half committing through `StagedStore` with records put ahead, beside 3 concurrent collectors: 248 commits were fenced and retried, every head stayed whole and no record was left unheld.
- `RowStore` (core/durability, `nanocode::durability::RowStore`) is a `StateStore` over the same `Sql` host as `SqlStore` that writes one row per commit, for hosts that pay per row written and keep one store per conversation, such as a Durable Object per session. `state_rows` holds each state's owner, fence, revision, head payload and `tail`, the records committed since the last seal as `<key> <length>\n<value>`: a commit is one `UPDATE` guarded by owner, fence and revision that sets the head and appends its new records, and when the tail would pass 16 KiB (`SEAL_BYTES`, the head's inline record bound; rewriting a tail cost about 0.35 us per KB in node:sqlite) the commit moves it with its new records into a `state_pages` row (its keys as JSON and its body) and empties it, two rows. The store holds every state's tail and every page's keys in memory, loaded on first use, so a record is stored once across its states and a read costs no query unless it is in a sealed page (every page a read needs comes in one transaction); a key it does not know rereads the states whose tail size or page count changed, and an acquisition that finds its state's tail changed rereads it, so processes can share a file. It keeps no events, summaries or collection and loads the whole index, so hosts with many states, such as Pyramid on PostgreSQL, keep `SqlStore`. Its format is the `rows` component, version 1. On first use it converts a `SqlStore`'s tables in the same database (after `SqlStore::migrate` brings them to format 3): each record goes to one state that holds it (its `holder`, else a `state_records` row), packed into pages of about 16 KiB per state, each owner becomes a state row with its fence, revision and payload, and `states`, `owners`, `records`, `state_records` and the `records` format are dropped, so builds that use `SqlStore` on that database start empty; `vsagents`' `CONVERT` wrote a session of four turns with `SqlStore`, reopened it with `RowStore` and ran a turn that saw the earlier transcript (2026-11-04). On 2026-11-02 (output/hand-wiring `vsagents`, Codex Code Mode turns against a local mock on a SQLite file, load average 24) a text turn wrote 2.2 rows in 2 transactions instead of 6.1, a turn with one cell and one tool call 3.2 rows in 3 instead of 9.3, a session's file took 8,620 bytes instead of 14,856 and turn times matched within noise; the crash checks (`vsagents` CRASH with tool-x1 and slowtool, `claudecrash`) recovered as with `SqlStore`, and `swarm`'s tree of 3,030 agents settled in 3.79 s instead of 2.98 s in an 81 MB file instead of 107 MB, since each unknown key rereads every state's tail size.
- `SessionStore::with_state_format(format)` caps the state format the session drivers write, for hosts whose older builds share the store during a rolling deploy or after a rollback: 6 writes operation inputs and outputs and continuations as records rather than inline in the head (what formats 8 and 7 add), 4 or 5 also writes step inputs and outputs as records (what 6 adds), and only 4 to 8 are accepted. A head written under a cap is readable by every build that reads up to it, and a capped driver writes a head an uncapped build left at a higher format back down at its next commit, staging the records its inline payloads become. `JournalState::stage_records_unless`, `checkpoint_payload` and `seal_receipts` set the cap in a thread-local `payload::Inline` for their synchronous work, which the payload serializer and staging read, and operation inputs and outputs serialize through `payload::receipt` (the wire form of `payload::step`). Exports, branch imports and the acquisition update are not capped; the acquisition update writes back only what it read. On 2026-11-05 on tempoxyz/pyramid main's public schema with pyramid-core's migrations (ParadeDB 0.26.1-pg18, Codex turns against the mock server through `StagedStore` as pyramid-core commits them; `output/hand-wiring`'s `pcorecheck` and `output/pyramid-core-check/old`, a build of nanocodex 52a64018c, which tempoxyz/pyramid runs): uncapped heads were format 8 and the older build refused them (`unsupported state format 8`); with `with_state_format(6)` heads were format 4 and the older build continued those sessions, a capped turn wrote an uncapped session's head back to format 4 and the older build then continued it, and the newer build continued sessions the older one wrote.
- `SessionStore::with_logged_prompts()` makes the drivers of root sessions (a session record without a parent) admit operations in memory: `OperationAccepted` is applied within the current revision and written with the session's next commit, such as a tool step's admission or the turn's completion, instead of a commit of its own. A crash before that commit loses the admission, so only hosts that log every prompt and run it again use it: `TurnInbox::drive` runs a turn the SDK does not hold from the log's prompt. Subagent sessions still commit their admissions, and an admission commits when another operation was already accepted within the revision (a turn queued behind a running one). With it a text turn commits once: `vsagents` (Codex, SQLite file, 2026-11-04) wrote 1.2 rows in 1 transaction per text turn with `RowStore` instead of 2.2 in 2, and 2.2 in 2 with one cell and one tool call instead of 3.2 in 3, and nanocode-ts turns killed before any commit ran again from the log.
- `nanocode::durability::RecordCache::new(budget_bytes)` is a process-wide cache of durable records for hosts that reopen sessions often, such as tempoxyz/pyramid, which opens every thread's session at the start of each turn and loads it again for its checkpoint: `cache.wrap(store)` is a `CachedStore` that serves `read_record`/`read_records` from memory, reads only the missing keys from the store, writes through every record a `replace`, `replace_to`, `commit` or `commit_many` stores, and forwards everything else, including concurrent handles. Records are immutable and named by their content, so a cached record never goes stale; misses are not cached, and once the budget is spent the oldest entries go first. One `nanocode_sync::Actor` owns the entries, so the cache is lock-free and wrappers on any number of tasks share it. Against a local PostgreSQL 15 on 2026-10-26, reading a session's 300 records of 4 KB took 2.4 ms from `SqlStore` and 0.16 ms warm, and 3,000 took 30.1 ms and 1.25 ms.
- `BlobStore` (core/durability, re-exported as `nanocode::durability::BlobStore`) is the host's store for large immutable bytes, keyed by `BlobKey` (`b3:` and the BLAKE3 hex of the raw bytes). `put` resolving Ok means the bytes are durable, and putting a key again is harmless; `get` returns the bytes or None, and `get_blob` checks them against their key. `BlobUrl` names media whose bytes are in the store as `nanocode-blob:<media type>;b3:<hex>`: `store_data_url` decodes a base64 data URL and stores the raw bytes, and `load_data_url` rebuilds the data URL. The SDK puts a blob before it commits any record that names it and never deletes one: a host keeps each session's blobs in a namespace of its own and deletes it with the session, and a crash between a put and its commit leaves a blob no record names. `MemoryBlobStore` (runtime/durability) keeps blobs in memory for tests and hosts without persistent storage.
- `Sql::dialect` names the SQL its host runs: `SqlDialect::Sqlite` by default, so existing hosts are unchanged, or `SqlDialect::Postgres`, for which `SqlStore` and `SessionLog` (and so `TurnInbox`) send PostgreSQL statements over the same tables and parameters: `$n::text` placeholders, BIGINT identity columns instead of rowids (`states.ord` keeps `list_states` newest first), `json_array_elements` and `->>` instead of `json_each` and `json_extract`, `GREATEST`, `IS DISTINCT FROM`, `information_schema.columns` for the retry column check, `json_build_object` for the retry fields, which keeps their order, and every result column cast to text, so a host returns each column as an optional string; PostgreSQL resolves an unqualified `ORDER BY seq` to the text output column of `CAST(seq AS TEXT)` and sorts `1, 10, 2`, so ordered statements name the table column (`events.seq`, `turns.first_seq`, and `sized.seq` for the outer sort of the byte-bounded event and turn pages). A PostgreSQL `SqlStore` has no legacy `records` table, and its log's `MAX(seq)` lookups are limited to the scope, so a host that serializes a scope's writes (as SQLite's single writer does) gets the same `first_seq` and `last_seq`. Its migrations run through `migrate` or on first use like SQLite's; `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` can race between PostgreSQL sessions, so hosts with migrations create the tables first, and a host whose database already has `turns` or `events` tables keeps a log in another schema. `nanocode_store_postgres::PostgresSql` is the tokio-postgres host, running each statement group in its own transaction. PostgreSQL `SqlStore` record inserts and replacements check the owner with `FOR SHARE` on its `owners` row and the replacement's upsert updates only the expected revision, so at the default READ COMMITTED an acquire waits for an uncommitted replacement and then sees it, and a replacement whose owner was fenced or whose revision moved inserts nothing and reports Fenced or Conflict. `SqlStore` is a `StateStore` for any host lifetime, so a host can run it on a transaction it borrows.
- `nanocode_store_sqlite::SqliteSql` is a `Sql` host over one rusqlite connection on its own worker thread (WAL, `synchronous = FULL`, a 5 s busy timeout), so a native host runs `SqlStore`, `SessionLog` and `TurnInbox` over one SQLite file as WASM hosts do over theirs; gakonst/nanocode-swift does. Each `transaction` runs its statements in one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction with text parameters and returns every cell as text or null (integers and reals as their decimal text); a failure before `COMMIT` rolls back and is `NotCommitted`, a failed `COMMIT` is `Backend`. A transaction the worker received runs to completion even when its caller dropped the future, as `SqliteStore`'s writes do.
- `nanocode-store-sqlx` runs `SqlStore`'s PostgreSQL statements on sqlx 0.9 (the version tempoxyz/pyramid's backend uses, so a host builds one sqlx): `SqlxSql` over any `PgConnections` source (a `PgPool`, or a host's own connections that wait out restarts) runs each statement group in its own transaction, `SqlxTransaction` runs them on a `PgConnection` inside a transaction its caller commits, and `commit_staged(writes, connection)` commits a `StagedStore`'s writes there, so staged session state commits with the host's own transaction; `unreachable(state, connection)` and `collect(state, revision, keys, connection)` run `SqlStore`'s collection there. Each column comes back as an optional string, as `SqlRow` expects. It builds natively only, like `nanocode-store-postgres`.
- `StagedStore::new(base)` wraps any `StateStore` and keeps every acquisition and commit in memory, lock-free (an `Atom` of `Arc`'d states whose payloads and records are `Arc<str>`, so a write copies pointers, not content, and a read looks up its state instead of cloning everything staged; it used to hold a `std::sync::Mutex` and clone every staged payload and record on each read): reads see staged states and records before `base`, acquisitions read `base`'s owner without fencing and stage the next fence, and `StagedStore::writes` returns the `StagedWrites` handle the host keeps. `StagedWrites::commit_to(store)` acquires each changed state in `store` for its staged owner, fails with `Fenced` unless that fence is the one staged (no one acquired it since) and with `Conflict` unless the revision is still the one staging started from, then writes the last staged payload at the staged revision with every staged record through `StateStore::replace_to(state, owner, expected, revision, payload, records)`, which advances a head by more than one revision in one fenced replacement (`SqlStore` implements it; other stores answer `Ok(None)` and `commit_to` replays every staged commit in order, the records with the first, as it always did), so revisions, which durable state payloads embed, match what the session saw and nothing reads the intermediate heads anyway. On PostgreSQL a replacement inserts its records in one statement (`INSERT_RECORDS`, over a JSON array of `[id, key, value]`) instead of one per record; SQLite hosts keep one statement per record, since Durable Objects and D1 bound each value. tempoxyz/pyramid commits a turn's staged writes inside the transaction that holds its deployment-wide write lock, so this is time every other writer waits: against a local PostgreSQL 15 (`output/durability-bench`, p50 of 6 runs), 10 staged commits with 50 records committed in 5.4 ms instead of 23.5, 30 with 150 in 10.7 instead of 67.8, and 60 with 300 in 20.4 instead of 77.6, in 4 statements instead of 2 + records + commits; 60 commits of 64 KB with 300 records of 16 KB (8.4 MB) took 77.8 ms against 70.1, Postgres parsing the 4.7 MB array costing what 358 sub-millisecond loopback round trips did, which across a network each cost a round trip. `commit_to` leaves the writes staged, so a host whose transaction fails to commit can call it again on a new transaction; the host calls `discard` once its transaction has committed, and a `commit_to` after that commit fails with `Fenced` instead of writing twice. `StagedWrites::stage_records(store)` (`nanocode_store_sqlx::stage_records(writes, connection)`) writes every staged record ahead of the host's transaction through `StateStore::put_records`, which stores records no head names yet (`SqlStore`: one statement on PostgreSQL, one per record and one for the state's holds on SQLite; other stores answer `Ok(false)` and nothing is staged ahead), and `commit_to` then sends only the records not staged that way, so the host's transaction holds the acquisition and one head replacement whatever the turn wrote. Records are immutable and content-addressed, so writing them early exposes nothing, and a turn whose transaction then fails leaves records nothing names, as a crash between a blob put and its commit does, which the state holds until a collection releases them. `commit_staged` and `stage_records` skip `SqlStore`'s format check (`migrated()`), which the turn's own store ran on its first read. Against a local PostgreSQL 15 (p50 of 6 runs), the transaction of a turn with 10 commits and 50 records took 0.7-0.9 ms instead of 4.2-7.1, and one with 60 commits and 300 records of 16 KB 0.9 ms instead of 76-82, the records going in 3.8-78 ms before it; tempoxyz/pyramid commits inside the transaction holding its deployment-wide write lock, so this is the time every other writer waits per turn. Staged states have no events or summaries. Give `commit_to` a store that runs on a transaction the host commits or rolls back, such as an `SqlStore` over the host's transaction, so state commits atomically with the host's own writes: tempoxyz/pyramid opens each turn's `SessionStore` over a `StagedStore` of its PostgreSQL `SqlStore` and commits the staged writes in the transaction that sets the turn's final status, only when the turn succeeds, discarding them after that transaction commits, so a failed or cancelled turn leaves the model context of the last successful turn. A session over a `StagedStore` ends when its writes are committed or dropped. `StagedWrites::changed_states()` lists the states with staged changes and `retain(keep)` drops every other staged state before `stage_records` or `commit_to`, so a host that runs subagents within one turn can commit only its root (`retain(|state| state == root)`): its children and the `<root>:subagents` journal are then never written, and the next turn's root finds no journal and resumes or announces no child of an earlier turn, which tempoxyz/pyramid's review of PR 219 asked for. `keep` may run more than once and must be pure.
- `SessionStore::export_branch(id, at)`, `export_page` and `import_branch` move a settled branch into an independent store, which the same-store `branch` cannot do, for hosts that keep one store per conversation, such as a Durable Object per thread that forks a thread into a new object or runs a throwaway fork for a review. `BranchExport` is small: the source record, the boundary, the selected checkpoint's key, the number of covered turns and the key of an inherited-turn index, a `Sequence` of `{turn, checkpoint}` records (with `before` where the checkpoint before a turn is not the previous turn's, as at an import's first own turn) behind a record that also names the history's start. `export_page(id, export, cursor, max_bytes)` recomputes the covered history from the source on every call, so the source keeps nothing between pages, and fails when retention or a rewrite changed it; its pages walk the start checkpoint, the settled checkpoint of every covered turn, the selected checkpoint and the index, each as the physical records its root reaches (inline payloads, manifests, tree nodes and chunks) minus those the previous root reaches, in key order, and hold at least one record and stop before the record that would pass `max_bytes`. Records are found by following keys held as JSON strings, which is how `EncodedPayload` serializes, so every checkpoint format transfers without format code, and a page call keeps only the key graph of the checkpoints it walks and reads the previous root's records once, instead of a key list for the whole history or a walk per turn. The imported record keeps the index key, not a growing metadata list, so the copy can be branched in its own store or exported again through the turns it inherited, after its source is gone: Through takes the entry's checkpoint, Before the checkpoint before the entry, the history's start or the checkpoint the import began from. Exports allow pending operations after a Through or Before boundary and refuse Latest while any is pending. `import_branch` acquires the target once and commits a staged head whose record holds the import's next cursor; `SessionStore` treats that head as absent (load, resume, turns, list and branch), and a session described over it replaces it. `BranchImport::import(page)` verifies every record against its content address and its kind's size bound (inline payloads and manifests below 16 KiB, chunks at most `CHUNK_MAX` plus a UTF-8 boundary), rejects records it cannot verify, such as the chunked payloads of SHA-256 builds, ignores a page that ends at or before its cursor, refuses one that starts after it and commits the page's records with the advanced cursor in one fenced commit, so a host that loses an import calls `import_branch` again and continues from `next()` without counting SDK rows. `publish` refuses an incomplete import, hydrates the selected checkpoint and checks every indexed checkpoint root, then makes the session visible in one fenced commit; `import_branch` of an export already published returns it without writing. The session log moves separately, through `SessionLog::import` and `import_events`.
- `SessionLog::import_events(after, events)` writes the events another log's `page` returned under their own seq, request ID, creation time and body, so a host that forks a conversation into a new store, such as a Durable Object per thread, copies its log as well as its durable state: with `import` given each copied turn's `first_seq` and `last_seq`, which keeps it from synthesizing a result event, the target's `page` and `turn_page` answer the source's events for the copied range and its turns report the same results. `after` is the cursor the events follow, as `page(after, limit)` takes it; the events must ascend above it and fit `logical_limit`, bodies above `value_limit` spill into `log_parts` as appended ones do, and the call answers the last imported seq, the cursor for the next page. A read transaction finds where the target's log ends and reads back the rows in the range, and the import is refused before anything is written when the log ends before `after`, holds a different event at an imported seq (every column is compared, a spilled body after reading its parts back), holds an event of its own in the range that the import lacks, or another scope owns an imported seq. Since format 2 the import first writes the log's buffer, reads the range under the log's writer and writes the events after the log's end as chunks, so a replayed or retried import writes nothing and continues where a lost one stopped; seqs are per scope, and the log's next seq moves past the imported ones. Copying with `append` cannot do this: it renumbers events, replaces their creation times and duplicates them when retried.
- StateStore: `peek` is required, because SessionStore::load, resume, branching and child journals read through it without fencing, and `owner` reports the current owner token without fencing; its unsupported default makes AgentRuntime treat a live agent's ownership as unknown. `acquire_with` runs its owned, synchronous StoreUpdate once against the acquired state; updates only calculate, None acquires without changing the revision, and the default acquire-then-replace reports failures after acquisition as Backend errors. `acquire_with_companion` acquires a distinct companion with the primary: an equal companion is NotCommitted before any write, NotCommitted means neither state changed, any other error leaves both uncertain, and a batching adapter validates both fences and revisions first and acknowledges only once the whole batch is durable. `commit` checks the fence and revision like `replace`, replaces the payload, advances the revision once, inserts absent records and appends events atomically; conflicting record content or a seq overflow is NotCommitted before any write, the default rejects events with NotCommitted, and events take contiguous seqs after the state's last, from 1, never reused. `commit_many` returns one result per commit in order, each judged on its own fence and revision; a shared write acknowledges none before it is durable, and a failed group may leave any subset committed. `supports_events`, `supports_summaries` and `batches_companion_acquisition` stay fixed per adapter and wrappers forward them; `concurrent_handle` returns a handle only when operations stay correct concurrently, including on one state; `flush` is an ordered barrier. `list_records` returns every record key of a state, for repairs that must find records by content; it is unsupported by default, and SqlStore, KeyValueStore (over `list_keys`), JSONL, SQLite and Postgres implement it and SharedStore forwards it.
- StateReader never acquires, fences or changes a revision: `read_state`, `read_records` and `read_events` are strongly consistent per state, while `query_states` and `query_events` come from an index that may trail commits. A summary belongs to the revision that committed it; index updates are idempotent by (state_id, revision) and (state_id, seq), Remove keeps a tombstone that queries never return, and an asynchronous indexer persists the publication intent in the commit's transaction. `query_states` orders by (updated_at_ms, state_id) and `query_events` by (at_ms, state_id, seq), both descending, and `read_events` by seq between exclusive bounds; pages are live keyset pages, a zero limit returns an empty page, `usize::MAX` is valid and adapters impose no hidden caps. scope, parent, state_id and attributes match exactly, kinds match any listed kind, and text is an ASCII-case-insensitive literal substring of the title, preview or event texts.
- Payloads are BLAKE3-addressed: one inline `b3:` record under 16 KiB, otherwise content-defined `c:` chunks under a manifest keyed by its own hash. A manifest that would reach 16 KiB becomes a tree cut where a hash prefix is divisible by CHUNK_AVG / 64, so a save rewrites only the groups around its edits: on a 60-turn, 4 MB local Claude session manifest bytes fell from 2.39 MB to 1.55 MB. Keys without `b3:` are SHA-256 records from earlier builds that still load and verify, and `EncodedPayload::matches_key` treats a SHA-256 key of the same content as equal, so receipts and step inputs recorded before the change still match. Records are immutable, and a store deletes one only after fencing every writer that could know it (`SqlStore::collect`), so a session driver counts every record its loads read (the latest checkpoint at agent acquisition and `LoadPayload(s)`, through `EncodedPayload::load_noting` and `load_many_noting`) among its committed records, and the first save after a restore stages only chunks the load did not read: before, it resent every chunk of a re-encoded checkpoint, and in the Worker's `measure-store.mjs` on 2026-10-20 a Claude turn restored after 60 turns of 200 text deltas sent 172 statements and 2.29 MB over the WASM bridge, against 71 and 1.31 MB now and 70 and 1.24 MB for a warm turn, and took 43.0 ms instead of 48.3 ms. `SessionStore::record(id)` is a session's `SessionRecord` (its model, lineage and settings) without the first prompt `summary` also reads, for hosts that only ask whether a session exists and which model it runs, as tempoxyz/pyramid does before every turn. `SessionStore::load` keeps the checkpoint payload it read and the record keys that load noted, `prepare_resume_loaded` hands them to the session with the checkpoint preload, and the driver's agent acquisition uses them instead of reading and verifying the checkpoint again while the state's latest checkpoint key still matches, since records are immutable; the Claude backend takes the loaded value from the preload as Codex does instead of decoding the payload again. The load also notes the records a format's `CheckpointFormat::hydrate_noting` reads, Codex's history pages and items, and `DurableSession::take_checkpoint_preload_with_records` hands them to the Codex backend, which remembers them instead of re-encoding and hashing the whole history to check the checkpoint key; `load_many` reads the manifest levels and chunks of every payload it loads in one store read per level instead of one per payload; `prepare_resume_loaded` skips its head peek when the load found a session record; and `StoredSession::checkpoint_thinking` reads Codex's thinking without converting the checkpoint.
- Durable state transactions grow with their content, not with history: `EncodedPayload` binds at most one chunk (under 32 KiB) per record and `SqlStore::replace` sends one statement per new record on SQLite (one for all of them on PostgreSQL), but an image held inline still costs a megabyte-scale transaction and its whole size again on every reopen, which a blob store avoids.
- Step inputs and outputs under 1 KiB (`STEP_INLINE_BYTES`) that a driver holds with their content are written inside the state row as `"=<json>"` instead of as `b3:` records, so a step's payload costs no row of its own: a model call's `{"wire","blake3","bytes"}` input, its output that references the message records, and a tool call's input. `OperationState`'s staging keeps their content, since the state row is rewritten on every commit while the step lives, and still stages their child records. Steps retire at the next `ExecutionAdvanced` or completion, so an idle session's head carries none. A head with any inline step has state format 6, which older builds refuse as an unsupported state format instead of failing on a missing record, and other heads keep format 4 or 5, so a rollback reads every session that is not mid-turn. In a Node sqlite harness on 2026-10-25 (nanocode-ts Code Mode, trivial `text("ok")` cells) a cell committed 5.1 rows and 10.1 statements to the store instead of 8.1 and 15.1 (with 5 event rows either way), and in the Worker's `measure-store.mjs` a Codex turn with three tool rounds wrote 58.5 Durable Object rows instead of 64.5 and read 176 instead of 224. An operation's input and a completed operation's output under 1 KiB are held the same way (2026-11-04): inline in the head while the operation is retained there and inline in its sealed receipt page after, so a typical prompt and a turn's output cost no record of their own; a head that inlines them or references sealed pages is state format 8, which older builds refuse. `EncodedPayload::load_many` and `read_many` return a payload that already holds its content instead of reading its key, since an inline payload has no record. On 2026-11-04 through `vsagents` (in-memory SQLite) a text turn wrote 6.2 rows instead of 7.2 and a turn with one cell and one tool 10.3 instead of 11.3, with the same prompt every turn (a new prompt each turn used to add its input record too); a turn left pending by a crash resumed after a reopen on both backends and both SQLite and JSONL stores (`claudecrash`, `vsagents` `CRASH=1`). An operation's continuation (the turn's resumable state, 2 to 4 KB for a Codex turn) is written in the state row the same way while it is under `INLINE_BYTES` (16 KiB, the bound below which a payload is one record anyway; `payload::head`, `stage_head_unless`), since every commit of a running turn rewrites both: each commit used to store a new continuation record and its hold, which a collection had to delete later. A head holding one is state format 7, which builds before 2026-11-04 refuse; an idle session's head holds none and keeps its format. Through output/hand-wiring's `vsagents` (a native Codex agent on an in-memory SQLite `SqlStore` against the agents-vs-nanocode mock, 2026-11-04) a text turn wrote 13.3 rows and 5.8 KB instead of 15.3 and 9.4 KB, a cell with one tool call 19.6 rows and 7.2 KB instead of 23.6 and 16.8 KB, and a session 15.4 KB after one turn instead of 19.6 KB; a turn whose `exec` was running when its runtime shut down resumed from a format 7 head on reopen, reported the call's unknown outcome to the model and completed, and a Claude agent on SQLite wrote 448 KB in 40 rounds instead of 672 to 784 KB.
- Durable per-step work grows with new content, not with history (`EncodedPayload` trees, `SequenceLog`, Claude's incremental tool-ID sets, the receipt archive; see those entries). Through gakonst/nanocode-ts's Node harness (`scripts/profile-turns.mjs`: a mock provider, one host tool call per turn, node:sqlite on disk) on 2026-10-26, a Claude turn's WASM time at turn 600 went from 11.0-14.1 ms to 4.1-6.1 ms (3.4 ms at turn 50) and a Codex turn's from 13.3-14.6 ms to 4.9 ms (3.8 ms at turn 50). `SessionSnapshot` keeps the model's `ResponseHistory` (its committed segments shared, serialized as the same array) and its request prefix as the model's `Arc`, so taking a snapshot copies no items, and `DurableExecution` encodes completions, failures, cancellations and standalone checkpoints through the continuation cache (`prepare_cached_snapshot`, `prepare_cached_completion`): the snapshot diffs against the history the last continuation encoded and attaches the `SequenceLog` frontier, where each turn's snapshot used to clone and re-encode the whole history. The cache is held across the commit and released only once it succeeds. A reopen after each run resumed with every message, and the Worker's measure-faults passed 1,220 checks on every scenario but legacy, which needs a bundle from an older SDK.
- `Sequence` in runtime/durability stores a list as one content-addressed record per item: each run of 16 item references is sealed into an immutable group page and each 16 pages of a level into a page of the next, and the payload holding the sequence keeps `{"tail":{"levels":[...],"items":[...]}}` with fewer than 16 unsealed references per level, so a save writes its new items, at most one page per level and references that grow with the logarithm of the length. `SequenceLog` keeps a sequence incrementally: `sync(unchanged, items)` truncates it to the unchanged prefix and pushes the rest, sealing a page whenever a level fills, and each sealed page carries its 16 entries as child payloads, so `frontier()`, the unsealed entries of every level, reaches every record of the sequence through the pages' children, and `sequence()` is the frontier's references. Backends attach the frontier to the payload that holds the sequence, so a save hands the driver O(16 log n) payloads instead of every item and page, and `release()` turns the entries into references once their commit is confirmed (Codex releases after `commit_writes` succeeds; Claude's items keep their content on their `HistoryMessage` anyway). `PagedWriter::sequence` builds a sequence from scratch for exports and full snapshots. `load_sequence` reads each level in one batched load and still reads the chained 64-item pages (`{"tail":"<key>"}`) of earlier builds, whose every save rewrote the tail page, while older binaries fail to parse a sealed tail instead of reading an empty history. `EncodedPayload::with_payloads` attaches the records a payload references, and the driver stages only those its committed records lack, including records its loads read, so callers need not track what is durable. Staging walks a payload's tree by reference and skips a node whose key is committed together with everything under it (`stage_tree` checks the node before its children), which holds because a commit stages a record's whole closure minus records already committed, and `EncodedPayload`'s children and pending records are `Arc` slices, so cloning a payload, as transitions and the cursor caches do, copies no list.
- The SDK's Claude durability policies set `ClaudeExecutionPolicy::pages_messages`, so root checkpoints and cursors are version 4: each conversation message and each retained Code Mode round (`conversation.code_calls`) is its own record, cached on its `HistoryMessage` or `CodeRound` beside its JSON, a checkpoint is `{"provider":"claude","version":4,"messages":<Sequence>,"code_calls":<Sequence>,"admitted_base","admitted_tool_ids","unadmitted_tool_ids","snapshot":<version 1 snapshot without them>}` and a cursor `{"version":4,"messages","code_calls","admitted_base","admitted_tool_ids","unadmitted_tool_ids","pending":<the whole working list>,"template":<record of the template's system and tools>,"cursor":<version 1 cursor without them>}`. The admitted IDs that no `tool_use` block of the stored messages carries are split by when they were admitted: those admitted before the last settled checkpoint, such as every ID of compacted messages, are one record `{"ids":[...]}` named by `admitted_base`, and the rest are listed in `admitted_tool_ids`; `unadmitted_tool_ids` are the `tool_use` IDs that are not admitted, all sorted, so the set is rebuilt exactly. `Conversation.admitted_tool_ids` is `AdmittedIds`, the IDs in admission order with their positions, and the durable `Paging` keeps the three sets incrementally: a save lists and unlists only the `tool_use` IDs of messages that changed since the last save (found by `HistoryMessage` pointer identity) and classifies only the IDs admitted since, and the base record is re-encoded only when a compaction or rewind changes it. Each save used to collect every listed ID into a set and scan every admitted ID, and after a compaction every cursor carried every earlier ID. A model call's receipt names its assistant message by `content_record` instead of repeating its content, and a completed turn's output names the last assistant message by `final_record` when its text blocks are the final message; both records are the ones the history pages reference, so a reply is stored once instead of three times. `ClaudeCheckpointFormat::hydrate_noting`, the policy's continuation, step replay, admission replay and acquisition rebuild version 1 values, so everything that reads a checkpoint as a value is unchanged; version 1 to 3 checkpoints and cursors (version 1 cursors with `pending_base`) still load, forks, compactions, developer-notice checkpoints, subagent child states and exports stay version 1, and older binaries reject version 4.
- KeyValueStore keeps a state's head at `{prefix}state/{state_id}` and its content-addressed records at `{prefix}record/{state_id length}/{state_id}/{key}`, written before the head swap and never deleted, so a failed replace leaves only unreferenced records. Versions are `fence.revision.owner` strings the adapter chooses and hosts store and compare verbatim; a failed swap rereads the head to report Fenced or Conflict, and acquisition retries until it installs the next fence. compare_and_swap must be linearizable per key, so eventually consistent stores such as Cloudflare KV cannot back it.
- runtime/durability session drivers: SessionStore::session and resume and DurableSession::open, open_shared, open_shared_described and open_sibling acquire ownership and persist the record before returning, fencing earlier owners. prepare_session, prepare_resume and prepare_shared_described return a driver without writing, and its first command acquires once through acquire_with with the prepared record merged, which JSONL commits with one disk sync, so use them only where construction consumes the driver at once. `acquire_agent_with_initial_checkpoint` commits the backend's initial checkpoint in that acquisition when the state has no record, checkpoint or operations and returns `InitialAcquisition::Existing` otherwise, and `seeded_child_journal` with `acquire_agent_with_initial_checkpoint_and_journal` folds the subagent tree acquisition into the same synced batch only when the store batches companion acquisition, never on WASM. NotCommitted acquisition failures may be retried; other failures poison the driver. Startup durability is bounded by disk syncs (0.6 ms each with the JSONL store's F_BARRIERFSYNC on APFS, 5 ms with F_FULLFSYNC), so measure entries, syncs and latency when changing acquisition paths.
- `DurableOwner::commit_writes` applies one operation's `OwnerWrite` values in order with one commit and one revision; nothing commits when any write fails, and a BeginStep for an existing step returns its Interrupted or Replay outcome without writing (three commits per tool step took 5.9 ms instead of 18.6 ms). `DurableSession::stage` queues a `SessionWrite` onto the driver's next commit of any kind, including a live agent's owner commits. NotCommitted keeps it queued; any other error peeks the state: the payload at the expected revision means the commit landed, the previous revision is retried once with the same revision-conditional commit, and anything else poisons the driver. `append` commits staged writes at once, `commit_checkpoint` commits a standalone checkpoint with its write, and after either fails with NotCommitted flush with an empty append, because retrying the same write duplicates its events. `MetadataWrite` is a data-only compare-and-set that cannot change identity fields. `DurableOwner::abandon_stale_kinds` registers maintenance kinds, Codex standalone_compaction and Claude compact and session_end, that the driver cancels when unclaimed at the head of the pending queue: once when they are registered, which each backend does right after its owner acquires the session and before any attempt of its own, and again before each admission, attempt and standalone checkpoint; prompt turns are never abandoned.
- A durable state's head keeps its pending operations, the terminal receipts accepted after its last sealed one and references to sealed receipt pages instead of every receipt. Before each commit of a driver without a terminal receipt limit, `JournalState::seal_receipts` seals each terminal receipt that no pending operation precedes into an immutable content-addressed page record of its own (`RECEIPT_LEAF`), and each 16 page references of one level into a page of the next, so the head holds no terminal receipt and fewer than 16 references per level; it used to seal runs of 16 receipts and keep up to 15 in the head, which every commit rewrote, and a Claude tool turn's six head writes now total 11.5 KB instead of 23.7 KB for one extra 0.4 KB record (nanocode-ts Node harness, 100 turns, 2026-10-26); a limit keeps its retained receipts in the head and seals nothing. `SessionStore::with_terminal_receipt_limit(limit)` gives that limit to every session the catalog opens (`session`, `prepare_session`, `resume`, `prepare_resume`, `prepare_resume_loaded` and the Claude and Codex backends' own acquisitions, and their siblings), as `DurableSession::open_with_terminal_receipt_limit` does for one; without it they keep every receipt, and with it each finished operation past the limit leaves the head with its input, output and checkpoint, which a collection then releases. In `output/storage-check` on 2026-10-27, a catalog session of 12 operations, each with three 4 KB continuations and swept after each, held 5 more records every operation without a limit (60 after 12) and 7 from the fourth on with a limit of 1. Every receipt stays available: the driver's acquisition, `SessionStore` reads, `SessionReader::state` and `CheckpointBranch` load the pages, one record read per level, before they use the operations, so idempotent admission, replay and rewind see all of them, and accepting an operation before they are loaded fails. Loaded and sealed receipts live in `JournalState`'s archive, an `Arc` beside the head's own operations that sealing extends (its operations an `imbl::OrdMap`, so extending it while a transition holds the previous state shares the sealed receipts instead of copying every one: until 2026-11-04 it was a `BTreeMap` that each sealed receipt copied whole, 4.7% of a 2,500-turn Codex session's CPU (`vsagents` under `sample`)), so cloning the state for each transition, the prior-operation check of every transition, staging and the head's serialization touch only the head; `operations()` is an `Operations` view over both, and `first_accepted()` answers the oldest without a scan. They used to share one map, which every transition cloned and scanned whole. A head that references pages is state format 5: format 4 heads load and are sealed on their next commit, a head with nothing sealed is still written as format 4, and older binaries reject format 5. Tools that read operations from a stored head follow `sealed.levels` to the page records, as the Worker's measure-faults does.
- Codex completes a model call's step in memory through `DurableOwner::complete_step_deferred`, and the driver persists it with its next commit of any kind, such as the next step's admission, a steer binding or the turn's completion, as Claude's `Journal` defers its completions; a crash or shutdown before that commit runs the model call again instead of replaying it. A tool call that is alone in its batch completes the same way (`complete_step_deferred`), as Claude's do, so a crash after it ran and before the next commit reports its outcome as unknown; calls in a parallel batch stay immediate (see the shared step rule below). A new session's first turn skips the phase-only continuation before its first model call when its warmup sends no request (`websocket_warmup` off, or HTTPS), because a crash before that call restarts the turn from the same state without it, and a follow-on turn's PrepareTurn continuation is applied in memory through `DurableOwner::advance_deferred` and persisted with the next commit, such as a pre-turn compaction's step admission, or replaced by the Generate continuation before it is written; the deferred advance leaves the continuation cache and known records unmarked, so the next continuation stages its records again. Both backends admit steps through one rule in runtime/durability: `DurableOwner::admit_step(operation, writes, step, kind, input, recovery)` applies the writes and the step's admission in memory for a `StepRecovery::Rerun` step (a model call, which a resume runs again anyway) and commits them, with every deferred write before them, for an `AtMostOnce` step (a tool call, which must be recorded before it runs); `ResultTiming::of_batch(calls)` leaves the result of a call alone in its batch for the next commit (`NextCommit`, `DurableOwner::complete_step_at`) and commits that of a call among others at once (`Now`). Claude's policy (`BeginRequest::recovery`, set by `begin_model` and `begin_tool`) and Codex's (`model_call` reruns, every other kind is at most once) both use it, so a round with one tool call commits once, at the tool's admission: a turn with one cell and one tool makes 3 store transactions on either backend (admission, the tool's admission, completion) and a text turn 2. The price is the same on both: a crash during the model call after a lone tool call loses that call's result, which the resumed turn reports as `Tool execution interrupted by a host crash; outcome unknown`, and a tool never runs twice (checked on Codex with a mock that answers that model call after 1.5 s and a reopen during it, on Claude with `claudecrash`). Until 2026-11-04 Codex committed before each model call that followed a tool result, and Claude deferred only the last batch of a round; through `vsagents` (in-memory SQLite) a Codex turn with one cell and one tool now makes 3 transactions instead of 4 (9.3 rows instead of 10.3, a text turn 6.2), and Claude's commits are unchanged (`overhead`: 3 for one round, 6 for four). A receipt names its output items by `output_records`, the history items' own records, and leaves out `pipeline_stats` and `timing`, so a replayed call reports default telemetry; a completed turn's output names the last history item by `final_record` when it is the assistant message whose output text is the final message; and the continuation stores `stats` without zero or null fields. Older binaries refuse all three.
- SharedStore checks `concurrent_handle` once: with handles each operation runs on its own handle and task and flush waits for every earlier operation; otherwise its actor sends mutations already queued for other states together through commit_many, and a read, flush or second mutation of a grouped state ends the group (20 sessions committing 150 transitions each took 2.87 s instead of 17.5 s). Every driver commit carries the current summary when supports_summaries is true, `catalog::summarize` owns the StateSummary mapping, and summary preparation failures are NotCommitted. SessionStore::load renders a running turn from its continuation through `CheckpointFormat::hydrate_continuation` and names it in `StoredSession.in_progress`, `SessionReader` serves clients that hold only a StateReader, and Codex's `ContinuationCache` releases its `SequenceLog` entries only after `commit_writes` confirms them.
- `JsonlStore` keeps one file per session (2026-11-03), where it kept every session in one shared `sessions.jsonl` (28.6 GB on the user's machine that day). `open_home(home)` and its async and background forms store sessions in `<home>/sessions/`, one `<key>.jsonl` per session key, the key being a state ID up to its first `:`, so a root's `:subagents` journal and `:process-routes` state share its file and its companion acquisition stays one atomic append (`acquire_with_companion` refuses states of two files as not committed); a file name percent-encodes every byte outside `[A-Za-z0-9_-]`, a `.` after the first byte excepted. `open(dir)` and `open_async(dir)` take such a directory and refuse a file. A session file is the journal the shared file was, with the same lines, fencing, seals, compaction and stand-ins: the worker opens one when an operation first names it (at most 32 at once, least recently used closed first, two descriptors each), replays it whole (no index sidecar; a synthetic 700 MB session file opened in 1.0 to 2.2 s at load average 100), and never creates one for a read, which answers empty. `list_states` and `list_summaries` read the directory, newest file first, and list each file's key. Session files take two more line kinds: `import` sets a state's owner, at the next fence, and its revision and payload when the state's current owner equals its `after` (absent for none), and `records` adds records under the current owner, so a moved state keeps the revision its payload embeds. Opening a home moves the sessions of a legacy `<home>/sessions.jsonl` into their own files (`migrate.rs`): it opens the legacy journal with its index sidecar and, per session key, writes each state as an `import` owned by `legacy-import-<uuid>` at fence 1 and `records` lines of about 4 MiB (stand-ins resolved, literal `\u{1}` values escaped, values read in legacy file order and coalesced across gaps under 1 MiB into reads of up to 16 MiB) to a temporary file synced with `sync_journal` and linked into place without replacing an existing file, copies the legacy summaries still current, and records in `sessions/.legacy.json` the legacy file's identity and length and each state's revision moved, after a directory sync, every 32 sessions and at the end. When the legacy journal has grown since (an older binary still appends to it), the next open moves each state whose revision changed: an `import` batch with the records the file lacks goes into a file whose state an import still owns, nothing goes into one a writer of this build acquired since (its file wins), and nothing recreates the file of a moved session that was deleted. Before writing a session it checks that at least `HEADROOM_BYTES` (1 GiB, policy so that other writers on the disk keep working) would remain free, and otherwise saves its progress and fails the open naming the bytes needed. The legacy file stays for older binaries, which keep their sessions there; it is no longer read once nothing appends to it, and `sessions.jsonl` and `sessions.jsonl.index` can be deleted when no older binary runs. A one-turn session file holds the instructions and tool chunks a session in the shared journal shared with others (about 40 KB against 13.7 KB). Checked on 2026-11-03 on an APFS clone of the user's journal (28.6 GB, 773 states): the move took 50 s and 486 MB of RSS (243 s before reads were ordered and batched, 108 s with ordering alone) and wrote 752 files of 8.3 GB; `nc sessions --all` listed the same 253 sessions as the previous binary on the legacy file and `nc sessions <id>` printed the same conversation for all 752 state IDs; a session written by the previous binary, moved and resumed by this one kept its context, a turn the previous binary added to a moved session afterwards was moved on the next open and seen by the following resume, and a turn the previous binary added to a session this build had resumed was left out. Over 1,000 session files a listing took 2.5 s once and then as long as one over two sessions.
- store-jsonl takes no file locks: each session file's O_APPEND journal is the linearization point and keeps owner fencing across processes. A writer replays the tail, validates, appends its entries with one write and one disk sync, then replays everything that landed before them and judges its own entries by the rule every reader applies: an acquire needs the next fence and a replacement the current owner and the next revision. On Apple platforms that sync is `fcntl(F_BARRIERFSYNC)` (`sync_journal`; `sync_all` where the filesystem refuses it), which writes the entries to the drive like `fsync` and orders them before any later write: an acknowledged commit survives a process or OS crash, and a power loss can lose only the latest commits, never reorder them, where `sync_all`'s F_FULLFSYNC also flushed the drive's cache. Every durable commit used to pay that flush: on 2026-10-27 on an M1 Max (APFS) a 2 KB append took 5.15 ms with F_FULLFSYNC, 0.64 ms with F_BARRIERFSYNC and 0.04 ms with `fsync`, and a native brain's turn (6 commits: admission, two model steps, the cell, completion) with an empty Code Mode cell and a scripted model took 33.9 ms and now 4.8 ms (`output/native-brain-bench`, a temporary `brain_bench` example over `ClaudeClient::messages_api` and `JsonlStore`; with `MemoryStore` 2.1 ms). The SQLite store already synced with `fsync` (`synchronous = FULL` without `fullfsync`). Other platforms keep `sync_all`. Superseded entries are skipped by every reader and reported as Fenced or Conflict, or retried for acquisitions, and a fence, revision or owner fence beyond the next one is corruption. Multi-entry batches carry a batch id and count and apply all or nothing, commit_many appends independent unbatched lines, a writer after a torn tail starts with a newline, and readers never truncate. Every process sharing a journal must run this protocol; older binaries lock and truncate, so restart every writer after upgrading.
- The legacy shared journal's offset index (`nanocode-jsonl-index-v3`; session files have none and are replayed whole when first opened) is a disposable sidecar bound to the journal identity, committed offset and final indexed entry digest, and any mismatch rebuilds it once from the journal before appending. It is saved at flush, at orderly close and when the idle worker's saved index trails the journal by more bytes than the index file holds, since long-running windows otherwise left openers replaying up to 2.1 GB. Index copies use a 128 KiB buffer: copying and hashing an 11.8 MB sidecar took 37.9 ms with 8 KiB, 18.9 ms with 128 KiB and 18.8 ms with 1 MiB.
- `JsonlStore::compact` is explicit maintenance on the worker and compacts each session file in turn: it keeps every valid acquisition and revision, each state's current payload and every unique record, blanks older payloads so older binaries still read the result, and never prunes records by guessing reachability. It first appends a seal naming its pid; writers whose entries land after a seal poll from 1 ms, doubling to 100 ms (the 12.6 GB production snapshot took 31.4 s to compact, so the cap adds about 0.3%), and retry on the replacement, a dead sealer is unsealed by the next writer, and processes sharing a journal must share a pid namespace. The first acquire entry carries the `nanocode_journal` lineage, `<journal>.compact-<pid>` and `.index-<pid>` temporaries of exited processes are removed at open, and hard-linked journals are rejected.
- `SqliteStore::open`, `open_home` and their async forms put the file in WAL mode with `synchronous = FULL`, as `SqliteSql` already does, so readers do not wait for the writer and a commit stays durable on power loss; a connection a host passes to `from_connection` keeps its own journal mode.
- JsonlStore and SqliteStore are handles to one worker thread each with a one-command job channel: a dispatched mutation or flush runs to completion when its caller drops the future, only reads whose receiver is gone are skipped, dropping the handle never blocks and close awaits the worker. open and open_home validate synchronously before moving the journal or connection to the worker, while open_async, open_home_async and SQLite's from_connection_async do that on the worker. SQLite runs every IMMEDIATE transaction, including busy-timeout waits on another writer, on its worker: a 1 s external write hold left 2.7-4.9 ms maximum heartbeat gaps instead of 1.09 s.
- Local development keeps incremental compilation enabled, omits debug information and skips the post-link strip, which costs about 0.8 s per binary on macOS. .cargo/config.toml compiles workspace crates through scripts/rustc-parallel-frontend, which enables rustc's parallel front end with `-Zthreads=0`; dependencies compile unchanged, clippy keeps its own wrapper, and `RUSTC_WORKSPACE_WRAPPER=` disables it. Release builds use `lto = "off"`: cross-crate thin LTO cost about eleven times the CPU of an edit-to-install rebuild without measurable TUI runtime gains, and the per-crate ThinLTO that `lto = false` still runs cost 37% of an uncached release build (969 against 1,535 CPU-seconds) for 5-9% faster TUI rendering benchmarks. No profile uses fat LTO: on 2026-10-24 dropping it from the wasm profile halved nanocode-ts relinks (16 s against 34 s) with turn times unchanged within run-to-run variation. Use the profiling profile for full symbols and the ci profile for disposable CI builds without incremental artifacts. Measure fresh-target builds separately from cached and edited rebuilds.
- .cargo/config.toml also routes rustc through scripts/buildcache, which shares compiled crates across target directories through ~/.cache/nanocode/build and needs no other tool. The shell script only dispatches: every call runs a helper that it compiles from scripts/buildcache.rs with the calling rustc into ~/.cache/nanocode/build/bin, in the background on first use while those calls run uncached. Clippy units are cached like other workspace units, keyed additionally by the clippy-driver digest, CARGO_PRIMARY_PACKAGE and CLIPPY_* variables, which clippy-driver reads without recording them as env-deps; their lint diagnostics replay from the cached stderr. A registry unit is keyed by rustc version, its arguments without target-directory paths, the identities of its `--extern` inputs and the CARGO_* environment, and its entry is immutable. A workspace unit is keyed the same way plus the workspace root and wrapper contents; its single entry is replaced on each store and is restored only when its `--extern` identities, package and workspace Cargo.toml and every dep-info source digest still match (a unit, or a C object, whose dep-info names a file modified after its compiler started is not stored, since the compiler may have read it before the change: on 2026-10-28 a commit that landed in a checkout during a build stored nanocode-tool-runtime's old rlib under the new sources' digests, and later builds restored code without the new `Hand::environment`), so a fresh target directory of an unchanged tree in the same checkout restores instead of compiling, while other checkouts miss because cargo's `-C metadata` hashes differ there. An output's identity is memoized next to it: the unit key for registry outputs and the rmeta SHA-256 for workspace outputs, written when rustc reports the artifact and before the notification reaches cargo, so pipelined dependents never hash or race it; restores write the recorded identities before replaying stderr. Every restore is a copy-on-write clone checked against the env-deps rustc recorded, and units whose dep-info reaches outside the registry, the workspace or the content-addressed OUT_DIR copies are not stored. Build-script OUT_DIR and native search paths are swapped for content-addressed copies in which cc-rs object names derived from the absolute OUT_DIR, and the matching archive members, are renamed to the names a relative OUT_DIR would produce, so -sys crates key identically in every target directory. C objects compiled by build scripts are cached in direct mode: keyed by compiler version, cwd, arguments, environment and source digest with OUT_DIR mapped out, checked against the digests of every header in the compiler's `-MD` dependency file, and compiled with `-ffile-prefix-map`; sources or headers that use `__DATE__`, `__TIME__` or `__TIMESTAMP__` are not cached. SHA-256 uses the ARMv8 SHA2 instructions when the CPU has them. A target directory filled from the cache has no incremental state, so the first edit there recompiles the affected crates in full. Use `RUSTC_WRAPPER=` for cold-build measurements; deleting ~/.cache/nanocode/build only makes cargo rebuild the crates that referenced it.
- Use cargo check --locked --lib and cargo check --locked --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown for SDK checks. Use explicit -p nanocode-tui or -p nanocode-cli for terminal work; --workspace is an intentional whole-repository check.
- Do not add tests or test-only dependencies. Validate changes with focused native and WASM builds and reproducible runtime measurements.
- Benchmark performance changes with reproducible commands, inputs, and before/after results. Separate cold builds, incremental builds, and runtime performance.
- [Principles for fast Tokio applications](https://dial9-rs.github.io/blog/principles-for-fast-tokio-applications/)
- Write lock-free code: no Mutex, RwLock or Condvar from std, Tokio, futures or parking_lot anywhere in the workspace. Use the nanocode-sync primitives: own mutable state in one task and talk to it over channels, an Actor or a Mailbox, publish shared state as an Atom or ArcSwap value changed copy-on-write with side effects performed after the change commits, use Slot, Signal, atomics and OnceLock for take-once values and flags, Ordered to publish effects in commit order, and Sequencer or Owned only where a protocol requires one caller at a time across awaits.
- nanocode-sync semantics: `Atom::update` clones the value, applies the change and retries the compare-and-swap, so a change may run more than once and must be free of side effects; `try_update` skips the store on None and `modify` on an error. `Signal` fires once for current and future waiters (its sender sits in a `Slot`, so firing and dropping one write no arc-swap value), `Slot` put and take swap atomically and never read in place: `is_empty` reads an occupancy count kept beside the value, because loading the value for the check made a concurrent take or put find it shared and drop it. Its value is a crossbeam-utils `AtomicCell<Option<Box<T>>>`, one pointer-sized atomic swap (lock-free on every target, since the type is pointer-sized), not an `ArcSwapOption`: arc-swap's default strategy makes every store, swap and compare-and-swap pay the outstanding read debts of every thread that ever loaded through arc-swap (`Debt::pay_all`), which a `Slot`, never loaded, gains nothing from. In a `sample` profile of 200 concurrent Claude sessions `pay_all` was 59% of the process's CPU, mostly the event channel's per-event `Slot` swaps; with `AtomicCell` 10,000 one-turn sessions (`claude_pool` binary) used 2.59-2.67 s of user CPU instead of 4.14-4.41 s and first-turn p50 fell from 12.4-15.0 ms to 8.6-9.2 ms, and Codex sessions (`pool`) 6.80-6.85 s instead of 7.60-7.73 s. `Inbox::drain` loads the head first and returns without a swap when it is empty, which every event's drain was. `Ordered` delivers values in ticket order, so every ticket must be published exactly once. `Inbox` pushes by compare-and-swap and drains in push order. `SingleFlight` runs one future per concurrent burst, and a cancelled leader hands the flight to the next caller. `Sequencer` admits one `Turn` at a time in arrival order, and a cancelled `enter` passes an admission it already received on. Its queue keeps the waiters in an `imbl::OrdMap` by ticket, so queuing and passing a turn cost O(log n) with n waiters; it was a `VecDeque` that every `enter` and release copied, which was 67% of the CPU of a 3,000-agent task tree whose registry state had thousands of waiters. Until a caller first has to wait, a sequencer admits and releases with one compare-and-swap on a mode byte (free, held), touching no `Atom`; the first `enter` that finds it held switches it (through a brief `switching` mode, which the holder's release spins past) to the queued mode, records the fast holder in the queue as its holder and waits there, and from then on every turn goes through the queue as before, so arrival order and cancellation behave as they did. 300 rounds of 64 tasks making 50 `enter`/`try_enter` attempts each, some cancelled by a 20 us timeout, held no two turns at once and deadlocked none, and `Owned` counted 64,000 of 64,000 increments (`output/hand-wiring`, `sequencer` binary); 10,000 one-turn Claude sessions used 1.91-1.93 s of user CPU instead of 2.63-2.66 s, first-turn p50 6.7-6.9 ms instead of 8.3-8.9 ms. Every arc-swap store (each `Atom` update, `Inbox` push, queued `Sequencer` turn and `Signal` fire) pays the read debts of every thread that ever loaded through arc-swap, exited threads included: 75 ns with no other threads, 0.4-0.5 us with 8, 1.3-1.5 us with 32, 4.6-6.4 us with 128 and 17-21 us with 512, staying at the high-water mark (`output/arcswap-scan`), so keep frequently written state out of `Atom`s (in an owning task or a `Slot`) and SDK state away from large or growing thread pools. `Owned` moves its value to the admitted caller as `Held` or `Leased` and back before the next turn. `Actor` runs jobs on its own task in call order and cancels a job whose caller drops its future; it needs a Tokio runtime natively or an installed platform host on WASM. `Mailbox<S>` owns a value without a task of its own and runs synchronous jobs on it one at a time in submission order: `send(job)` posts a job and returns, `call(job)` posts one and resolves to its result (a job whose caller dropped the future before it ran is skipped), and whoever posts to an idle mailbox runs the queued jobs on its own thread, its own and those other callers post meanwhile, so a caller never waits for a lock, only for its reply. The value sits in a crossbeam `AtomicCell` beside an unbounded channel of jobs and a count of queued jobs: a job is sent before the count is raised, the poster that raises it from 0 runs jobs, putting the value back before each decrement, until the count returns to 0, so no job is stranded and two runners never hold the value. After 128 jobs (Tokio's cooperative budget per task poll) it hands the rest to a spawned task, so a busy mailbox does not keep one caller from returning; a job that panics is dropped (its `call` panics in the caller) and the value is kept. Jobs must be `Send + 'static`, so they capture owned copies of their arguments. With 64 tasks making 50,000 jobs each, a mailbox ran a job in 248-400 ns against 3.0-8.7 us for `Owned` (output/hand-wiring `mailbox`, 2026-11-03), with none lost or out of order.
- Open question, not reproduced: on 2026-10-26 one run of the admission-gate stress (`output/hand-wiring`, `gate` binary, release build, Apple Silicon, arc-swap 1.9.3) died with SIGSEGV at address 0x6. The faulting thread was cloning an `Atom<GateState>`'s value inside `Atom::update` while five other workers were in arc-swap's hybrid `compare_and_swap`, dropping old values (`Arc::drop_slow`). `Atom::update` is safe code, so the freed value points at arc-swap or something below it, and arc-swap has shipped ARM ordering fixes as recently as 1.9.1. No crash followed in about 115 more runs of that stress, about 70 of the previous gate's, 3 under AddressSanitizer, or 32M operations of a plain-thread arc-swap stress. The crash report, harnesses and next steps (an ASan loop, arc-swap's `rwlock-strategy` for comparison) are in `output/arcswap-crash/NOTES.md` on the machine that saw it. Every `Atom`, `Inbox` and `SingleFlight` is built on arc-swap, so record any recurrence (the .ips, the faulting frame, the arc-swap and rustc versions) here.
- Keep blocking native storage work behind an owning worker and asynchronous message/response channels. Batch complete transactions there, including tail replay, index refresh, serialization and disk sync; native journals take no file locks. Async SDK construction and shutdown must await their completion without running filesystem work or joining worker threads on Tokio workers. Preserve synchronous compatibility entry points and durable acknowledgements.
- Route subagent journal writes through the per-root journal writer. Keep subagent message, residency and journal serialization per root so a slow or fenced store for one root cannot stall another.
- A subagent message the recipient's harness refuses leaves the subagent journal: `deliver_message` journals a message before releasing it to the harness and, when the harness refuses it, journals again after the rollback, so a sender told that its message failed never sees it return. After a journal restore, every message still pending in it goes back to its recipient through the harness admission a live send uses (`HarnessHandle::redeliver`): in message ID order, after the interrupted children's resume notices, with its ID, thread, purpose and priority, so it starts, steers or queues as it would have; a message that cannot be delivered, such as one to a closed child, settles as Failed with a delivery update, and no new message is created.
- A finished root child's completion counts as delivered only when the root turn that carries its notice completes without cancellation. `continue_parent` follows the route: a started turn's result, or for a notice steered into a running turn the root's `run.steered` naming the notice's request ID (Codex names it now, as Claude already did) and that turn's `run.completed` or `run.failed`. A notice the root accepted as an identified steer before a reopen produces no new `run.steered`, because the backend answers its route from the durable receipt and the resumed turn skips steers it already delivered, so a carrying turn that completes without one counts the notice as read when the root's steer receipt for its request ID is `Accepted` (`Agent::steer_receipt`). A completed turn acknowledges the completion; a failed turn, or one that ended before the steer was read, takes the next attempt and retries with the completion backoff, and the attempt suffixes the notice's request ID (`subagent-completion:<child session>:<revision>:<attempt>`; the first keeps `…:<revision>`), so idempotent admission cannot answer a retry with the turn that failed; an execution-policy retry keeps the ID and a stopped agent leaves the completion to the next owner. A cancelled carrying turn parks the completion, and while one is parked every other completion of that root parks too: parked completions are journaled with their attempt (agent records gain `completion_parked` and `completion_attempt`, which older binaries reject), are left out of `SubagentActivity::pending_completions`, so `settle` reports the root quiescent, and start no turn, until the root accepts a prompt that is not a completion notice, into which they are steered. The work a root turn delegated parks too when that turn is cancelled, so cancelled work never starts a root turn on its own: while a root has running children or parked completions, the subagent runtime watches its `input.accepted`, `run.completed` and `run.failed` events, attributes each child to the root turn whose `spawn_agent` call created it, or after a restart to the turn whose request ID or turn ID equals the child's recorded host context (a root without a host context of its own gives its children the spawning turn's operation), and when that turn ends cancelled while the agent is not stopping it journals the child's current instruction revision (agent records gain `cancelled_revision`, which older binaries reject); a completion settled at or below that revision parks, and one that follows a later instruction, such as a message from the next user turn, is delivered as usual. The revision stays recorded after the completion settles, so a restore can tell a child whose mark never reached the journal: when a process exits after the cancel but before that write, the cancelled turn is already terminal and nothing replays it, so restore asks the store (`ChildJournalStore::cancelled_turns`, through `SubagentStore::cancelled_turns`) which recorded host contexts of running root children without a mark name a root operation recorded as cancelled, and gives those children the revision their resumed work settles at, journaled with the resume attempts. Delivery is at least once.
- A notice turn that fails with a definite error (`BackendFailure::retryable` false, such as a 403 without `x-should-retry: true`, a 400 or a replayed failure) parks its completion as a cancelled notice turn does: journaled as parked with its attempt, left out of `SubagentActivity::pending_completions` and `deliver_completions`, and steered into the next prompt the root accepts that is not a completion notice, also after a restart, under the next attempt's request ID. A retryable failure leaves the notice turn pending and retries it under the same request ID after `completion_retry_delay` (2 s doubling to at most 300 s), each retry spending the backend's in-process attempts. Cancellation, stops and execution-policy dispositions are unchanged, and a carrying user turn that fails or ends before reading a steered notice still takes the next attempt. This replaces the retry of a failed notice turn described above, so hosts that refuse notice turns once the spawning turn's authority is gone (`completion_origin`), as Pact does, no longer get one every 2, 4, 8, 16 s indefinitely.
- A finished root child's result that wait_agent or list_agents returns counts as read only when a model request carrying that tool output completes. The output holds a `ToolReceipt` (`ToolOutput::with_receipt`) naming the call whose output carries it, at first the wait call itself; Code Mode holds the receipts of nested calls (`carried_by(None)`) and names the exec or wait call that returns the cell's finished output, the embedded runner at the cell's terminal observation and `RemoteClient` when a terminal reply arrives; `RemoteClient` keeps them by callback origin apart from the callback state, because a runner reports a finished cell (`DetachedComplete`) before the reply that returns its output, and holds them weakly so receipts of settled results are pruned, so a yielded chunk, a cell nobody collects and a failed host reply carry nothing.
