The subagent runtime claims the completion, so no notice duplicates it and list_agents still returns it; every later output that returns a result a tool output routes joins that result's readers, each result has its own watcher, and a result routed to a notice is left to the notice; follows the root's shared `tool.result`, `model.call.started` and `model.call.completed` events, and acknowledges it when the model call that started after the carrier's `tool.result` completes; a root turn that ends first sends it as a notice, a cancelled one parks it as a cancelled notice turn does, and a wait that returns while the root runs no turn, from a cell that outlived its turn, sends the notice at once. The root watcher tracks whether the root runs a turn from `run.started` and its terminal events. A root without a resolvable agent acknowledges at return.
- A reopened root's parked completions are released by the next prompt it accepts, which the subagent runtime observes through the root watch that restoration arms when it finishes. `TurnInbox::drive` first awaits that restoration and arms the watch with its own agent (`SubagentControl::await_restored`), so an inbox host may drive at once; a host that prompts the `Agent` directly calls `await_restored` before its first prompt after a reopen.
- `TurnInbox::steer` and `move_queued` reach any root turn the agent admitted under a request ID, not only the turn `drive` runs from its log: a subagent completion turn, a Claude background-command notice, and an SDK-pending turn `drive` resumes after a restart. `Agent::turn_controls()` lists the `TurnControl` of every unsettled turn admitted under a request ID, oldest first. `steer(None, …)` offers the steer to each in turn until the backend takes it, `steer(Some(id), …)` only to that request's turn, and `cancel` finds a running turn the same way. A turn whose cancellation the host requested, or that `drive` admits only to cancel, refuses steers, because a Claude turn admitted to cancel takes steers until its run ends and never reads them. Only turns `drive` runs from the log map their Codex turn IDs to the request; a resumed or completion turn's events stay under its own turn ID. A steer into a completion turn leaves the child's completion accounting unchanged, and a steer accepted before a restart is answered from its durable receipt, so the resumed turn reads it once and `drive` withdraws a moved prompt instead of running it. Hosts that let a user steer the work a completion turn continues, such as Pact, name the completion's request ID from `turn_controls()`.
- Restoring a subagent journal reads every agent record in one batch and every profile in a second (`SubagentStore::load_records`, `ChildJournalStore::load_records`, which the durable journal serves with `EncodedPayload::load_many` and, for keys it cannot load that way, one load per key as before); it used to read each agent's record and then its profile, two sequential reads per agent ever journaled, at every reopen, which tempoxyz/pyramid does at the start of every turn. The subagent journal `<root>:subagents` is format 6 (2026-11-04): agent state records are stored inline in leaves of 16 consecutive agent IDs (`LEAF_AGENT_BITS`; 16 records of the 600 to 700 bytes one takes stay under `EncodedPayload`'s 16 KiB inline bound, so a leaf is one record, while leaves of 32 crossed it and were chunked and leaves of 8 wrote 21 to 27% more records for the same bytes), under a tree of 64-entry pages of leaf keys whose top the payload holds, and messages inline in leaves of 64 consecutive log positions under a second such tree, so a reopen reads each level of either tree in one batch where format 5 read its message pages one at a time along their chain; each agent's record references a profile record holding the descriptor, binding task, output schema and host context, written only when it changes. At 5 x 2,000 children on SQLite (two interleaved runs at load average 200 to 232) the journal held 28,154 and 31,270 records instead of 109,924 and 117,222 (83 and 97 MB instead of 71 and 76 MB, since a leaf rewrites its unchanged agents too), the database 158,221 and 161,199 instead of 239,791 and 246,951, and a reopen restored the 10,000 agents in 0.51 and 0.77 s instead of 1.47 and 1.49 s. The journal names every record by the key its store files it under, `EncodedPayload`'s: the BLAKE3 hash of the content below 16 KiB and of its manifest above. Builds from b22a444ae until 2026-10-19 named large records by their content hash, so a Codex child's checkpoint (38 KB, mostly its instructions and tool definitions) or a profile with a long task failed with `missing payload record` after a restart, every delegation tool of that root failed with it and the session never became quiescent. The child journal store resolves a key it cannot find by listing the journal's records once per journal (`StateStore::list_records`), assembling every manifest root and matching the BLAKE3 hash of its content (`EncodedPayload::manifests_by_content`), and `matches_key` accepts that content key, so journals those builds wrote load again; records written after a restore use manifest keys, and a child's old checkpoint key is replaced by its next checkpoint. A restored child that cannot resume, such as one whose checkpoint is still unreadable, settles as Failed with an error naming the cause and its in-flight calls, as a failed turn does, so the rest of the tree resumes and the root gets a completion turn for its direct children; a journal that cannot be restored at all keeps failing every delegation tool of its root with the restoration error, writes nothing over it, and counts as quiescent for `settle`, because nothing from it can run. Versions 1 to 5 still load and older binaries reject 6. Every ChildSession change goes through `Sessions::get_mut` or `insert`, which assign a fresh journal generation; a save encodes every leaf that holds a changed agent from the live sessions, since the writer keeps no agent's JSON (only its generation, checkpoint and profile keys, and the JSON of the messages in the last, unfilled message leaf), and an unchanged agent in such a leaf reuses its profile key and checkpoint reference instead of encoding its checkpoint again; the writer updates its cache only after the store acknowledges a save.
- A root's task tree runs 10,000+ subagents on the SQLite and Postgres stores. output/hand-wiring's `swarm` drives an `AgentRuntime` over `SqlStore` (`SqliteSql` or `PostgresSql`) with an in-process Messages mock: `SWARM flat N` spawns N root children from one cell, `SWARM tree K x M` spawns K coordinators that each spawn M workers, `SWARM waited N` spawns N and waits on each with `wait_agent`; every worker messages its predecessor and submits, and the run then reopens the store and restores the tree. On master (2026-11-02, load average 23 to 50) 1,000 root children settled on SQLite in 10.3 s and 22.8 s of CPU, the root's 1,138 notice turns sending 384 MB of requests, a 1,010-agent tree took 11.5 s on SQLite and 56.3 s on Postgres (418 MB of database, mostly dead tuples of the rewritten journal head), and a 3,030-agent tree never settled: children evicted before their first turn began stayed `Running` with no harness. Now 1,000 root children take 4.0 s and 7.9 s of CPU with 8 root requests (1.4 MB); the 1,010-agent tree 2.8 s on SQLite and 11.0 s on Postgres (67 MB); 3,030 agents 21 s and 42 s; 10,100 (100 coordinators of 100) 104 s on SQLite and 153 s on Postgres, restored in 2.9 s and 1.3 s; 10,000 root children 117 s on SQLite with 15 root requests, restored in 3.7 s. Peak RSS (5.3 to 5.9 GB at 10,000) is set by the cells and harnesses running at once. What changed: (1) `TaskTree` indexes agents by session and children by parent; `agent_for_session`, which nearly every registry call made, scanned every agent, and postorders were quadratic. (2) Eviction never takes a `Pending` child, whose first turn has not started (evicting it stopped the turn as it began: 164 of 3,000 children stuck in one run), and picks among resident agents only, where it compared every pair. (3) Each agent has a residency `Sequencer`: a delivery holds its recipient's, and while rehydrating them its evicted ancestors' (child first), eviction takes it with `try_enter`, and the scope's message lock was held only while a message was prepared, enqueued and committed (since replaced by one registry job, below); the scope-wide residency and message locks were held across the journal save and the recipient's admission, so a tree's messages went one at a time. (4) The registry's per-agent `Atom` maps, `CodeModeExecutors`' per-session maps, `RunnerAgents`' agent lists and the runtime's children are `imbl` persistent maps (the runtime prunes dead children when the list's length is a power of two); every update copied the whole map, 35% of the CPU at 3,000 agents. (5) Journal format 5 keeps agent record keys in a tree of 64-entry pages (leaves by agent ID / 64, each level above by page index / 64, the payload holding the top level, at most 64 pages, and its height), and the writer encodes only agents marked changed since its last successful save (`Sessions::get_mut` and checkpoint inserts mark them; a writer's first save encodes every agent) and messages appended since (a removal or an out-of-order commit re-encodes the list), where every save wrote one key per agent (900 KB at 10,000); restore reads each page level, every message record and every agent record in one batch each. (6) A child's available events are tracked under one state acquisition, a progress capture reads its target and settles it in one each, and session-to-root lookups read a lock-free mirror: about 100 acquisitions of the registry state per agent each waited 10 ms at 3,000 agents. (7) `wait_agent` wakes on the listed agents' own `watch` (sent when a session changes and someone listens), not on every registry change. (8) Root children's completions go through one task per root: those that arrive while it routes are routed together, per host context, as one notice joining their prompts under `subagent-completion:<first child session>:<revision>:batch-<b3 key of the members>`, each acknowledged, retried or parked as it would be alone; a lone completion keeps its request ID and prompt, and delivery stays at least once. A root turn's steers and steer receipts live in its durable head, so one steer per notice made every notice rewrite all of them. (9) `list_agents` takes `after_agent_id` and `limit` and answers `next_after_agent_id` while more agents remain. (10) `spawn_agent` reads the parent's settings before the root's spawn admission and runs the spawn on its own task, aborted when the call is dropped, as it already did on WASM, so a cell's `Promise.all` of spawns uses every worker; a new child reads its parent's `SessionRecord` (`SessionStore::record`) for its lineage instead of loading the parent's whole session. (11) A Code Mode cell waited for a QuickJS worker thread when the OS refused one instead of failing: macOS allows 8,192 threads per process, and 1,913 of 10,100 workers whose cells all ran at once failed with `Script failed`; cells now run on a pool of worker threads (below). Half the database is the journal's child checkpoints, which progress captures write several times per turn although a durable child's own session holds the same state (300 MB of 620 MB of records at 10,100 agents).
- Native Code Mode cells run on a process-wide pool of QuickJS worker threads instead of one thread per host (`code-mode-engine/src/native.rs`): `available_parallelism()` workers, each multiplexing many QuickJS runtimes (still one runtime per cell host, so isolation is unchanged) as an event loop over host messages and an ordered set of timer deadlines. `EmbeddedHost::spawn` picks a worker that is not running JavaScript and has a spare runtime (or the least loaded one). A host holds a runtime only while it has a cell running or yielded (since 2026-11-04): a cell's start takes one of its worker's spares, or a new runtime, and the cell's end returns it after the collection that frees the cell's context, so a host between cells holds none. A worker keeps as many runtimes, running and spare, as it ran at once in the last second (`SPARE_WINDOW`, counting the current one), at least one, and trims its spares at each window; only a spare returned to an empty list gets a fresh context, the rest wait bare and prepare one when a cell takes them, because under a swarm's bursts most spares were trimmed again unused, each freeing the context it had been given (at 5 x 2,000 children on SQLite, load average 169 to 193, `JS_FreeRuntime` fell from 6.8% to 3.5% of the process's samples and user CPU from 34.2 and 34.4 s to 32.2 and 33.2 s, while output/hand-wiring's `cell` kept a first cell's p50 at 0.31 to 0.44 ms against 0.29 to 0.34 ms). Until then a host kept its runtime from its open to its close and returned it with a fresh context; before that it kept the peak of live hosts in the window: a swarm's children hold their hosts while their cells wait, so a worker saw thousands live and prepared a fresh context for every host that closed, needed or not; late in a 5 x 25,000 swarm, when children finished and nothing opened new hosts, 90% of the process's samples were in `Prepared::new` under a host's close (at 5 x 10,000 the CPU and settle time did not change: 195.8 and 202.3 s of user CPU before, 197.6 and 197.4 after). Natively a session no longer keeps an idle host in its `CodeModeRuntime` slot; WASM keeps per-session reuse, since its hosts prepare contexts later. The trade-off: a cell running synchronous JavaScript for a long time delays the other cells on its worker, which a thread per host did not; new hosts avoid a busy worker. On 2026-11-02 (output/hand-wiring's `engine` binary, CPU per cell) 5,000 cells each awaiting a 50 ms timer took 0.49-0.53 ms instead of 1.23-1.31 ms, 1.4-1.7 GB instead of 2.2 GB and a p50 of 0.16-0.25 s instead of 0.92-0.95 s, trivial and 16-tool cells cost what they did (0.38-0.40 ms and 0.61-0.66 ms), and the 10,000-agent swarm ran 170-430 threads instead of 3,100 to 8,192 (the macOS limit, which `EmbeddedHost::spawn_available` waited on). output/hand-wiring's `cells` printed the same output for its 33 cells.
- A 10,000-agent swarm on SQLite starts in half the time and settles in less than half (output/hand-wiring `swarm sqlite <path> 'SWARM flat 10000'` with `AGENTS=10000`, which prints when every `spawn_agent` returned and when every child sent its first model request; load average 8 to 15, 2026-11-02): every child added in 3.8-4.0 s instead of 8.1 s, every child started in 6.8-7.1 s instead of 14.0 s, settled in 22 s instead of 53 s, 75 s of CPU instead of 110 s; RSS 9.5-10.3 GB instead of 8.8 GB (the larger SQLite page cache and the cell spares). A 3,030-agent tree settles in 5.8 s instead of 8.5 s on SQLite. What changed: (1) the QuickJS pool above. (2) Eviction keeps an index of resident and idle children in `Sessions` (refreshed from the sessions `get_mut` and `insert` touched since the last check) and checks only idle candidates in last-use order, testing whether a candidate holds a resident descendant through its own subtree; `take_resident_eviction` scanned every session, computed every resident's ancestors and sorted all candidates under the registry state, 318 µs per call and 9.5 s of the 10.4 s the state was held at 10,000 agents, which serialized every other registry call behind it. (3) `admit_spawn` enters the root's spawn admission only while its journal restoration has not finished; afterwards spawns waited in its queue one registry acquisition at a time (2.3 s each at 10,000). (4) The subagent tools' definitions are memoized process-wide (`memoized`, keyed by tool, the spawn policy and whether the root is continued), where every child parsed and cloned their JSON schemas each time a definition was read, about 175 KB of live schema JSON per running child. (5) A child whose session lives in the same store as its root (`SubagentStore::records_sessions` false: the persistent recipe resumes it from its own state and drops the journal's copy) stops writing progress captures once the journal holds a checkpoint with its conversation, which restore needs to skip the assignment prompt; every tool boundary wrote one (9 per child), 13% of the CPU and 105 MB of the database at 10,000. (6) `SharedStore` groups every commit queued, not the first 64 (its command channel is unbounded: each caller awaits its reply, which bounds what is queued), carries a second commit of a state already in the group to the next group instead of ending it, and sends reads and acquisitions that arrive meanwhile on the pipeline instead of ending the group: groups grew from 21 to about 1,000 commits. (7) `SqliteSql` runs WAL checkpoints on a connection of its own (`wal_autocheckpoint = 0` and a `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(PASSIVE)` after each transaction), where automatic checkpoints ran inside commits and took 31% of the writer thread, and sets a 256 MiB page cache and a 4 GiB memory map: with the 2 MiB default cache, inserts keyed by record hashes read pages back on almost every statement (`unixRead` 22% of the writer); together they took the store's commit latency from 1.8 s to 0.2 s at 10,000.
- `PostgresSql::pool(clients)` runs the store over several tokio-postgres connections, each taken whole by one transaction (`Owned`, tried round robin), and every `SELECT`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE` or `WITH` statement is prepared once per connection (`prepare_typed` with text parameters) instead of sent as an unnamed statement that Postgres parsed and planned on every execution; a batch holding any other statement, such as migrations' DDL, still runs unprepared. `SharedStore`'s grouped driver runs a group on the store's pipeline handle and keeps dispatching reads and acquisitions meanwhile, one group at a time, where it awaited each group and every read queued behind it; a flush still waits for everything before it. Groups stay one at a time on Postgres too: concurrent groups serialized on the shared records every child names (the instructions template), each `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` locking the row for its whole transaction, and settled slower. A store-backed child (`records_sessions` false) records a journal checkpoint only until one with its conversation is recorded: status settles and evictions skipped it after progress captures already had, where each wrote a 4 KB copy of a checkpoint its own session holds (two per child), 137 MB of 752 MB at 10,000 children. On 2026-11-02 (load average 15 to 25) the 3,030-agent tree settled in 9.5-11 s on Postgres instead of 16-18 s and in 4.8-5.5 s on SQLite; 10,000 root children on Postgres stay bound by the one writer (Postgres spent 60% of its backend time in `heap_hot_search_buffer`, walking versions of the rows a long transaction rewrote) and took 67-190 s.
- Claude defers a model step's admission (`begin_model`, kinds `model` and `model_call`) to the next commit instead of committing it before the request: the SDK policy's `commit_writes` sends model admissions through `DurableOwner::commit_writes_deferred`, which applies the writes and the step's begin to the driver's state within the current revision and persists them with the next commit of any kind, such as the following tool step's admission or the turn's settlement, as `advance_deferred` and `complete_step_deferred` already did. A crash during the model call therefore finds no record of it and sends it again, as a crash before its admission always did; tool admissions still commit before the tool runs. A child's turn made 5 commits and now makes 3: at 10,000 root children the store took 50,000 commits instead of 80,000 and the 3,030-agent tree 15,100 instead of 24,200 (2026-11-02), settling in 4.3 s on SQLite and 9.3 s on Postgres.
- `SharedStore` runs a store's reads and acquisitions next to its grouped commits when the store offers `StateStore::pipeline_handle()` (default none): each peek, owner, record read, listing and acquisition then runs on its own handle, commits and replacements still group through `commit_many` on the actor, and a flush waits for every pipelined operation. Each read used to wait for the one before it to come back through the actor, about a millisecond per hop on a loaded runtime, four per subagent. `SqlStore` offers a handle when its host answers `Sql::concurrent(self: Arc<Self>)` (default none; `SqliteSql` and `PostgresSql` answer themselves, `SqlxSql` and `SqlxTransaction` do not), and its handles share the migrated flag. `PostgresSql` sends a transaction's statements together with `query_typed` and text parameters, one round trip for the whole transaction, where `query` prepared, executed and closed each statement in turn (three round trips per statement); the server still runs them in order, so a statement may use a table an earlier one created.
- The subagent journal writes behind: `SharedJournal`'s writer (`child_journal.rs`) acknowledges a save once it is queued on the `SharedStore` instead of once it commits, coalesces saves into the newest write the store has not yet taken (`SharedStore::replace_latest` sends a `ReplaceLatest` whose `LatestWrite` slot keeps the latest payload and the union of the records staged since; the store takes it when it forms a group, and a later save then queues a new write expecting the revision after it), serves records it queued but has not committed from an overlay (`load_record`, `load_records`), and settles writes in order: a failed write keeps its records for the next save, which first waits for every write in flight, re-reads ownership when the failure was not `NotCommitted` or `Fenced`, and returns the first failure. So a waiter told that its journal write succeeded may lose it in a crash, but nothing ordered after it survives without it: `SharedStore`'s grouped driver now takes writes strictly in arrival order (a write after one for a state already in the group, or while a group is in flight, waits for the next group, with every write behind it), so a commit queued after the acknowledgement, such as the parent's `spawn_agent` result, lands in the same transaction or a later one; this holds when the root's session and its journal share one `SharedStore`, as `AgentRuntime`'s store does. `SqlStore::commit_many` stores each record of a group once: on SQLite one unconditional insert per distinct key, then each commit's holds and head as before; on Postgres three statements for the whole group, an upsert of the distinct records sorted by key (locking each once for the transaction), the holds of every commit whose owner and revision match, and the heads (`json_to_recordset`), without the replacement's hold check, which the holds statement makes true under the owner and record locks it took. Each commit used to insert the records it named, so the instructions and tool records every child shares were rewritten by every commit of a group: on Postgres each `DO UPDATE` added a version of the same row to one long transaction, and the per-commit hold check planned as a nested loop over the state's holds, which for the journal (200,000 holds at 10,000 children) took 27 to 67 s per group. Records of a refused commit in a group may stay stored with no state holding them. `PostgresSql` sets `plan_cache_mode = force_custom_plan` on each connection before it prepares statements: generic plans chosen while the tables were empty kept that nested loop after they grew. On 2026-11-03 (load average 22 to 28, output/hand-wiring `swarm`, `SWARM flat 10000`, one run each) 10,000 root children settled in 35 s on Postgres instead of 142 s and in 18.1 s on SQLite instead of 23.9 s, a journal persist waited 0.15 s instead of 15.8 s (Postgres) and 0.14 s instead of 1.09 s (SQLite), and a commit 1.7 s instead of 8.8 s on Postgres; the 3,030-agent tree settled in 4.9 s on SQLite and 8.3 s on Postgres, 2,000 waited children in 3.5 s and 7.0 s, and every run restored its tree. The database grows (655 MB instead of 555 MB on SQLite): each save still encodes its own agent pages and records, so the coalesced writes store page versions that the payload they commit no longer names (on Postgres 183 MB of the 433 MB of records were agent pages, nearly all of them superseded).
- A live agent holds about half the memory it did. Tool definitions share their text: `nanocode_types_core::ToolDefinition`'s `description` fields are `Arc<str>` (constructors take `impl Into<Arc<str>>`, so `&str`, `String` and `Box<str>` still work) and `JsonSchema::shared()` hands out its `Arc<Value>`, and the Claude client's `ToolDefinition` holds `description: Arc<str>` and `input_schema: Arc<Value>` (serialized as before), so `claude_tools::install`, request templates, side-question templates and cursor templates clone pointers where each copied every schema and description (the `exec` description and a description with an output schema appended are built per install and reused while a tool of that name keeps the same text). The encoded request template a cursor records (`TemplatePrompt`, 24 KB of system prompt and tools) is shared process-wide while the next agent's system and tools are equal. `wait_spec` and the `hands` tool definition are built once per process. The per-agent channels box their messages (the durable session's `CommandRequest`, the subagent harness's commands and deliveries), since a Tokio channel allocates a block of 32 slots of its message when it is created: the durable session's was 16 KB. The registry's per-agent status queue runs a drainer only while it has jobs (a counter of queued jobs, the receiver handed between drainers through a `Slot`) instead of a task per agent for the agent's lifetime. On 2026-11-03 (output/hand-wiring `swarm` with `LATENCY_MS=8000 AGENTS=3000`, `SWARM flat 3000` on SQLite, `heap -s` one second after every child sent its first model request, so every agent waits on the model with no cell running) the process held 701-703 MB of malloc'd memory instead of 1,351-1,367 MB, 234 KB per agent instead of 452, and 925-1,035 MB of RSS instead of 1,393-1,487; peak RSS fell from 7.3-9.3 GB to 4.3 GB at 10,000 root children, from 3.1 GB to 2.2 GB for the 3,030-agent tree on SQLite and from 3.1 GB to 1.8 GB on Postgres (load average 60 to 110, so their times are not comparable). The system prompt is shared too: `MessagesRequest::system` is `Option<Arc<Value>>` (mutated copy-on-write; `apply_cache_ttl` copies only when a block lacks its TTL), the default Claude Code blocks are built once per process, an agent builds its system value once and takes the process's previous one when equal, and the request body a native `post` sends is the prepared wire's bytes (`Bytes::from_owner` over its `Arc<str>`) instead of a copy of them. The same snapshot then held 583-585 MB instead of 693-700 MB, 195 KB per agent. What a waiting agent still holds (malloc stacks of the same run): its request body (about 20 KB, mostly the system prompt and tools every agent sends alike) and the futures of its durable driver, harness and turn (about 45 KB).
- The subagent registry's state is a `Mailbox<RegistryState>` instead of an `Owned` lock: every registry operation is a job posted to it (`call` when the caller needs the result, `send` for updates nobody waits on, such as tracking a child's tool calls and settling its progress capture), so concurrent agents never queue for the state, and the job that wins runs the others back to back on its thread. Operations that took the lock twice with work between, such as preparing, enqueuing and committing a message or publishing a message's state and marking it terminal, are one job each, and updates sent while the lock was held are sent from the job. The scope's message lock is gone: a delivery (and a redelivery, a side question's harness lookup and a resident eviction) runs in one job that waits only while a close pauses the scope's messages (`Registry::when_messages_flow`, `MessagePause`, a count in the scope that interrupt, close, close-all and cancel-all raise for their duration under a `closing` sequencer and lower when the pause drops, waking waiters through the registry's revision), so deliveries still wait for a close as they waited for its lock, and no longer for each other. At 10,000 root children on SQLite the registry lock was taken 435,904 times with waits of 194 ms on average and 3.3 s at most (load average 73, 2026-11-03); the mailbox answered 412,143 calls in 40.7 ms on average and 778 ms at most (load average 88 to 104), and the run settled in 44 s instead of 71 s. Each agent still makes about 41 registry calls, and under load a runner that the OS deschedules holds up the jobs behind it. A progress capture also encodes the child's journal checkpoint on its own task instead of through `spawn_blocking`: with 10,000 children the captures kept Tokio's blocking pool busy, and 37.6% of the process's samples were in `parking_lot`'s `lock_slow` on the pool's shared mutex (taking work, spawning it and the idle threads' condvar); without it none were, the process ran 25 threads, and the run settled in 49.7 s at load average 67.
- At five roots of 25,000 subagents the write path is what limits a swarm, and these keep it moving. (1) `SharedStore`'s grouped driver forms a group only when none is in flight and takes it from the front of its queue, stopping at the first second write of a state (strict FIFO) or at a barrier (a flush, or a read on a store without a pipeline handle); reads go to the pipeline as they arrive unless a barrier is queued. It used to move every queued request into a new queue on each wakeup, so with 125,000 children its thread spent 92% of its samples in `memmove` and children waited minutes for their first commit. (2) The subagent journal is written deferred: `ChildJournalStore::save_deferred` (default `None`, `SharedJournal` implements it, `SubagentStore::save_deferred` forwards it) queues a `Command::ReplaceDeferred` whose content is a future, and the journal writer acknowledges every persist request while such a write is queued and untaken; when the store forms the group that holds it, the future asks the writer (through the `JournalFill` sender) for the payload and records, which it encodes from the registry state at that moment and the store then stages, so one encode covers every change since the previous group and the journal stores one version of each agent page per group instead of one per save. A close's final journal waits for the open slot to be filled first. After a fill the writer waits for the next registry change or request before it reserves another write: as first committed (24ef1d91f) it reserved one at once, and since a background write is always wanted, the store's next group asked for another fill with nothing changed, so under `nc exec --ephemeral` (an in-memory store) a root with three children encoded its journal about 40,000 times a second from its first spawn until exit, used 1m42s of CPU in a 90 s run and wrote 12.4 GB of OTLP traces while dropping spans past the export queue; the same run now encodes it 44 times (the build before 24ef1d91f, 95). At 5 x 2,000 children (2026-11-03) journal encodes fell from 70,000-78,000 to 133-217, the database from 627 MB to 400 MB (SQLite) and 662 MB to 393 MB (Postgres), and group time from 48 s to 33 s and 79 s to 36 s. (3) A child whose session lives in the journal's store (`records_sessions` false) no longer captures its whole checkpoint into the journal at its first tool boundary to tell restore that it has a conversation: its agent record gains `conversation: true` (journaled in `AgentRecord`, which older binaries reject), restore skips the assignment prompt for it, and every later capture, status settle and eviction already skipped such children; the capture serialized the child's snapshot and was a third of the process's CPU at 125,000 children. (4) `SqlStore::commit_many` shares one plan across dialects (distinct records once, then each commit's holds and head, commits sorted by state): Postgres runs it as three set-based statements, the records one now `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE ... WHERE false`, which locks an existing record against collection without writing a new version of it (every group used to rewrite every shared record), and none of them sorts (the records arrive sorted by key and the heads by state; the sort spilled to disk past `work_mem`); SQLite, which pays little per statement and parses JSON per access, runs one prepared insert per distinct record and a hold and head statement per commit (the set-based form over `json_each` took 70% longer). (5) `SqliteSql` sets `temp_store = MEMORY`, a 64-statement cache, and serves select-only transactions from read-only connections (one per CPU, on their own threads, WAL readers) while one connection writes. Each multi-row insert in a transaction kept a statement journal in a temporary file: the hold insert took 228 us and the head upsert 73 us, and take 27 us and 12 us in memory, so a 5 x 2,000 run's group time fell from 34 s to 8 s. (6) A finished child turn checks its root's resident limit through one loop per root (`Registry::evicting`): a turn that ends while the loop runs makes it run once more instead of starting a check of its own, and the eviction scan rules a candidate out by its pending messages and harness before it walks the candidate's subtree, which it now walks only for a candidate with children. Every finished turn used to start its own check, each scanning the idle children and allocating each one's subtree, and late in a 5 x 10,000 run the registry's mailbox spent 92% of its samples in that scan. At 5 x 10,000 on SQLite (2026-11-03, two interleaved runs each at load average 20 to 33) the swarm settled in 133 s and 116 s instead of 145 s and 139 s. (7) Acquisitions are grouped writes: `StateStore::acquire_many(Vec<(state, owner)>)` (default one `acquire` each; `CachedStore` forwards it) acquires states together, and `SqlStore` does it in one transaction on both dialects, a set-based owner upsert returning each fence and one read of the heads, falling back to one acquisition at a time for a single state or repeated states. `SharedStore`'s grouped driver treats an `acquire` or `acquire_with` as a write of its state, so the acquisitions at the front of its queue join the group with the commits beside them: the group acquires them in one `acquire_many`, runs each update against its acquired state and commits the replacements in the group's `commit_many`, where a replacement that fails reports the same error as before (`state update failed after acquiring its owner`). Each acquisition used to be a barrier that ran alone, as two transactions (the acquisition and the initial checkpoint), while every other write waited behind it: at 5 x 10,000 on SQLite (2026-11-03) an `acquire_with` took 10.9 s on average and now 0.7 to 0.9 s, in 6 acquisition groups, every child sent its first model request after 12.7 to 17.6 s instead of 25.9 s, and at 5 x 2,000 the swarm settled in 7.2 s and 9.0 s instead of 13.0 s and 11.1 s, with 4.5 to 5.6 s of system CPU instead of 8.9 to 9.2; on Postgres 5 x 2,000 settled in 17.2 s and 20.1 s against 17.8 s and 19.3 s, as its time goes to the commit groups. (8) The journal stages an agent's profile record only when its key differs from the one the journal last wrote for that agent, and an agent record only when its key differs from the agent's last one (`CachedAgent::profile`): every change to an agent re-encoded its record and staged its unchanged profile again, which the store then inserted with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` and held again. At 5 x 10,000 on SQLite (2026-11-03) the writer ran 1.48 million record inserts instead of 2.17 million; at 5 x 5,000, 117,569 of the 500,000 records staged had been profiles already stored. (9) While a group commits, the next one forms: `SharedStore`'s grouped driver keeps taking queued writes into the forming group and starts each one's deferred content (a journal fill) at once (`Grouped::prefetch`), so a group no longer waits for its fills after the one before it finished. A store whose host takes several transactions at once runs that many groups in flight (`StateStore::group_writers`, default 1, `SqlStore` asks `Sql::writers`, default 1, which `PostgresSql` answers with its connection count; `CachedStore` forwards it), each with states no other group in flight holds: the forming group stops before a write of a state in flight while a writer is free, and otherwise waits for that state's group before it starts. SQLite and every other store keep one group at a time. `output/hand-wiring`'s `sqlbench` (125,000 states, 7 rounds of 10,000-commit groups, 4 new records and 1 shared record per commit, 2026-11-03) committed 7,000 commits/s to Postgres with one writer and 13,400 with 8, and 5 x 10,000 on Postgres settled in 126 s instead of 146 s. Postgres reads a group's records with `json_to_recordset` over `{key, value}` objects instead of indexing `[key, value]` arrays, which took a 40,000-record group's scan from 250 ms to 188 ms, and record keys are encoded without copying each one into an owned `String` first. (10) `SqliteSql` creates databases with 16 KiB pages (`PRAGMA page_size`, which applies only to a database that has no content yet) and bounds its WAL by the database: after a commit, when the `-wal` file is larger than the database file, the writer stops the background checkpointer's PASSIVE checkpoints and runs `wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` itself after each commit until one completes. The background checkpointer never let the WAL restart while the writer kept committing, so `sqlbench` (125,000 states x 7 commits) left a 14.4 GB WAL beside a 4.1 GB database; a TRUNCATE on the checkpointer's own connection blocked the writer past its 5 s busy timeout (`database is locked`), and SQLite's own `wal_autocheckpoint` in the writer (1,000 or 16,384 pages) took 205-208 us a commit. With 4 KiB pages commits took 114.7 us, with 8, 16 and 32 KiB pages and a 2 GiB cache 63.0, 57.6 and 68.0 us, and with 16 KiB pages, the default 256 MiB cache and the bounded WAL 90.5 us (74.6 us unbounded), the WAL staying under 1.8 GB. Random 67-byte content keys put each new record in a random leaf of the key index, so a group's commit writes every leaf it touched to the WAL; larger pages need fewer leaves and levels for the same keys. (11) Natively, `SharedStore`'s driver and its groups and pipelined reads run on one process-wide thread with a current-thread Tokio runtime of its own (`nanocode-durability-store`, started by the first store), not on the host's runtime, so the store pipeline is never queued behind agent tasks; `SharedStore::new` therefore no longer needs a Tokio runtime natively. At 5 x 25,000 on SQLite (2026-11-03, load average 23 to 31 from other processes) a 37,629-commit group's `commit_many` took 46.6 s while the SQLite writer ran no job longer than 8.7 s, its reply waiting for a busy worker to poll it; on the dedicated thread a 21,946-commit group took 3.9 s for 3.6 s of SQL, and the whole run settled in 452 s with 581 s of user and 737 s of system CPU, against 2,166 s, 1,144 s and 1,455 s on 24ef1d91f (every change in (6) to (11) included). (12) With several writers a launched group takes a `writers`th of the forming group (rounded up) and leaves the rest for the next free writer, where it used to take all of it: once a backlog had formed, one Postgres backend inserted a group of 100,000 commits for a minute while the other connections idled. At 5 x 10,000 on Postgres (2026-11-03, two interleaved runs each, load average 22 to 41) the swarm settled in 73.2 s and 71.9 s instead of 126.5 s and 101.0 s, with 76 and 66 s of system CPU instead of 161 and 100; one writer (SQLite) takes the whole forming group as before. (13) `PostgresSql::connect(config, connections)` opens a pool whose connection tasks run on a process-wide thread with a current-thread Tokio runtime of its own (`nanocode-postgres`, no TLS); `pool(clients)` still takes clients whose connections the host drives. Hosts that drive them on the runtime their agents run on starve them under load: at 5 x 25,000, with the connections spawned on the swarm's runtime, `pg_stat_activity` showed a group's transaction waiting on `ClientWrite` for 2 min 9 s, Postgres unable to send rows nobody read, while every other group waited on the rows it held. 5 x 25,000 then settled in 568 s on Postgres with (12) and this; at 5 x 10,000 the two measured within noise (83.7 s and 79.3 s on the agents' runtime, 66.6 s and 98.0 s with the second run at load average 55). (14) `SubagentControl::set_queued_spawns(true)` makes a spawn beyond `set_max_concurrency` wait for a turn slot instead of failing with `sub-agent concurrency limit of N has been reached`, so a root can hand a million spawns to `Promise.allSettled` and the runtime runs `limit` of them at once. Turn slots are a FIFO (`Capacity::acquire`, a `Mailbox` owning the waiters, `Acquire` a future that releases a slot granted after it was dropped): a running child's message turn waits in a queue served before the spawn queue (`Waiter::Resident` before `Waiter::Spawn`), because a child that has queued messages cannot be evicted, and behind a hundred thousand queued spawns every finished child that got a peer message stayed resident (at 1 x 5,000 with a limit of 1,000, 3,441 of 4,224 resident children were idle with queued messages). Harnesses wait on that queue rather than on a `watch` every release woke. `spawn_agent` takes its slot before it starts the task that builds the child, whose future was 13.8 KB while it waited; a waiting spawn now costs about 8.6 KB. Finished children also free what they held: a child's `AgentHandle` leaves `Registry::session_handles` when its harness closes (rehydration restores ancestors, which register theirs again), whose builder recipes kept about 5 KB of each finished child; `RunnerAgents` drops entries of dead agents when its map reaches a power of two, which ends their notice tasks (4.2 KB a child); and children with the same output schema share one compiled validator (1.4 KB each). At 1 x 10,000 with a limit of 1,000 (2026-11-04, `output/hand-wiring/heaptree-end.sh`, MallocStackLogging), the live heap when every child had finished, the SQLite cache aside, fell from about 59 KB a child to 12.5 KB; 5 x 2,000 on SQLite peaked at 1,380 MB of RSS with a limit of 1,000 and 4,702 MB without one, settling in 8.0 s and 9.0 s. (15) `ToolsBuilder::nested_call_limit(n)` (native, `CodeModeRuntime::set_nested_call_limit`; unbounded by default) bounds the nested calls a Code Mode cell has in flight: past it the cell's calls wait in the cell in call order, each input kept as its JSON text, and start as earlier ones finish (a journaled cell records them as queued), so a root that hands `Promise.allSettled` 100,000 `spawn_agent` calls holds 100,000 pending promises instead of 100,000 dispatched spawns. The cell's `tools.*` facade (`code-tools.mjs`) now returns the invoker's promise instead of wrapping it in an async function and marks it observed with one shared no-op handler, so each call holds two fewer promises and its result settles two microtask turns earlier. At 1 x 50,000 with 40,000 calls pending (2026-11-04, MallocStackLogging) the root cell's allocations fell from 273.8 MB to 155.9 MB (6.8 KB to 3.9 KB a pending call), and 1 x 50,000 with a limit of 500 peaked at 2,372 MB of RSS against 2,566 MB without one. (16) A finished child holds about half what it did. When a child's harness closes the registry calls the hook `Registry::set_session_released` installs, which the runtime points at `HostServices::unregister`, so `CodeModeExecutors`, `RunnerAgents` and the code-mode host map forget the child's session (children registered through `track_children` were never unregistered; about 1.5 KB each); a per-agent status queue retires once it drains (its count moves from one job to `RETIRED_QUEUE`, and a later status starts a new queue, so per-agent order holds) instead of keeping its channel for good (1.3 KB); the child journal adds to `Fenced::committed` only the keys its non-deferred saves staged, since the deferred path stages every record anyway (1.4 KB); children with the same output schema share one `Arc<Value>` beside the shared validator; a session's change `watch` is created when a waiter subscribes and dropped once none is left; `completion_dispatch` drops an agent's sequencer once nothing else holds it (`Sequencer::is_unshared`); and the registry boxes each `ChildSession` (920 bytes, 690 with `StoredRuntime::Checkpoint` boxed too), so the session map's spare buckets cost a pointer each. At 2 x 25,000 with a limit of 2,000 (2026-11-04, `heaptree3.sh`, MallocStackLogging) the live heap when every child had finished, the SQLite cache aside, fell from 636 MB to 306 MB (12.7 KB to 6.1 KB a child); without logging the run peaked at 3,316 MB of RSS instead of 4,002 MB with the same CPU (171.6+33.7 s against 168.7+33.2 s), and 5 x 2,000 at 1,184 to 1,198 MB instead of 1,313 to 1,344. (17) `SqliteSql::open_with`, `open_async_with` and `from_connection_with` take `SqliteOptions { cache_bytes }`, the writer connection's page cache (256 MiB by default, as before); SQLite allocates cache pages only as it fills them, so a small database costs nothing more under a large setting. Every record's random 67-byte key lands in a random leaf of the key index, and once that index outgrows the cache each insert reads its leaf back: with 256 MiB, `sqlbench` (125,000 states x 7 rounds of 10,000 commits, 4.4 GB, 2026-11-04, two runs at load average 14 to 31) committed at 133.7 and 107.0 us a commit and its writer spent half its busy samples in `pread`; with 2 GiB at 79.6 and 68.9 us, with 4 GiB at 76.8 us, and with a 1 TiB `mmap_size` (`SQLITE_MAX_MMAP_SIZE` raised at build, since SQLite caps mmap at 2 GiB) at 109.7 us. 10 x 100,000 (one million subagents, `QUEUED`, a limit of 5,000, `NESTED_LIMIT=500`, `SQLITE_CACHE_MB=4096`; 2026-11-04) completed every agent in 2,801 s with 4,560+3,701 s of CPU and 9.5 GB of peak RSS, wrote a 32 GB database, and a reopen restored all 1,000,000 agents in 295 s; the same run with the 256 MiB cache was at 274,000 agents after 430 s where this one took 300 s (that one shared the disk with `sqlbench` from 270 s on). Late in the run the writer is bound by the WAL instead: each commit's 4 or so new keys dirty that many 16 KiB leaves, so the WAL grew at 250 MB/s and `pwrite` took 45% of the writer's busy samples. (18) `Sql::readers` (default 1; `SqliteSql` answers its read-only connections, one per CPU, and `PostgresSql` its connections) lets `SqlStore::read_records` split a batch over that many transactions run at once, since records never change and need no common snapshot; a reopened root reads its 100,000 agent records and then their profiles each as one batch, which one reader used to serve alone. At 10 x 10,000 on SQLite with a 4 GiB cache (2026-11-04) a reopen restored the 100,000 agents in 4.61 s instead of 8.94 s. Smaller pages do not help the WAL-bound writer of (17): `sqlbench` (250,000 states x 6 rounds of 2,000-commit groups, 7.5 GB, 4 GiB cache) committed at 124.2 us a commit with 16 KiB pages, 133.6 with 8 KiB and 153.5 with 4 KiB. (19) The registry answers its per-completion and per-root-turn questions from indexes instead of scanning a root's sessions: `Sessions` keeps the parked completions, the active direct children, the direct children marked `in_spawning_turn` and the direct children by host context, the first three refreshed from the ids `get_mut` and `insert` mark stale (as residency already was) and the last at `insert`, since a child's host context never changes. `park_completion` asked whether any session's completion was parked for every completion it routed, `needs_root_watch` on every root run event, and `unpark_completions` and `root_turn_ended` on every root turn, each a walk of all 100,000 sessions of a root inside the registry's one mailbox; with 100,000 children a root, `park_completion`'s closure took 6.9% of the process's busy samples on Postgres at 450,000 agents. (20) A Postgres group commit locks the records it references `FOR KEY SHARE` (`GROUP_LOCK_RECORDS_PG`, in key order) and inserts the missing ones with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, where it used to take each existing record's row lock with `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE ... WHERE false`. A key-share lock still holds off a collection's delete until the group commits, but key-share locks do not conflict with each other, while the update lock serialized every concurrent group that referenced one shared record (a child's shared prompt chunks, or any identical message, such as a reply of `ok`): at 450,000 agents two to four of the eight backends waited on `Lock: transactionid` in the records insert. With (19), 10 x 10,000 on Postgres (2026-11-04) settled in 77.2 s instead of 86.6 s at 5.7 GB of peak RSS instead of 6.6, and 5 x 2,000 in 8.3 s instead of 14.1; SQLite measured the same within noise (77.5 s against 79.3 s). (21) `SharedStore`'s grouped driver no longer stops forming a group at a queued write whose state another group is committing: it converts that write (prefetching its deferred content while groups are in flight, as before), holds it beside the forming group until its state's group completes, and keeps taking later writes of other states, skipping any further write of a state that is forming, held or skipped, so each state's writes stay in order and none passes a barrier. Before, the first such write at the head of the queue (usually a root's journal, which every group carries) stopped the forming group, and a group holding one could not launch while any writer was free, so with eight Postgres writers most backends sat `ClientRead` while commits waited: at 10 x 100,000 a commit waited 1,019 ms on average for groups that took 224 ms. At 10 x 10,000 (2026-11-04) a commit waited 248 ms instead of 473 ms on Postgres, which settled in 75.3 s instead of 82.4 s, and 194 to 200 ms instead of 278 to 304 ms on SQLite, which settled in 82.4 and 83.8 s against 85.8 s (its one writer was already the limit). Ten roots of 100,000 subagents on Postgres with (19) and (20) and before this (2026-11-04, eight connections, a limit of 5,000) completed every agent in 1,749 s with 3,658+1,656 s of CPU and 6.8 GB of peak RSS, settled in 1,933 s, wrote 31 GB, and a reopen restored all 1,000,000 agents in 118 s. (22) Ten roots of 100,000 subagents on SQLite with (16) to (21) (2026-11-04, a 4 GiB cache, sharing the machine with a WASM build, two deploys and a workerd fault run for its last half) completed in 2,801 s as with (17) alone, at 8.8 GB of peak RSS, while a commit waited 856 ms instead of 1,147 ms and a reopen restored the 1,000,000 agents in 102 s instead of 295 s. At 10 x 10,000 on Postgres a concurrency limit of 10,000 or 20,000 finished no sooner than 5,000 (84.4 s and 85.8 s against 81.2 s) while a commit's wait grew with it (555 and 784 ms against 272 ms): the swarm's process used 6.2 of the 10 cores and Postgres 1.2 to 1.7, so past 5,000 running children the CPU, not the store's latency, bounds it. (23) `PostgresSql` runs a statement list that only reads (every statement a `SELECT`) on the connection without `BEGIN` and `COMMIT`: at Postgres's default READ COMMITTED each statement takes its own snapshot inside a transaction too, so the two round trips bought nothing, and a peek or record read held one of the eight connections a group commit also needs for three round trips instead of one. And `SqlStore::read_records` splits a batch over the readers only into parts of at least `READ_PART_KEYS` (1,024) keys, so a reopened root's 100,000 agents still read over every connection while a child's few-key load stays one statement on one connection: (18) split every batch of two keys or more over all eight, so each such load held eight connections. At 10 x 10,000 on Postgres (2026-11-04, two interleaved runs each at load average 45 to 59 from other processes) the swarm settled in 78.2 s and 96.0 s instead of 101.2 s and 107.7 s, with 22.6 to 23.9 s of system CPU instead of 35.0 to 35.9, and a reopen restored the 100,000 agents in 3.0 to 3.2 s instead of 6.4 to 8.2 s. Separate read connections did worse: with eight more for reads alone, a commit waited 13 to 50 ms but a peek 0.57 to 1.36 s, the faster children drove each root through 1,316 to 7,447 turns instead of about 320, each resending a history of up to 4 MB, and the swarm settled in 70 to 165 s. Ten roots of 100,000 subagents on Postgres with (21) and this (2026-11-04, the machine at load average 45 to 78 from other builds for most of the run) completed every agent in 2,244 s and settled in 2,653 s, at 7.5 GB of peak RSS, and a reopen restored the 1,000,000 agents in 116 s; under that load a commit waited 602 ms on average but up to 56 s, and one child's harness ran 22 minutes.
- Spawned and restored Claude children share their parent's system context (the environment section and project instructions, `share_system_context`, which forks already used) when they run in the parent's workspace and the parent's environment section still names today's date, instead of each walking the workspace for git and instruction files: at 5 x 2,000 children building them was 4.1% of the process's samples (`ProjectHome::discover` and the `stat`s of `environment_section`). A child spawned the day after its parent started computes its own, so its date is current, and a child sees the instruction files its parent read, as a fork does. The Code Mode catalog a cell offers (`shared_nested_tool_metadata`, every nested tool's name, description and schemas as JSON) is shared process-wide by registries with the same definitions, through a list of weak references, where each registry built its own (3.9% of the samples). At 5 x 2,000 children on SQLite (2026-11-04, two interleaved runs at load average 150 to 202) the swarm used 28.4 and 29.2 s of user CPU instead of 29.7 and 30.2 s, 3.1 and 3.5 s of system CPU instead of 6.2 and 4.6 s, and peaked at 4,386 and 4,246 MB instead of 4,695 and 5,244 MB. A cell's encoded catalog (`CellTools`, the definitions, descriptions and schemas JSON that each `CodeModeRuntime` kept for its last catalog) is shared the same way by runtimes whose catalogs are equal, found first by the shared metadata's pointer: at 3,000 children running at once each session held its own (36.7 MB, 12 KB a child, the largest Rust allocation site after SQLite's cache), and at 5 x 2,000 children the swarm used 25.3 and 26.4 s of user CPU instead of 27.3 and 27.2 s.
- `nanocode::process::Redactor` (crate `nanocode_process_contracts`) replaces secret values in text (`text`), in every string of a JSON value (`json`, object keys untouched) and in stored JSON text (`json_text`, redacted as text when it does not parse), longest form first, skipping values shorter than its minimum. `Redactor::new(min)` also matches each value JSON-escaped (`json_escape`), as JSON text or a string literal in Code Mode code holds one with quotes, backslashes or control characters; `Redactor::raw(min)` matches it only as it is. Each value reads as the placeholder `add(value, placeholder)` gives (which says whether it added a new form), `[NAME]` for `named` pairs or `[REDACTED]` for `values`; `forms()` lists them for hosts that also replace them themselves, such as with SQL. `redact_and_limit` runs on `Redactor::raw(MIN_REDACTED_SECRET_BYTES)` unchanged; tempoxyz/pyramid redacts organization tool results, secrets saved from a message and typed text in turn traces with it instead of three copies of its own.
- [Fast Rust Builds](https://matklad.github.io/2021/09/04/fast-rust-builds.html)
- Keep logs, traces, and benchmark artifacts in ignored output/. Never commit credentials or personal test data.
- Answer performance and telemetry questions from the recorded OTLP data with `node scripts/otel.mjs`: `tails`, `outliers`, `trace <id>`, `sessions`, `report` and raw `sql` over an incremental SQLite cache in output/telemetry.
- The local OTLP trace file is written by `file::TraceProcessor`: span end reserves a queue slot with a compare-and-swap and sends over an unbounded channel without I/O or blocking, and the worker writes each frontier it snapshots as one grouped OTLP JSON line. At most `OTEL_BSP_MAX_QUEUE_SIZE` ended spans wait, 2048 by default as in the OpenTelemetry specification and opentelemetry_sdk 0.32.1, and an unparsable value fails installation; beyond that span end drops and counts spans and the next force_flush or shutdown reports the total, because an unbounded queue reached 12 GB RSS in 2 s at 1M spans/s against 190-310 MB bounded. force_flush and shutdown are synchronous, so call them outside Tokio worker threads.
- tracing-opentelemetry ends a span at its last exit, so keep a span entered for the whole operation it measures and record `duration_ns` when the measured work excludes queueing. `--telemetry` and NANOCODE_TELEMETRY select off, agent (the default) or all, and `nanocode hand` defaults to off (`ObservabilityOutputArgs::builder` takes the command's default): a hand's agent telemetry recorded every nested tool call's arguments and output into `<home>/log/otel/<process>/traces.jsonl`, which nothing on a visitor's machine prunes (about 3.8 KB per small brain callback and a 1 MiB argument in full; one benchmark hand wrote 57 MB in a minute and this machine held 3.7 GB in 906 process directories on 2026-10-28), and its writer thread cost the hand a fifth of its CPU: in nanocode-ts's `bench/hands.ts` hand CPU per turn fell from 163-166 to 112-131 ms for 500 sequential callbacks and from 101-107 to 81-86 ms for 500 parallel ones, with turn times unchanged within noise. The brain's telemetry covers the hand's calls; pass `--telemetry agent` to record them on the hand too; spans with `target: TUI_TARGET` are exported only with all, and `nc_metrics` events feed only the metrics layer. Agent-path spans (model.call, tool.call, tool.execute, code_mode.cell, code_mode.cell_actor and code_mode.wait) carry `session.id` themselves because parents are exported only when they end, and `typed::restore_integers` turns u64 fields, which tracing-opentelemetry records as Debug strings, back into integers.
- otel.mjs ingests complete new lines of every `<home>/log/otel/*/traces.jsonl` and metrics.jsonl by byte offset into output/telemetry/telemetry.sqlite; table changes bump SCHEMA_VERSION and views are recreated on every open. `spans.dur_ms` is the larger of the span length and `duration_ns`, `session_id` comes from the span, its nearest exported ancestor (`session_from = 'parent'`) or the only session among its trace's agent spans (`'trace'`), `ui = 1` marks terminal spans, other attributes are JSON in `attrs`, and tool arguments and outputs are `events` rows. Resources carry process.pid, process.executable.path and nanocode.executable.modified for `builds` and `--by build`. `breakdown` assigns each moment of a turn to model calls, tools, store writes or other, `outliers` counts slow overlapping spans in other processes to expose machine-wide stalls, and `prune --older-than` deletes fully ingested raw directories that no live process writes.
- Prefer simple code and small changes. Preserve unrelated work. Use subagents only when explicitly requested.
- `nanocode::ModelInfo` is a model as a catalog lists it, from its `ModelCapabilities`: harness, ID, display name, accepted efforts (lowest first), default effort, fast mode, service tiers and reasoning modes, with `runs(thinking, fast)`; `ModelInfo::of(&selection)` reads native capabilities and `model_catalog(transport)` lists every built-in harness's models. Context windows stay with each backend. tempoxyz/pyramid's modes keep their own model allowlist and read efforts, defaults and fast mode from it instead of deriving them. Native hosts that call a provider directly with an API key build its client with `ClaudeClient::messages_api(base_url, api_key, connect_timeout, idle_timeout)` (the Messages endpoint under `base_url`) and `OpenAiBuilder::http_timeouts(connect_timeout, idle_timeout)`, which install the rustls provider and build the reqwest client with those connect and read timeouts.
- A host that runs the CLI with a Codex endpoint of its own (`ModelClients::openai_endpoint`) names the models that endpoint serves with `ModelClients::models(models)`: `-m`, `/model`, the model picker and `nanocode models` then offer exactly those as the Codex models, in the host's order, beside the built-in Claude models, and refuse a catalog Codex model the host did not list (`ModelClients::parse_model`; `host::parse_model` and hosts that list no models are unchanged). An ID outside the catalog is a `Model::Custom`, so it gets the custom-model prompt naming it, low default thinking and the provider's reported cost instead of a GPT model's. gakonst/nanocode-machines lists what a paid endpoint's `/v1/models` returns, where it used to send one `CUSTOM_MODEL` by rewriting each request's `model` while the CLI showed and prompted as Luna. `NANOCODE_OPENAI_BASE_URL` (`custom_endpoint.rs`) does the same without a host of its own: unless the host supplies its own Codex client, Codex sends to `<base>/responses` over HTTPS with `NANOCODE_OPENAI_API_KEY`, else `OPENAI_API_KEY`, as its bearer token (none without either), and after parsing its arguments the CLI reads the OpenAI model list at `NANOCODE_MODELS_URL` (default `<base>/models`; a host with its own client may set only that), failing when the list fails or is empty. Each listed ID is a `Model::Custom` (`nanocode::CustomModel`), even one the catalog knows, so an endpoint's model never gets a built-in model's service tiers or thinking limits. With models listed, a session started without `-m` (or with only `--harness codex`) runs the first of them at its own default thinking, where it ran Claude Opus 5.5 at xhigh. Reasoning summary and content stream events without `summary_index` or `content_index` read as index 0, as LiteLLM's Responses stream leaves them out, where every such turn failed with "Responses API event did not match its declared type" (#1115; its author checked a LiteLLM endpoint of 43 models: `nanocode models` listed only them, `-m gpt-6.1-sol` was refused with their list, and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, GLM-5.3 Flash and Kimi K3 completed turns naming themselves).
- Model a value's lifecycle as one enum whose variants carry only what that phase has, not as booleans and `Option`s that must agree with each other, so a state no transition reaches cannot be built: Codex's `Response` stream (`Progress`: `Running`, `Succeeded`, `Failed`, where three flags and an `Option` result allowed a finished run without a result), the zsh drain's `Reading`, the lazy durable executions' `Deferred` (the existing flag, journal claim, attempt and Claude's initial settings, which only a deferred acquisition has) and `Acquired` (whether the acquisition reopened state, known only once it ran), a cell journal's `Saving` (`Unsaved`, `Saved` with what was persisted and the next update's sequence, `Stopped`, held through `Owned` instead of a `Sequencer` beside four atomics), the ChatGPT connection's `Handshake`, a cell's `CellLife`, and a zsh server command's `Busy` (`Stage`: `Forking` holding the caller's reply and release channel, `Running`, `Unreported` once it exited without its EXIT trap's status and the server's wait was asked, `Waited`; and `Owed`, the exit sender and the FIFO keepers that always went together, where an optional reply, an optional PID and two booleans could disagree), and an adopted agent's `Lifecycle` (`Live`, `Stale` once a failure needs a reopen, `Closed` for good after `disconnect` or `shutdown`), where `stopped` and `closed` flags let a heal that finished after a close mark the closed agent live again; a closed agent now reports `is_stopped`. A subagent's `ChildSession` keeps its completion as `Completion` (`None`, `Pending { revision, attempt }`, `Parked { revision, attempt }`, where a parked flag without a pending revision was possible and checked for everywhere), its harness as `Residency` (`Detached`, `Resident(harness)`, `Evicted`, which an `evicted` flag beside an optional harness encoded) and its runtime when not resident as `StoredRuntime` (`None`, `Checkpoint`, `Journaled`, which an optional checkpoint beside a `journaled_runtime` flag encoded); the journal record keeps its three completion fields, so stored journals are unchanged. A child's in-flight tool call keeps its state as `CallState` (`Restored` with the two flags its journal record carries, or `Live` with its parent call and a `CallProgress` of `Started`, `Recorded`, `Yielded`, `Settled` or `Committable`) instead of `result_recorded`, `yielded`, `live`, `settled` and `committable` booleans; it serializes through `JournaledCall`, the record's former shape, field for field. A Claude agent's `State` keeps one `AgentLife` (running, stopped by a failure or `disconnect`, closing after `shutdown`) moved by `stop` (only from running) and `close`, where `stopped` and `closing` flags were stored one after the other. On 2026-11-01 a nanocode-ts brain in Node (`output/subagents-check.ts`: two children with `max_resident_subagents` 1, a follow-up message to the first after it completed, then a reopen) behaved the same on the release build-f964a9d module and on this build. On 2026-11-03 the same went for: the Codex HTTPS stream (`ResponsesHttpStream` is `Chat` or `Sse`, and an `SseStream` holds one `SseBody`, `Reqwest`, `Layered` or `Host`, until the body ends, where four `Option`s of which one was set and an `ended` flag said it), a Chat Completions stream's `ChatPhase` (`Reading(body)`, `Drained`, `Done`, for an optional body, `ended` and `finished`), a freeform call's `InputScan` (`Seeking`, `Reading(at)`, `Closed`, for a cursor and `closed`), the Codex WebSocket and the time it connected as one `Option<(ResponsesSocket, Instant)>`, the Codex driver's `Intake` (`Open`, `Closed`, `Detached`, for `commands_open` and `detached`; `close` keeps `Detached`), a Codex tool call's `ToolStep` (`Run { recover }` or `Recorded { output, persist }`, for `interrupted`, `recover` and an optional recovered output) and the turn loop's `first_batch`, which `can_drain_steers` only ever negated; the Claude Messages SSE reader's pending data as `Option<FrameData>` (for a line range and a joined string) and its stream as `unfold` over `Option<SseState>` (for `done`); the MCP SSE parser's `Start` (`Bom(matched)`, `Body`, for `started` and `bom_matched`) and its event as `Option<Event>` (for `has_fields`); an attachment connection's `Drain` (`Attached`, `Requested`, `Acknowledged`, for `detaching` and `draining`), its ping as `Option<(nonce, sent at)>` (for two `Option`s and an `expect`) and a retained call's `Progress` (`Running(task)`, `Pending`, `Settled(receipt)`, for an optional task and an optional receipt, and the receipt is `Utf8Bytes`, so a resend shares it instead of copying it); a durable session driver's `Ownership` (`Unacquired(Seed)`, with `Existing`, `Described(record)` or `New(record)`, or `Owned(token)`, for `owner`, `pending_record` and `create`) and `SharedStore`'s group scan `Admits` (`Writes`, `Reads`, `Nothing`, for `ordered` and `barrier`); a spawned process's `Reaping` (`Waiting(task)`, `Reaped(status)`, for two `Option`s of which one was set, an unreachable error and an `expect`) and a zsh server owner's `Life` (`Serving`, `Retiring`, `Stopped`, for `retiring` and `alive`); and the TUI `RenderScheduler`'s pending `Option<Frame { deadline, streaming }>` (for a deadline and a `streaming` flag that only a deadline can have). Then a Claude background Bash job's `AtomicPhase` (`Foreground`, `Background`, `Delivered`, advanced with `fetch_max`, for `backgrounded` and `delivered` atomics that could disagree) and a subagent harness's `Contract` (`Pending { assignment }`, `Delivered`, for `contract_delivered` and `rehydrated_assignment`). Then a subagent child's turn, `Option<ActiveTurn { revision, stage }>` with `TurnStage` `Working`, `Steering` or `Submitted(output)` (for `active`, `active_instruction_revision`, `steering` and `submitted_output`, which a turn's end cleared together; the journal record keeps `accepted_output` and `accepted_revision`, and the settled revision of a finished turn is its own revision, without the fallback that could not be reached); a subagent scope's `ScopeLife` (`Open`, `Closing`, `Frozen`, for `closing` and `journal_frozen`, set only together by `close_all`); `SubagentActivity::restoration`, a public `Restoration` (`Restored`, `Restoring`, `Failed(error)`) where `restoring` and `restore_failure` were two fields (serialized as `restoration`; no host on this machine read either); the Claude agent's `ThinkingMode` (`Automatic`, `Adaptive`, `Disabled`), one `AtomicThinkingMode` where `set_thinking` stored `adaptive_thinking` and `thinking_disabled` one after the other (the checkpoint records keep their two fields); and the zstd decoder's `DecoderState::Frame { frame, stage }` (for a state and an optional current frame that had to agree; whether input arrived is read from the bytes received). Also a Codex run's `UsageCost` (`Unreported`, or `Reported { rate_estimated, estimated }`, for `reported`, `rate_estimated` and `estimated_cost`; a turn's usage is built with its `CostStatus`, so the hidden `TurnUsageCounts` and `TurnUsage::from_counts` went), a Responses attempt's `Replay` (`Incremental(previous_response_id)` or `Full`, for `full_replay` and a `previous_response_id` it masked), the Codex builder's `PromptCacheKey` (`Prefix`, `PerSession`, `Explicit(key)`, for an optional key and `per_session`), a tool-host admission's `Grant` (`Fast`, `Shared { token, origin, epoch }`, `Exclusive { token }`, for `fast`, `exclusive` and the fields only a gated admission has; output/hand-wiring's `gate2` ran 1,800 rounds of 48 tasks, 384,647 exclusive and 161,425 cancelled, with no violation), the zsh dispatcher's `Probed { probe, direct }` (the direct-spawn snapshot lives with the probe it was taken under, and a probe of another fingerprint drops it), and the egress proxy's response `Framing` (`Chunked`, `Length`, `UntilClose`, for `chunked` and `length`). output/hand-wiring's `swarm` (200 flat, 5 x 20 tree, 100 waited, each restored), `pool` and `claude_pool` (300 sessions each), `execcheck` and `directcheck` (no differences between the server and direct paths) ran on a copy of master with these changes. Checked on 2026-11-03 by a throwaway decoder harness over the `zstd` CLI's frames (levels 1 and 19, no checksum, `--long=27`, two frames, a skippable frame) fed 1, 7, 4,096, 65,536 and 1 MiB bytes at a time, all decoded exactly, with truncation, a bad checksum, `single_frame` over two frames and a mid-frame reset failing as before. Left as they are: `*_explicit` flags beside a configured value (whether a host chose it, a product rather than exclusive states), Claude's `automatic_cache` and `cache_one_hour` (the TTL applies to every cache marker, automatic or not), the flags of Claude's durable `Conversation` and `Cursor` (independent facts in stored checkpoints), and single-flag drop guards, which are already two-state. Nothing a peer, store or model sees changed: the eight SDK crates build natively and for WASM and pass clippy, as does nanocode-tui, and output/hand-wiring's `pool` (Codex over HTTPS) and `claude_pool` ran 200 sessions each against `responses-mock.mjs` with 200 deltas a reply, `claude_pool` also with each event's data split over several `data:` lines ending in CRLF, and `execcheck` gave the same exit codes and output for five commands through the zsh server.
- Avoid magic constants, arbitrary caps, and speculative defensive checks. Limits must come from a real protocol constraint, measured resource requirement, or explicit caller policy; document their source. Surface invalid states instead of hiding them with silent truncation, clamping, or fallback values.
- Work in the main checkout on master and push each completed change directly to master. Never create worktrees or feature branches. Commit only your own changes and leave other dirty or untracked files alone; if a push is rejected, rebase onto origin/master, resolve conflicts and push again.
- Hosts that drive a root agent themselves (`without_subagent_continuation`) wait for its subagents with `SubagentControl::running(root)`, the pending, running or closing ones, and `RunningSubagents::next_completion(timeout)`, which is woken by the registry's change notifications rather than polling and returns the first to complete, fail or be interrupted with its `completion_continuation_prompt` (`None` once all are closed, or on timeout); `SubagentControl::stop_all(root, timeout)` interrupts them as `cancel_all` does and says whether they stopped in time (`None` when none is pending, running or closing: the root started none, or every one already ended, so a call still running is the root's own and the host need not treat the turn as interrupted). `RunSteered` carries the `message_id` a host steered with (`steer_with_id`), as both backends already emitted, so a host knows which messages a pass read instead of counting `run.steered` events. `nanocode_types_core::events::AssistantText` folds streamed assistant deltas and final messages into each message's text so far, keyed by item ID or `model-<model_call_index>`, and `AssistantTextUpdate::delta` is the text a streamed delta added (`None` for a complete message), so a host that persists or forwards incremental text appends it instead of copying the whole message on every delta, which tempoxyz/pyramid's PR 219 did, quadratic in the message's length. `UsageRecord` (`UsageRecord::new(model, &TurnUsage)`, `From<&RunTerminal>`, `TurnResult::usage_record`) is one turn's or run's tokens and cost under its model. tempoxyz/pyramid's engine used to poll `agents()` every 500 ms, count steers, key and append deltas, and map both usages itself.
- Image eviction boundaries take precedence over optional Claude prefix markers (2026-11-10). A custom system prompt on the subscription wire used all four protocol cache breakpoints for tool, system, identity and automatic tail, so the production prefix-first allocator left no image boundary; its zero-spare guard could not even reclaim a system slot. The production request now marks image floors first and then adds optional system/tool boundaries only within the final wire budget, including the subscription identity. The existing prefix API retains its reserve-one policy; the floors-first API can use all remaining slots. Invalid over-budget layouts are errors rather than saturating to zero. The previous image floor excludes the latest image-bearing message rather than the last two history messages, so an image-free text tail cannot erase the boundary needed when eviction advances. output/cache-budget-v1 compares authentic pinned full upstream tools/system blocks and production HistoryMessage, image eviction and prepare_cached_wire: 168 baseline and 168 candidate requests over default/custom systems, API/subscription wire, top-level/tool-result images, image-free tails, 11-image bursts and no-image controls. All 147 candidate image-bearing requests carry every requested floor and all 168 requests stay within four markers; custom subscription baseline has no floors in 84 requests. At the thirteenth ordinary image read the prior unchanged message prefix is explicitly cached through message 129 (custom tool) or 124 (top-level), instead of no message-prefix boundary; with an image-free tail it is message 105. These are offline wire observations, not measured server cache hits. Commands: CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/home/ubuntu/nanocode-eval/repo/output/cache-prefix-repro-v1/target cargo run --offline --locked --manifest-path output/cache-budget-v1/{baseline,candidate}-harness/Cargo.toml -- output/cache-budget-v1/{baseline,candidate}; python3 output/cache-budget-v1/analyze.py; cargo check --locked --offline --lib -p nanocode-backend-claude -p nanocode-backend-claude-client, again with --target wasm32-unknown-unknown (passed); git diff --check (passed). No live inference in this repair validation.
- On Linux every TLS verifier shares one parsed copy of the system trust roots (2026-11-10). reqwest 0.13 builds a `rustls-platform-verifier` `Verifier` for each client, and the vendored verifier called `rustls_native_certs::load_native_certs()` in each, which reads `/etc/ssl/certs/ca-certificates.crt` and every file in `/etc/ssl/certs` and parses them all: one `nc exec` opened 2,420 files under `/etc/ssl/certs` (ten loads of 241 entries) and read the bundle 20 times, and PEM base64 decoding was the largest user-space cost in a profile of it. `verification::others::system::roots` keeps the parsed `RootCertStore` in an `ArcSwapOption` keyed on `SSL_CERT_FILE` and `SSL_CERT_DIR` and reloads it after 300 s, the age `nanocode_tls` already uses; the crate exports it as `system_roots`, and the sandbox's egress `Tls::new` takes its roots from it instead of loading them again, so a process loads them once. Interleaved on an AMD EPYC 4245P (Ubuntu 24.04, bench/cli on the cli-bench branch: `suite.mjs --parts=firstframe,oneshot --rounds=30` under `tools/netguard.sh`, release builds of 5aa3971ee with and without the change, 2026-11-10): a one-shot `nc exec --ephemeral` took 8.96 ms instead of 18.9 (CPU 9.9 ms instead of 22.0, spawn to request 6.7 ms instead of 16.9), and the terminal interface drew its first frame in 4.99 ms instead of 7.96 (CPU 12.2 ms instead of 23.7); `--version` did not change.
- `nc exec` flushes stdout whenever it has written every event already delivered, instead of at most once per display frame (2026-11-10). `stream_turn` still takes every queued event with `Turn::try_next` before one flush, so under load deltas share a write; at low rates each delta goes out as it arrives. The 60 Hz pacing (entry above) made each token wait 8 to 10 ms on average and 16 to 18 ms at p99 between the fixture writing it and stdout showing it, where fx, pi and Claude Code show it in 0.01 to 0.03 ms. Interleaved on an AMD EPYC 4245P (bench/cli `sweep.mjs --mode=cli --rates=100,1000,10000 --samples=10` under `tools/netguard.sh`, release builds, 2026-11-10): per-token lag p50 0.015, 0.010 and 0.008 ms at 100, 1,000 and 10,000 tok/s instead of 10.0, 8.0 and 8.3 (p99 0.04, 0.012 and 0.010 instead of 18, 16 and 16.5), whole-run CPU 268 ms instead of 251 at 10,000 tok/s (5.4 µs per token instead of 5.0), with the trust-root change also in the new build.
- The terminal interface paces streamed text at 120 Hz (`app::DISPLAY_FRAME` 8.333 ms) instead of 60 Hz (2026-11-10). At 60 Hz a token waited half a frame on average before it reached the screen, so nc trailed Codex and pi on on-screen lag at 1,000 tok/s while leading on CPU. Interleaved on an AMD EPYC 4245P (bench/cli `sweep.mjs --mode=tui --rates=100,1000,10000 --samples=10`, 120x40 PTY replayed through pyte, under `tools/netguard.sh`, release builds of 0fbd0054a with each pace, 2026-11-10): on-screen lag p50 0.12, 4.1 and 3.1 ms at 100, 1,000 and 10,000 tok/s instead of 7.1, 7.4 and 8.1 (p99 0.25, 8.3 and 8.5 instead of 16), against Codex 5.5 and pi 7.7 at 1,000 and fx 22 at 10,000; whole-session CPU 71, 119 and 592 ms instead of 57, 103 and 561 (fx 48, 217 and 2,452), and terminal bytes per token 58.6 instead of 32.8 at 10,000 tok/s.
